1
1. The canons of this Code are only for the Latin Church.

2
2. The Code, ordinarily, does not determine the rites that must be observed in the celebration of liturgical actions; Therefore, the liturgical laws in force until now retain their force except when any of them are contrary to the canons of the Code.
2. The violation of ecclesiastical laws and everything that contains reason for sin, with regard to the determination of guilt and the imposition of ecclesiastical penalties.
2. § 2 . As soon as the sentence has become enforceable, the judicial Vicar must notify it to the Ordinary of the place where the marriage was celebrated. And he must ensure that the nullity that has been declared and the prohibitions that may have been added are noted as soon as possible in the marriage book and in the baptism book.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Mitis Iudex Dominus Iesus, dated August 15, 2015)]:
Art. 4 — EVIDENCE

3
3. The canons of the Code do not abrogate or repeal the agreements of the Holy See with nations or with other political societies; Therefore, these agreements remain in force as before, without prejudice to the contrary provisions of this Code.

4
4. The acquired rights, as well as the privileges hitherto granted by the Apostolic See, both to natural and legal persons, that are in use and have not been revoked, remain intact unless they are expressly revoked by the canons of this Code.

5
5. § 1 . Universal or particular customs currently in force and contrary to these canons are totally suppressed if they are rejected in the canons of this Code, and they must not be allowed to revive in the future; The others are also suppressed, unless the Code expressly establishes otherwise, or they are centuries old or immemorial, which can be tolerated when, weighing the circumstances of the places and people, the Ordinary judges that it is not possible to suppress them.
§ 2 . The extralegal customs, both universal and particular, that are currently in force remain.

6
6. § 1 . Since the entry into force of this Code, the following are repealed:
1 the Code of Canon Law promulgated in 1917;
2 other laws, universal or particular, contrary to the prescriptions of this Code, unless otherwise expressly established regarding the particular ones;
3 any penal laws, universal or particular, promulgated by the Apostolic See, unless they are received in this same Code;
4 the other universal disciplinary laws on matters that are completely regulated in this Code.
§ 2 . To the extent that they reproduce ancient law, the canons of this Code must be understood also taking into account canonical tradition.

7
7. The law is established when it is promulgated.

8
8. § 1 . Universal ecclesiastical laws are promulgated by publication in the official Gazette Acta Apostolicae Sedis, unless, in particular cases, another method of promulgation has been prescribed; and come into force after three months from the date indicated in the corresponding number of the Minutes, unless they are immediately obligatory due to the very nature of the matter, or unless a longer or shorter vacation is specially and expressly established in the same law.
§ 2 . Particular laws are promulgated according to the manner determined by the legislator, and begin to be binding after one month from the day they were promulgated, unless another term is established in the same law.

9
9. Laws are for future events, not for past events, unless something is expressly provided for them.

10
10. Only those laws in which it is expressly established that an act is void or a person is unskilled must be considered invalidating or disqualifying.

11
11. Purely ecclesiastical laws bind those baptized into the Catholic Church and those who have been received into it, provided that they have sufficient reason and, if the law does not expressly provide otherwise, have reached the age of seven years.

12
12. § 1 . Universal laws bind all those for whom they have been given throughout the world.
§ 2 . All those who are in fact located in that territory are exempt from universal laws that are not in force in a given territory.
§ 3 . The laws enacted for a peculiar territory are binding, without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 13, to those for whom they have been given, if they have their domicile or quasi-domicile there and also live in fact in that place.

13
13. § 1 . Particular laws are not presumed personal, but territorial, unless otherwise stated.
§ 2 . Passers-by are not subject:
1 to the particular laws of their territory when they are outside it, unless their transgression causes damage in their own territory or they are personal laws;
2 nor to the laws of the territory in which they are located, except those that concern the protection of public order, determine the formalities that must be observed in the acts, or refer to immovable things located in the territory.
§ 3 . Vagrants are bound by the laws, both universal and particular, that are in force in the place where they are.

14
14. Laws, even if they are invalidating or disqualifying, do not bind in doubt of law; In case of factual doubt, the Ordinaries can dispense from them, provided that, in the case of a reserved dispensation, the authority usually grants it to the person who reserves it.

15
15. § 1 . Ignorance or error about invalidating or disabling laws does not prevent their effectiveness, as long as nothing else is expressly established.
§ 2 . Ignorance or error about a law, a penalty, one's own fact, or a well-known fact of another is not presumed; It is presumed, until the contrary is proven, about an external, non-notorious fact.

16
16. § 1 . The laws are authentically interpreted by the legislator and by the person to whom he has entrusted the power to interpret them authentically.
§ 2 . The authentic interpretation manifested in the form of a law has equal force with the law itself, and must be promulgated; It has retroactive effect if it only clarifies words of the law that are true in themselves; But if it restricts the law or extends it or explains what is doubtful, it has no retroactive effect.
§ 3 . But the interpretation made by a judicial ruling or administrative act in a particular case does not have the force of law, and only binds people and affects the things for which it was given.

17
17. Ecclesiastical laws must be understood according to the proper meaning of the words, considered in the text and in the context; If it is doubtful and obscure, one must resort to parallel places, when there are any, to the purpose and circumstances of the law and to the intention of the legislator.

18
18. Laws that establish a penalty, restrict the free exercise of rights, or contain an exception to the law must be strictly interpreted.

19
19. When, on a given matter, there is no express prescription of universal or particular law or custom, the case, unless it is criminal, must be decided taking into account the laws given for similar cases, the general principles of law applied with canonical equity, the jurisprudence and practice of the Roman Curia, and the common and constant opinion of doctors.

20
20. The subsequent law repeals or repeals the preceding one, if it expressly establishes it, or is directly contrary to it, or completely orders the matter that was the object of the previous law; However, universal law does not derogate in any way from particular or special law, unless something else is expressly provided in the law.

21
21. In case of doubt, the revocation of the preceding law is not presumed, but subsequent laws must be compared and, to the extent possible, reconciled with the previous ones.

22
22. The civil laws to which the law of the Church refers must be observed in canon law with the same effects, as long as they are not contrary to divine law or otherwise provided in canon law.

23
23. Only that custom has the force of law that, introduced by a community of faithful, has been approved by the legislator, in accordance with the canons that follow.

24
24. § 1 . No custom can achieve the force of law if it is contrary to divine right.
§ 2 . Nor can a custom against the law or extralegal achieve the force of law if it is not reasonable; The custom expressly condemned by law is not reasonable.

25
25. No custom can achieve the force of law except that which is observed, with the intention of introducing law, by a community capable, at least, of being the passive subject of a law.

26
26. Except in the case where it has been specially approved by the competent legislator, custom against law or extralegal only achieves the force of law if it has been legitimately observed for thirty continuous and complete years; But, against canon law that contains a clause prohibiting future customs, only a centuries-old or immemorial custom can prevail.

27
27. Custom is the best interpreter of laws.

28
28. Subject to the provisions of c. 5, custom, whether against the law or extralegal, is revoked by contrary custom or law; But, unless it expressly cites them, the law does not revoke centuries-old or immemorial customs, nor does universal law revoke particular customs.

29
29. General decrees, by which the competent legislator establishes common prescriptions for a community capable of being the passive subject of a law, are properly laws and are governed by the provisions of the canons relating to them.

30
30. Whoever enjoys only executive power cannot give the general decree referred to in c. 29, except in particular cases in which this has been expressly granted by the competent legislator, in accordance with the law, and if the conditions established in the act of concession are met.

31
31. § 1 . Those who enjoy executive power can issue, within the limits of their own competence, general executory decrees, that is, those by which the manner to be observed in compliance with the law is determined in more detail, or the observance of the laws is urged.
§ 2 . Regarding the promulgation and vacation of the decrees referred to in § 1, observe the prescriptions of c. 8.

32
32. General executory decrees bind those who bind the laws whose conditions of execution determine or whose observance those same decrees urge.

33
33. § 1 . General executory decrees, even if published in directories or documents of another name, do not repeal the laws, and their prescriptions that are contrary to the laws have no value.
§ 2 . Such decrees lose their force by explicit or implicit revocation made by the competent authority, and also upon the cessation of the law for whose execution they were given; but they do not cease when the power of the person who dictated them expires, unless otherwise expressly provided.

34
34. § 1 . The instructions, by which the prescriptions of the laws are clarified, and the ways in which the law is to be executed are developed and determined, are directed to those whose responsibility it is to ensure that the laws are fulfilled, and bind them to the execution of the same; Those who have executive power can legitimately give instructions, within the limits of their competence.
§ 2 . What is ordered in the instructions does not repeal the laws, and what is incompatible with them has no value.
§ 3 . The instructions cease to have force, not only due to explicit or implicit revocation of the competent authority that issued them, or of its superior, but also when the law for whose clarification or execution they were given ceases.

35
35. The singular administrative act, whether a decree or precept, or a rescript, can be given by whoever has executive power, within the limits of his or her jurisdiction, remaining firm what is prescribed in c. 76 § 1 .

36
36. § 1 . The administrative act must be understood according to the proper meaning of the words and the common way of speaking; In case of doubt, those that refer to litigation or the summons or imposition of penalties must be strictly interpreted, as well as those that restrict the rights of the person, harm the acquired rights of third parties or are contrary to a law in favor of individuals; all others should be interpreted broadly.
§ 2 . The administrative act should not extend to other cases outside of those expressed.

37
37. The administrative act that affects the external jurisdiction must be recorded in writing; Likewise its act of execution, if it is carried out in a commissionary manner.
37. 1 § 1 . The apostolic vicariate or apostolic prefecture is a certain portion of the people of God that, due to peculiar circumstances, has not yet been constituted as a diocese, and is entrusted to the pastoral care of a Vicar Apostolic or an Apostolic Prefect to govern them in the name of the Supreme Pontiff.
§ 2 . The apostolic administration is a certain portion of the people of God that, for special and particularly serious reasons, is not erected as a diocese by the Roman Pontiff, and whose pastoral care is entrusted to an Apostolic Administrator, who governs it in the name of the Supreme Pontiff.

38
38. Any administrative act, even if it is a motu proprio rescript, has no effect to the extent that it damages the acquired right of a third party or is contrary to a law or an approved custom, unless the competent authority has expressly added a derogatory clause.

39
39. Only those conditions that are expressed by the particles "if", "unless" or "as long as" affect the validity of the administrative act.

40
40. The executor of an administrative act invalidly performs his function if he acts before receiving the corresponding document and having recognized its authenticity and integrity, unless he had been previously informed of the document with the authority of the person who gave the act.

41
41. The executor of an administrative act, who is merely entrusted with the service of executing it, cannot deny its execution, unless it is clearly stated that said act is null, or that for another serious reason it is not appropriate to execute it, or that the conditions expressed in the same administrative act have not been met; but if the execution of the administrative act seems inopportune due to the circumstances of the person or place, the executor must suspend said execution; In such cases, it will immediately be brought to the attention of the authority that issued the act.

42
42. The executor of an administrative act must proceed in accordance with the mandate; and the execution is void if it does not comply with the essential conditions indicated in the document, or does not observe the substantial procedure.

43
43. The executor of an administrative act may appoint a substitute at his discretion, unless the substitution has been prohibited, or the person has been chosen based on his personal qualities, or the person of the substitute has been determined in advance; but, even in these cases, the executor may entrust the preparatory acts to another.

44
44. The person who succeeds the executor in his office may also execute the administrative act, unless the executor had been chosen based on his personal qualities.

45
45. If, in the execution of an administrative act, the executor has made any error, it is lawful for him to carry it out again.

46
46. The administrative act does not cease when the power of the person who did it is extinguished, unless the law expressly provides otherwise.

47
47. The revocation of an administrative act by another administrative act of the competent authority only takes effect from the moment in which it is legitimately notified to its addressee.

48
48. A singular decree means the administrative act of the competent executive authority, by which, according to the rules of law and for a particular case, a decision is made or a provision is made that, by its nature, does not presuppose the request of an interested party.

49
49. The singular precept is a decree by which the obligation to do or omit something is directly and legitimately imposed on a specific person or persons, especially to urge observance of the law.

50
50. Before issuing a singular decree, the authority should gather the necessary information and evidence, and to the extent possible, hear from those whose rights may be harmed.
50. 4 The erection, innovation or suppression of a cathedral chapter is reserved to the Apostolic See.

51
51. The decree must be given in writing, and if it is a decision, stating the reasons, at least summarily.

52
52. The singular decree affects only the things it deals with and the people to whom it is addressed; but it obliges them anywhere, unless otherwise stated.

53
53. If there are decrees contradictory to each other, the peculiar prevails over the general with respect to those things that are established peculiarly; If they are equally peculiar or general, the later one repeals the previous one, to the extent that it contradicts it.

54
54. § 1 . The singular decree whose application is entrusted to an executor takes effect from the moment of execution; Otherwise, from the moment in which the recipient is notified by order of the person who decreed it.
§ 2 . In order to demand compliance with a singular decree, it must have been issued by a legitimate document, in accordance with the law.

55
55. Without prejudice to the provisions of cc. 37 and 51, when a very serious cause prevents the text of the decree from being delivered in writing, it will be considered notified by reading it to the recipient before a notary or before two witnesses, drawing up a record that must be signed by all those present.

56
56. The decree is considered intimated if the recipient, duly summoned to receive it or hear its reading, does not appear, or refuses to sign, without just cause.

57
57. § 1 . When the law prescribes that a decree be issued, or when the interested party legitimately presents a petition or appeal to obtain a decree, the competent authority must provide within three months following receipt of the petition or appeal, unless another period is prescribed by law.
§ 2 . After this period, if the decree has not yet been issued, a negative response is presumed for the purposes of proposing a subsequent appeal.
§ 3 . The presumption of a negative response does not exempt the competent authority from the obligation to issue the decree, and even to repair the damage that may have been caused in accordance with c 128.

58
58. § 1 . The singular decree ceases to have force due to the legitimate revocation made by the competent authority, as well as when the law for whose execution it was given ceases.
§ 2 . The singular precept not imposed by a legitimate document loses its value when the power of the person who ordered it ceases.

59
59. § 1 . The rescript is an administrative act that the competent executive authority issues in writing, and that by its very nature grants a privilege, a dispensation or other grace, at the request of the interested party.
§ 2 . What is established on the rescripts also applies to the granting of a license, and to the granting of thanks by word of mouth, unless otherwise stated.

60
60. All those who are not expressly prohibited may obtain any rescript.

61
61. If nothing else is stated, a rescript can be obtained in favor of another even without his or her consent, and it is valid before acceptance, without prejudice to contrary clauses.

62
62. The rescript in which the executor is not designated takes effect from the moment the document has been issued; the others, from the moment of their execution.

63
63. § 1 . The subreption or concealment of the truth prevents the validity of a rescript, if the prayers have not set out everything that, according to canonical law, style and practice, must be manifested for its validity, unless it is a rescript of grace granted Motu proprio.
§ 2 . The obstruction or exposure of something false is also an obstacle to the validity of a rescript, if not even one of the alleged motivational causes is true.
§ 3 . In rescripts that do not have an executor, the motivating cause must be true at the time the rescript is granted; in the other rescripts, at the time of their execution.

64
64. Without prejudice to the right of the Penitentiary to the internal forum, a grace denied by any dicastery of the Roman Curia cannot be validly granted by another dicastery of the same Curia or by another competent authority lower than the Roman Pontiff, without the consent of the dicastery with which it began to be dealt with.
64. 9 § 1 . Remaining safe from what the CC prescribes

65
65. § 1 . Without prejudice to the provisions of §§ 2 and 3, no one asks another Ordinary for a favor that has been denied by his own Ordinary, without stating such denial; and, when it is stated, the Ordinary must not grant the grace without first having received from the first the reasons for the refusal.
§ 2 . The grace denied by the Vicar General or by an Episcopal Vicar cannot be validly granted by another Vicar of the same Bishop, even having obtained from the denying Vicar the reasons for the denial.
§ 3 . The grace that, having been denied by the Vicar General or by an Episcopal Vicar, is obtained after the diocesan Bishop without mentioning that refusal is invalid; but the grace denied by the diocesan Bishop cannot be validly obtained from the Vicar General, or from an Episcopal Vicar, without the consent of the Bishop, not even making mention of such refusal.

66
66. The rescript is not invalid when there is an error in the name of the person to whom it is granted or who grants it, of the place in which he resides, or of the matter in question, provided that, in the opinion of the Ordinary, there is no doubt about the identity of the subject and object.

67
67. § 1 . If, on the same matter, two contradictory rescripts are obtained, the peculiar prevails over the general with respect to those things that are expressed peculiarly.
§ 2 . If they are equally peculiar or general, the previous one prevails over the later, unless the second expressly refers to the first, or unless the first applicant who obtained the rescript did not use it due to fraud or notable negligence.
§ 3 . In case of doubt about whether or not a rescript is invalid, one must resort to the person who granted it.
67. 1 A religious must not accept positions or offices outside his own institute without permission from the legitimate Superior.

68
68. A rescript from the Apostolic See in which an executor is not designated must be presented to the Ordinary of the applicant who obtained it, only when this is stated in the grant document, it concerns public matters, or it is necessary to verify some conditions.

69
69. The rescript for the presentation of which no deadline is determined, may be presented at any time to the executor, as long as there is no fraud or fraud.

70
70. If in the rescript the concession itself is entrusted to the executor, it is up to him, according to his prudent discretion and conscience, to grant or deny the grace.

71
71. No one is obliged to use a rescript granted only in his favor, unless he is canonically obliged to do so for another reason.

72
72. Rescripts granted by the Apostolic See that have expired can be extended only once and with just cause by the diocesan Bishop, but not for more than three months.

73
73. No rescript is revoked by a contrary law, if said law does not provide otherwise.

74
74. Although anyone can use in the internal forum a grace that has been granted to him orally, he has the obligation to prove it for the external forum as many times as this is legitimately demanded of him.

75
75. If the rescript contains a privilege or dispensation, the prescriptions of the canons that follow must also be observed.

76
76. § 1 . The privilege, that is, the grace granted by a peculiar act in favor of certain persons, both natural and legal, can be granted by the legislator and also by the executive authority to which the legislator has granted this power.
§ 2 . Centenary or immemorial possession presumes the granting of a privilege.

77
77. The privilege must be interpreted in accordance with c. 36 § 1 ; but it must always be interpreted in such a way that those who have it really get some advantage.

78
78. § 1 . The privilege is presumed perpetual, until the contrary is proven.
§ 2 . Personal privilege, which follows the person, extinguishes with him.
§ 3 . The royal privilege ceases when the object or place is completely destroyed; However, local privilege revives if the place is rebuilt within fifty years.

79
79. The privilege ceases by revocation of the competent authority, in accordance with c. 47, without prejudice to the provisions of c. 81.

80
80. § 1 . No privilege ceases due to renunciation, unless it has been accepted by the competent authority.
§ 2 . Any natural person may renounce a privilege granted solely in his favor.
§ 3 . Individuals cannot renounce the privilege granted to a legal entity, either by reason of the dignity of the place or the object; nor can the same legal entity renounce a privilege that has been granted to it, if the renunciation results in detriment to the Church or others.

81
81. The privilege is not extinguished when the right of the person who granted it ceases, unless it was granted with the clause of our approval or another equivalent.

82
82. The privilege that is not onerous for others does not cease due to disuse or contrary use; but that which results in the burden of others is lost by legitimate prescription.

83
83. § 1 . The privilege ceases when the term expires or the number of cases for which it was granted is exhausted, without prejudice to what is prescribed in the c

84
84. Whoever abuses the power that has been granted to him by privilege, deserves to be deprived of it; Consequently, the Ordinary, after having uselessly admonished the holder of the privilege, deprives the person who seriously abuses the privilege if he himself granted it; but if the privilege was granted by the Holy See, the Ordinary must inform it of the matter.

85
85. The dispensation, or relaxation of a purely ecclesiastical law in a particular case, can be granted within the limits of their competence, by those who have executive power, as well as by those who have the power to dispense explicitly or implicitly, either by right or by legitimate delegation.

86
86. Laws that determine the essential constituent elements of institutions or legal acts are not dispensable.

87
87. § 1 . The diocesan Bishop, whenever, in his opinion, this results in the spiritual good of the faithful, may exempt them from the disciplinary laws, both universal and particular, promulgated for his territory or for his subjects by the supreme authority of the Church; but not from procedural or criminal laws, nor from those whose dispensation is specially reserved for the Apostolic See or other authority.
§ 2 . If it is difficult to resort to the Holy See and there is also a danger of serious harm in the delay, any Ordinary may dispense from such laws, even if the dispensation is reserved for the Holy See, provided that it is a dispensation that the Holy See usually grants in the same circumstances, without prejudice to the provisions of c. 291.

88
88. The local Ordinary can dispense from diocesan laws, and, when he considers that it is for the good of the faithful, from the laws promulgated by the regional or provincial Council, or by the Episcopal Conference.

89
89. The parish priest and other priests or deacons cannot dispense from universal and particular law unless this power has been expressly granted to them.

90
90. § 1 . Do not dispense with ecclesiastical law without just and reasonable cause, taking into account the circumstances of the case and the seriousness of the law from which you dispense; Otherwise, the dispensation is illicit and if it has not been granted by the legislator himself or by his superior, it is also invalid.
§ 2 . When there is doubt about the sufficiency of the cause, the dispensation is validly and lawfully granted.

91
91. Whoever has the power to dispense can exercise it with respect to his subjects, even when he is outside the territory, and even if they are absent from it; and if the contrary is not expressly established, also with respect to transients who are actually in the territory, and with respect to itself.

92
92. Not only the dispensation must be strictly interpreted, in accordance with c. 36 § 1 , but also the same power to dispense granted for a specific case.

93
93. The dispensation that has a successive tract ceases in the same way as the privilege, as well as by the certain and total cessation of the motivating cause.

94
94. § 1 . Statutes, in the proper sense, are the rules established by law in corporations or foundations, by which their purpose, constitution, regime and way of acting are determined.
§ 2 . The bylaws of a corporation bind only those persons who are legitimate members of it; the statutes of a foundation to those who care for its government.
§ 3 . The prescriptions of the statutes that have been established and promulgated by virtue of the legislative power are governed by the norms of the canons about the laws.

95
95. § 1 . Regulations are rules or norms that must be observed in meetings of people, both convened by the ecclesiastical authority and freely promoted by the faithful, as well as in other celebrations; They determine what is related to its constitution, regime and procedure.
§ 2 . In meetings or celebrations, these regulatory norms bind those who take part in them.

96
96. Through baptism, man joins the Church of Christ and becomes a person in it, with the duties and rights that are proper to Christians, taking into account the condition of each one, as long as they are in the ecclesiastical communion and are not prevented by a legitimately imposed sanction.

97
97. § 1 . The person who has turned eighteen years old is older; before that age, it is minor.
§ 2 . The minor, before turning seven years old, is called an infant, and is considered without the use of reason; Once he is seven years old, he is presumed to have the use of reason.

98
98. § 1 . The elderly person has the full exercise of their rights.
§ 2 . The minor is subject to the power of the parents or guardians in the exercise of their rights, except in those cases in which, by divine law or canon law, minors are exempt from that power; Regarding the appointment and power of guardians, the prescriptions of civil law must be observed unless otherwise established by canon law, or unless the diocesan Bishop, with just cause, deems that in specific cases it must be provided by appointment of another guardian.

99
99. Those who habitually lack the use of reason are considered not to be masters of themselves and are equated to infants.

100
100. The person is called: "neighbor", in the place where he or she resides; "stranger", where he has his quasi-domicile; "transient", if he is outside the domicile or quasi-domicile that he still maintains; "vague", if he does not have a domicile or quasi-domicile anywhere.

101
101. § 1 . The place of origin of a child, even that of a neophyte, is that where its parents, at the time of the child's birth, had their domicile, or failing that, the quasi-domicile; or where the mother had them, if the parents did not have the same domicile or quasi-domicile.
§ 2 . If it is a child of lazy people, his place of origin is the one where he was born; if a foundling, the place where it was found.

102
102. § 1 . Domicile is acquired by residence in the territory of a parish or at least a diocese, which is either linked to the intention to remain there perpetually if nothing prevents it, or has lasted for a complete five-year period.
§ 2 . Quasi-domicile is acquired by residence in the territory of a parish or at least a diocese, which is either linked to the intention to remain there for at least three months if nothing prevents it, or has actually been extended for three months.
§ 3 . The domicile or quasi-domicile in the territory of a parish is called parochial; in the territory of a diocese, although not in a parish, diocesan.
102. § 2 .

103
103. Members of religious institutes and societies of apostolic life acquire domicile where the house to which they belong is located; and quasi-domicile, in the place of the house where they reside in accordance with the c

104
104. Whether the spouses have a common domicile or quasi-domicile; In the event of legitimate separation or for another just cause, each person can have their own domicile or quasi-domicile.

105
105. §1 . The minor necessarily has the domicile and quasi-domicile of the person to whose power he is subject. He who has left childhood can also acquire his own quasi-domicile; and if he is legitimately emancipated in accordance with civil law, including his own domicile.
§ 2 . He who is legitimately subject to guardianship or curatorship for reasons other than minority, has the domicile and quasi-domicile of the guardian or curator.

106
106. The domicile and the quasi-domicile are lost when leaving the place with the intention of not returning, remaining safe as prescribed by c. 105.

107
107. § 1 . For both domicile and quasi-domicile, each person has their own parish priest and Ordinary.
§ 2 . The parish priest and ordinary of the vagrant are those of the place where he is currently located.
§ 3 . The parish priest is also the one who has only the diocesan domicile or quasi-domicile of the place where he currently resides.

108
108. §1 . Consanguinity is computed by lines and degrees.
§ 2 . In a straight line, there are as many degrees as there are generations or people, discounting the trunk.
§ 3 . In a collateral line, there are as many degrees as there are people in both lines, discounting the trunk.

109
109. §1 . Affinity arises from a valid marriage, even if not consummated, and occurs between the man and the woman's blood relatives, and equally between the woman and the man's blood relatives.
§ 2 . It is counted in such a way that the blood relatives of the man are in the same line and degree related to the woman, and vice versa.

110
110. Children who have been adopted in accordance with civil law are considered children of the person or persons who adopted them.

111
111. n § 1 . With the reception of baptism, the child of the parents who belong to it is assigned to the Latin Church or, if one of the two does not belong to it, when both parents by common agreement have chosen that the offspring be baptized in the Latin Church; If there is no common agreement, it is assigned to the Church sui iuris to which the father belongs.
§ 2 . If only one of the parents is Catholic, he or she is attached to the Church to which this Catholic parent belongs.
§ 3 . Any baptized person who has reached fourteen years of age can freely choose to be baptized in the Latin Church or in another Church sui iuris; In this case, it belongs to the Church you have chosen.
111. § 1 . The child whose parents belong to the Latin Church joins it by receiving baptism, or if one of them does not belong to the Latin Church, when they decide by common agreement that the offspring be baptized into it; If there is no agreement, he joins the Church of the rite to which the father belongs.
§ 2 . The baptized person who has reached the age of fourteen can freely choose to be baptized in the Latin Church or in another autonomous ritual Church; In this case, he belongs to the Church he has chosen.

112
112. n § 1 . After receiving baptism they are assigned to another Church sui iuris:
1. Whoever obtains a license from the Apostolic See.
2. The spouse who, upon or during marriage, declares that he or she passes to the Church sui iuris of the other spouse; but once the marriage is dissolved he can freely return to the Latin Church.
3. The children of those mentioned in numbers 1 and 2 before reaching fourteen years of age and, similarly, in a mixed marriage, the children of the Catholic party who has legitimately passed to another Church sui iuris; However, upon reaching that age, they themselves can return to the Latin Church.
§ 2 . The custom, however prolonged, of receiving the sacraments according to the rite of another Church sui iuris does not entail affiliation to said Church.
§ 3 . Any transfer to another Church sui iuris has value from the moment of the declaration made in the presence of the Ordinary of the place of said Church or the parish priest itself or the priest delegated by one of them and two witnesses, unless a rescript of the Apostolic See provides otherwise; and it will be recorded in the baptism book.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio De concordia inter Codices, dated May 31, 2016)]:
112. § 1 . After receiving baptism, they join another autonomous ritual Church:
1 who obtains a license from the Apostolic See;
2 the spouse who, upon or during marriage, declares that he or she passes to the autonomous ritual Church to which the other spouse belongs; but, once the marriage is dissolved, he can freely return to the Latin Church;
3 the children of those mentioned in nos. 1 and 2 before turning fourteen years of age, and likewise, in a mixed marriage, the children of the Catholic party who have legitimately passed to another ritual Church; but, upon reaching that age, they can return to the Latin Church.
§ 2 . The custom, however long-standing, of receiving the sacraments according to the rite of some autonomous ritual Church does not entail affiliation to said Church.

113
113. § 1 . The Catholic Church and the Apostolic See are moral persons by the same divine ordination.
§ 2 . In the Church, in addition to natural persons, there are also legal persons, who are subject in canon law to obligations and rights consistent with their own nature.

114
114. § 1 . Legal persons are constituted, either by the same prescription of law or by special concession of the competent authority given by decree, groups of people (corporations) or things (foundations) ordered to a purpose consistent with the mission of the Church that transcends the purpose of individuals.
§ 2 . The purposes referred to in § 1 are understood to be those that correspond to works of piety, apostolate or charity, both spiritual and temporal.
§ 3 . The competent authority of the Church does not confer legal personality except on those corporations or foundations that pursue a truly useful goal and that, weighing all the circumstances, have means that are expected to be sufficient to achieve the goal they propose.
114. § 2 and established as legal entities by the competent ecclesiastical authority;
2 non-autonomous pious foundations, that is, temporal goods, given in any way to a public legal entity with the burden of celebrating Masses and fulfilling other ecclesiastical functions determined with the annual income, for a long period of time, which will be determined by particular law, or otherwise pursuing the purposes indicated in c.
114. § 2 .
§ 2 . Once the term has expired, the assets of a non-autonomous pious foundation, if they have been entrusted to a legal entity subject to the diocesan Bishop, must be allocated to the institute referred to in c.

115
115. § 1 . In the Church, legal persons are either corporations or foundations.
§ 2 . The corporation, whose constitution requires at least three people, is collegial if its activity is determined by the members, who, with or without equal rights, participate in the decisions in accordance with the law and the statutes; Otherwise, it is uncollegial.
§ 3 . The patrimonial legal entity or autonomous foundation consists of assets or things, spiritual or material, and is directed, according to the rule of law and the statutes, by one or several natural persons, or by a college.

116
116. § 1 . Public legal entities are corporations and foundations constituted by the competent ecclesiastical authority so that, within the limits indicated to them, they fulfill in the name of the Church, in accordance with the prescriptions of law, the mission entrusted to them with a view to the public good; The other legal entities are private.
§ 2 . Public legal entities acquire this personality, either by virtue of the same right, or by special decree of the competent authority that expressly grants it to them; Private legal entities obtain this personality only through a special decree of the competent authority that expressly grants it.
116. 3 § 1 . A marriage void due to impediment or defect of the legitimate form can be healed, as long as the consent of both parties persists.
§ 2 . A marriage void due to an impediment of natural or positive divine law can only be healed once the impediment has ceased.
116. 8 The clergyman provided with the due power is the minister of sacramentals; But, according to what is established in the liturgical books and in the judgment of the Ordinary, some sacramentals can also be administered by lay people who possess the proper qualities.

117
117. No corporation or foundation that wishes to obtain legal personality can obtain it if its statutes have not been approved by the competent authority.

118
118. Those who are recognized by universal or particular law, or by the statutes themselves, represent the public legal entity, acting on its behalf; Those to whom the statutes attribute such competence represent the private legal entity.

119
119. Regarding collegiate acts, as long as the law or the statutes do not provide otherwise:
1 when it comes to elections, what has legal value is that which, with the majority of those who must be called present, is approved by an absolute majority of those present; After two ineffective canvasses, the vote is held on the two candidates who have obtained the greatest number of votes, or if there are more, on the two oldest; After the third vote, if the tie persists, the oldest is elected;
2 when dealing with other matters, what is legally valid is what, being
most of those who must be summoned are present, it is approved by an absolute majority of those present; If after two counts the equality of votes persists, the president can resolve the tie with his vote;
3 but what affects each and every one must be approved by all.

120
120. § 1 . Every legal entity is, by nature, perpetual; However, it expires if it is legitimately suppressed by the competent authority, or if its activity has ceased for a period of one hundred years; The private legal entity is also extinguished when the association itself is dissolved in accordance with its statutes, or if, in the opinion of the competent authority, the same foundation has ceased to exist according to its statutes.
§ 2 . When there remains only one member of the collegiate legal entity and, according to its statutes, the corporation has not ceased to exist, that member is responsible for the exercise of all the rights of the corporation.

121
121. If the corporations and foundations that are public legal entities join together to form a single entity with legal personality, this new legal entity takes over the assets and property rights of the previous ones, and assumes the burdens that weighed on them; but they must remain safe, especially regarding the destination of the assets and compliance with the obligations, the will of the founders and donors, and the acquired rights.

122
122. When a public legal entity is divided in such a way that a part of it is united with another public legal entity, or with the dismembered part a new public legal entity is erected, the ecclesiastical authority responsible for carrying out the division, respecting above all the will of the founders and donors, the acquired rights and the approved statutes, must ensure by itself or by an executor:
1 that the common assets and property rights that can be divided, as well as debts and other charges, are distributed in due proportion and equitably between the legal entities in question, taking into account all the circumstances and needs of both;
2 that the two legal entities enjoy the use and usufruct of the common assets that cannot be divided, and that the burdens inherent to those assets fall on both, also maintaining the due proportion, which must be determined equitably.

123
123. When a public legal entity is extinguished, the destination of its assets and property rights, as well as its charges, is governed by the law and the statutes; In the event of their silence, they pass to the immediately superior legal entity, always remaining safe the will of the founders or donors, as well as the acquired rights; When a private legal entity is extinguished, the destination of its assets and liabilities is governed by its own statutes.

124
124. § 1 . For a legal act to be valid, it is required that it have been carried out by a capable person, and that the elements that essentially constitute that act are present, as well as the formalities and requirements imposed by law for the validity of the act.
§ 2 . The legal act duly carried out is presumed valid in terms of its external elements.

125
125. § 1 . The act that a person executes due to external violence that he has in no way been able to resist is considered to have not been carried out.
§ 2 . The act carried out due to serious fear unjustly instilled, or due to fraud, is valid, unless the law determines otherwise; but it can be rescinded by a judge's ruling, both at the request of the injured party or those who succeed him or her, or ex officio.

126
126. An act carried out through ignorance or error is void when it affects what constitutes its substance or falls on a sine qua non condition; Otherwise, it is valid, unless the law establishes otherwise, but the act carried out through ignorance or error may give rise to rescission action in accordance with the law.

127
127. § 1 . When the law establishes that, to carry out certain acts, the Superior needs the consent or advice of some college or group of people, the college or group must be convened in accordance with c. 166, unless, in the case of only asking for advice, the particular or proper law provides otherwise; For the validity of the acts, it is necessary to obtain the consent of the absolute majority of those present, or to ask for the advice of everyone.
§ 2 . When the law establishes that, to carry out certain acts, the Superior needs the consent or advice of some individual persons:
1 if consent is required, the act of the Superior is invalid if he does not ask for the consent of those people, or acts against the opinion of them or any of them;
2 if advice is required, the act of the Superior is invalid if he does not listen to those people: the Superior, although he has no obligation to follow that opinion, even if it is unanimous, should not nevertheless deviate from the opinion, especially if it is concordant, without a reason that, in his opinion, is more powerful.
§ 3 . All those whose consent or advice is required are obliged to sincerely express their opinion, and also, if the seriousness of the matter requires it, to carefully maintain secrecy, an obligation that the Superior may urge.

128
128. Anyone who illegitimately causes damage to another by a legal act or by another act carried out with intent or negligence is obliged to repair the damage caused.

129
129. § 1 . Of the power of regime, which exists in the Church by divine institution, and which is also called the power of jurisdiction, those sealed by the sacred order are capable subjects, in accordance with the norm of the prescriptions of law.
§ 2 . In the exercise of this power, the lay faithful can cooperate in accordance with the law.

130
130. The power of regime, in itself, is exercised in the external forum; However, sometimes it is exercised only in the internal forum, so that the effects that its exercise must have in the external forum are not recognized in this forum, unless the law establishes it in a specific case.

131
131. § 1 . The power of ordinary regime is that which is attached by itself to an office; A delegate is that which is granted to a person for himself, and not by reason of his office.
§ 2 . The ordinary power of regime can be own or vicarious.
§ 3 . The burden of proving delegation falls on the person who claims to be a delegate.

132
132. § 1 . The usual powers are governed by the provisions on delegated power.
§ 2 . However, if nothing else has been expressly provided for in the act of concession, nor has personal qualities been taken into account, the habitual power granted to an Ordinary is not extinguished when the power of the Ordinary to whom it has been granted ceases, even if he had already begun to exercise it, but passes to the Ordinary who succeeds him in the government.

133
133. § 1 . What a delegate does in excess of the limits of his mandate, with respect to the object or the people, is void.
§ 2 . A delegate who performs the acts for which he has received a delegation in a manner other than that determined in the mandate is not understood to exceed the limits of his mandate, unless the delegator has prescribed a certain manner for the validity of the act.

134
134. § 1 . By the name of Ordinary, in addition to the Roman Pontiff, the diocesan Bishops and all those who, even temporarily, have been appointed to govern a particular Church or a community equivalent to it according to c. 368, and also those who have ordinary executive power in them, that is, the Vicars General and Episcopalians; Likewise, with respect to their members, the major Superiors of clerical religious institutes of pontifical right and of clerical societies of apostolic life of pontifical right, who have, at least, ordinary executive power.
§ 2 . The name of Ordinary of the place is understood to mean all those listed in the § 1, except the Superiors of religious institutes and societies of apostolic life.
§ 3 . Whatever is nominally attributed in the canons to the diocesan Bishop in the area of ​​executive power, it is understood that it is the responsibility only of the diocesan Bishop and those who are equivalent to him according to c.

135
135. §1 . The power of the regime is divided into legislative, executive and judicial.
§ 2 . The legislative power must be exercised in the manner prescribed by law, and that which the lower legislator has cannot be validly delegated to the supreme authority, unless the law explicitly provides otherwise; Nor can the lower legislator validly give a law contrary to the law of higher rank.
§ 3 . The judicial power that judges or courts have must be exercised in the manner prescribed by law, and cannot be delegated, except to carry out the preparatory acts of a decree or sentence.
§ 4 . Regarding the exercise of executive power, observe the prescriptions of the canons that follow.

136
136. Executive power may be exercised, even when outside the territory, over one's own subjects, even if they are absent from the territory, if nothing else is established by the nature of the matter or by prescription of law; also on the pilgrims who are currently in the territory, if it is about granting favors or executing the universal laws and the particular ones that are obligatory for them according to the norm of c. 13 § 2, 2.

137
137. § 1 . Ordinary executive power can be delegated both for an act and for the generality of cases, unless the law expressly provides otherwise.
§ 2 . The executive power delegated by the Apostolic See can be subdelegated, both for an act and for the generality of cases, unless personal qualities have been taken into account, or subdelegation has been expressly prohibited.
§ 3 . Executive power delegated by another authority with ordinary power that was delegated for all matters can only be subdelegated for each case; but if it was delegated for a specific act or acts, it cannot be subdelegated without express concession from the delegator.
§ 4 . No subdelegated power can be subdelegated again, unless it has been expressly granted by the delegator.

138
138. The ordinary executive power, as well as that delegated for the generality of cases, must be interpreted broadly, but all others must be interpreted strictly; However, it is understood that whoever has delegated power is also granted everything necessary for that power to be exercised.

139
139. § 1 . If the law does not establish otherwise, the executive power, both ordinary and delegated, of a competent authority, is not suspended by the fact that someone goes to another competent authority, even if it is superior.
§ 2 . However, the lower authority does not interfere in a cause that has been brought to the higher authority, if it is not for a serious and urgent reason; in which case immediately report the matter to the higher authority.

140
140. § 1 . When several delegates for the same matter are jointly delegated, the one who begins to act excludes the action of the others in the same matter, unless he is later prevented or does not want to continue processing the matter.
§ 2 . When the various delegates for a matter are collegially, they must all proceed according to the norm of c. 119, unless otherwise provided in the mandate.
§ 3 . The executive power delegated to several is presumed to be jointly delegated.

141
141. When several have been successively delegated, the matter is resolved by the one whose mandate is previous, if it has not been subsequently revoked.

142
142. § 1 . The delegated power is extinguished: once the mandate has been fulfilled; the deadline has elapsed or the number of cases for which it was granted has been exhausted; having ceased the final cause of the delegation; by revocation of the delegator communicated directly to the delegate, and also by resignation of the delegate presented to the delegator and accepted by him; but it is not extinguished because the power of the delegator has ceased, unless this is stated in the clauses included in the mandate.
§ 2 . However, the act of delegated power that is exercised only in the domestic jurisdiction is valid even if, by inadvertence, it is carried out after the concession period has elapsed.
142. § 2 .
§ 2 . It also ceases if, with the passage of time, the actual circumstances have changed in the opinion of the competent authority in such a way that it is harmful, or its use becomes illegal.

143
143. § 1 . The ordinary power is extinguished by the loss of the office to which it is attached.
§ 2 . Unless the law provides otherwise, the ordinary power is suspended when an appeal is legitimately filed against the deprivation or removal of office.

144
144. § 1 . In the common error of fact or law, as well as in positive and probable doubt of law or fact, the Church supplies the executive power of the regime, both for the external and internal forum.
§ 2 . The same rule applies to the powers discussed in the ccs. 882, 883, 966 and

145
145. § 1 . Ecclesiastical office is any position, stably constituted by divine or ecclesiastical disposition, that must be exercised for a spiritual purpose.
§ 2 . The obligations and rights of each ecclesiastical office are determined either by the same law by which it is established, or by the decree of the competent authority that constitutes it and at the same time confers it.

146
146. An ecclesiastical office cannot be validly obtained without canonical provision.

147
147. The provision of an office is made through free collation by the competent ecclesiastical authority; by its institution when presentation has preceded; by confirmation or admission by the same when an election or nomination has preceded; finally, by simple choice and acceptance of the chosen one when the choice does not need to be confirmed.

148
148. The provision of offices is the responsibility of the same authority that is responsible for erecting, innovating or suppressing them, unless the law establishes otherwise.

149
149. § 1 . For someone to be promoted to an ecclesiastical office, they must be in communion with the Church and be suitable, that is, endowed with those qualities that are required for that office by universal or particular law, or by the founding law.
§ 2 . The provision of an ecclesiastical office made in favor of someone who lacks the required qualities is only invalid when such qualities are expressly required for the validity of the provision by universal or particular law, or by the law of foundation; Otherwise, it is valid, but can be rescinded by decree of the competent authority or by ruling of the administrative court.
§ 3 . The provision of an office made with simony is invalid by virtue of the law itself.

150
150. The office that entails the full care of souls, for whose fulfillment the exercise of the priestly order is required, cannot be validly conferred on someone who has not yet been elevated to the priesthood.

151
151. The provision of an office that involves the care of souls must not be delayed without serious cause.

152
152. No one is conferred with two or more incompatible offices, that is, they cannot be exercised at the same time by the same person.

153
153. § 1 . The provision of an office that, according to law, is not vacant, is ipso facto invalid, and is not validated by the subsequent vacancy.
§ 2 . However, if it is an office that, according to law, is conferred for a specific period of time, the provision can be made within the six months prior to the expiration of that period, and takes effect from the day of the vacation of the office.
§ 3 . The promise of an office, whoever makes it, does not produce any legal effect.

154
154. The vacant office according to law that someone holds illegitimately may be conferred on someone as long as it has been duly declared that said possession was not legitimate, and this declaration is mentioned in the collation document.

155
155. He who confers an office, replacing someone who was unable or neglected to do so, does not thereby acquire any power over the person to whom it has been conferred, but rather the legal status of the latter is the same as if the collation had been made according to the ordinary rule of law.

156
156. Record the provision of any office in writing.

157
157. Unless the law expressly establishes otherwise, it is the responsibility of the diocesan Bishop to provide by free collation the ecclesiastical offices in his own particular Church.
157. 7 § 1 . Taking into account what the litigants have argued, the judge will determine by decree each of the issues that the expert's opinion must consider.
§ 2 . The minutes of the case and any other documents and gadgets that he may need to carry out his task well and faithfully must be delivered to the expert.
§ 3 . After hearing the expert, the judge will set a deadline within which he will have to carry out his study and present the opinion.

158
158. § 1 . The presentation for an ecclesiastical office by the person to whom the right of presentation belongs must be made to the authority to whom it corresponds to grant its institution, and if nothing else has been legitimately established, it will be done within a period of three months from when he became aware of the vacation of the office.
§ 2 . If the right of presentation belongs to a school or group of people, designate the one that is to be presented in accordance with the provisions of cc. 165-179.
158. § 1 and 161, as well as whoever presents an unsuitable person twice, loses for that occasion the right to present, and the vacant office must be freely provided to the competent authority to grant the institution, provided that the appointed Ordinary gives its consent.

159
159. No one is brought against his will; Therefore, the proposed candidate can be presented if, when asked about his will, he does not refuse within a period of eight working days.

160
160. § 1 . Whoever has the right to present may present one or more, both simultaneously and successively.
§ 2 . No one can introduce himself, but a school or group of people can introduce one of its members.

161
161. §1 . If the law does not establish otherwise, whoever has presented one who was not considered suitable, can only present another within a period of one month.
§ 2 . If the person presented resigns or dies before becoming his institution, whoever has the right of presentation can exercise it again within a period of one month from the moment in which he has received the news of the resignation or death.

162
162. Whoever does not make the presentation within the useful period, in accordance with the CC standard

163
163. The authority that, according to law, is responsible for instituting the person presented, will institute the person legitimately presented that it considers suitable, and that has accepted; If several are legitimately presented and considered suitable, one of them must be instituted.

164
164. If the law does not determine otherwise, in canonical elections the prescriptions of the canons that follow must be observed.

165
165. Unless the law or the legitimate statutes of the college or group provide otherwise, if a college or group has the right to elect for an office, it must not defer the election beyond one useful quarter, counting from the day on which it became aware of the vacation of the office; Once that period has elapsed unnecessarily, the ecclesiastical authority that has the right to confirm the election, or, alternatively, to fill, will freely fill the vacant office.

166
166. § 1 . The president of the college or group must convene all its members; and the call, when it must be personal, will be valid if it is made at the place of domicile, quasi-domicile or residence.
§ 2 . If any of those who were to be summoned had been absent, and therefore were absent, the election is valid; but at his request, after proving its pretermination and absence, the election must be rescinded by the competent authority, even after being confirmed, as long as it is legally established that the appeal was filed at least within three days after receiving the news of the election.
§ 3 . But if more than a third of the electors have been absent, the election is null and void, unless all those not summoned had in fact been present.

167
167. § 1 . Once the call has been legitimately made, those who are present at the place and on the day indicated in the call have the right to vote, excluding the right to vote by letter or by proxy, if the statutes do not legitimately provide otherwise.
§ 2 . If any of the electors is present at the house where the election is being held, but cannot attend due to illness, the tellers will collect their written vote.

168
168. Although someone has the right to vote in their own name for several Titles, they may only cast one vote.

169
169. For the election to be valid, no person outside the college or group can be admitted to vote.

170
170. The election whose freedom has been prevented for any reason is invalid in its own right.

171
171. § 1 . They are ineligible to vote:
1 the incapable of human acts;
2 who lacks an active voice;
3 the subject to a penalty of excommunication imposed by judicial sentence or by condemnatory or declaratory decree;
4 he who has notoriously separated himself from the communion of the Church.
§ 2 . If any of the above is admitted, his vote is null, but the election is valid, unless it is established that, without him, the person elected would not have obtained the necessary number of votes.

172
172. § 1 . For the vote to be valid, it must be:
1 free; Therefore, the vote of someone who, due to serious fear or fraud, directly or indirectly, was forced to elect a certain person or several disjunctively, is invalid;
2 secret, certain, absolute, determined.
§ 2 . The conditions added to the vote before the election are considered not set.

173
173. § 1 . Before beginning the election, at least two tellers must be appointed from among the members of the college or group.
§ 2 . The tellers must collect the votes and check with the election president whether the number of ballots corresponds to the number of voters, as well as examine the votes and make public how many each one has obtained.
§ 3 . If the number of votes is greater than the number of voters, the vote is void.
§ 4 . Whoever performs the function of actuary must carefully prepare minutes of the election, which, signed at least by the actuary, the president and the tellers, will be diligently kept in the college archive.

174
174. § 1 . The election, if the law or the statutes do not provide otherwise, may also be made by compromise, provided that the electors, after unanimous and written agreement, transfer for that time the right of election to one or more suitable persons, whether among their members or not, so that, by virtue of the power received, they can proceed to the election on behalf of all.
§ 2 . If it is a college or group formed only by clerics, the delegates must have been ordained; If not, the election is invalid.
§ 3 . The delegates must comply with the requirements of the law regarding the election and must adhere, for the validity of the election, to the conditions set in the commitment that are not contrary to the law; Conditions contrary to the law will be deemed not to have been established.
174. 1 The causes for which a parish priest can be legitimately removed from his parish are mainly the following:
1 a way of acting that produces serious detriment or disturbance to the ecclesiastical communion;
2 incompetence or a permanent illness of mind or body, which renders the parish priest incapable of usefully carrying out his duties;
3 the loss of good reputation in the eyes of honest and prudent parishioners or the aversion against the parish priest, if it is foreseen that they will not cease shortly;
4 serious negligence or transgression of parish duties, if it persists after a reprimand;
5 the poor administration of temporal goods with serious damage to the Church, when there is no other remedy for this evil.

175
175. The commitment ceases and the voters recover the right to vote:
1 for revocation made by the school or group, while the thing is intact;
2 for not having met any condition placed on the commitment;
3 once the election has been made, if it was void.

176
176. If nothing else is provided in the law or in the statutes, the person who has obtained the necessary number of votes, in accordance with the norm of c. 119, 1.

177
177. § 1 . The election must be immediately notified to the elected person, who, within eight business days after receiving the communication, must inform the president of the college or group whether or not he accepts the election; Otherwise, the election has no effect.
§ 2 . If the chosen one does not accept, he loses all rights acquired by the election and does not recover it by a subsequent acceptance, but he can be elected again; The school or group must proceed to a new election within one month of learning of the non-acceptance.
177. § 1 .
§ 3 . Whoever accepts the application that has been admitted, immediately obtains full office.

178
178. By accepting an election that does not need to be confirmed, the elected person immediately obtains full office; Otherwise, you only acquire a right to it.

179
179. § 1 . If the election needs to be confirmed, the person elected must request confirmation from the competent authority, by himself or by another, within a period of eight business days from the day of acceptance of the election; In other cases, he is deprived of all rights, unless he proves that due to just impediment it was not possible for him to request confirmation.
§ 2 . The competent authority, if it finds the chosen one suitable in accordance with the norm of c.149 § 1, and the election was made according to law, cannot deny confirmation.
§ 3 . Confirmation must be given in writing.
§ 4 . Before the confirmation is notified, the chosen one cannot interfere in the administration of the office, neither spiritually nor temporally, and the acts possibly put forward by him are null.
§ 5 . The elected person acquires the office by full right once confirmation has been notified, unless the law establishes otherwise.

180
180. §1 . If the election of the person considered most suitable and preferred by the voters is opposed by a canonical impediment that can and is usually waived, they can, by means of suffrage, nominate him or her to the competent authority, unless the law provides otherwise.
§ 2 . The delegates cannot make this nomination if they have not been expressly empowered in the commitment.

181
181. § 1 . For the nomination to be valid, at least two-thirds of the votes are required.
§ 2 . The vote for the nomination must be expressed through the word postulate or another equivalent; and the formula I elect or postulate, or another equivalent, is valid for the election if there is no impediment, and if there is, for the nomination.

182
182. § 1 . Within eight business days, the president must send the nomination to the competent authority whose responsibility it is to confirm the election and grant dispensation from the impediment, or request it, if he or she lacks this power, to the higher authority; When confirmation is not required, the application must be transmitted to the competent authority to grant the dispensation.
§ 2 . If the application is not sent within the established period, it is ipso facto null and void, and the school or group is deprived for that time of the right to elect or nominate, unless it is proven that the president did not send the application on time due to a just impediment or due to fraud or negligence.
§ 3 . Whoever has been nominated does not acquire any rights by virtue of the nomination; The competent authority has no obligation to admit it.
§ 4 . Voters cannot revoke the nomination made to the competent authority, if not with the latter's consent.

183
183. § 1 . If the application is not accepted by the competent authority, the right to choose returns to the school or group.
§ 2 . But if the application is admitted, the postulate will be notified, who must respond in accordance with the norm of c.

184
184. § 1 . The ecclesiastical office is lost due to the passage of the predetermined time, due to reaching the age determined by law, and due to resignation, transfer, removal or deprivation.
§ 2 . The ecclesiastical office is not lost when the right of the authority that conferred it ceases in any way, unless the law provides otherwise.
§ 3 . The loss of an office when it has been effective, must be notified as soon as possible to all those who have any right in the provision of the office.

185
185. The title of "emeritus" may be conferred on someone who has ceased to hold a position due to having reached the age of majority or due to an accepted resignation.

186
186. The loss of an office due to the expiration of the predetermined time or due to reaching the age of age only takes effect from the moment in which the competent authority notifies it in writing.

187
187. He who is of sound mind may, with just cause, renounce an ecclesiastical office.

188
188. A renunciation made due to serious fear unjustly caused, fraud, substantial error or simony is void by virtue of the law itself.

189
189. § 1 . For the resignation to be valid, whether its acceptance is required or not, it must be presented, in writing or orally before two witnesses, to the authority who is responsible for conferring the office in question.
§ 2 . The authority should not accept a resignation that is not based on a just and proportionate cause.
§ 3 . The resignation that requires acceptance does not have any effect if it is not accepted within a period of three months; The one that does not require acceptance produces its effect through the notification of the renouncer, made according to the rule of law.
§ 4 . As long as the resignation has not taken effect, it can be revoked by the renunciant; Once it has been produced, it cannot be revoked, but whoever resigns can obtain the office under another title.

190
190. § 1 . The transfer can only be done by someone who has the right to confer both the office that is lost and the one that is entrusted.
§ 2 . If the transfer is made against the will of the holder of the office, serious cause is required and, while the right to present contrary reasons remains, the procedure established by law must be observed.
§ 3 . For the transfer to take effect, it must be communicated in writing.

191
191. § 1 . In case of transfer, the first office becomes vacant with the canonical possession of the second, unless otherwise provided by law or prescribed by the competent authority.
§ 2 . The transferred person receives the remuneration corresponding to the first office, until he or she takes canonical possession of the second.

192
192. One is removed from an office, both by a legitimate decree given by the competent authority, without prejudice to the rights that could have been acquired by contract, and by the right itself in accordance with the norm of c. 194.

193
193. § 1 . No one can be removed from an office conferred for an indefinite period, except for serious causes and observing the procedure determined by law.
§ 2 . The same applies to those who received an office for a specific period of time, without prejudice to the provisions of c.

194
194. § 1 . The following are automatically removed from the ecclesiastical office:
1 who has lost the clerical state;
2 who has publicly departed from the Catholic faith or from the communion of the Church;
3 the clergyman who attempts to marry, even if it is only civil.
§ 2 . The removal discussed in nos. 2 and 3 can only be urged if it is confirmed by a declaration from the competent authority.

195
195. If someone is removed from an office with which he provided his support, not by law, but by decree of the competent authority, the same authority must ensure that it is provided for a time convenient to his support, unless it has been provided otherwise.

196
196. § 1 . Deprivation of office, as a penalty for a crime, can only be done according to the rule of law.
§ 2 . The deprivation produces effect as prescribed by the canons of criminal law.

197
197. The Church receives, as regulated in the civil legislation of the respective nation, prescription as a way of acquiring or losing a subjective right, as well as of freeing oneself from obligations, leaving aside the exceptions determined by the canons of this Code.

198
198. No prescription is valid if it is not based on good faith, not only at the beginning, but throughout the entire course of time required for it, except as established in c. 1362.

199
199. The following are not subject to prescription:
1 the rights and obligations that are of natural or positive divine law;
2 the rights that can only be obtained by apostolic privilege;
3 the rights and obligations that refer directly to the spiritual life of the faithful;
4 the certain and indubitable limits of the ecclesiastical circumscriptions;
5 the stipends and charges for Masses;
6 the provision of an ecclesiastical office which, by right, requires the exercise of holy orders;
7 the right of visit and the duty of obedience, the prescription of which would mean that the faithful could no longer be visited by any ecclesiastical authority, nor would they remain subject to any authority.

200
200. Unless the law expressly provides otherwise, time must be computed in accordance with the following canons.

201
201. § 1 . Continuous time means time that does not admit any interruption.
§ 2 . Useful time is understood to be that which concerns the person who uses or claims his right, in such a way that it does not run for those who do not know or cannot claim.

202
202. § 1 . In law, a day is understood to be the space of 24 hours counted as continuous, and begins at midnight, unless otherwise expressly provided; the week is a space of seven days; the month, a space of 30, and the year, a space of

203
203. § 1 . The day in question is not counted in the period, unless its beginning coincides with the beginning of the day or unless the law expressly provides otherwise.
§ 2 . Unless otherwise established, the ad quem day is included in the period, which, if it consists of one or more months or years, or one or more weeks, ends on the last day of the same number or, if the month does not have a day of the same number, on the end of the last day of the month.

204
204. § 1 . They are faithful Christians who, incorporated into Christ through baptism, are integrated into the people of God, and made participants in their own way for this reason in the priestly, prophetic and royal function of Christ, each one according to his own condition, are called to carry out the mission that God entrusted the Church to fulfill in the world.
§ 2 . This Church, constituted and ordered as a society in this world, subsists in the Catholic Church, governed by the successor of Peter and by the Bishops in communion with him.

205
205. Those baptized who are united to Christ within the visible structure of the Catholic Church in this land are in full communion with the Catholic Church, that is, through the bonds of the profession of faith, the sacraments and the ecclesiastical regime.

206
206. § 1 . In a special way, catechumens relate to the Church, that is, those who, moved by the Holy Spirit, explicitly request to be incorporated into it, and who by this same desire, as well as by the life of faith, hope and charity that they lead, are united to the Church, which already welcomes them as its own.
§ 2 . The Church pays special attention to catechumens and, while inviting them to lead an evangelical life and introducing them to the celebration of sacred rites, it already grants them some prerogatives typical of Christians.

207
207. § 1 . By divine institution, among the faithful there are sacred ministers in the Church, who in law are also called clerics; the rest are called laymen.
§ 2 . In these two groups there are faithful who, by the profession of the evangelical counsels through vows or other sacred bonds, recognized and sanctioned by the Church, consecrate themselves to God according to their own peculiar way and contribute to the saving mission of the Church; Their status, although it does not affect the hierarchical structure of the Church, nevertheless belongs to the life and sanctity of the Church.

208
208. Through their regeneration in Christ, there is true equality among all the faithful in terms of dignity and action, by virtue of which all, according to their own condition and office, cooperate in the building up of the Body of Christ.

209
209. § 1 . The faithful are obliged to always observe communion with the Church, even in their way of acting.
§ 2 . They fulfill with great diligence the duties they have both with respect to the universal Church and in relation to the particular Church to which they belong, according to the prescriptions of law.

210
210. All the faithful must strive, according to their own condition, to lead a holy life, as well as to increase the Church and promote its continued sanctification.

211
211. All the faithful have the duty and the right to work so that the divine message of salvation reaches more and more to men of all times and the entire world.

212
