212. § 1 . The faithful, aware of their own responsibility, are obliged to follow, out of Christian obedience, everything that the sacred Shepherds, as representatives of Christ, declare as teachers of the faith or establish as rectors of the Church.
§ 2 . The faithful have the right to express their needs, mainly spiritual ones, and their desires to the Pastors of the Church.
§ 3 . They have the right, and sometimes even the duty, by reason of their own knowledge, competence and prestige, to express to the sacred Pastors their opinion on that which pertains to the good of the Church and to express it to the other faithful, always preserving the integrity of faith and customs, reverence towards the Pastors and taking into account the common usefulness and dignity of the people.

213
213. The faithful have the right to receive from the sacred Pastors the help of the spiritual goods of the Church, mainly the word of God and the sacraments.

214
214. The faithful have the right to worship God according to the norms of the rite itself approved by the legitimate Pastors of the Church, and to practice their own way of spiritual life, as long as it is in accordance with the doctrine of the Church.

215
215. The faithful have the right to freely found and direct associations for purposes of charity or piety, or to promote the Christian vocation in the world; and also to meet together to pursue those same goals in common.

216
216. All the faithful, since they participate in the mission of the Church, have the right to promote and sustain apostolic action also with their own initiatives, each according to their state and condition; but no initiative claims to be Catholic without the consent of the competent ecclesiastical authority.

217
217. The faithful, since they are called by baptism to lead a life consistent with evangelical doctrine, have the right to a Christian education through which they are appropriately instructed in order to achieve the maturity of the human person and at the same time know and live the mystery of salvation.

218
218. Those who dedicate themselves to the sacred sciences enjoy fair freedom to investigate, as well as to prudently express their opinion on everything in which they are experts, maintaining due submission to the teaching of the Church.

219
219. In choosing their state of life, all the faithful have the right to be immune from any coercion.

220
220. No one is allowed to illegitimately harm the good reputation that someone enjoys, nor violate the right of each person to protect their own privacy.

221
221. § 1 . It is up to the faithful to legitimately claim the rights they have in the Church, and defend them in the competent ecclesiastical forum in accordance with the rule of law.
§ 2 . If they are called to trial by the competent authority, the faithful also have the right to be judged according to legal norms, which must be applied fairly.
§ 3 . The faithful have the right not to be sanctioned with canonical penalties, if it is not in accordance with the legal norm.

222
222. § 1 . The faithful have the duty to help the Church in its needs, so that it has what is necessary for divine worship, works of apostolate and charity, and the appropriate sustenance of the ministers.
§ 2 . They also have the duty to promote social justice, as well as, remembering the Lord's precept, to help the poor with their own goods.

223
223. § 1 . In the exercise of their rights, both individually and united in associations, the faithful must take into account the common good of the Church, as well as the rights of others and their duties towards others.
§ 2 . It is up to the regular ecclesiastical authority, in attention to the common good, to exercise the rights of the faithful.

224
224. The lay faithful, in addition to the obligations and rights that are common to all Christian faithful and those established in other canons, have the obligations and rights that are enumerated in the canons of this title.

225
225. § 1 . Since, by virtue of baptism and confirmation, the laity, like all other faithful, are destined by God to the apostolate, they have the general obligation, and enjoy the right both personally and associatively, to work so that the divine message of salvation is known and received by all men throughout the world; obligation that presses them even more in those circumstances in which only through them can men hear the Gospel and know Jesus Christ.
§ 2 . They also have the peculiar duty, each according to their own condition, to permeate and perfect the temporal order with the evangelical spirit, and thus bear witness to Christ, especially in the accomplishment of those same temporal things and in the exercise of secular tasks.

226
226. § 1 . Those who, according to their own vocation, live in the married state, have the peculiar duty of working to build up the people of God through marriage and the family.
§ 2 . For having transmitted life to their children, parents have the very serious duty and right to educate them; Therefore, it is up to Christian parents in the first place to ensure the Christian education of their children according to the doctrine taught by the Church.

227
227. The lay faithful have the right to be recognized in earthly matters that freedom that belongs to all citizens; However, when using this freedom, they must ensure that their actions are inspired by the evangelical spirit, and they must pay attention to the doctrine proposed by the Magisterium of the Church, avoiding at the same time presenting their own criteria as the doctrine of the Church, in matters of opinion.

228
228. § 1 . Lay people who are considered suitable have the capacity to be called by the sacred Pastors for those ecclesiastical offices and assignments that they can fulfill according to the prescriptions of the law.
§ 2 . Lay people who are distinguished by their science, prudence and integrity have the capacity to help the Pastors of the Church as experts and advisors, also being part of councils, in accordance with the rule of law.

229
229. § 1 . So that they can live according to the Christian doctrine, proclaim it, defend it when necessary and exercise their corresponding part in the apostolate, the laity have the duty and the right to acquire knowledge of that doctrine, according to the capacity and condition of each one.
§ 2 . They also have the right to acquire the most profound knowledge of the sacred sciences taught in universities or ecclesiastical faculties or in institutes of religious sciences, by attending their classes and obtaining academic degrees.
§ 3 . Adhering to the established prescriptions on the necessary suitability, they also have the capacity to receive from the legitimate ecclesiastical authority a mandate to teach sacred sciences.

230
230. § 1 . n Lay people who have the age and conditions determined by decree of the Episcopal Conference, may be called to the stable ministry of reader and acolyte, through the prescribed liturgical rite; However, the collation of these ministries does not give them the right to be supported or remunerated by the Church.
§ 2 . By temporary assignment, lay people can perform the role of reader in liturgical ceremonies; Likewise, all lay people can perform the functions of commentator, singer and others, in accordance with the rule of law.
§ 3 . Where the need of the Church advises it and there are no ministers, lay people, even if they are not readers or acolytes, may also supply them in some of their functions, that is, exercising the ministry of the word, presiding over liturgical prayers, administering baptism and giving Holy Communion, according to the prescriptions of law.
230. § 1, have the right to adequate remuneration that responds to their condition, and with which they can decently provide for their own needs and those of their family, also in accordance with the prescriptions of civil law; and they also have the right to have their pension and social security and so-called health care duly provided for.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canon modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Spiritus Domini of January 10, 2021)]:
230. § 1 . Lay men who have the age and conditions determined by decree of the Episcopal Conference, may be called to the stable ministry of reader and acolyte, through the prescribed liturgical rite; However, the collation of these ministries does not give them the right to be supported or remunerated by the Church.
230. § 3 .

231
231. § 1 . Lay people who permanently or temporarily dedicate themselves to a special service of the Church have the duty to acquire the appropriate training required to perform their role well, and to exercise it with conscience, generosity and diligence.
§ 2 . Maintaining what is prescribed in c.

232
232. The Church has the duty, and the proper and exclusive right, to train those who are destined for sacred ministries.

233
233. § 1 . It is the duty of the entire Christian community to encourage vocations, so that the needs of the sacred ministry in the entire Church are sufficiently provided for; This duty especially obliges Christian families, educators and, in a peculiar way, priests, especially parish priests. The diocesan Bishops, whose responsibility it is to ensure that vocations are promoted, instruct the people entrusted to them about the greatness of the sacred ministry and the need for ministers in the Church, promote and sustain initiatives to encourage vocations, especially through the works that already exist for this purpose.
§ 2 . Priests, and more specifically diocesan Bishops, should also have special interest in ensuring that those men of mature age who feel called to sacred ministries are helped with prudence, in word and deed, and that those men of mature age who feel called to sacred ministries are adequately prepared.

234
234. § 1 . Minor seminaries and other similar institutions should be preserved where they exist and encouraged, in which, in order to promote vocations, a peculiar religious formation is given, along with humanistic and scientific teaching; and it is even convenient for the diocesan Bishop, where he considers it appropriate, to provide for the erection of a minor seminary or a similar institution.
§ 2 . Unless, in certain cases, circumstances dictate otherwise, young people who wish to reach the priesthood must be equipped with the humanistic and scientific training with which young people from their own region prepare to pursue higher studies.

235
235. § 1 . Young people who wish to reach the priesthood must receive both the appropriate spiritual formation and that which is adequate for the fulfillment of the duties of the priesthood in the major seminary, during the entire time of formation or, at least, for four years, if in the opinion of the diocesan Bishop circumstances so require.
§ 2 . For those who legitimately reside outside the seminary, the diocesan Bishop must entrust a pious and suitable priest to ensure that they are diligently formed in spiritual life and discipline.

236
236. Those who aspire to the permanent diaconate must be formed according to the prescriptions of the Episcopal Conference so that they cultivate the spiritual life and carry out with dignity the offices of that order:
1 young people, staying at least three years in a residence intended for that purpose, unless the diocesan Bishop determines otherwise for serious reasons;
2 men of mature age, both celibate and married, according to the three-year plan established by the Episcopal Conference.

237
237. § 1 . In each diocese, when possible and convenient, there must be a major seminary; Otherwise, the students to prepare for sacred ministries will be entrusted to another seminary, or an interdiocesan seminary will be erected.
§ 2 . n An interdiocesan seminary must not be erected without the Episcopal Conference, in the case of a seminary for its entire territory, or, otherwise, the interested Bishops having first obtained confirmation from the Apostolic See, both of the erection of the seminary itself and of its statutes.
237. § 2 An interdiocesan seminary should not be erected without the Episcopal Conference, when it is a seminary for its entire territory, or otherwise the interested Bishops, having first obtained the approval of the Apostolic See, both for the erection of the seminary itself and for its statutes.

238
238. § 1 . Legitimately erected seminaries have by law legal personality in the Church.
§ 2 . The rector represents the seminary in all matters, unless the competent authority has established otherwise for some of them.

239
239. § 1 . Throughout the seminary there must be a rector who is in charge and, if necessary, a vice-rector, a treasurer and, if the students study in the same seminary, also professors who teach the different subjects in a coordinated manner.
§ 2 . In every seminary there must be at least one spiritual director, although the students are free to go to other priests who have been designated by the Bishop for this function.
§ 3 . The seminary's statutes must determine the way in which the other directors, professors and even the students participate in the rector's responsibility, especially with regard to maintaining discipline.

240
240. § 1 . In addition to the ordinary confessors, other confessors go regularly to the seminary; and, keeping the discipline of the center safe, students will also always be able to address any confessor, both in the seminary and outside of it.
§ 2 . You can never ask the opinion of the spiritual director or the confessors when deciding on the admission of students to the orders or on their departure from the seminary.

241
241. § 1 . The diocesan Bishop must only admit to the major seminary those who, taking into account their human and moral, spiritual and intellectual gifts, their physical health and their psychological balance, and their right intention, are considered capable of dedicating themselves to the sacred ministries in perpetuity.
§ 2 . Before being admitted, they must present the baptism and confirmation certificates, as well as the other documents required in accordance with the prescriptions of the Priestly Formation Plan.
§ 3 . When it comes to admitting those who have been dismissed from another seminary or a religious institute, a report from the respective superior is also required, especially regarding the cause of their expulsion or departure.

242
242. § 1 . n In each nation there must be a Plan for priestly formation, which will be established by the Episcopal Conference, taking into account the norms given by the supreme authority of the Church, and which must be confirmed by the Holy See; and it must adapt to new circumstances, also with the confirmation of the Holy See; This Plan will establish the general principles and norms, adapted to the pastoral needs of each region or province.
§ 2 . The norms of the Plan referred to in § 1 must be observed in all seminaries, both diocesan and interdiocesan.
242. § 1 . In each nation there must be a Plan for priestly formation, which will be established by the Episcopal Conference, keeping in mind the norms given by the supreme authority of the Church, and which must be approved by the Holy See; and it must adapt to new circumstances, also with the approval of the Holy See; This Plan will establish the general principles and norms, adapted to the pastoral needs of each region or province.

243
243. Each seminary will also have its own regulations, approved by the diocesan Bishop, or by the interested Bishops if it is an interdiocesan seminary, in which the norms of the Priestly Formation Plan are adapted to the particular circumstances, and the aspects, especially disciplinary, that refer to the daily life of the students and the order of the entire seminary are determined more precisely.

244
244. The spiritual formation and doctrinal preparation of the students in the seminary should be in perfect harmony, and their goal should be that they, according to the character of each one, achieve, along with due human maturity, the spirit of the Gospel and a close relationship with Christ.

245
245. § 1 . Through spiritual formation, students must become qualified to exercise the pastoral ministry profitably and must acquire a missionary spirit, persuading themselves that the ministry, always carried out with living faith and charity, contributes to their own sanctification; and also learn to cultivate those virtues that are most appreciable in human coexistence, so that they can adequately reconcile human and supernatural goods.
§ 2 . The students must be formed so that, full of love for the Church of Christ, they are united with humble and filial charity to the Roman Pontiff, successor of Peter, they adhere to the Bishop himself as faithful cooperators and work together with their brothers; Through life in common in the seminary and the bonds of friendship and understanding with others, they must prepare for a fraternal union with the diocesan presbytery, of which they will be members for the service of the Church.

246
246. § 1 . The Eucharistic celebration is the center of the entire life of the seminary, so that daily, participating in the charity of Christ, the students draw strength above all from this very rich source for their apostolic work and for their spiritual life.
§ 2 . They must be trained for the celebration of the liturgy of the hours, through which the ministers of God pray to the Lord on behalf of the Church for the people entrusted to them and for the entire world.
§ 3 . The cult of the Blessed Virgin Mary should be encouraged, including through the recitation of the holy rosary, mental prayer and other practices of piety with which students acquire the spirit of prayer and are strengthened in their vocation.
§ 4 . Students should get into the habit of attending the sacrament of penance frequently, and it is recommended that each one have a spiritual director, freely chosen, to whom they can open their soul with complete confidence.
§ 5 . The students will do spiritual exercises every year.

247
247. § 1 . By means of adequate training, the students should be prepared to observe the state of celibacy, and learn to hold it in high esteem as a peculiar gift of God.
§ 2 . Students must be made aware of the obligations and burdens of sacred ministers, without hiding from them any of the difficulties that priestly life entails.

248
248. The doctrinal training to be imparted must aim to ensure that the students, together with the general culture appropriate to the needs of the time and place, acquire a broad and solid knowledge of the sacred disciplines, so that, by founding and nurturing their own faith in them, they can conveniently announce the doctrine of the Gospel to the men of their time, in a manner appropriate to their mentality.

249
249. The Priestly Formation Plan must provide that the students are not only carefully instructed in their own language, but also that they master the Latin language, and also acquire that convenient knowledge of other languages that is necessary or useful for their formation or for the pastoral ministry.

250
250. The philosophical and theological studies provided for in the seminary can be done successively or simultaneously, in accordance with the Priestly Formation Plan; and they must last at least six years, so that the time allocated to philosophical subjects comprises a biennium and that corresponding to theological studies is equivalent to a quadrennium.

251
251. Philosophical training, which must be based on the heritage of perennial philosophy and take into account at the same time the philosophical research carried out with the progress of time, must be given in a way that completes the human formation of the students, contributes to sharpening their minds and prepares them so that they can better carry out their theological studies.

252
252. §1 . Theological training, in the light of faith and under the guidance of the Magisterium, must be given in such a way that students know all Catholic doctrine, founded on divine Revelation, make it food for their own spiritual life and know how to communicate and defend it appropriately in the exercise of their ministry.
§ 2 . Students must be trained with particular diligence in sacred Scripture, so that they acquire a complete vision of all of it.
§ 3 . There must be classes of dogmatic theology, always based on the written word of God, together with sacred Tradition, with which students know in a deeper way the mysteries of salvation, having Saint Thomas mainly as a teacher; and also classes in moral and pastoral theology, canon law, liturgy, ecclesiastical history and other auxiliary and special disciplines, in accordance with the norms of the Priestly Formation Plan.

253
253. § 1 . For the position of professor of philosophical, theological and legal disciplines, the Bishop or Bishops concerned will appoint only those who, standing out for their virtues, have obtained a doctorate or bachelor's degree from a university or faculty recognized by the Holy See.
§ 2 . Efforts should be made to appoint different professors for sacred Scripture, dogmatic theology, moral theology, liturgy, philosophy, canon law, ecclesiastical history and for the other disciplines, which must be explained according to their own methods.
§ 3 . A teacher who seriously fails to fulfill his or her position must be removed by the authority mentioned in the § 1.

254
254. § 1 . In teaching, teachers must constantly pay special attention to the intimate unity and harmony of the entire doctrine of the faith, so that students understand that they are learning a unique science; To better achieve this, there must be someone in the seminary who directs the entire organization of the studies.
§ 2 . Teach students in such a way that they become capable of examining questions with a scientific method through appropriate investigations carried out by themselves; There will, therefore, be exercises in which, under the direction of the teachers, students learn to carry out studies with their own work.

255
255. Although all the training of students in the seminary has a pastoral purpose, specifically pastoral instruction must be given in it, with which, also attending to the needs of the place and time, the students learn the principles and methods proper to the ministry of teaching, sanctifying and governing the people of God.

256
256. § 1 . Students should be diligently trained in that which in a peculiar way refers to the sacred ministry, especially in the practice of the catechetical and homiletic method, in divine worship and in a peculiar way in the celebration of the sacraments, in dealing with men, also with non-Catholics or non-believers, in the administration of a parish and in the fulfillment of other tasks.
§ 2 . Students should be taught the needs of the universal Church, so that they may be solicitous in promoting vocations, for missionary, ecumenical tasks and those others, including social ones, that are more urgent.

257
257. § 1 . The formation of the students must be carried out in such a way that they feel interested not only in the particular Church in whose service they are incardinated but also in the universal Church, and they are willing to dedicate themselves to those particular Churches that are in serious need.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop must ensure that clerics who wish to move from their own particular Church to a particular Church in another region are suitably prepared to carry out the sacred ministry there, that is, that they learn the language of that region and become familiar with its institutions, social conditions, uses and customs.

258
258. So that they also learn in practice the method of carrying out the apostolate, the students, during the period of study but mainly during vacations, must be initiated in pastoral practice, through the appropriate tasks to be determined by the Ordinary, appropriate to the age of the students and the circumstances of the place, always under the direction of an expert priest.

259
259. § 1 . It is up to the diocesan Bishop, or the interested Bishops in the case of an interdiocesan seminary, to decide what refers to the superior regime and administration of the seminary.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop, or the Bishops concerned if it is an interdiocesan seminary, personally and frequently visit the seminary, supervise the formation of its students and the teaching of philosophical and theological subjects, and obtain knowledge of the vocation, character, piety and achievement of the students, especially with a view to conferring sacred orders on them.

260
260. In carrying out their own tasks, everyone must obey the rector, who is responsible for the immediate direction of the seminary in accordance always with the Priestly Formation Plan and the regulations of the seminary.

261
261. §1 . The rector of the seminary, and also, under his authority and to the extent that is their responsibility, the superiors and professors must ensure that the students perfectly comply with the norms established in the Priestly Formation Plan and in the regulations of the seminary.
§ 2 . The rector of the seminary and the director of studies should diligently ensure that the professors properly carry out their task, according to the prescriptions of the Priestly Formation Plan and the regulations of the seminary.

262
262. The seminary is exempt from the parish regime; and it is the rector or his delegate who performs the function of parish priest for all those who are in the seminary except what refers to marriage and without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 985.

263
263. The diocesan Bishop or, in the case of an interdiocesan seminary, the interested Bishops, with a quota determined by common agreement, must contribute to the establishment and maintenance of the seminary, to the support of the students, to the remuneration of the teachers and to other needs of the seminary.

264
264. §1 . To provide for the needs of the seminary, in addition to the collection discussed in c. 1266, the Bishop can impose a tribute in his diocese.
§ 2 . All ecclesiastical legal entities, including private ones, that have their headquarters in the diocese are subject to the tribute in favor of the seminary, unless they are supported only by alms or there is actually a school of students or teachers that aims to promote the common good of the Church; This tribute must be general, proportionate to the income of those who must pay it and determined according to the needs of the seminary.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio of February 11, 2022)]:

265
265. n It is necessary that every cleric be incardinated in a particular Church or in a personal prelature, or in an institute of consecrated life or in a society that enjoys this faculty, or also in a public clerical association that has obtained such faculty from the Apostolic See, so that headless or lazy clerics are in no way admitted.
265. It is necessary that every cleric be incardinated in a particular Church or in a personal prelature, or in an institute of consecrated life or in a society that enjoys this faculty, so that headless or lazy clerics are in no way admitted.

266
266. § 1 . By receiving the diaconate, one becomes a clergyman and is incardinated in a particular Church or in a personal prelature for whose service he was promoted.
§ 2 . The professed member with perpetual vows in a religious institute or definitively incorporated into a clerical society of apostolic life, upon receiving the diaconate, is incardinated as a clergyman in that institute or society, unless, as regards societies, the constitutions say otherwise.
§3 . By receiving the diaconate, the member of a secular institute is incardinated in the particular Church for whose service he has been promoted, unless, by concession of the Apostolic See, he is incardinated in the same institute.
266. § 2, is excardinated from his own particular Church, by perpetual or definitive admission into the institute of consecrated life or into the society of apostolic life
266. § 1, if they are destined for works specific to the institute or its government, they depend on the Bishop in the same way as the religious.

267
267. § 1 . For an already incardinated cleric to be validly incardinated in another particular Church, he must obtain from his diocesan Bishop letters of excardination signed by him, and likewise the letters of incardination signed by the diocesan Bishop of the particular Church in which he wishes to be incardinated.
§ 2 . The excardination granted in this way has no effect if incardination in another particular Church has not been achieved.

268
268. § 1 . The clergyman who has legitimately transferred from his own to another particular Church is incardinated into it by virtue of the same right after a five-year period has elapsed if he expresses this desire in writing both to the diocesan Bishop of the Church that welcomed him and to his own diocesan Bishop, and neither of them has communicated his refusal in writing, within a period of four months from the moment in which they received the request.
§ 2 . The cleric who is incardinated into an institute or society in accordance with the norm of c.

269
269. The diocesan Bishop must not proceed with the incardination of a clergyman unless:
1 the need or usefulness of his particular Church requires it, and the prescriptions of law that refer to the honest support of clerics remain safe;
2 he is certified by a legitimate document that the excardination has been granted and has also obtained, if necessary under secrecy, the appropriate reports from the diocesan Bishop who grants the excardination, about the life, conduct and studies of the clergyman in question;
3 the clergyman has declared in writing to the same diocesan Bishop that he wishes to be assigned to the service of the new particular Church, in accordance with the law.

270
270. Excardination can only be licitly granted with just causes, such as the usefulness of the Church or the good of the clergyman himself; and it cannot be denied unless there are serious causes, but in this case, the cleric who considers himself harmed and has found a Bishop willing to receive him, can appeal against the decision.

271
271. § 1 . Outside of the case of true need of the particular Church itself, the diocesan Bishop must not deny permission to transfer to another place to the clerics whom he knows are willing and consider suitable to go to regions that suffer a serious shortage of clergy to carry out the sacred ministry there; but provide so that, by written agreement with the diocesan Bishop of the place where they will go, the rights and duties of those clerics are determined.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop may grant his clergy a license to move to another particular Church for a certain period of time, which may be renewed successively, in such a way, however, that these clerics remain incardinated in the particular Church itself and, upon returning, have all the rights that would correspond to them if they had dedicated themselves to the sacred ministry there.
§ 3 . The cleric who legitimately passes to another particular Church, remaining incardinated to his own Church, can be called with just cause by his own diocesan Bishop, as long as the agreements agreed upon with the other Bishop and natural equity are observed; Likewise, and complying with the same conditions, the diocesan Bishop of the other particular Church may deny with just cause to that clergyman the license to continue remaining in his own territory.

272
272. The diocesan Administrator cannot grant excardination or incardination, nor the license to transfer to another particular Church, unless one year has passed since the episcopal see became vacant, and with the consent of the college of consultants.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Competentias quasdam decernere of February 11, 2022)]:

273
273. Clerics have a special obligation to show respect and obedience to the Supreme Pontiff and his own Ordinary.

274
274. § 1 . Only clerics can obtain offices for the exercise of which the power of order or the power of ecclesiastical regime is required.
§ 2 . Unless excused by a legitimate impediment, clerics must accept and faithfully perform the task entrusted to them by their Ordinary.

275
275. §1 . The clerics, since they all work in the same work, the building of the Body of Christ, are united among themselves with the bond of fraternity and prayer, and encourage mutual cooperation, according to the prescriptions of particular law.
§ 2 . Clergy must recognize and promote the mission that, for their part, the laity exercise in the Church and in the world.

276
276. §1 . The clerics, in their own conduct, are obliged to seek holiness for a peculiar reason, since, consecrated to God by a new title in the reception of orders, they are administrators of the mysteries of the Lord in the service of his people.
§ 2 . In order to achieve this perfection:
1 carry out, above all, faithfully and tirelessly the tasks of the pastoral ministry;
2 nourish your spiritual life at the double table of sacred Scripture and the Eucharist; Therefore, priests are strongly invited to offer the Eucharistic Sacrifice every day, and deacons to participate daily in the same oblation;
3 priests and deacons who wish to receive the priesthood have the obligation to celebrate the liturgy of the hours every day according to their own and approved liturgical books; and the permanent deacons must pray that part determined by the Episcopal Conference;
4 are also obliged to attend spiritual retreats, according to the prescriptions of particular law;
5, it is recommended that they perform mental prayer every day, frequently access the sacrament of penance, have a special veneration for the Virgin Mother of God and practice other means of sanctification, both common and particular.
276. § 2, 3, and they will perform other exercises of piety.
§ 4 . They will pay a special cult, also through the recitation of the holy rosary, to the Virgin Mother of God, model and protection of all consecrated life.
§ 5 They will faithfully observe the annual times of spiritual retreat.
276. § 2 , 3; and to the members of the institutes of consecrated life and societies of apostolic life, in accordance with their constitutions.
§ 2 . The other faithful are also strongly invited to, depending on the circumstances, participate in the Liturgy of the Hours, since it is the action of the Church.

277
277. § 1 . The clergy are bound to observe perfect and perpetual continence for the sake of the Kingdom of heaven, and are therefore bound to observe celibacy, which is a peculiar gift of God by which sacred ministers can more easily unite themselves to Christ with a whole heart, and dedicate themselves more freely to the service of God and men.
§ 2 . Clergy must exercise due caution in relation to those whose treatment may jeopardize their obligation to maintain continence or cause scandal to the faithful.
§ 3 . It is up to the diocesan Bishop to establish more specific norms on this matter and to issue a judgment in particular cases on the fulfillment of this obligation.

278
278. § 1 . Secular clerics have the right to associate with others to achieve ends that are in accordance with the clerical state.
§ 2 . Secular clerics must especially hold in high esteem those associations that, with statutes reviewed by the competent authority, through an adequate and appropriately approved plan of life as well as through fraternal help, encourage the search for holiness in the exercise of ministry and contribute to the union of clerics among themselves and with their own Bishop.
§ 3 . Clergymen must refrain from forming or participating in associations whose purpose or actions are incompatible with the obligations of the clerical state or may be an obstacle to the diligent fulfillment of the task entrusted to them by the competent ecclesiastical authority.

279
279. § 1 . Even after receiving the priesthood, clerics must continue sacred studies, and must profess that solid doctrine founded on sacred Scripture, transmitted by the elders and received as common in the Church, as determined above all in the documents of the Councils and the Roman Pontiffs; avoiding profane innovations of terminology and false science.
§ 2 . According to the prescriptions of particular law, priests, after ordination, must frequently attend the pastoral lessons that must be established, as well as other lessons, theological meetings or conferences, at times also determined by the same particular law, through which they are offered the opportunity to deepen their knowledge of sacred sciences and pastoral methods.
§ 3 . They should also try to know other sciences, especially those that are connected with the sacred, mainly to the extent that this knowledge helps the exercise of pastoral ministry.
279. § 2 ;
2 Take care that the priests of your district do not lack spiritual means, and be especially solicitous of those who find themselves in difficult circumstances or who are overwhelmed by problems.
§ 3 . The archpriest should ensure that the parish priests of his district who he knows are seriously ill do not lack spiritual and material aid, and that the funeral of those who die is celebrated with dignity; and also provide that, when they become ill or die, the books, documents, sacred objects and ornaments or other things belonging to the Church do not perish or are removed from their place.
§ 4 . The archpriest has the duty to visit the parishes in his district, as determined by the diocesan Bishop.

280
280. Clergy are strongly advised to have a certain life in common, which, as far as possible, must be preserved wherever it is in force.

281
281. § 1 . Clergymen dedicated to ecclesiastical ministry deserve remuneration appropriate to their condition, taking into account both the nature of the office they perform and the circumstances of the place and time, so that they can provide for their own needs and the fair remuneration of those people whose service they need.
§ 2 . Care must also be taken to ensure that they enjoy social assistance, through which their needs are adequately provided for in the event of illness, disability or old age.
§ 3 . Married deacons fully dedicated to ecclesiastical ministry deserve such compensation that they can support themselves and their families; But those who, for exercising or having exercised a civil profession, already receive remuneration, must provide for their own needs and those of their family with what they earn for that title.
281. to the support of clerics who provide service in the diocese, unless another way of meeting this requirement has been established.
§ 2 . Where social security for the clergy is not yet adequately organized, the Episcopal Conference should ensure that there is an institution that sufficiently provides for the social security of the clergy.
§ 3 . Let there be established in each diocese, to the extent necessary, a common mass, with which the Bishops can fulfill their obligations towards other people who serve the Church and meet the different needs of the diocese, and by which the richest dioceses can also help the poorest.
§ 4 . Depending on the circumstances of each place, the purposes discussed in §§ 2 and 3 can be best achieved through diocesan institutions federated among themselves, or through cooperation, and even by an association agreed upon between several dioceses or constituted for the entire territory of the same Episcopal Conference.
§ 5 . If possible, these institutions should be constituted in such a way that they obtain effectiveness even before the civil order.

282
282. § 1 . Clergymen must live simply and abstain from everything that seems vanity.
§ 2 . They voluntarily allocate to the good of the Church and to works of charity the surplus of those goods that they receive on the occasion of the exercise of an ecclesiastical office, once they have provided with them for their honest support and the fulfillment of all the obligations of their state.

283
283. § 1 . Even if they do not have a residential office, clerics must not leave their diocese for a significant period of time, which will be determined by particular law, without at least presumed permission from the Ordinary himself.
§ 2 . It is also the responsibility of clergy to have adequate and sufficient vacation time every year, determined by universal or particular law.

284
284. Clergy must wear dignified ecclesiastical dress, according to the norms given by the Episcopal Conference and the legitimate customs of the place.

285
285. § 1 . Clergymen should abstain completely from anything that discredits their state, according to the prescriptions of particular law.
§ 2 . Clergymen must avoid those things that, although not indecorous, are foreign to the clerical state.
§ 3 . Clergymen are prohibited from accepting those public positions that involve participation in the exercise of civil power.
§ 4 . Without permission from their Ordinary, they must not accept the administration of property belonging to lay people or secular offices that carry with them the obligation to render accounts; They are prohibited from being guarantors even with their own property, without having consulted their own Ordinary; and they must refrain from signing documents in which the obligation to pay an amount of money is assumed without specifying the cause.
285. §§ 3 and 4, 286, 287 § 2, do not bind permanent deacons.

286
286. Clergymen are prohibited from engaging in negotiation or commerce without license from the legitimate ecclesiastical authority, both personally and through others, whether for their own benefit or that of third parties.

287
287. § 1 . Let clerics always encourage, as much as possible, that peace and concord founded on justice be preserved among men.
§ 2 . They must not actively participate in political parties or in the leadership of trade union associations, unless, according to the judgment of the competent ecclesiastical authority, the defense of the rights of the Church or the promotion of the common good requires it.
287. and 289, and to those who are clerics, also those of c. 279 § 2 ; in lay institutes of pontifical right, the license referred to in c. 285 § 4, can be granted by the Major Superior himself.

288
288. Unless particular law establishes otherwise, the prescriptions of cc. 284,

289
289. § 1 . Since military service is less congruent with the clerical state, clerics and also candidates for sacred orders do not volunteer for military service, except with the permission of their Ordinary.
§ 2 . Clergy must also make use of the exemptions granted to them by laws and conventions or customs, in order not to exercise public civil positions and offices outside the clerical state, unless the Ordinary itself determines otherwise in particular cases.

290
290. Once validly received, sacred ordination is never annulled. However, a clergyman loses clerical status:
1 by judicial ruling or administrative decree, in which the invalidity of the sacred ordination is declared;
2 for the legitimately imposed resignation penalty;
3 by rescript of the Apostolic See, which is only granted, by the Apostolic See, to deacons, when there are serious causes; to priests, for very serious reasons.

291
291. Outside of the cases referred to in c. 290, 1, the loss of the clerical state does not entail the dispensation from the obligation of celibacy, which only the Roman Pontiff grants.

292
292. The cleric who, in accordance with the rule of law, loses the clerical state, loses with it the rights inherent to that state, and is no longer subject to the obligations of the clerical state, without prejudice to the provisions of c. 291; He is prohibited from exercising the power of order, except as established in c. 976; For this reason, he is deprived of all offices, functions and any delegated power.

293
293. The cleric who has lost the clerical state cannot be assigned again among the clerics, except by rescript of the Apostolic See.

294
294. In order to promote a convenient distribution of priests or to carry out peculiar pastoral or missionary works in favor of various regions or diverse social groups, the Apostolic See, after hearing the interested Episcopal Conferences, may erect personal prelatures consisting of priests and deacons of the secular clergy.

295
295. § 1 . The personal prelature is governed by the statutes given by the Apostolic See and its government is entrusted to a Prelate as its own Ordinary, who has the power to erect a national or international seminary as well as incardinate students and promote them to the orders as a service to the prelature.
§ 2 . The Prelate must take care of the spiritual formation of those ordained with the aforementioned title as well as their appropriate sustenance.

296
296. Through agreements established with the prelature, lay people can dedicate themselves to the apostolic works of the personal prelature; But the mode of this organic cooperation and the main duties and rights attached to it must be adequately determined in the statutes.

297
297. The statutes will determine the relations of the personal prelature with the local Ordinaries of those particular Churches in which the prelature exercises or wishes to exercise its pastoral or missionary works, prior to the consent of the diocesan Bishop.
_________________________________________

298
298. § 1 . There are associations in the Church other than institutes of consecrated life and societies of apostolic life, in which the faithful, clergy or laity, or clerics together with lay people, working together, seek to foster a more perfect life, promote public worship, or Christian doctrine, or carry out other apostolate activities, namely, initiatives for evangelization, the exercise of works of piety or charity, and the animation of the temporal order in a Christian spirit.
§ 2 . The faithful should preferably register in those associations that have been established, praised or recommended by the competent ecclesiastical authority.
298. § 1, without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 301 § 1 .
§ 2 . These associations are called private even though they have been praised or recommended by ecclesiastical authority.
§ 3 . No private association is admitted into the Church if its statutes have not been reviewed by the competent authority.

299
299. § 1 . The faithful have the right, by private agreement among themselves, to establish associations for the purposes referred to in the c

300
300. No association can call itself "Catholic" without the consent of the competent authority, in accordance with the norm of c. 312.

301
301. § 1 . It is exclusively the responsibility of the competent ecclesiastical authority to establish associations of the faithful that propose to transmit Christian doctrine in the name of the Church, or promote public worship, or that pursue other purposes reserved by their very nature to the ecclesiastical authority.
§ 2 . If it considers it appropriate, the competent ecclesiastical authority may also establish associations that directly or indirectly seek to achieve other spiritual goals, which are not sufficiently provided for by private initiative.
§ 3 . The associations of faithful erected by the competent ecclesiastical authority are called public associations.

302
302. Those associations of faithful that are under the direction of clerics, endorse the exercise of the sacred order and are recognized as such by the competent authority are called clerical.

303
303. Third orders, or by another appropriate name, are those associations whose members, living in the world and participating in the spirit of a religious institute, dedicate themselves to the apostolate and seek Christian perfection under the high direction of that institute.

304
304. § 1 . All associations of the faithful, both public and private, whatever their name or title, must have their own statutes, which determine the purpose or social objective of the association, its headquarters, the government and the conditions required to be part of them, and also indicate its way of acting, taking into account the need or convenience of the time and place.
§ 2 . They will choose a title or name that responds to the mentality of the time and place, preferably inspired by the goal they pursue.

305
305. § 1 . All associations of the faithful are under the supervision of the competent ecclesiastical authority, whose responsibility is to ensure that the integrity of faith and customs is preserved in them, and to prevent abuses from being introduced into ecclesiastical discipline; Therefore, she has the duty and right to visit them in accordance with the law and the statutes; and they are also under the regime of that authority, in accordance with the prescriptions of the canons that follow.
§ 2 . All associations, whatever their type, are under the supervision of the Holy See; Diocesan associations are under the supervision of the local Ordinary, as well as other associations to the extent that they work in the diocese.

306
306. To have the rights and privileges of an association and the indulgences and other spiritual graces granted to it, it is necessary and sufficient to have been validly admitted to it and not to have been legitimately expelled according to the prescriptions of the law and the statutes of the association.

307
307. § 1 . The admission of members must take place in accordance with the law and the statutes of each association.
§ 2 . The same person can belong to several associations.
§ 3 . Members of religious institutes may register in associations, with the consent of their Superiors, in accordance with the norm of their own law.

308
308. No one who has been legitimately admitted to an association can be expelled from it, except for just cause, in accordance with the rule of law and the statutes.

309
309. Legitimately established associations have the power, in accordance with the rule of law and the statutes, to establish specific regulations that refer to the association, to hold meetings and to appoint presidents, officers, dependents, and property administrators.

310
310. The private association not constituted as a legal entity cannot, as such, be subject to obligations and rights; but the faithful who are members of it can contract obligations jointly, and acquire and possess property as co-owners and co-possessors; and they can exercise these rights and obligations through an agent or attorney.

311
311. Members of institutes of consecrated life who preside or provide assistance to associations linked in some way to their institute, ensure that these associations provide help to the works of apostolate in the diocese, collaborating above all, under the direction of the local Ordinary, with the associations that seek to exercise the apostolate in the diocese.

312
312. § 1 . It is the competent authority to establish public associations:
1 the Holy See, for universal and international associations;
2 the Episcopal Conference within its territory, for national associations that is, which by the same erection aim to exercise their activity throughout the nation;
3 the diocesan Bishop, within his own territory, but not the diocesan Administrator, for diocesan associations; Exceptions, however, are those associations whose right of erection is reserved for other people.
§ 2 . For the valid erection of an association or a section thereof in a diocese, the consent of the diocesan Bishop is required, given in writing even in the case that such erection is made by apostolic privilege; However, the written consent of the diocesan Bishop to erect a house of a religious institute is also valid to erect, in the same house or in the attached church, an association that is specific to that institute.
312. §1 .
312. § 1, confirm the president of a public association elected by it, or establish the one who has been presented or appoint him in his own right; but it is up to the ecclesiastical authority to appoint the chaplain or ecclesiastical assistant, after hearing, when appropriate, the senior officers of the association.
§ 2 . The rule established in the § 1 also applies to associations established by members of religious institutes by virtue of apostolic privilege, outside their churches or houses; But in associations established by members of religious institutes in their own church or house, the appointment or confirmation of the president and the chaplain is the responsibility of the Superior of the institute, in accordance with the norm of the statutes.
§ 3 . In non-clerical associations, lay people may serve as president and this function should not be entrusted to the chaplain or ecclesiastical assistant, unless the statutes determine otherwise.
§ 4 . In public associations of the faithful, which are directly ordered to the exercise of the apostolate, those who hold leadership positions in political parties should not be presidents.
312. § 1, can appoint a commissioner, who temporarily directs the association on his or her behalf.
§ 2 . The authority that appointed or confirmed him may remove from office the president of a public association, with just cause, after first hearing, however, said president and the senior officers according to the statutes; according to the cc standard. 192-195, the person who appointed him can remove the chaplain.
312. §1, to which the administration must report every year.
§ 2 . He must also give an exact account to the same authority of the use of the offerings and alms received.
312. § 1 ; but the approval of the statutes does not modify the private nature of the association.

313
313. A public association, and also a confederation of public associations, is constituted as a legal entity by virtue of the same decree by which it is established by the competent ecclesiastical authority in accordance with the norm of c. 312, and thus receives the mission to the extent that he needs it, for the purposes that he intends to achieve in the name of the Church.

314
314. The statutes of every public association, as well as their review or change, require the approval of the ecclesiastical authority responsible for their erection, in accordance with the norm of the c

315
315. Public associations can freely adopt initiatives that are in accordance with their character, and are governed in accordance with the norm of their statutes, although always under the high direction of the ecclesiastical authority of which the c

316
316. § 1 . Whoever publicly rejects the Catholic faith or departs from ecclesiastical communion, or is subject to an imposed or declared excommunication, cannot be validly admitted to public associations.
§ 2 . Those who, being legitimately affiliated, fall into the case of § 1, must be expelled from the association, after having been previously reprimanded, in accordance with the statutes themselves and preserving the right to resort to the ecclesiastical authority referred to in c.

317
317. § 1 . Unless otherwise provided in the statutes, it corresponds to the ecclesiastical authority referred to in the c

318
318. § 1 . In special circumstances, when serious reasons require it, the ecclesiastical authority referred to in the c

319
319. § 1 . Unless otherwise provided, a legitimately established public association administers the property it possesses in accordance with the rules of the statutes and under the superior direction of the ecclesiastical authority referred to in the c

320
320. § 1 . Associations erected by the Holy See can only be suppressed by it.
§ 2 . For serious reasons, the Episcopal Conferences may suppress the associations erected by them; the diocesan Bishop, those erected by himself, as well as the associations erected, by virtue of apostolic indult, by members of religious institutes with the consent of the diocesan Bishop.
§ 3 . The competent authority does not suppress a public association without hearing its president and other senior officials.

321
321. The faithful direct and govern private associations, in accordance with the prescriptions of the statutes.

322
322. § 1 . A private association of faithful can acquire legal personality by formal decree of the authority indicated in c. 312.
§ 2 . Only those private associations whose statutes have been approved by the ecclesiastical authority referred to in c.

323
323. § 1 . Although private associations of the faithful have autonomy in accordance with the norm of c. 321, are subject to the supervision of the ecclesiastical authority according to c. 305, and also to the regime of said authority.
§ 2 . It is also up to that ecclesiastical authority, respecting the autonomy of private associations, to monitor and ensure that the dispersion of forces is avoided, and that the exercise of the apostolate is ordered to the common good.

324
324. § 1 . A private association of faithful freely appoints its president and officers, in accordance with the statutes.
§ 2 . If a private association of faithful desires a spiritual advisor, it can freely choose him from among the priests who legitimately exercise ministry in the diocese; However, this requires confirmation from the local Ordinary.

325
325. § 1 . Private associations of faithful freely administer the assets they possess according to the prescriptions of the statutes, safeguarding the right of the competent ecclesiastical authority to supervise so that the assets are used for the purposes of the association.
§ 2 . In accordance with the norm of c. 1301, is under the authority of the local Ordinary with regard to the administration and expenditure of the goods that have been received as a donation or legacy for pious causes.

326
326. § 1 . The private association of faithful is extinguished in accordance with the norm of the statutes; It can also be suppressed by the competent authority, if its activity is seriously damaging to doctrine or ecclesiastical discipline, or causes scandal to the faithful.
§ 2 . The destination of the assets of an association that has been extinguished must be determined in accordance with the rules of the statutes, safeguarding the acquired rights and the will of the donors.

327
327. The lay faithful must hold in great esteem the associations that are established for the spiritual purposes enumerated in c. 298, especially those that try to inform the temporal order with a Christian spirit, and thus foster a more intimate union between faith and life.

328
328. Those who preside over lay associations, even if they have been erected by virtue of apostolic privilege, must ensure that their association collaborates with other associations of the faithful, where appropriate, and that they willingly provide help to the various Christian works, especially those that exist in the same territory.

329
329. The presidents of lay associations must ensure that the members of their association are properly formed for the exercise of the apostolate proper to the laity.

330
330. Just as, by divine determination, Saint Peter and the other Apostles constitute a College, in the same way the Roman Pontiff, successor of Peter, and the Bishops, successors of the Apostles, are united with each other.

331
331. The Bishop of the Roman Church, in whom remains the function that the Lord singularly entrusted to Peter, first among the Apostles, and which was to be transmitted to his successors, is head of the College of Bishops, Vicar of Christ and Pastor of the universal Church on earth; which, therefore, has, by virtue of its function, ordinary power, which is supreme, full, immediate and universal in the Church, and which it can always exercise freely.

332
332. § 1 . The Roman Pontiff obtains full and supreme power in the Church through the legitimate election accepted by him together with the episcopal consecration. Therefore, the person chosen for the supreme pontificate who already has episcopal character, obtains that power from the moment of his acceptance. But if the chosen one lacks episcopal character, he must be ordained Bishop immediately.
§ 2 . If the Roman Pontiff were to renounce his office, for validity it is required that the resignation be free and formally expressed, but not that it be accepted by anyone.

333
333. § 1 . By virtue of his office, the Roman Pontiff not only has power over the entire Church, but also holds the primacy of ordinary power over all the particular Churches and their groups, thereby strengthening and defending at the same time the own, ordinary and immediate power that belongs to the Bishops in the particular Churches entrusted to their care.
§ 2 . In exercising his office as Supreme Pastor of the Church, the Roman Pontiff is always united by communion with the other Bishops and even with the entire Church; He has, however, the right to determine the way, personal or collegial, of exercising that office, according to the needs of the Church.
§ 3 . There is no appeal or appeal against a sentence or decree of the Roman Pontiff.

334
334. In the exercise of their office, the Bishops are at the disposal of the Roman Pontiff, who can provide their cooperation in different ways, among which is the synod of Bishops. The Cardinal Fathers also help him, as well as other people and, according to the needs of the times, various institutions. All these people and institutions fulfill in the name of the Roman Pontiff and with his authority the function entrusted to them, for the good of all the Churches, in accordance with the norms determined by law.

335
335. When the Roman see is vacant or totally impeded, nothing has to be innovated in the regime of the universal Church: however, the special laws given for such cases must be observed.

336
336. The Episcopal College, whose head is the Supreme Pontiff and of which the Bishops are members by virtue of sacramental consecration and hierarchical communion with the head and members of the College, and in which the apostolic body continually perseveres, is also, in union with its head and never without that head, subject of supreme and full power over the entire Church.

337
337. § 1 . The power of the College of Bishops over the entire Church is exercised solemnly in the Ecumenical Council.
§ 2 . This same power is exercised through the joint action of the Bishops dispersed throughout the world, promoted or freely accepted as such by the Roman Pontiff, so that it becomes a truly collegial act.
§ 3 . It is up to the Roman Pontiff, in accordance with the needs of the Church, to determine and promote the ways in which the College of Bishops is to collegially exercise its function for the entire Church.

338
338. § 1 . It is exclusively the responsibility of the Roman Pontiff to convene the Ecumenical Council, to preside over it personally or through others, to transfer it, suspend it or dissolve it, and to approve its decrees.
§ 2 . It is up to the Roman Pontiff to determine the issues to be discussed in the Council, as well as to establish its regulations; To the questions determined by the Roman Pontiff, the Council Fathers can add others, which must be approved by the Pope.

339
339. § 1 . All Bishops who are members of the Episcopal College, and only they, have the right and duty to attend the Ecumenical Council with a deliberative vote.
§ 2 . Others who lack episcopal dignity may also be called to participate in the Council by the supreme authority of the Church, which is responsible for determining the role they should have in the Council.

340
340. If the Apostolic See becomes vacant during the Council, it is interrupted by its own right until the new Supreme Pontiff decides to continue it or dissolve it.

341
341. § 1 . The decrees of the Ecumenical Council only have obligatory force if, having been approved by the Roman Pontiff together with the Council Fathers, they are confirmed by the Pope and promulgated by his mandate.
§ 2 . In order for them to have mandatory force, the decrees given by the Episcopal College through a properly collegial act according to another method promoted or freely accepted by the Roman Pontiff need the same confirmation and promulgation.

342
342. The synod of Bishops is an assembly of Bishops chosen from the different regions of the world, who meet on specified occasions to foster close union between the Roman Pontiff and the Bishops, and to assist the Pope with their advice for the integrity and improvement of faith and customs and the conservation and strengthening of ecclesiastical discipline, and to study questions relating to the action of the Church in the world.

343
343. It is the responsibility of the synod of Bishops to debate the issues that are to be addressed, and to express its opinion, but not to settle these issues or give decrees regarding them, unless in specific cases it has been granted deliberative power by the Roman Pontiff, who is responsible in this case to ratify the decisions of the synod.

344
344. The synod of Bishops is directly subject to the authority of the Roman Pontiff, to whom corresponds:
1 convene the synod, as many times as it deems appropriate, and determine the place in which the meetings should be held;
2 ratify the election of those members who are to be chosen according to the rule of special law, and designate and appoint the other members;
3 determine in advance of the celebration of the synod, according to the specific law, the topics that must be discussed therein;
4 establish the agenda;
5 preside over the synod personally or through others;
6 close the synod, move it, suspend it and dissolve it.

345
345. The synod of Bishops can meet, either in a general assembly, in which issues that directly concern the good of the universal Church are discussed, and this assembly can be either ordinary or extraordinary, or in a special assembly, for problems that directly concern one or several specific regions.

346
346. § 1 . The synod of Bishops, when it meets in an ordinary general assembly, is made up of members who are, for the most part, Bishops, some elected for each assembly by the Episcopal Conferences according to the manner determined by the peculiar law of the synod; others are appointed by the same right; others, appointed directly by the Roman Pontiff; To them are added some members of clerical religious institutes chosen in accordance with the norm of the same peculiar right.
§ 2 . The synod of Bishops gathered in an extraordinary general assembly to deal with issues that require a quick resolution are made up of members who are, for the most part, Bishops designated by the peculiar law of the synod due to the office they perform; others, appointed directly by the Roman Pontiff; To them are added some members of clerical religious institutes, also elected according to the same peculiar right.
§ 3 . The synod of Bishops gathered in a special assembly is made up of members selected mainly from those regions for which it has been convened, according to the norm of the peculiar law by which the synod is governed.

347
347. § 1 . When the Roman Pontiff closes the assembly of the synod of Bishops, the function that had been entrusted to the Bishops and other members in it ceases.
§ 2 . The assembly of the synod is suspended ipso iure, when once convened or during its celebration, a vacancy occurs in the Apostolic See; and the function entrusted to its members is also suspended until the new Pontiff declares the assembly dissolved or decrees its continuation.

348
348. § 1 . The synod of Bishops has a permanent general secretariat, presided over by a General Secretary, appointed by the Roman Pontiff, who is assisted by the council of the secretariat, which consists of Bishops, some of whom are elected by the synod itself according to the norm of its peculiar right, and others are appointed by the Roman Pontiff, whose function ends at the beginning of a new general assembly.
§ 2 . For any type of assemblies of the synod of Bishops, one or more special secretaries designated by the Roman Pontiff are also appointed, who only remain in said office until the conclusion of the synod assembly.

349
349. The Cardinals of the Holy Roman Church constitute a peculiar College, which is responsible for providing for the election of the Roman Pontiff, according to the norm of peculiar law; Likewise, the Cardinals assist the Roman Pontiff both collegially, when they are summoned to discuss more important issues together, and personally, through the different offices they perform, helping the Pope above all in his daily government of the universal Church.

350
350. § 1 . The College of Cardinals is divided into three orders: the episcopal, to which belong the Cardinals to whom the Roman Pontiff assigns a suburban Church as a title, as well as the eastern Patriarchs assigned to the College of Cardinals, the presbyteral and the diaconal.
§ 2 . To each Cardinal of the presbyteral and diaconal order the Roman Pontiff assigns a title or diakonia of the City.
§ 3 . The Eastern Patriarchs who are part of the College of Cardinals have their patriarchal seat as their title.
§ 4 . The Cardinal Dean holds the diocese of Ostia as his title, as well as the other Church of which he was already head.
§ 5 . Respecting the priority of order and promotion, through an option made in the Consistory and approved by the Supreme Pontiff, the Cardinals of the presbyteral order can access another title and those of the diaconal order to another diakonia, and, after a full decade in the diaconal order, they can also access the presbyteral order.
§ 6 . The Cardinal of the diaconal order who accesses the priestly order by option, precedes the other Cardinal priests elevated to the Cardinalate after him.

351
351. § 1 . To be promoted to Cardinals, the Roman Pontiff freely chooses among those men who have received at least the priesthood and who stand out notably for their doctrine, customs, piety and prudence in the management of affairs; but those who are not yet Bishops must receive episcopal consecration.
§ 2 . The Cardinals are created by decree of the Roman Pontiff, which is made public in the presence of the College of Cardinals; From the moment of publication, they have the duties and rights determined by law.
§ 3 . However, he who has been promoted to the dignity of cardinal, with the Roman Pontiff announcing his creation but reserving his name in pectore, does not meanwhile have any of the duties or rights of Cardinals; He acquires those duties and rights when the Roman Pontiff makes his name public, but, for the purposes of precedence, the day on which his name was reserved in pectore is taken into account.

352
352. § 1 . The Dean presides over the College of Cardinals and, when he is prevented, the Subdean takes his place; However, neither the Dean nor the Sub-Dean have any governing power over the other Cardinals, but are considered first among their equals.
§ 2 . When the office of Dean becomes vacant, the Cardinals who have a suburban Church as their title, and only they, under the presidency of the Subdean, if present, or of the most senior of them, must elect one within the group to be Dean of the College; They will present their name to the Roman Pontiff, who is responsible for approving the chosen one.
§ 3 . In the same manner established in § 2, under the presidency of the Dean, the Subdean is elected; It is also up to the Roman Pontiff to approve the election of the Subdean.
§ 4 . The Dean and Subdean, if they do not have a domicile in the City, will acquire it there.

353
353. §1 . The Cardinals all collegially help the Supreme Pastor of the Church, especially in the Consistories, in which they meet by order of the Roman Pontiff and under his presidency; There are ordinary and extraordinary Consistories.
§ 2 . At least all the Cardinals present in the City are summoned to the ordinary Consistory to consult them on some serious questions, which nevertheless arise more commonly, or to carry out certain acts of maximum solemnity.
§ 3 . All the Cardinals are summoned to the Extraordinary Consistory, which is held when the special needs of the Church or the seriousness of the matters to be discussed dictate it.
§ 4 . Only the ordinary Consistory in which certain solemnities are celebrated can be public, that is, when, in addition to the Cardinals, Prelates, diplomatic representatives of civil societies and other guests are admitted to the event.

354
354. The Cardinal Fathers who are at the head of dicasteries or other permanent institutes of the Roman Curia and Vatican City are requested that, upon reaching seventy-five years of age, they present the resignation of their office to the Roman Pontiff, who will provide, taking into account all the circumstances.

355
355. § 1 . It is up to the Cardinal Dean to ordain as Bishop the person who has been elected Roman Pontiff, if the chosen one lacks that ordination; If the Dean is prevented, this right falls to the Subdean, and if he is prevented, to the most senior Cardinal in the episcopal order.
§ 2 . The Cardinal Protodeacon announces to the people the name of the new elected Supreme Pontiff; and likewise, on behalf of the Roman Pontiff, he imposes the pallium on the Metropolitans or delivers it to his procurators.

356
356. The Cardinals have the duty to cooperate diligently with the Roman Pontiff; Therefore, Cardinals who perform any office in the Curia and are not diocesan Bishops are obliged to reside in the City; The Cardinals to whom a diocese has been entrusted as diocesan Bishop must go to Rome as many times as they are summoned by the Roman Pontiff.

357
357. § 1 . The Cardinals to whom a suburban Church or a church in the City has been assigned as a title, once they have taken possession of it, must promote the good of those dioceses and churches with their advice and patronage, but they do not enjoy any power of regime over them, and they must in no way interfere with what concerns the administration of their property, discipline or service of the churches.
§ 2 . As regards their own person, the Cardinals who are outside Rome and their own diocese are exempt from the power of regime of the Bishop of the diocese in which they are located.

358
358. The Cardinal to whom the Roman Pontiff entrusts the task of representing him in some solemn celebration or meeting as Legatus a latere, that is, as if he were "himself", and also to the one to whom he entrusts the fulfillment of a certain pastoral task as his special envoy, is responsible only for what the Roman Pontiff himself has entrusted to him.

359
359. When the Apostolic See becomes vacant, the College of Cardinals only has in the Church that power attributed to it in the peculiar law.

360
360. The Roman Curia, through which the Roman Pontiff usually transacts the affairs of the universal Church, and which carries out its function in his name and by authority for the good and service of the Churches, consists of the State or Papal Secretariat, the Council for the public affairs of the Church, the Congregations, Courts, and other Institutions, whose constitution and competence are determined by peculiar law.

361
361. In this Code, under the name of the Apostolic See or Holy See is understood not only the Roman Pontiff, but also, unless by its very nature or by the context otherwise stated, the Secretariat of State, the Council for the public affairs of the Church, and other Institutions of the Roman Curia.

362
362. The Roman Pontiff has the native and independent right to appoint his own Legates and send them both to the particular Churches in the various nations or regions and at the same time to the States and public Authorities; He also has the right to transfer them and have them cease from office, observing the norms of international law regarding the sending and dismissal of Legates to the States.

363
363. § 1 . The Legates of the Roman Pontiff are entrusted with the task of representing him in a stable manner before the particular Churches or also before the States and public Authorities to which they are sent.
§ 2 . Those who are sent on a Pontifical Mission as Delegates or Observers before International Organizations or before Conferences and Meetings also represent the Apostolic See.

364
364. The main function of the Pontifical Legate is to ensure that the bonds of unity that exist between the Apostolic See and the particular Churches are increasingly firm and effective. It is therefore up to the pontifical Legate, within its circumscription:
1 inform the Apostolic See about the conditions in which the particular Churches find themselves and everything that affects the very life of the Church and the good of souls;
2 provide help and advice to the Bishops, without prejudice to the exercise of their legitimate power;
3 maintain frequent relations with the Episcopal Conference, providing it with all types of collaboration;
4 regarding the appointment of Bishops, transmit or propose to the Apostolic See the names of the candidates as well as instruct the information process of those who are to be promoted, according to the norms given by the Apostolic See;
5 strive to promote initiatives in favor of peace, progress and cooperation among peoples;
6 collaborate with the Bishops so that appropriate relations are fostered between the Catholic Church and other Churches or ecclesial communities, and even non-Christian religions;
7 defend together with the Bishops before the state authorities, everything that belongs to the mission of the Church and the Apostolic See;
8 also exercise the powers and fulfill the other mandates entrusted to him by the Apostolic See.

365
365. days, unless it is said that the month and year are to be taken according to the calendar.
§ 2 . If time is continuous, the month and year must always be computed according to the calendar.
365. § 1 . The pontifical Legate, who at the same time exercises his legation before the States according to the norms of international law, is responsible for the peculiar office of:
1 promote and foster relations between the Apostolic See and the State Authorities;
2 deal with those issues that refer to the relations between the Church and the State; and, in particular, work on the negotiation of concordats, and other conventions of this type, and ensure that they are put into practice.
§ 2 . When processing the matters dealt with in the § 1, as circumstances dictate, the Papal Legate will not fail to ask the opinion and advice of the Bishops of the ecclesiastical circumscription, and will inform them of the progress of the proceedings.

366
366. Taking into account the peculiar nature of the function of the Legacy:
1 the seat of the Pontifical Legation is exempt from the power of regime of the local Ordinary, unless it involves the celebration of marriages;
2 the Pontifical Legate, by previously communicating it to the Ordinaries of the places to the extent possible, can celebrate liturgical ceremonies, including pontifical ones, in all the churches of his legation.

367
