Cooperate with your own Bishop and with the diocesan presbytery, also striving to ensure that the faithful live parish communion and feel at the same time members of the diocese and the universal Church, and take part in initiatives that aim to foster this communion and consolidate it.
529. and 530; The power to assist at marriages, as well as all the powers of dispensation granted by law to the parish priest, are the responsibility of all of them, but must be exercised under the direction of the moderator.
§ 2 . All priests who belong to the group:
1 are obliged to comply with the residence law;
2 will determine by common agreement the order according to which one of them will celebrate the Mass for the people, in accordance with c. 534;
3 in legal affairs, only the moderator represents the parish or parishes entrusted to the group.

530
530. The following are functions that are especially entrusted to the parish priest:
1 the administration of baptism;
2 the administration of the sacrament of confirmation to those who are in danger of death, in accordance with the norm of c. 883, 3;
3 the administration of Viaticum and the anointing of the sick without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 1003 §§ 2 and 3; likewise, impart the apostolic blessing;
4 attendance at marriages and wedding blessing;
5 the celebration of funerals;
6 the blessing of the baptismal font at Easter time, the presidency of the processions outside the church and the solemn blessings outside the church;
7 the most solemn Eucharistic celebration on Sundays and holy days of obligation.

531
531. Even if another has performed a certain parish function, the oblations received from the faithful on that occasion will be included in the parish mass, unless, with respect to voluntary alms, the contrary intention of the person offering them is evident; It is up to the diocesan Bishop, after hearing the presbyteral council, to establish norms by which the destination of these oblations is provided and as well as the remuneration of the clerics who fulfill that function.

532
532. The parish priest represents the parish in all legal transactions, in accordance with the rule of law; He must ensure that the assets of the parish are administered in accordance with the norms of the ccs. 1281-1288.

533
533. § 1 . The priest has the obligation to reside in the parish house, near the church; However, when in particular cases there is a just cause, the local Ordinary may allow him to live in another place, especially in a common house of several priests, as long as adequate and effective provision is made for the fulfillment of parish tasks.
§ 2 . Unless there is a serious reason, the parish priest may be absent from the parish, as a vacation, for a maximum of one continuous or interrupted month; But this vacation time does not include the days during which the parish priest attends the spiritual retreat once a year; However, to be absent from the parish for more than a week, the parish priest has the obligation to notify the local Ordinary.
§ 3 . It is the responsibility of the diocesan Bishop to establish the norms according to which, during the absence of the parish priest, the care of the parish is provided by a priest endowed with the appropriate faculties.
533. § 3, and a parish Administrator has not been established, what is prescribed in c. 541 § 1 ; In this case, the vicar has all the obligations of the parish priest, except that of applying the Mass for the people.

534
534. § 1 . Once he has taken possession of the parish, the parish priest is obliged to perform the Mass for the people entrusted to him on all Sundays and holidays that are obligatory in his diocese; Anyone who is legitimately prevented from doing so will apply it on those same days through another person, or on other days personally.
§ 2 . On the days indicated in the § 1, the parish priest to whom the care of several parishes has been entrusted is obliged to hold a single Mass for the entire town entrusted to him.
§ 3 . The parish priest who has failed to fulfill the obligation referred to in §§ 1 and 2 must apply as soon as possible for the people as many Masses as he has omitted.

535
535. § 1 . In each parish, parish books must be kept, that is, of baptized people, of marriages and of the deceased, and those others prescribed by the Episcopal Conference or by the diocesan Bishop; Let the parish priest ensure that these books are accurately noted and diligently kept.
§ 2 . n In the book of the baptized, the affiliation to a sui iuris Church or the transition to another Church will also be noted, as well as confirmation and everything that refers to the canonical status of the faithful by reason of marriage, leaving aside what is prescribed in c. 1133, by reason of adoption, reception of sacred orders and perpetual profession issued in a religious institute; and these notations must always be recorded in the baptism certificate.
§ 3 . Each parish must have its own seal; The certificates that refer to the canonical status of the faithful, as well as other acts that may have legal value, must bear the signature of the parish priest or his delegate, and the parish seal.
§ 4 . In every parish there must be a shelf or archive, where the parish books are kept, together with the letters of the Bishops and other documents that must be preserved for reasons of necessity or usefulness; All of this must be reviewed by the diocesan Bishop or his delegate during visiting time or on another opportune occasion, and the parish priest must ensure that it does not end up in strange hands.
§ 5 . The oldest parish books must also be diligently preserved, according to the prescriptions of particular law.
535. § 2 .

536
536. § 1 . If it is appropriate, in the opinion of the diocesan Bishop, after hearing the presbyteral council, a pastoral council will be established in each parish, chaired by the parish priest and in which the faithful, together with those who participate through their office in the pastoral care of the parish, lend their collaboration for the promotion of pastoral activity.
§ 2 . The pastoral council has a purely advisory vote, and is governed by the norms established by the diocesan Bishop.

537
537. In every parish there must be a council of economic affairs that is governed, in addition to universal law, by the norms established by the diocesan Bishop, and in which the faithful, elected according to those norms, lend their help to the parish priest in the administration of the parish's assets, without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 532.

538
538. § 1 . The parish priest ceases in his office due to the removal or transfer made by the diocesan Bishop in accordance with the norm of law, by resignation presented by the parish priest with just cause, which, for its validity, must be accepted by the Bishop; also because time has elapsed, if, according to the prescriptions of the particular law referred to in c. 522, would have been established for a specific time.
§ 2 . The removal of a parish priest who is a member of a religious institute or incardinated in a society of apostolic life is governed by the norms of Can. 682, § 2.
§ 3 . The parish priest, once he reaches seventy-five years of age, is asked to present the resignation to the diocesan Bishop, who, weighing all the circumstances of the person and the place, will decide whether he should accept it or defer it; The diocesan Bishop must provide for the appropriate support and housing of those who resign, taking into account the norms established by the Episcopal Conference.

539
539. When a parish becomes vacant or the parish priest is unable to exercise the pastoral function in it, due to captivity, banishment or deportation, incapacity, illness or other cause, the diocesan Bishop must provide as soon as possible with a parish administrator, that is, a priest who replaces the parish priest, in accordance with the norm of c. 540.

540
540. § 1 . The parish administrator has the same duties and rights as the parish priest, unless the diocesan Bishop establishes otherwise.
§ 2 . It is not lawful for the parish administrator to do anything that could harm the rights of the parish priest or cause damage to parish property.
§ 3 . Once his task is completed, the parish administrator must report to the parish priest.

541
541. § 1 . When a parish becomes vacant, or the parish priest is prevented from exercising his pastoral function, until the parish administrator is established, the parish vicar provisionally assumes the regime of the parish; If there are several, the oldest by appointment, and, where there are no vicars, the parish priest determined by particular law.
§ 2 . Whoever takes charge of the regime of a parish according to the norm of § 1 must immediately inform the local Ordinary about the vacancy of the parish.

542
542. Priests who, according to the c

543
543. § 1 . If priests are jointly entrusted with the pastoral care of a parish or several parishes at the same time, each of them, according to the distribution established by themselves, has the obligation to carry out the duties and functions of the parish priest in question in the ccs. 528,

544
544. When one of the priests of the group mentioned in the c

545
545. § 1 . When necessary or appropriate for the proper performance of the pastoral care of a parish, in addition to the parish priest, there may be one or more parochial vicars who, as collaborators of the parish priest and participants in his care, united with the parish priest by the same will and commitment, work under his authority in the pastoral ministry.
§ 2 . A parochial vicar can be established either to help in the performance of the entire pastoral ministry in a parish or in a certain part of it or to a specific group, or to assign him to a specific ministry that must be carried out at the same time in several parishes.

546
546. For someone to be validly appointed parochial vicar, he or she must have received the sacred order of the priesthood.

547
547. The diocesan Bishop freely appoints the parochial vicar, after hearing, if he deems it appropriate, the parish priest or priests of the parishes for which it is established, and also the archpriest, without prejudice to the provisions of the c

548
548. § 1 . The obligations and rights of the parochial vicar are determined by the canons of this chapter, and also by the diocesan statutes and the document of the diocesan Bishop, and especially by the mandate of the parish priest.
§ 2 . If nothing else is established in the document of the diocesan Bishop, the parochial vicar, by reason of his office, has the obligation to assist the parish priest in the fulfillment of the entire parish ministry, except the application of the Mass by the people, and to replace the parish priest, if necessary, in accordance with the law.
§ 3 . The parochial vicar must regularly inform the parish priest about the pastoral initiatives planned or undertaken, so that the parish priest and the vicar or vicars can provide in unity of efforts for the pastoral care of the parish, for which they are jointly responsible.

549
549. In the absence of the parish priest, if the diocesan Bishop has not provided otherwise in accordance with the norm of the c

550
550. § 1 . The parochial vicar is obliged to live in the parish, or in one of them, if it has been established for several; However, for just cause, the local Ordinary may allow him to reside elsewhere, especially in the house where several priests live together, as long as the fulfillment of pastoral functions does not suffer any harm.
§ 2 . When possible, the local Ordinary should ensure that the parish priest and the vicars have a certain coexistence in the parish house.
§ 3 . As regards vacation time, the parochial vicar has the same right as the parish priest.

552. With just cause, the parochial vicar may be removed by the diocesan Bishop or by the diocesan Administrator, without prejudice to what is prescribed by the c

553
553. § 1 . The archpriest, also called foreign vicar, dean or otherwise, is a priest who is placed in charge of an archpriest.
§ 2 . Unless particular law establishes otherwise, the archpriest is appointed by the diocesan Bishop, after hearing, according to his prudent judgment, the priests who exercise the ministry in the archpriest in question.

554
554. § 1 . For the office of archpriest, which is not linked to that of parish priest of a specific parish, the Bishop must choose that priest whom he considers suitable according to the circumstances of place and time.
§ 2 . The archpriest must be appointed for a specific period of time, which will be specified in the particular law.
§ 3 . According to his prudent discretion, the diocesan Bishop may, with just cause, freely remove an archpriest from his office.

555
555. § 1 . In addition to the powers that are legitimately attributed to him by private law, the archpriest has the duty and right:
1 to promote and coordinate common pastoral activity in the archpriestship;
2 to see that the clergy of his district live in a manner in accordance with their state and diligently fulfill their duties;
3 to ensure that religious functions are celebrated according to the prescriptions of the sacred liturgy; the decorum and splendor of the churches and of the sacred objects and ornaments are diligently cared for, especially in the Eucharistic celebration and in the custody of the Blessed Sacrament; parish books are completed and stored appropriately; ecclesiastical assets are diligently administered; and the parish house is preserved with due diligence.
§ 2 . In the archpriestship entrusted to him, the archpriest:
1 ensure that clerics, according to the prescriptions of particular law and at the times determined by it, attend conferences, theological meetings or colloquiums, in accordance with the norm of c.

556
556. Church rectors are here understood as those priests who are entrusted, to celebrate services there, with the care of a church that is not parochial or capitular, nor is it attached to the house of a religious community or a society of apostolic life.

557
557. § 1 . The diocesan Bishop freely appoints the rector of a church, without prejudice to the right of election or presentation, when this legitimately concerns someone; In this case, it is up to the diocesan Bishop to confirm or establish the rector.
§ 2 . Although the church belongs to a clerical religious institute of pontifical right, it is up to the diocesan Bishop to confer the institution on the rector presented by the Superior.
§ 3 . The rector of the Seminary or of a college run by clergy is also rector of the church attached to the Seminary or college, unless the diocesan Bishop has established otherwise.

558
558. Without prejudice to the provisions of c. 262, the rector cannot carry out in the church entrusted to him the parish functions referred to in c. 530, nos. 1-6 without the consent or, if necessary, the delegation of the parish priest.

559
559. In the church entrusted to him, the rector can also celebrate solemn liturgical functions, keeping the legitimate laws of foundation safe, and provided that, in the opinion of the local Ordinary, they in no way cause harm to the parish ministry.

560
560. When it seems appropriate, the local Ordinary can order the rector to celebrate certain functions for the people, including parish ones, and also that the church be open to specific groups of faithful, so that they can celebrate liturgical functions there.

561
561. Without permission from the rector or other legitimate superior, no one is permitted to celebrate the Eucharist, administer sacraments or perform other sacred functions in the church; This license must be granted or denied in accordance with the rule of law.

562
562. Under the authority of the local Ordinary and respecting the legitimate statutes and acquired rights, the rector of the church has the duty to ensure that the sacred functions are celebrated there with dignity, in accordance with the liturgical norms and the prescriptions of the canons, that the charges are faithfully fulfilled, the goods are diligently administered, the conservation and decorum of the sacred objects and buildings are provided for, and nothing is done that would any way detracts from the sanctity of the place and the respect due to the house of God.

563
563. With just cause and according to his prudent discretion, the local Ordinary may remove the rector of a church from his office, even if he had been elected or presented by others, remaining firm what is prescribed by the c

564
564. The chaplain is a priest who is stably entrusted, at least in part, with the pastoral care of some community or group of faithful, so that he may exercise it in accordance with universal and particular law.

565
565. The chaplain is appointed by the local Ordinary, who is also responsible for instituting the one presented to him or confirming the one chosen, if nothing else is established by law or other special rights do not legitimately apply to anyone.

566
566. § 1 . The chaplain must be provided with all the powers required for good pastoral care. In addition to those granted by private right or special delegation, the chaplain, by reason of his position, has the power to hear the confessions of the faithful entrusted to his care, preach the word of God to them, administer Viaticum and the anointing of the sick, and also confer the sacrament of confirmation on those who are in danger of death.
§ 2 . In hospitals, prisons and maritime voyages, the chaplain also has the power, which he can only exercise in those places, to absolve from unreserved or undeclared latae sententiae censures, remaining firm, however, as prescribed in c. 976.

567
567. § 1 . The local Ordinary must not proceed with the appointment of chaplain of the house of a lay religious institute without consulting the Superior, who has the right, after hearing the community, to propose a priest.
§ 2 . It is the chaplain's responsibility to celebrate or organize liturgical functions, but he is not allowed to interfere in the internal regime of the institute.

568
568. To the extent possible, chaplains should be established for those who, due to their lifestyle, cannot enjoy ordinary parish care, such as emigrants, exiles, refugees, nomads, and sailors.

569
569. Military chaplains are governed by special laws.

570
570. If there is a non-parish church attached to the headquarters of a community or group, the rector of the same church shall be the chaplain, unless the care of the community or the church requires otherwise.

571
571. The chaplain must maintain due union with the parish priest in the performance of his pastoral function.

572
572. Regarding the removal of the chaplain, observe the provisions of c. 563.

573
573. § 1 . The life consecrated by the profession of the evangelical counsels is a stable way of living in which the faithful, following Christ more closely under the action of the Holy Spirit, dedicate themselves totally to God as to their supreme love, so that, given by a new and peculiar title to his glory, to the edification of the Church and the salvation of the world, they achieve the perfection of charity in the service of the Kingdom of God and, having become a clear sign in the Church, they announce heavenly glory.
§ 2 . This form of life is freely adopted in institutes of canonically consecrated life erected by the competent authority of the Church by those faithful who, through vows or other sacred bonds, according to the laws proper to the institutes, profess the evangelical counsels of chastity, poverty and obedience, and, by the charity to which these lead, are united in a special way to the Church and its mystery.

574
574. § 1 . The status of those who profess the evangelical counsels in these institutes belongs to the life and holiness of the Church, and therefore everyone in the Church must support and promote it.
§ 2 . God especially calls some faithful to this state, so that they may enjoy this peculiar gift in the life of the Church and favor its saving mission in accordance with the purpose and spirit of the institute.

575
575. The evangelical counsels, founded on the doctrine and example of Christ the Master, are a divine gift that the Church has received from the Lord and always preserves with His grace.

576
576. It is up to the competent authority of the Church to interpret the evangelical counsels, regulate their practice with laws and determine through canonical approval the stable ways of living them, as well as to ensure that the institutes grow and flourish according to the spirit of their founders and healthy traditions.

577
577. In the Church there are many institutes of consecrated life, which have received diverse gifts, according to the grace proper to each one: for they follow Christ more closely when he prays, when he announces the Kingdom of God, when he does good to men, and when he lives with them in the world, although always fulfilling the will of the Father.

578
578. Everyone must faithfully observe the mind and purposes of the founders, corroborated by the competent ecclesiastical authority, regarding the nature, purpose, spirit and character of each institute, as well as its healthy traditions, all of which constitutes the heritage of the institute.

579
579. n In their own territory, diocesan Bishops may erect institutes of consecrated life by formal decree, with prior written permission given by the Apostolic See.
579. In their own territory, diocesan Bishops may erect institutes of consecrated life by formal decree, provided that the Apostolic See has been previously consulted.
[New paragraph introduced by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf., Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Competentias quasdam decernere of February 11, 2022)]:

580
580. The aggregation of one institute of consecrated life to another is reserved to the competent authority of the institute that aggregates, without prejudice to the autonomy of the added institute.

581
581. It is the responsibility of the competent authority of an institute, in accordance with the constitutions, to divide it into circumscriptions, whatever their name, to erect new ones and to unite those already erected or to delimit them in another way.

582
582. Mergers and unions of institutes of consecrated life are reserved exclusively to the Apostolic See; and likewise confederations and federations are reserved for him.

583
583. In institutes of consecrated life, modifications that affect what is approved by it cannot be introduced without permission from the Apostolic See.

584
584. It is exclusively the responsibility of the Apostolic See to suppress an institute, and it is also reserved to it to decide on its temporal goods.

585
585. The suppression of parts of an institute corresponds to the competent authority thereof.

586
586. § 1 . Each of the institutes is recognized with a fair autonomy of life, especially in government, so that they have their own discipline within the Church, and can preserve in its entirety their own patrimony referred to in c. 578.
§ 2 . It is up to the local Ordinaries to preserve and defend this autonomy.

587
587. § 1 . To more faithfully defend the vocation and identity of each institute, the fundamental code or constitutions of each of them must contain, in addition to what is ordered to be observed in c. 578, the fundamental norms on the government of the institute and the discipline of its members, their incorporation and training, as well as the proper object of the sacred bonds.
§ 2 . This code is approved by the competent authority of the Church, and can only be modified with its consent.
§ 3 . In this code, the spiritual and legal elements must be appropriately harmonized; but norms should not be multiplied unnecessarily.
§ 4 . The other standards established by the competent authority of the institute will be conveniently included in other codes, standards that can be reviewed and adjusted when appropriate, according to the demands of the places and times.

588
588. § 1 . The state of consecrated life, by its nature, is neither clerical nor lay.
§ 2 . A clerical institute is called one that, taking into account the end or purpose desired by its founder or by legitimate tradition, is under the direction of clerics (Cf.

Rescriptum ex Audientia Ss.mi: Rescript of the Holy Father 
on the repeal of Can. 588 §2 CIC, May 18, 2022 – 
Congregation for Institutes of Consecrated Life and Societies 
of Apostolic Life), assumes the exercise of sacred order and is recognized as such by the authority of the Church.
§ 3 . A lay institute is called one that, recognized as such by the authority of the Church, by virtue of its nature, nature and purpose, has its own function determined by the founder or by legitimate tradition that does not include the exercise of sacred orders.

589
589. An institute of consecrated life is called pontifical right when it has been erected by the Apostolic See or approved by it by formal decree; and of diocesan right, when, having been erected by a diocesan Bishop, it has not received the decree of approval from the Apostolic See.

590
590. § 1 . The institutes of consecrated life, precisely because they are dedicated in a special way to the service of God and the entire Church, are subject for a peculiar reason to the supreme authority of the Church.
§ 2 . Each of its members is obliged to obey the Supreme Pontiff, as his supreme Superior, also by virtue of the sacred bond of obedience.

591
591. To better provide for the good of the institutes and the needs of the apostolate, the Supreme Pontiff, by virtue of his primacy over the entire Church and in attention to the common utility, can exempt the institutes of consecrated life from the regime of the local Ordinaries, and subject them exclusively to himself or to another ecclesiastical authority.

592
592. § 1 . To better promote the communion of the institutes with the Apostolic See, every Supreme Moderator must send to the latter in the manner and at the time determined by it a brief report on the situation and life of the institute.
§ 2 . The Moderators of each institute promote knowledge of the documents of the Holy See that affect the members who depend on them, and ensure their observance.

593
593. Without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 586, the institutes of pontifical right depend immediately and exclusively on the power of the Apostolic See, with regard to internal regime and discipline.

594
594. An institute of diocesan law, leaving c. 586, is under the special care of the diocesan Bishop.

595
595. § 1 . It is up to the Bishop of the main see to approve the constitutions and confirm the amendments that are legitimately introduced in them, except for those in which the Apostolic See has put its hands, as well as to deal with the most important matters that refer to the entire institute and are above the power of the internal authority, consulting however the other diocesan Bishops, if the institute has extended to different dioceses.
§ 2 . In particular cases, the diocesan Bishop can dispense from the constitutions.

596
596. § 1 . The Superiors and chapters of the institutes have the power over the members determined by universal law and the constitutions.
§ 2 . In the clerical religious institutes of pontifical right they also have ecclesiastical power of regime, both for the external and internal forum.
§ 3 . The prescriptions of ccs apply to the power discussed in § 1. 131, 133 and 137-144.

597
597. § 1 . Any Catholic of right intention who has the qualities required by universal law and by his own law, and is free of impediment, may be admitted to an institute of consecrated life.
§ 2 . No one can be admitted without adequate preparation.

598
598. § 1 . Taking into account its own character and purposes, each institute must determine in its constitutions the way of observing the evangelical counsels of chastity, poverty and obedience, in accordance with its way of life.
§ 2 . All members must not only faithfully and fully observe the evangelical counsels, but also organize their lives according to the law proper to the institute, and thus strive to achieve the perfection of their state.

599
599. The evangelical counsel of chastity assumed by the Kingdom of heaven, which is a sign of the future world and source of more abundant fertility in an undivided heart, carries with it the obligation to observe perfect continence in celibacy.

600
600. The evangelical counsel of poverty, in imitation of Christ, who, being rich, became indigent for us, in addition to a life poor in fact and in spirit, vigorously sober and detached from earthly riches, carries with it dependence and limitation in the use and disposition of goods, in accordance with the norm of the law proper to each institute.

601
601. The evangelical counsel of obedience, embraced with a spirit of faith and love in the following of Christ obedient to death, obliges one to submit one's will to the legitimate Superiors, who act as God, when they command something according to one's own constitutions.

602
602. The fraternal life, proper to each institute, by which all members are united in Christ as in a peculiar family, must be determined in such a way that it is mutual help for all in the fulfillment of their own personal vocation. Through fraternal communion, rooted and founded in charity, members are to be an example of universal reconciliation in Christ.

603
603. § 1 . In addition to the institutes of consecrated life, the Church recognizes the eremitic or anchoretic life, in which the faithful, with a stricter separation from the world, the silence of solitude, assiduous prayer and penance, dedicate their lives to the praise of God and the salvation of the world.
§ 2 . A hermit is recognized by law as devoted to God within the consecrated life, if he publicly professes the three evangelical counsels, corroborated by vote or other sacred bond, in the hands of the diocesan Bishop, and follows his own way of life under his direction.

604
604. § 1 . These forms of consecrated life are similar to the order of virgins, who, formulating the holy purpose of following Christ more closely, are consecrated to God by the diocesan Bishop according to the approved liturgical rite, celebrate mystical betrothal with Jesus Christ, Son of God, and dedicate themselves to the service of the Church.
§ 2 . Virgins can associate, to fulfill their purpose with greater fidelity and to carry out, through mutual aid, service to the Church consistent with their own state.
§ 3 . n The admission and erection of such associations at the diocesan level is the responsibility of the diocesan Bishop, within the scope of his territory; At the national level it is the responsibility of the Episcopal Conference, within its own territory.
604. § 3

605
605. The approval of new forms of consecrated life is reserved exclusively to the Apostolic See. However, diocesan Bishops must seek to discern the new gifts of consecrated life granted to the Church by the Holy Spirit and help those who promote them to formulate their purposes in the best possible way and protect them through appropriate statutes, applying above all the general norms contained in this part.

606
606. What is established about the institutes of consecrated life and their members is valid with equal rights for both sexes, unless otherwise stated by the context or by the very nature of the matter.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canon modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Authenticum charismatis, dated November 1, 2020)]:

607
607. § 1 . Religious life, as a total consecration of the person, manifests the admirable espousal established by God in the Church, a sign of the future life. In this way the religious completes the full donation of himself as a sacrifice offered to God, through which his entire existence becomes continuous worship of God in charity.
§ 2 . A religious institute is a society in which the members, according to their own law, issue perpetual public vows, or temporary vows that must however be renewed upon expiration of the term, and live a fraternal life in common.
§ 3 . The public testimony that religious people must give to Christ and the Church entails a separation from the world that is typical of the character and purpose of each institute.

608
608. The religious community must live in a legitimately constituted house, under the authority of the Superior designated in accordance with the rule of law; Each house must have at least one oratory, in which the Eucharist is celebrated and reserved, so that it is truly the center of the community.

609
609. § 1 . The houses of a religious institute are erected by the competent authority according to the constitutions, with the prior consent of the diocesan Bishop, given in writing.
§ 2 . To erect a monastery of nuns, a license from the Apostolic See is also required.

610
610. § 1 . The erection of the houses is done taking into account the usefulness of the Church and the institute, and ensuring everything that is necessary for the members to properly live the religious life, according to the proper purposes and the spirit of the institute.
§ 2 . No religious house shall be erected unless it is prudently anticipated that the needs of the members can be adequately served.

611
611. The consent of the diocesan Bishop to erect a house of a religious institute carries with it the right to:
1 live according to the character and purposes of the institute;
2 carry out the institute's own works in accordance with the rule of law, respecting the conditions set when granting consent;
3 clerical institutes have a church, without prejudice to what is prescribed in c.

612
612. The consent of the diocesan Bishop is required so that a religious house can be used for apostolic works other than those for which it was established; but not if it is a change that, leaving the founding laws safe, affects only the government and internal discipline.

613
613. § 1 . A religious house of regular canons or monks under the regime and care of its own Superior is autonomous, unless the constitutions determine otherwise.
§ 2 . The Superior of an autonomous house is by right a major Superior.

614
614. Monasteries of nuns associated with a men's institute maintain their own way of life and government in accordance with the constitutions. Reciprocal rights and obligations must be determined so that such association can serve spiritual good.

615
615. The autonomous monastery which, apart from its own Superior, does not have another major Superior, nor is it associated with an institute of religious such that its Superior has true power over said monastery, determined by the constitutions, is entrusted to the peculiar supervision of the diocesan Bishop, in accordance with the norm of law.
615. They must report to the local Ordinary once a year; The local Ordinary also has the right to know the financial situation of a religious house of diocesan law.

616
616. §1 . A legitimately erected religious house can be suppressed by the Superior General, in accordance with the norm of the constitutions and after consulting the diocesan Bishop. The institute's own law must provide for the assets of the suppressed house, leaving the will of the founders or donors and the legitimately acquired rights safe.
§ 2 . The suppression of a house that is the only one of an institute corresponds to the Holy See; to whom it is also reserved, where appropriate, to decide on the destination of the assets.
§ 3 . Unless the constitutions say otherwise, it is up to the General Chapter to suppress the autonomous house referred to in c. 613.
§ 4 . It is up to the Apostolic See to suppress an autonomous monastery of nuns, observing what the constitutions prescribe regarding property.

617
617. Superiors must fulfill their function and exercise their power in accordance with proper and universal law.

618
618. Superiors should exercise in a spirit of service the power they have received from God through the ministry of the Church. Therefore, showing themselves docile to the will of God in the fulfillment of their function, they govern their subjects as children of God, encouraging their voluntary obedience with respect for the human person, listen to them willingly and encourage their initiatives for the good of the institute and the Church, always remaining safe, however, in their authority to decide and command what must be done.

619
619. Superiors must diligently dedicate themselves to their office and, in union with the members entrusted to them, must seek to build a fraternal community in Christ, in which, above all, God is sought and loved. Therefore nourish the members with the frequent nourishment of the word of God and induce them to celebrate the sacred liturgy. They must give them an example in the exercise of virtues and in the observance of the laws and traditions of the institute itself; Help them appropriately with their personal needs, care for and visit the sick, correct the unruly, console the faint-hearted, and be patient with everyone.

620
620. Major Superiors are those who govern the entire institute, a province of it or another part equivalent to it, or an autonomous house, as well as its vicars. To these are added the Primate Abbot and the Superior of a monastic congregation, who, however, do not have all the power that universal law attributes to major Superiors.

621
621. The group of several houses canonically erected by the legitimate authority that forms an immediate part of an institute, under the same Superior, is called a province.

622
622. The Superior General has power, which he must exercise according to his own law, over all the provinces, houses and members of the institute; the other Superiors have it within the limits of their position.

623
623. For members to be validly appointed or elected to the position of Superior, it is required that a suitable time has elapsed since their perpetual or definitive profession, determined in the proper law or, in the case of major Superiors, by the constitutions.

624
624. § 3 .
§ 3 . He may be removed, for just cause in the judgment of the competent authority, to whom, according to the prescriptions of law, an office has been conferred for a time that is left to the prudent discretion of the authority.
§ 4 . For the removal decree to take effect, it must be communicated in writing.
624. § 1 . The Superiors must be appointed for a determined and convenient period of time, according to the nature and needs of the institute, unless the constitutions establish otherwise with regard to the Superior General or the Superiors of an autonomous house.
§ 2 . The proper law must provide through adequate regulations so that Superiors appointed for a specific period do not hold government positions for a long time and without interruption.
§ 3 . They may, however, be removed from the position they hold or transferred to another, for reasons determined in their own law.

625
625. § 1 . The Superior General of an institute must be appointed by canonical election, in accordance with the constitutions.
§ 2 . The Bishop of the main see presides over the election of the Superior of the autonomous monastery referred to in c. 615, and the Superior General of a diocesan law institute.
§ 3 . The other Superiors must be appointed in accordance with the constitutions, so that, if they are elected, they need confirmation from the competent major Superior; and if they are appointed by the Superior, an appropriate consultation precedes them.

626
626. Both the Superiors when conferring the offices and the members in the elections must observe the norms of universal law and their own, and must abstain from any abuse and respect of persons and, keeping in mind only God and the good of the institute, they will appoint or elect those who they consider in the Lord truly worthy and suitable. In elections, moreover, they will avoid capturing votes, directly or indirectly, both for themselves and for others.

627
627. § 1 . In accordance with the norm of the constitutions, the Superiors have their own council, whose collaboration they must use in the exercise of their office.
§ 2 . In addition to the cases prescribed in universal law, proper law will determine the occasions on which, to act validly, consent or advice is required, which must be requested in accordance with the rule of c. 127.

628
628. § 1 . The superiors designated for this function by the proper law of the institute will visit the houses and the members entrusted to their care at the established times, according to the prescriptions of the same proper law.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop has the right and duty to visit, also with regard to religious discipline:
1 the autonomous monasteries discussed in c. 615;
2 all the houses of an institute of diocesan law that are within its territory.
§ 3 . The members must deal with confidence with the visitor, and respond truthfully and with charity when he asks them something legitimately; and no one is permitted to hinder in any way the members from fulfilling this obligation or otherwise impede the purpose of the visit.

629
629. The Superiors reside in their own house, and do not leave it except in accordance with their own law.

630
630. § 1 . The Superiors grant the members due freedom with regard to the sacrament of penance and spiritual direction, without prejudice to the discipline of the institute.
§ 2 . In accordance with the norm of proper law, Superiors must be solicitous so that members have suitable confessors with whom they can confess frequently.
§ 3 . In monasteries of nuns, houses of formation and larger lay communities, there must be ordinary confessors approved by the local Ordinary, after an exchange of opinions with the community, but without imposing the obligation to go to them.
§ 4 . Superiors should not hear the confessions of their subjects, unless they spontaneously request it.
§ 5 . Members must go with confidence to their Superiors, to whom they can open their hearts freely and spontaneously. However, Superiors are prohibited from inducing members in any way to express their conscience to them.
630. § 4 .

631
631. § 1 . The general chapter, which holds supreme authority in the institute in accordance with the constitutions, must be constituted in such a way that, representing the entire institute, it is a true sign of its unity in charity. Above all, it is responsible for defending the heritage of the institute, which is dealt with in c. 578, and seek accommodation and renewal in accordance with it, elect the Superior General, deal with the most important matters, as well as dictate norms that are obligatory for all.
§ 2 . The composition and scope of power of the chapter must be determined in the constitutions; The proper law will also establish the way of proceeding in the celebration of the chapter, especially with respect to elections and the way of conducting affairs.
§ 3 . According to the norms determined in the proper law, not only the provinces and local communities, but also each member can freely send their wishes and suggestions to the general chapter.

632
632. The proper law must determine precisely which matters correspond to other chapters of the institute or to similar assemblies, with regard to their nature, authority, composition, procedure and time in which they must be held.

633
633. § 1 . The bodies of participation or consultation must faithfully fulfill the function assigned to them, in accordance with the norm of universal law and their own, and, each in its own way, they will be a channel for the request and participation of all members with regard to the good of the entire institute or the community.
§ 2 . When establishing and making use of these means of participation and consultation, prudent discretion must be observed, and their procedure must be in accordance with the character and purpose of the institute.

634
634. § 1 . The institutes, provinces and houses, as legal entities that are in their own right, have the capacity to acquire, possess, manage and dispose of temporal goods, unless this capacity is excluded or limited by the constitutions.
§ 2 . They must avoid, however, any appearance of luxury, immoderate profit and accumulation of goods.

635
635. § 1 . The temporal goods of religious institutes, being ecclesiastical goods, are governed by the prescriptions of Book V Of the Temporal Goods of the Church, unless otherwise expressly established.
§ 2 . However, each institute must establish appropriate rules on the use and administration of assets, with which it promotes, defends and manifests the poverty that is its own.

636
636. § 1 . In each institute, and also in each province governed by a major Superior, there is a treasurer distinct from the major Superior and designated in accordance with proper law, who manages the administration of goods under the direction of the respective Superior. Also in local communities, as far as possible, a treasurer separate from the local Superior should be established.
§ 2 . In the time and manner determined by law, treasurers and other administrators must render accounts of their administration to the competent authority.

637
637. The autonomous monasteries discussed in the c

638
638. § 1 . Within the limits of universal law, it is up to proper law to determine which acts go beyond the purpose and method of ordinary administration, as well as to establish the necessary requirements to validly carry out an act of extraordinary administration.
§ 2 . In addition to the Superiors, those charged with this function by right carry out expenses and legal acts of ordinary administration, within the limits of their position.
§ 3 . For the validity of a sale or any operation in which the financial condition of a legal entity may suffer damage, the license of the competent Superior given in writing, with the consent of its council, is required. But if it is an operation in which the sum determined by the Holy See for each region is exceeded, or of goods donated to the Church, due to a vote, or of objects of great price due to their artistic or historical value, the license of the Holy See itself is also required.
§ 4 . The autonomous monasteries, of which c. 615, and institutes of diocesan law also need to obtain the consent of the local Ordinary, granted in writing.
638. and 639, and also of proper law.
§ 2 . According to the rule of proper law, members are also capable of acquiring, possessing, managing and disposing of temporal goods, but everything they acquire by reason of it belongs to society.
638. § 3, when the value of the assets whose disposal is proposed is within the minimum and maximum limits set by each Episcopal Conference for its respective region, the competent authority is determined by the statutes themselves, if they are legal entities not subject to the diocesan Bishop; but, if they are subject to him, the diocesan Bishop is competent, with the consent of the council of economic affairs and the college of consultants as well as that of the interested parties. The diocesan Bishop also needs their consent to alienate property of the diocese.
§ 2 . If, however, it concerns property whose value is greater than the maximum amount, or votive offerings donated to the Church, or precious property for artistic or historical reasons, the license of the Holy See is also required for the validity of the alienation.
§ 3 . If the thing to be sold is divisible, when requesting the license for the sale, the parts previously sold must be specified; Otherwise, the license is invalid.
§ 4 . Those who must intervene in the disposal of assets with their advice or consent must not give them if they have not previously been exactly informed, both of the economic situation of the legal entity whose assets are to be sold, and of the sales carried out previously.

639
639. § 1 . If a legal entity incurs debts and obligations, even if it does so with permission from Superiors, it must be responsible for them.
§ 2 . If a member contracts them on his own property with the permission of the Superior, he is personally responsible; but if he carried out a business of the institute with a mandate from the Superior, the institute must respond.
§ 3 . If a religious contracts them without any license from the Superiors, he is personally responsible, and not the legal entity.
§ 4 . But let it be clear that action can always be brought against the person who increased his assets due to the contract made.
§ 5 . Religious Superiors should take care not to allow debts to be contracted, unless it is certain that with the usual income it will be possible to pay the interest and return the capital by legitimate amortization within a period of time that is not too long.

640
640. Taking into account the circumstances of the different places, the institutes should strive to bear witness, in some collective way, to charity and poverty and, to the extent possible, they should allocate some of their own assets to the needs of the Church and to the sustenance of the poor.

641
641. The right to admit candidates to the novitiate belongs to the major Superiors, in accordance with the norm of proper law.

642
642. With vigilant care, the Superiors will admit only those who, in addition to the necessary age, have health, adequate character and sufficient qualities of maturity to embrace the life of the institute; These qualities of health, character and maturity must be verified, if necessary, with the collaboration of experts, without prejudice to the provisions of c. 220.

643
643. § 1 . He is invalidly admitted to the novitiate:
1 who has not yet reached the age of seventeen;
2 a spouse, during the marriage;
3 who is at that moment bound by a sacred bond with some institute of consecrated life or is incorporated into a society of apostolic life, without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 684;
4 who enters the institute induced by violence, serious fear or fraud, or whoever the Superior admits induced in the same way;
5 who has hidden his incorporation into an institute of consecrated life or a society of apostolic life.
§ 2 . The proper law may also add other impediments to the validity of the admission, or impose other conditions.

644
644. Superiors do not admit secular clerics as novices without consulting their own Ordinary, nor those who have incurred debts that they cannot pay.

645
645. § 1 . Before admission to the novitiate, candidates must present a certificate of baptism and confirmation, as well as their free status.
§ 2 . If it is a question of receiving clerics or those who have been admitted to another institute of consecrated life, to a society of apostolic life or to a seminary, a report is also required, respectively, from the local Ordinary or from the Major Superior of the institute or society, or from the rector of the seminary.
§ 3 . The law itself may require other reports on the suitability of the candidates and their lack of impediments.
§ 4 . The Superiors can also request, if they deem necessary, other information, even in secret.

646
646. The purpose of the novitiate, with which life in an institute begins, is for the novices to better know the divine vocation, particularly that of the institute, to try its way of life, to conform their mind and heart to its spirit, and so that their intention and suitability can be verified.

647
647. § 1 . The erection, transfer and suppression of the novitiate house must be carried out by written decree of the Superior General of the institute, with the consent of its council.
§ 2 . For the novitiate to be valid, it must take place in a house duly designated for this purpose. In particular cases and as an exception, by grant of the Superior General with the consent of his council, a candidate can do the novitiate in another house of the institute, under the direction of an experienced religious, who acts as master of novices.
§ 3 . The Major Superior may allow the group of novices to live, during certain periods of time, in another house of the institute designated by him.
647. § 3 .
§ 2 . To complete the formation of novices, in addition to the time established in the § 1, the constitutions may prescribe one or more periods of exercise of the apostolate outside the novitiate community.
§ 3 . The novitiate should not last more than two years.
647. § 3 and 648 § 2, absence for more than three months, continuous or interrupted, from the novitiate house, makes it invalid. Absences exceeding fifteen days must be made up.
§ 2 . With the permission of the competent Major Superior, the first profession may be anticipated, but not by more than fifteen days.

648
648. § 1 . For its validity, the novitiate must last twelve months spent in the same community of the novitiate, subject to what is prescribed by the c
648. § 1, should be used properly in the task of formation, and therefore novices should not engage in studies or work that do not directly contribute to this formation.

650
650. § 1 . The purpose of the novitiate requires that novices be trained under the direction of a master, according to the training plan that must be determined by law.
§ 2 . The regime of novices is reserved exclusively to the master, under the authority of the major Superiors.

651
651. §1 . The master of novices must be a professed member of the institute with perpetual vows and legitimately appointed.
§ 2 . If necessary, the master can be given assistants who depend on him regarding the direction of the novitiate and the formation plan.
§ 3 . Carefully prepared members must be assigned to attend to the formation of novices, who, without being impeded by other work, can fulfill their functions fruitfully and stably.

652
652. § 1 . It is up to the master and his assistants to discern and verify the vocation of the novices, and to gradually form them so that they live the life of perfection typical of the institute.
§ 2 . The novices should be encouraged to live human and Christian virtues; They must be led on a path of greater perfection through prayer and self-denial; Instruct them in the contemplation of the mystery of salvation and in the reading and meditation of the sacred Scriptures; They will be prepared to celebrate the worship of God in the sacred liturgy; They will be formed to lead a life consecrated to God and men in Christ through evangelical counsels; They will be instructed about the character, spirit, purpose, discipline, history and life of the institute; and they will be imbued with love for the Church and its sacred Pastors.
§ 3 . The novices, aware of their own responsibility, must actively collaborate with the master, so that they respond faithfully to the grace of the divine vocation.
§ 4 . The members of the institute must collaborate on their part in the formation of the novices, with the example of their life and with prayer.
§ 5 . The novitiate time indicated in c.

653
653. § 1 . A novice can freely leave the institute; his competent authority may dismiss him.
§ 2 . At the end of the novitiate, the novice must be admitted to temporary profession, if he is considered suitable; Otherwise, he must be fired; If there remains any doubt about his suitability, the Major Superior may extend the probationary period in accordance with proper law, but not for more than six months.

654
654. Through religious profession, members embrace with a public vote, to observe them, the three evangelical councils, consecrate themselves to God for the ministry of the Church and join the institute with the rights and duties determined by law.

655
655. Temporary profession must be made for the time established in the proper law, not less than a three-year period nor more than a six-year period.
655. and 657.
§ 2 The Superior of an autonomous monastery has this same power, with the consent of his council.

656
656. For the validity of temporary profession it is required that:
1 the person who is going to do it has reached at least eighteen years of age;
2 has validly completed the novitiate;
3 has been freely admitted by the competent Superior with the vote of his council in accordance with the norm of law;
4 the profession is express and has been issued without violence, serious fear or fraud;
5 the profession is received by the legitimate Superior, personally or through another.

657
657. § 1 . Once the time for which the profession was made has expired, the religious who spontaneously requests it and is considered suitable must be admitted to the renewal of profession or to perpetual profession; Otherwise, you will leave the institute.
§ 2 . But if it seems appropriate, the competent Superior may extend the time of temporary profession in accordance with proper law, so that the time during which a member remains bound by temporary vows does not exceed nine years.
§ 3 . Perpetual profession may be anticipated with just cause, but not by more than one quarter.
657. § 3 .

658
658. In addition to the conditions indicated in c. 656, nos. 3, 4 and 5 and the others added by proper law, for the validity of perpetual profession, the following is required:
1 have reached at least twenty-one years of age;
2 prior temporary profession for at least three years, without prejudice to what is prescribed in c.

659
659. § 1 . After the first profession, the formation of all members must continue in each institute, so that they may live more fully the life of the institute and better fulfill their mission.
§ 2 . Therefore, proper law must determine the plan of this formation and its duration, taking into account the needs of the Church and the circumstances of men and times, as required by the purpose and character of the institute.
§ 3 . The training of members preparing to receive sacred orders is governed by the institute's own curriculum and by universal law.

660
660. § 1 . The formation must be systematic, adapted to the capacity of the members, spiritual and apostolic, doctrinal and at the same time practical, and also, if appropriate, with the obtaining of the relevant titles, both ecclesiastical and civil.
§ 2 . During the time dedicated to this training, members should not be entrusted with functions and jobs that impede it.

661
661. Religious will diligently continue their spiritual, doctrinal and practical formation throughout their lives; The Superiors must provide them with the means and time for this.

662
662. Religious must have as the supreme rule of life the following of Christ as proposed in the Gospel and expressed in the constitutions of their own institute.

663
663. § 1 . The contemplation of divine things and assiduous union with God in prayer must be the first and principal duty of all religious.
§ 2 . To the extent possible, members will participate each day in the Eucharistic Sacrifice, receive the most holy Body of Christ, and worship the Lord present in the Sacrament.
§ 3 . They will dedicate time to the reading of the sacred Scripture and to mental prayer, they will celebrate the liturgy of the hours with dignity according to the prescriptions of proper law, the obligation of which c.

664
664. Let religious insist on the conversion of their soul to God, examine their conscience daily and frequently approach the sacrament of penance.

665
665. § 1 . The religious must reside in their own religious house, living in common and not being absent from it without permission from the Superior. In the case of a prolonged absence, the Major Superior, with the consent of his council and with just cause, may allow a member to live outside a house of the institute, but not for more than one year, except for reasons of illness, studies or to exercise the apostolate on behalf of the institute.
§ 2 . Let the Superiors diligently look for the member of the institute who is illegitimately absent from the religious house with the intention of freeing himself from his obedience, and help him to return and persevere in his vocation.
665. § 2, for more than one semester; and other causes of similar gravity, which may be determined by the institute's own law.
§ 2 . For the expulsion of a member with temporary votes, other less serious causes determined in the proper law are also sufficient.

666
666. The necessary discretion must be observed in the use of social media, and what may be harmful to one's vocation or dangerous to the chastity of a consecrated person will be avoided.

667
667. § 1 . In all houses, the cloister will be observed, adapted to the character and mission of the institute, as determined by its own law, and a part of the religious house must always be reserved exclusively for the members.
§ 2 . A stricter discipline of the cloister must be observed in monasteries of contemplative life.
§ 3 . The monasteries of nuns with an entirely contemplative life must observe the papal cloister, that is, according to the norms given by the Apostolic See. The other monasteries of nuns will experience the closure adapted to their own character and determined in the constitutions.
§ 4 . The diocesan Bishop has the power to enter with just cause into the enclosure of the monasteries of nuns that are in his diocese, and to allow, with serious cause, and the consent of the Abbess, that other people be admitted to the enclosure, and that the nuns leave it outside of it for the time truly necessary.

668
668. § 1 . Before the first profession, the members will transfer the administration of their property to whomever they wish, and, if the constitutions do not prescribe otherwise, they will freely dispose of its use and usufruct. And before, at least, perpetual profession, they will make a will that is also valid according to civil law.
§ 2 . They need a license from the competent Superior, in accordance with the norm of proper law, to modify these provisions with just cause, and to carry out any act regarding temporal goods.
§ 3 . Everything that a religious earns through his own work or because of the institute, he acquires for the institute. What you receive in any way as a pension, subsidy or insurance, you acquire for the institute, unless your own law establishes otherwise.
§ 4 . Whoever, due to the nature of the institute, must completely renounce his assets, must make this renunciation before perpetual profession so that it has effects from the day of profession, and is also valid, if possible, in civil law. The same will be done by the professed perpetual vows who, in accordance with his own law, wishes to renounce all or part of his property, with the permission of the Superior General.
§ 5 . The professed person who, due to the nature of the institute, has renounced all his assets, loses the ability to acquire and possess, so his acts contrary to the vow of poverty are null and void. What you acquire after the resignation will belong to the institute in accordance with the rule of its own law.

669
669. § 1 . Religious must wear the habit of their institute, made in accordance with the norm of proper law, as a sign of their consecration and testimony of poverty.
§ 2 . The clerical religious of an institute who do not have their own habit will wear clerical dress, in accordance with the norm of c. 284.

670
670. The institute must provide its members with all the necessary means, according to the constitutions, to achieve the goal of their vocation.

672
672. Religious are bound by the prescriptions of cc. 277, 285, 286,

673
673. The apostolate of all religious consists primarily of the witness of their consecrated life, which they must promote with prayer and penance.

674
674. Institutes of exclusively contemplative life always have a relevant part in the mystical Body of Christ, since they offer God an excellent sacrifice of praise, they enrich the people of God with abundant fruits of holiness, they move them with their example and they increase them with their hidden apostolic fruitfulness. Therefore, even when the need for an apostolate of action is urgent, the members of these institutes cannot be called to collaborate in the different pastoral ministries.

675
675. § 1 . In institutes that are dedicated to works of apostolate, apostolic activity is part of their very nature. Therefore, the entire life of the members must be filled with the apostolic spirit and all apostolic action must be informed by the religious spirit.
§ 2 . Apostolic activity must always spring from intimate union with God, and at the same time confirm and promote it.
§ 3 . Apostolic activity, which is carried out in the name of the Church and by its mandate, must be exercised in communion with it.

676
676. Lay institutes, both for men and women, participate in the pastoral function of the Church and provide very diverse services to men through spiritual and corporal works of mercy; They must, therefore, remain faithfully in the grace of their vocation.

677
677. § 1 . The Superiors and other members faithfully maintain the mission and works of their institute; prudently accommodate them, attending to the needs of time and place, also using new and appropriate means.
§ 2 . If you have associations of faithful united with you, help them with special diligence so that they are informed by the genuine spirit of your family.

678
678. § 1 . Religious are subject to the power of the Bishops, whom they must follow with pious submission and respect, in what concerns the care of souls, the public exercise of divine worship and other works of apostolate.
§ 2 . In the exercise of the external apostolate, religious also depend on their own Superiors and must remain faithful to the discipline of their institute; The Bishops will not fail to urge this obligation when appropriate.
§ 3 . It is necessary that the diocesan Bishops and the religious Superiors exchange opinions when directing the apostolate works of the religious.
678. §§ 2 and 3.
§ 2 . In these cases, a written agreement must be agreed between the diocesan Bishop and the competent Superior of the institute, in which, among other things, what refers to the work to be carried out, the members who will dedicate themselves to it and the economic regime are expressly and well defined.

679
679. For a very serious reason, the diocesan Bishop may prohibit the residence in his own diocese of a member of a religious institute, if, having been warned, his Major Superior had neglected to take measures; However, the matter must be placed immediately in the hands of the Holy See.

680
680. Orderly cooperation is encouraged between the different institutes, as well as between them and the secular clergy, and also, under the direction of the diocesan Bishop, the coordination of all apostolic works and activities, respecting the character, purpose and founding laws of each institute.

681
681. § 1 . The activities entrusted to religious by the diocesan Bishop remain under the authority and direction of the latter, without prejudice to the right of the religious Superiors, in accordance with the norm of the c

682
682. § 1, the provision of the office of parish priest is the responsibility of the diocesan Bishop, through free collation, unless someone enjoys the right of presentation or election.
682. § 1 .
682. § 2 .
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio De concordia inter Codices, dated May 31, 2016)]:
§ 2 . The confirmation will also be noted in the baptized book, as well as what refers to the canonical status of the faithful by reason of marriage, leaving aside what is prescribed in c. 1133, due to adoption, reception of sacred orders, perpetual profession issued in a religious institute and change of rite; and these notes must always be recorded in the baptism certificate.
682. § 1 . When it comes to conferring an ecclesiastical office on a religious in a diocese, he is appointed by the diocesan Bishop, after presentation or at least assent of the competent Superior.
§ 2 . That religious may be removed from his office at the discretion of both the authority that has entrusted him with it, warning the religious Superior, and the Superior, warning the person who entrusted the office, without requiring the consent of the other.

683
683. § 1 . The diocesan Bishop may visit, personally or through another, during the pastoral visit and also in case of need, the churches and oratories to which the faithful have regular access, as well as the schools and other works of religion or charity, both spiritual and temporal, entrusted to religious; but not schools open exclusively to the institute's own students.
§ 2 . If you discover any abuse, after having notified the religious Superior without result, you can personally provide it with your own authority.

684
684. § 1 . A member of perpetual vows cannot transfer from his own to another religious institute, except by concession of the Superior Generals of both institutes, and with the consent of their respective councils.
§ 2 . That member, after a trial that must last at least three years, can be admitted to perpetual profession in the new institute. But, if he refuses to make that profession or is not admitted to it by the competent Superiors, he must return to the first institute, unless he has obtained an indult of secularization.
§ 3 . For a religious to be able to move from an autonomous monastery to another of the same institute, federation or confederation, the consent of the major Superiors of the two monasteries and that of the chapter of the monastery that hosts him is required and sufficient, without prejudice to the other requirements established by proper law; no new profession is required.
§ 4 . The proper law must determine the duration and manner of the test that must precede the profession of the member in the new institute.
§ 5 . For the transition to a secular institute or a society of apostolic life or from these to a religious institute, a license is required from the Holy See, whose mandates must be subject to.
684. §§ 1, 2, 4 and 685; But to move to a religious institute or a society of apostolic life, or from them to a secular institute, a license from the Holy See is required, whose mandates must be adhered to.

685
685. § 1 . Until his profession in the new institute, the rights and obligations that a member had in the first are suspended, the vows remaining in force; However, from the moment the test begins, you are obliged to observe the law of the new institute.
§ 2 . By profession in the new institute, the member joins it, and the previous votes, rights and obligations cease.

686
686. § 1 . n The Superior General, with the consent of his council, may grant an indult of exclaustration to a perpetually professed person for serious reasons, but not for more than five years, and having previously obtained, in the case of a cleric, the consent of the Ordinary of the place in which he must reside. Extending this pardon or granting it for more than a five-year period is reserved to the Holy See or, when it is an institute of diocesan law, to the diocesan Bishop.
§ 2 . It is the exclusive responsibility of the Apostolic See to grant indult of exclaustration to nuns.
§ 3 . At the request of the Superior General, with the consent of his council, for serious reasons and observing equity and charity, exclaustration may be imposed by the Holy See on a member of an institute of pontifical law, and by the diocesan Bishop on a member of an institute of diocesan law.
686. § 1 . The Superior General, with the consent of his council, may grant for grave reasons an indult of exclaustration to a perpetually professed person, but not for more than one triennium, and having previously obtained, in the case of a cleric, the consent of the Ordinary of the place in which he must reside. Extending this indult or granting it for more than a three-year period is reserved to the Holy See or, when it is an institute of diocesan law, to the diocesan Bishop.

687
687. The exclaustrated member is free from obligations that are not compatible with his new condition of life, and remains under the dependence and care of his Superiors and also of the local Ordinary, especially if he is a clergyman. You may wear the habit of the institute, unless otherwise stated in the pardon. However, it lacks a voice, both active and passive.

688
688. § 1 . Whoever wants to leave an institute after the time of profession has elapsed can leave it.
§ 2 . n Whoever, during temporary profession, requests, for serious reasons, to leave the institute, may obtain from the Superior General, with the consent of his council, the pardon to leave; for a sui iuris monastery, of which c. 615, that pardon, to be valid, must be confirmed by the Bishop of the house to which the member is assigned.
688. § 2 . Whoever, during temporary profession, asks, with serious cause, to leave the institute, may obtain from the Superior General, with the consent of his council, an indult to leave if it is an institute of pontifical right; in the institutes of diocesan law and in the monasteries mentioned in c. 615, that pardon, to be valid, must be confirmed by the Bishop of the house to which the member is assigned.

689
689. § 1 . Once the time of the temporary profession of a member has expired, there being just causes, the competent Major Superior, after hearing his advice, may exclude him from subsequent profession.
§ 2 . The physical or mental illness, even if it has been contracted after profession, if it is of such a nature that in the opinion of the experts makes the member referred to in the § 1 unfit to live in the institute, constitutes cause for not admitting him to renew the profession or to issue perpetual profession, unless the disease had been contracted due to the negligence of the institute or due to the work carried out in it.
§ 3 . But if the religious, during temporary vows, falls into amencia, even if he is not capable of making a new profession, he cannot nevertheless be dismissed from the institute.

690
690. § 1 . Whoever has legitimately left the institute after completing the novitiate or even after profession, may be readmitted by the Superior General with the consent of his council, without the obligation to repeat the novitiate; It is up to the same Superior to determine the appropriate test prior to temporary profession and the duration of vows before perpetual profession, in accordance with the norm of the ccs.

691
691. § 1 . A professed person of perpetual vows should not ask for a pardon for leaving the institute if it is not for very serious reasons considered in the presence of God; and he will submit his request to the Superior General of the institute, who, together with his own opinion and that of his council, will transmit it to the competent authority.
§ 2 . In the institutes of pontifical right, this indult is reserved to the Apostolic See; In those of diocesan law, it can also be granted by the Bishop of the diocese of that house to which the religious is assigned.

692
692. The pardon of departure legitimately granted and notified to the member, carries with it by right the dispensation from the vows and from all the obligations arising from the profession, unless, in the act of notification, the pardon was rejected by the member himself.

693
693. If the member is a clergyman, the indult is not granted before he has found a Bishop who will incardin him in his diocese or, at least, admit him on trial there. If he is admitted on probation, he remains, after five years, incardinated by law in the diocese, unless the Bishop rejects him.

697. In the cases discussed in c. 696, if the Major Superior, after hearing his advice, considers that the expulsion process should begin:

700. The decree of expulsion against a professed person is valid from the moment it is notified to the interested party. However, for the decree to be valid, it must indicate the right of the expelled person to appeal, within ten days of receiving the notification, to the competent authority. The appeal has suspensive effect.

[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Competentias quasdam decernere of February 11, 2022)]:
700. The decree of expulsion is not valid until it is confirmed by the Holy See, to which said decree must be sent along with the minutes; If it is an institute of diocesan law, confirmation corresponds to the Bishop of the diocese where the house to which the religious is attached is located. However, for the decree to be valid, it must indicate the right of the expelled person to appeal, within ten days of receiving the notification, to the competent authority. The appeal has suspensive effect.

701
701. By legitimate expulsion, the votes cease ipso facto, as well as the rights and obligations arising from the profession. But if the member is a clergyman, he cannot exercise sacred orders until he finds a Bishop who, after a suitable test, receives him into his diocese in accordance with the norm of c. 693, or at least allows him to exercise sacred orders.

702
702. § 1 . Those who legitimately leave a religious institute or have been expelled from it do not have the right to demand anything for any type of service provided therein.
§ 2 . However, the institute must observe equity and evangelical charity with the member who separates from it.

703
703. In case of serious external scandal or very serious damage that threatens the institute, a member can be immediately expelled from the religious house by the major Superior or, if there is danger in delay, by the local Superior with the consent of his council. If necessary, the Major Superior will ensure that the expulsion process is conducted in accordance with the rule of law, or will refer the matter to the Apostolic See.

704
704. In the report that must be sent to the Apostolic See in accordance with c. 592
§ 1, the members who have separated from the institute for any reason must be indicated.
