§ 2 . The priest not prevented by canon law celebrates the Eucharist lawfully, observing the prescriptions of the canons that follow.

901
901. The priest has the power to apply the Mass for anyone, both living and deceased.

902
902. Priests may concelebrate the Eucharist, unless the usefulness of the faithful requires or advises otherwise, remaining, however, the freedom of each one to celebrate the Eucharist individually, but not while it is being concelebrated in the same church or oratory.

903
903. Even if the rector of the church does not know him, the priest may be admitted to celebrate as long as he presents a letter of recommendation from his Ordinary or Superior, given at least within the year, or it can be prudently judged that nothing prevents him from celebrating.

904
904. Priests, always keeping in mind that in the mystery of the Eucharistic Sacrifice the work of redemption is continually carried out, must celebrate it frequently; Furthermore, the daily celebration is highly recommended, which, although it cannot be held with the assistance of the faithful, is an action of Christ and the Church, in the realization of which the priests fulfill their main ministry.

905
905. § 1 . Except for those cases in which, according to law, the Eucharist can be celebrated or concelebrated more than once on the same day, it is not lawful for the priest to celebrate more than once a day.
§ 2 . If there is a shortage of priests, the local Ordinary can grant that, with just cause, they celebrate twice a day, and even, when a pastoral need demands it, three times on Sundays and holy days of obligation.

906
906. Without just and reasonable cause, the priest may not celebrate the Eucharistic Sacrifice without the participation of at least some of the faithful.

907
907. In the Eucharistic celebration, deacons and lay people are not allowed to say prayers, especially the Eucharistic prayer, nor to perform those actions that are proper to the celebrating priest.

908
908. Catholic priests are prohibited from concelebrating the Eucharist with priests or ministers of Churches or ecclesial communities that are not in full communion with the Catholic Church.

909
909. The priest must not fail to prepare properly with prayer to celebrate the Eucharistic Sacrifice, and give thanks to God at the end.

910
910. § 1 . The bishop, the priest and the deacon are ordinary ministers of holy communion.
§ 2 . The extraordinary minister of holy communion is the acolyte, or another faithful person designated according to c.

911
911. § 1 . They have the obligation and right to bring the Holy Eucharist to the sick as Viaticum, the parish priest and the parochial vicars, the chaplains and the Superior of the community in the religious institutes or societies of clerical apostolic life with respect to all those who are in the house.
§ 2 . In case of necessity, or with at least presumed permission from the parish priest, chaplain or Superior, who must be informed afterwards, any priest or other minister of Holy Communion must do so.

912
912. Every baptized person who is not prohibited by law can and must be admitted to Holy Communion.

913
913. § 1 . In order for the most holy Eucharist to be administered to children, it is required that they have sufficient knowledge and have received careful preparation, so that they understand the mystery of Christ to the best of their ability, and can receive the Body of the Lord with faith and devotion.
§ 2 . The most holy Eucharist may, however, be administered to children who are in danger of death, if they are able to distinguish the Body of Christ from common food and to receive communion with reverence.

914
914. Parents in the first place, and those who take their place, as well as the parish priest, have the obligation to ensure that children who have reached the use of reason are adequately prepared and nourished as soon as possible, after sacramental confession, with this divine food; It is also the responsibility of the parish priest to ensure that children who have not yet reached the age of reason, or those whom he deems not sufficiently willing, do not receive the Holy Eucharist.

915
915. Those who have been excommunicated and those who are in question after the imposition or declaration of the penalty, and those who obstinately persist in a manifest grave sin, should not be admitted to Holy Communion.

916
916. Anyone who is aware of being in serious sin should not celebrate Mass or receive communion from the Body of the Lord without first attending sacramental confession, unless there is a serious reason and there is no opportunity to confess; and in this case, keep in mind that you are obliged to make an act of perfect contrition, which includes the intention of confessing as soon as possible.

917
917. Whoever has already received the most holy Eucharist, can receive it again on the same day only within the Eucharistic celebration in which he participates, remaining safe as prescribed by the c

918
918. It is strongly advised that the faithful receive Holy Communion within the Eucharistic celebration; However, when they request it for just cause, communion must be administered outside of Mass, observing the liturgical rites.

919
919. § 1 . Whoever is going to receive the Holy Eucharist must abstain from taking any food and drink at least one hour before Holy Communion, with the exception of water and medicines.
§ 2 . The priest who celebrates the Most Holy Eucharist two or three times on the same day can take something before the second or third Mass, even if it does not last for an hour.
§ 3 . Elderly or sick people, and also those who care for them, can receive the Holy Eucharist even if they have had something to drink in the immediately preceding hour.

920
920. § 1 . Every faithful, after the first communion, is obliged to take communion at least once a year.
§ 2 . This precept must be fulfilled during the Easter season, unless for just cause it is fulfilled at another time within the year.

921
921. § 2 .
921. § 1 . Viaticum must be administered to the faithful who, for any reason, are in danger of death.
§ 2 . Even if they had received Holy Communion on the same day, it is highly advisable that those who find themselves in danger of death take communion again.
§ 3 . While the danger of death lasts, it is advisable to administer communion several times, on different days.

922
922. Viaticum for the sick should not be delayed too long; Those who practice the healing of souls must diligently monitor so that the sick receive it while they still have full use of their faculties.

923
923. The faithful can participate in the Eucharistic Sacrifice and receive Holy Communion in any Catholic rite, except as prescribed in c. 844.

924
924. § 1 . The sacrosanct Eucharistic Sacrifice must be offered with bread and wine, to which a little water must be mixed.
§ 2 . The bread must be exclusively wheat and made recently, so that there is no danger of corruption.
§ 3 . The wine must be natural, from the fruit of the vine, and not corrupted.

925
925. Holy Communion be administered under the single species of bread or, in accordance with the liturgical laws, under both species; if necessary, also under the sole species of wine.

926
926. According to the ancient tradition of the Latin Church, the priest, wherever he celebrates Mass, must do so using unleavened bread.

927
927. It is strictly prohibited, even in cases of extreme necessity, to consecrate one matter without the other, or both outside the Eucharistic celebration.

928
928. The Eucharistic celebration must be held in the Latin language, or in another language as long as the liturgical texts have been legitimately approved.

929
929. When celebrating and administering the Eucharist, priests and deacons must wear the sacred vestments prescribed by the rubrics.

930
930. § 1 . The sick or elderly priest, if he is not able to stand, may celebrate the Eucharistic Sacrifice seated, always observing the liturgical laws, but not with the assistance of the people, unless with the permission of the local Ordinary.
§2 . The priest who is blind or suffers from another illness can celebrate the Eucharistic Sacrifice with any text of the Mass from among those approved, and with the assistance, if the case requires it, of another priest or deacon, or also of a suitably instructed layman, to help him.

931
931. The celebration and administration of the Eucharist can take place every day and at any time, with the exceptions established in the liturgical norms.

932
932. § 1 . The Eucharistic celebration must be held in a sacred place, unless, in a particular case, necessity demands otherwise; In this case, the celebration must take place in a decent place.
§ 2 . The Eucharistic Sacrifice must be celebrated on a dedicated or blessed altar; Outside the sacred place, an appropriate table can be used, always using the tablecloth and corporal.

933
933. For just cause, with express permission from the local Ordinary and avoiding scandal, a priest may celebrate the Eucharist in the temple of a Church or ecclesial community that is not in full communion with the Catholic Church.

934
934. § 1 . The Holy Eucharist:
1 must be reserved in the cathedral church or equivalent to it, in all parish churches and in the church or oratory attached to the house of a religious institute or society of apostolic life;
2 can be reserved in the Bishop's chapel and, with the permission of the local Ordinary, in other churches, oratories and chapels.
§ 2 . In the sacred places where the Holy Eucharist is reserved, there must always be someone in charge and, to the extent possible, a priest will celebrate Mass there at least twice a month.

935
935. No one is permitted to keep the Holy Eucharist in his home or take it with him on trips, unless a pastoral need demands it, and observing the prescriptions dictated by the diocesan Bishop.

936
936. In the house of a religious institute or in another pious house, the most holy Eucharist must be reserved only in the church or in the main oratory attached to the house; but the Ordinary, for just cause, may allow it to also be reserved in another oratory in the same house.

937
937. The church in which the Most Holy Eucharist is reserved must be open to the faithful, at least a few hours a day, unless a serious reason obstructs, so that they can pray before the Most Holy Sacrament.

938
938. § 1 . Usually, the Holy Eucharist will be reserved in a single tabernacle of the church or oratory.
§ 2 . The tabernacle in which the Holy Eucharist is reserved must be placed in a truly noble part of the church or oratory, prominently decorated, appropriately decorated, and appropriate for prayer.
§ 3 . The tabernacle in which the Holy Eucharist is usually reserved must be immovable, made of solid non-transparent material, and closed in such a way as to avoid as much as possible the danger of desecration.
§ 4 . For serious reasons, the Holy Eucharist can be reserved in another worthy and safer place, especially during the night.
§ 5 . Whoever takes care of the church or oratory must ensure that the key of the tabernacle in which the Holy Eucharist is reserved is guarded with the greatest diligence.

939
939. The consecrated Hosts must be kept in a ciborium or container, in quantities that correspond to the needs of the faithful, and renewed frequently, duly consuming the previous ones.

940
940. Before the tabernacle in which the most holy Eucharist is reserved, a special lamp must constantly shine, with which the presence of Christ is indicated and honored.

941
941. § 1 . In churches and oratories where it is permitted to have the Holy Eucharist reserved, the exposition can be made with both the ciborium and the monstrance, complying with the norms prescribed in the liturgical books.
§ 2 . During the celebration of Mass, there is no exposition of the Blessed Sacrament in the same church or oratory.

942
942. It is advisable that in these same churches and oratories a solemn exposition of the Blessed Sacrament be made every year, lasting an adequate time, even if it is not continuous, so that the local community meditates more deeply on the Eucharistic mystery and adores it; However, this exhibition will be held only if a proportionate attendance of faithful is anticipated, and observing the established norms.

943
943. The priest or deacon is the minister of the exposition of the Blessed Sacrament and of the Eucharistic blessing; in peculiar circumstances, only for exposition and reservation, but without blessing, are the acolyte, the extraordinary minister of holy communion or another commissioned by the Ordinary of the place, observing the prescriptions dictated by the diocesan Bishop.

944
944. § 1 . As a public testimony of veneration of the Most Holy Eucharist, where it can be done at the discretion of the diocesan Bishop, there is a procession through the streets, especially on the solemnity of the Body and Blood of Christ.
§ 2 . It is up to the diocesan Bishop to give regulations on the processions, through which participation in them and their decorum are provided.

945
945. §1 . According to the approved usage of the Church, any priest who celebrates or concelebrates the Mass may receive an offering, to be applied toward a specific intention.
§ 2 . Priests are strongly encouraged to celebrate Mass for the intentions of the faithful, especially those in need, even if they do not receive any offerings.

946
946. The faithful who offer an offering so that the Mass can be applied for their intention, contribute to the good of the Church, and with it they participate in its concern to support its ministers and activities.

947
947. In matters of Mass offerings, avoid even the smallest appearance of negotiation or commerce.

948
948. A different Mass must be applied for each intention for which it has been offered and an offering has been accepted, even if it is small.

949
949. He who must celebrate and apply the Mass for the intention of those who have given offerings, remains obliged to do so, even if the offering received had perished through no fault of his own.

950
950. If an amount of money is offered for the application of Masses, without indicating how many should be celebrated, their number will be determined based on the offering set for the place where the offerer resides, unless it must be legitimately presumed that his intention was different.

951
951. § 1 . The priest who celebrates more than one Mass on the same day may apply each of them to the intention for which the offering was given; However, with the exception of Christmas Day, he should only keep the offering of one Mass, and allocate the others to the purposes determined by the Ordinary, although he may also receive some remuneration for an extrinsic title.
§ 2 . The priest who concelebrates a second Mass on the same day cannot receive an offering for it under any title.

952
952. § 1 . It is up to the provincial council or the meeting of Bishops of the province to establish by decree for the entire province the offering that must be offered for the celebration and application of the Mass, and it is not lawful for the priest to ask for a greater amount; Yes, it is licit for him to receive, through the application of a Mass, a greater offering than that established, if it is spontaneously offered, and also a lesser one.
§ 2 . In the absence of such a decree, the custom in force in the diocese will be observed.
§ 3 . Members of any religious institutes must also abide by the same decree or custom of the place mentioned in §§ 1 and 2.

953
953. It is licit for no one to accept so many offerings to celebrate Masses personally that he cannot satisfy them within a period of one year.

954
954. If in some churches or oratories orders are received for Masses in excess of those that can be said there, these may be celebrated elsewhere, unless the offerors have expressly expressed their contrary will.

955
955. § 1 . Whoever wishes to entrust to others the celebration of Masses that are to be applied, must transmit them as soon as possible to priests of his preference as long as he knows that they are trustworthy; He must deliver the entire offering received, unless he is certain that what exceeds what is established in the diocese was given in consideration to his person; and he continues to have the obligation to ensure that the Masses are celebrated, until he is satisfied with both the acceptance of the obligation and the reception of the offering.
§ 2 . The time within which Masses must be celebrated begins from the day on which the priest who is going to celebrate them received the order, unless otherwise stated.
§ 3 . Those who transmit to others Masses that are to be celebrated, write down without delay in a book, both the Masses they received and those they have commissioned from others, also noting their offerings.
§ 4 . Every priest must carefully write down the Mass orders received and those already fulfilled.

956
956. Each and every one of the administrators of pious causes, or those who in any way are obliged to ensure that Masses are celebrated, both clerics and laymen, will deliver to their Ordinaries the burdens of Masses that have not been fulfilled within the year, according to the manner determined by them.

957
957. The obligation and right to ensure that the Masses are fulfilled corresponds to the local Ordinary for the churches of the secular clergy; and to their Superiors, for the churches of religious institutes or societies of apostolic life.

958
958. § 1 . The parish priest and rector of a church or other pious place, where offerings are usually received for the celebration of Masses, must have a special book in which they will diligently take note of the number of Masses to be celebrated, the intention, the offering given and the fulfillment of the order.
§ 2 . The Ordinary has the obligation to review these books each year, personally or through others.
958. § 1, the parish priest or rector must carry and keep another in which each of the obligations, their fulfillment and alms are noted.

959
959. In the sacrament of penance, the faithful who confess their sins to a legitimate minister, repenting of them and with the purpose of amendment, obtain from God forgiveness of the sins committed after baptism, through the absolution given by the same minister, and, at the same time, they are reconciled with the Church, which they hurt by sinning.

960
960. Individual and complete confession and absolution constitute the only ordinary way with which a believer aware that he is in serious sin is reconciled with God and with the Church; Only the physical or moral impossibility excuses that confession, in which case reconciliation can also be had by other means.

961
961. § 1 . Absolution cannot be given to several penitents at the same time without prior individual and general confession unless:
1 a danger of death threatens, and the priest or priests do not have time to hear the confession of each penitent;
2 there is a serious need, that is, when, taking into account the number of penitents, there are not enough confessors to properly hear each one's confession within a reasonable time, so that the penitents, through no fault of their own, would be deprived for a considerable time of sacramental grace or holy communion; But it is not considered a sufficient necessity when confessors cannot be available simply because of a large gathering of penitents, as may happen at a great festival or pilgrimage.
§ 2 . It is up to the diocesan Bishop to judge whether the required conditions are met in accordance with § 1, 2, which, taking into account the criteria agreed with the other members of the Episcopal Conference, can determine the cases in which this need is verified.

962
962. § 1 . For a faithful person to validly receive the sacramental absolution given to several at the same time, it is required not only that he be duly prepared, but that he also proposes to make, in due time, an individual confession of all the serious sins that in the present circumstances he has not been able to confess in that way.
§ 2 . To the extent possible, also upon receipt of the general absolution, the faithful be instructed about the requirements expressed in the § 1, and before the general absolution, even in danger of death if there is time, each one is urged to make an act of contrition.

963
963. The obligation referred to in c. 989, he who is forgiven for serious sins with a general absolution, must approach individual confession as soon as possible, as soon as he has the opportunity, before receiving another general absolution, if no just cause is interposed.

964
964. § 1 . The proper place to hear confessions is a church or oratory.
§ 2 . As regards the venue for hearing confessions, the Episcopal Conference shall establish norms, ensuring in all cases that there are always in obvious places confessionals provided with grilles between the penitent and the confessor that can be freely used by the faithful who wish to do so.
§ 3 . Confessions should not be heard outside the confessional, unless it is for just cause.

965
965. Only the priest is minister of the sacrament of penance.

966
966. § 1 . To validly absolve sins, it is required that the minister, in addition to the power of order, has the power to exercise it over the faithful to whom he gives absolution.
§ 2 . The priest can receive this faculty both ipso jure and by grant from the competent authority, in accordance with c. 969.

967
967. § 1 . In addition to the Roman Pontiff, the Cardinals have ipso iure the power to hear confessions of the faithful throughout the world; and likewise the Bishops, who also exercise it licitly anywhere, unless the diocesan Bishop objects in a specific case.
§ 2 . Those who have the habitual power to hear confessions both by reason of their office and by concession of the Ordinary of the place of incardination or of the place in which they have their domicile, may exercise the same power anywhere, unless the Ordinary of some place objects in a specific case, remaining in place what is prescribed in c. 974 §§ 2 and 3.
§3 . Those who are endowed with the faculty of hearing confessions, by virtue of their office or by concession of the competent Superior in accordance with cc.
967. §2, the priest is deprived of it everywhere; If it is revoked by another local Ordinary, it is deprived of it only in the territory of the one who revokes it.
§ 3 . Any local Ordinary who revokes the faculty of hearing confessions from a priest must communicate this to the priest's own Ordinary by reason of incardination or, if it is a member of a religious institute, to his competent Superior.
§ 4 . If the faculty of hearing confessions is revoked by the proper Major Superior, the priest is deprived of it everywhere, with respect to the members of the institute; but if it is revoked by another competent Superior, he loses it only towards the subjects within the scope of his power.
967. § 2, ceases not only due to revocation, but also due to loss of office, excardination or change of address.

968
968. § 2 and 969 § 2, have ipso iure that power in any place, to confess the members and those who live day and night in the house of their institute or society; and they use this faculty also licitly, unless a major Superior objects in a specific case regarding his own subjects.
968. § 1 . Within the scope of their jurisdiction, by reason of their office, the local Ordinary, the penitentiary canon and also the parish priest and those who occupy his place have the power to confess.
§ 2 . By virtue of the office, those Superiors of a religious institute or of a society of clerical apostolic life of pontifical right who, according to the constitutions, are endowed with executive power of regime, remain the established in c.
968. § 2 is competent to grant any priests the power to hear confessions from their subjects and from those others who live day and night in the house.

969
969. § 1 . Only the local Ordinary is competent to grant the faculty of hearing confessions of any faithful to any priest; but priests who are members of a religious institute should not use it without permission, at least presumed, from their Superior.
§ 2 . The Superior of a religious institute or of a society of apostolic life referred to in c.

970
970. The power to hear confessions should only be granted to priests who have been deemed fit by an examination, or whose suitability is otherwise established.

971
971. The local Ordinary must not grant a priest the power to habitually hear confessions, even if he has a domicile or quasi-domicile within the scope of his jurisdiction, without first having heard the Ordinary of the priest, to the extent that this is possible.

972
972. The competent authority, indicated in c. 969, can grant the power to hear confessions for both an indefinite and a fixed period of time.

973
973. The power to habitually hear confessions must be granted in writing.

974
974. § 1 . The local Ordinary and the competent Superior must not revoke without serious cause the faculty to habitually hear confessions.
§ 2 . If the power to hear confessions is revoked by the Ordinary of the place that granted it, which is dealt with in c.

975
975. The power referred to in the c

976
976. Every priest, even if he lacks the power to confess, validly and licitly absolves any penitent who is in danger of death from any censures and sins, even if an approved priest is present.

977
977. Outside of danger of death, the absolution of an accomplice in a sin against the sixth commandment of the Decalogue is invalid.

978
978. § 1 . When hearing confessions, let the priest keep in mind that he acts as judge and doctor, and that he has been constituted by God as a minister of justice and at the same time of divine mercy, so that he may provide for the honor of God and the health of souls.
§ 2 . When administering the sacrament, the confessor, as a minister of the Church, must faithfully adhere to the doctrine of the Magisterium and the norms dictated by the competent authority.

979
979. When interrogating, the priest must behave with prudence and discretion, taking into account the condition and age of the penitent; and must refrain from asking about the name of the accomplice.

980
980. Absolution should not be denied or delayed if the confessor does not doubt the good disposition of the penitent and he asks to be absolved.

981
981. Depending on the severity and number of the sins, but taking into account the condition of the penitent, the confessor must impose a healthy and convenient satisfaction, which the penitent is obliged to fulfill personally.

982
982. Whoever is accused of having falsely denounced to the ecclesiastical authority an innocent confessor of the crime of soliciting a sin against the sixth commandment of the Decalogue, should not be acquitted until he formally retracts the false complaint, and is willing to repair the damage that may have been caused.

983
983. § 1 . The sacramental seal is inviolable; Therefore, the confessor is strictly prohibited from discovering the penitent, by word or in any other way, and for no reason.
§ 2 . The interpreter, if there is one, and all those who, in any way, would have become aware of the sins through confession, are also obliged to keep secret.
983. § 2 .

984
984. § 1 . The confessor is strictly prohibited from making use, to the detriment of the penitent, of the knowledge acquired in confession, even if there is no danger of revelation.
§ 2 . He who is constituted in authority cannot in any way make use, for external government, of the knowledge of sins that he has acquired by confession at any time.

985
985. The novice master and his assistant and the rector of the seminary or other educational institution must not hear sacramental confessions from their students residing in the same house, unless the students spontaneously request it in particular cases.

986
986. § 1 . All those who, by their office, are entrusted with the care of souls, are obliged to ensure that the confession of the faithful entrusted to them is heard and who reasonably request it; and that they be given the opportunity to approach individual confession, on certain days and times that are affordable to them.
§ 2 . If the need arises, every confessor is obliged to hear the confessions of the faithful; and, in danger of death, any priest.

987
987. To receive the healthy remedy of the sacrament of penance, the faithful must be so disposed that, rejecting the sins committed and having the intention of amendment, they convert to God.

988
988. § 1 . The faithful are obliged to confess according to their kind and number all serious sins committed after baptism and not yet directly forgiven by the power of the keys of the Church nor accused in individual confession, of which they are aware after a diligent examination.
§ 2 . The faithful are recommended to also confess venial sins.

989
989. Every believer who has reached the use of reason is obliged to faithfully confess his serious sins at least once a year.

990
990. Confession through an interpreter is not prohibited to anyone, as long as abuses and scandals are avoided, without prejudice to what is prescribed by the c

991
991. Every believer has the right to confess with the legitimately approved confessor of his choice, even if it is of another rite.

992
992. Indulgence is the remission before God of the temporal punishment for sins, already forgiven in terms of guilt, that a willing believer and fulfilling certain conditions, obtains through the mediation of the Church, which, as administrator of redemption, distributes and applies with authority the treasure of the satisfactions of Christ and the Saints.

993
993. The indulgence is partial or plenary, depending on whether it partially or totally releases the temporal punishment due for sins.

994
994. Every faithful can profit for himself or apply for the deceased, as a suffrage, both partial and plenary indulgences.

995
995. § 1 . In addition to the supreme authority of the Church, only those to whom the law recognizes this power, or to whom the Roman Pontiff has granted it, can only grant indulgences.
§ 2 . No authority lower than the Roman Pontiff can grant others the power to grant indulgences, unless it has been expressly granted by the Apostolic See.

996
996. § 1 . To be able to gain indulgences it is necessary to be baptized, not excommunicated, and to be in a state of grace at least at the end of the prescribed works.
§ 2 . However, for the capable subject to obtain them, he must have at least a general intention to obtain them, and complete the prescribed works within the determined time and in the proper manner, according to the tenor of the concession.

997
997. With regard to the granting and use of indulgences, the remaining prescriptions contained in the peculiar laws of the Church must also be observed.

998
998. The anointing of the sick, with which the Church commends the seriously ill faithful to the suffering and glorified Lord, so that he may relieve and save them, is administered by anointing them with oil and saying the words prescribed in the liturgical books.

999
999. In addition to the Bishop, they can bless the oil used in the anointing of the sick:
1 who by right are equal to the diocesan Bishop;
2 in case of need, any priest, but within the celebration of the sacrament.

1000
1000. § 1 . The anointings must be done carefully, with the words order and manner prescribed in the liturgical books; However, in case of need, a single anointing on the forehead, or also on another part of the body, is enough, saying the complete formula.
§ 2 . The minister must do the anointings with his hand, unless a serious reason advises the use of an instrument.

1001
1001. The shepherds of souls and the relatives of the sick must ensure that he is comforted in an opportune time with this sacrament.

1002
1002. The common celebration of the anointing of the sick for several sick people at the same time, who are duly prepared and correctly disposed, can be done in accordance with the prescriptions of the diocesan Bishop.

1003
1003. § 1 . Every priest, and only he, validly administers the anointing of the sick.
§ 2 . All priests with the care of souls have the obligation and the right to administer the anointing of the sick to the faithful entrusted to their pastoral task; but, for a reasonable cause, any other priest may administer this sacrament, with at least the presumed consent of the priest referred to above.
§ 3 . Every priest is permitted to carry the blessed oil with him, so that, in case of need, he can administer the sacrament of anointing of the sick.

1004
1004. § 1 . The anointing of the sick can be administered to the faithful who, having reached the use of reason, begins to be in danger due to illness or old age.
§ 2 . This sacrament may be repeated if the sick person, once restored to health, contracts a serious illness again, or if, during the same illness, the danger becomes more serious.

1005
1005. In doubt as to whether the sick person has reached the use of reason, suffers from a serious illness or has already died, administer this sacrament to him.

1006
1006. This sacrament must be administered to the sick who, when they were in possession of their faculties, have requested it at least implicitly.

1007
1007. Do not give the anointing of the sick to those who stubbornly persist in manifest grave sin.

1008
1008. n Through the sacrament of Orders, by divine institution, some of the faithful are constituted sacred ministers, being marked with an indelible character, and thus they are consecrated and destined to serve, according to the degree of each one, with a new and peculiar title, the people of God.
1008. Through the sacrament of Orders, by divine institution, some of the faithful are constituted sacred ministers, being marked with an indelible character, and thus they are consecrated and destined to shepherd the People of God according to the degree of each one, performing in the person of Christ the Head the functions of teaching, sanctifying and ruling.

1009
1009. § 1 . The orders are the episcopate, the presbyterate and the diaconate.
§ 2 . They are conferred by the laying on of hands and the consecratory prayer that the liturgical books prescribe for each degree.
§ 3 . n Those who have been constituted in the order of the episcopate or the presbytery receive the mission and the power to act in the person of Christ the Head; Deacons, on the other hand, are enabled to serve the people of God in the diakonia of liturgy, word and charity.
(n: Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canon modified by His Holiness Pope Benedict XVI (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Omnium inmentem of October 26, 2009)]:

1010
1010. Ordination must be celebrated within a solemn Mass on a Sunday or on a holy day of obligation, although for pastoral reasons it can also be done on other days, without excluding holidays.

1011
1011. § 1 . Ordination is generally to be celebrated in the cathedral; however, for pastoral reasons, it may take place in another church or oratory.
§ 2 . Clergy and other faithful should be invited to the ordination, so that they attend the celebration in the greatest number possible.

1012
1012. The consecrated Bishop is the minister of sacred ordination.

1013
1013. It is not licit for any Bishop to confer episcopal ordination without prior confirmation of the pontifical mandate.

1014
1014. Unless the Apostolic See has dispensed with it, in the episcopal consecration the main consecrating Bishop associates with him at least two other consecrating Bishops; and it is very convenient that, together with them, all the Bishops present consecrate the chosen one.

1015
1015. § 1 . Each one is ordained to the priesthood or diaconate by the Bishop himself or with legitimate resignations from the same.
§ 2 . The own Bishop, if he is not prevented for just cause, will personally ordain his subjects; but he cannot licitly ordain, without an apostolic indult, a subject of the Eastern rite.
§ 3 . Whoever can give resignations for orders can also personally confer those same orders, if he has an episcopal character.

1016
1016. With regard to the ordination of deacons of those who wish to join the secular clergy, the proper Bishop is that of the diocese in which the ordaining person resides, or that of the diocese to which he has decided to dedicate himself; for the presbyteral ordination of secular clerics, it is the Bishop of the diocese to which the ordinand is incardinated by the diaconate.
1016. and 1019, with the established liturgical rite, upon written and signed request in your own handwriting, which must also be accepted in writing by the same authority.
§ 2 . This admission rite is not obligatory for those who are incorporated by vows into a clerical institute.

1017
1017. The Bishop cannot confer orders outside the scope of his jurisdiction, except with the permission of the diocesan Bishop.

1018
1018. § 1 . You can give the resignations for seculars:
1 the proper Bishop, of which c. 1016;
2 the Apostolic Administrator and, with the consent of the College of Consultants, the Diocesan Administrator; with the consent of the council mentioned in c.

1019
1019. § 1 . It is up to the major Superior of a clerical religious institute of pontifical right or of a clerical society of apostolic life of pontifical right, for its subjects assigned according to the constitutions in a perpetual or definitive manner to the institute or society, to give resignations for the diaconate and for the priesthood.
§ 2 . The ordination of all other members of any institute or society is governed by the law of secular clerics, and any indult granted to Superiors is revoked.

1020
1020. Resignations should not be granted before having obtained all the testimonies and documents required by law, in accordance with the cc

1021
1021. Remittances may be sent to any Bishop in communion with the Apostolic See, except, except for an apostolic indult, Bishops of a rite other than that of the ordinand.

1022
1022. Once the legitimate resignations have been received, the Bishop should not ordain until his authenticity is confirmed without a doubt.

1023
1023. Resignations may be subject to limitations or be revoked by the person who issued them or by his successor; However, once given, they do not lose their effectiveness due to the loss of the right of the person who granted them.

1024
1024. Only the baptized man validly receives sacred ordination.

1025
1025. § 1 . For the lawful ordination of a priest or deacon, it is required that, after taking the tests prescribed by law, the candidate meets, in the opinion of his own Bishop or the competent major Superior, the due qualities, that he is not affected by any irregularity or impediment and that he has met the prerequisites, in accordance with cc. 1033-1039; It is also necessary to have the documents indicated in c. 1050, and that the scrutiny prescribed in c. 1051.
§ 2 . It is also required that, in the opinion of the legitimate Superior himself, it be considered useful for the ministry of the Church.
§ 3 . The Bishop who ordains his own subject assigned to the service of another diocese must be aware that the ordinand will be assigned to that diocese.

1026
1026. It is necessary that whoever is going to be ordained enjoy due freedom; It is strictly prohibited to force anyone, in any way and for any reason, to receive orders, as well as to separate one who is canonically suitable from receiving them.

1027
1027. Aspirants to the diaconate and the priesthood must be formed with careful preparation, in accordance with the law.

1028
1028. The diocesan Bishop or the competent Superior should ensure that the candidates, before receiving an order, properly know what it refers to, and the obligations it entails.

1029
1029. Only those should be ordained who, according to the prudent judgment of their own Bishop or of the competent major Superior, weighing all the circumstances, have an integral faith, are moved by right intention, possess due knowledge, enjoy a good reputation and impeccable customs, proven virtues and other physical and psychological qualities congruent with the order they are going to receive.

1030
1030. Only for a canonical cause, even if it is hidden, can the own Bishop or the competent major Superior prohibit the deacons assigned to the priesthood, his subjects, from receiving this order, leaving no recourse in accordance with the law.

1031
1031. § 1 . The priesthood should only be conferred on those who have reached the age of twenty-five and enjoy sufficient maturity, also leaving a gap of at least six months between the diaconate and the priesthood; Those destined for the priesthood can be admitted to the diaconate only after they have reached the age of twenty-three.
§ 2 . The candidate for the permanent diaconate who is not married can only be admitted to this order when he has reached at least twenty-five years of age; who is married, only after having reached at least thirty-five years of age, and with the consent of his wife.
§ 3 . Episcopal Conferences may establish norms requiring a higher age to receive the priesthood or permanent diaconate.
§ 4 . The dispensation from the required age according to §§ 1 and 2 is reserved to the Apostolic See, when the time is greater than one year.

1032
1032. § 1 . Aspirants to the priesthood can only be promoted to the diaconate after having completed the fifth year of the cycle of philosophical-theological studies.
§ 2 . After completing his studies, the deacon must take part in pastoral care, exercising the diaconal order, before receiving the priesthood, for an appropriate time to be determined by the Bishop or the competent major Superior.
§ 3 . The candidate for the permanent diaconate should not receive this order without having completed the time of his formation.

1033
1033. Only one who has received the sacrament of confirmation is licitly ordained.

1034
1034. § 1 . No aspirant to the diaconate or priesthood should receive the ordination of deacon or priest without having first been admitted as a candidate, by the authority indicated in the ccs.

1035
1035. § 1 . Before someone is promoted to the diaconate, both permanent and transitional, it is necessary that the candidate has received and has exercised for the appropriate time the ministries of reader and acolyte.
§ 2 . Between the acolyte and the diaconate there must be an interval of at least six months.

1036
1036. In order to receive the ordination of deacon or priest, the candidate must deliver to his own Bishop or to the competent major Superior a declaration written and signed in his own handwriting, in which he states that he will receive the ordination spontaneously and freely, and that he will dedicate himself perpetually to the ecclesiastical ministry, at the same time that he requests to be admitted to the order that he aspires to receive.

1037
1037. The candidate for the permanent diaconate who is not married, and the candidate for the priesthood, should not be admitted to the diaconate before they have publicly assumed, before God and before the Church, the obligation of celibacy according to the prescribed ceremony, or have taken perpetual vows in a religious institute.

1038
1038. A deacon who refuses to receive the priesthood cannot be prohibited from exercising the order received, unless he is affected by a canonical impediment or by another serious cause that must be judged by the diocesan Bishop or the competent major Superior.

1039
1039. All those who are to receive an order must do spiritual exercises, at least for five days, in the place and in the manner determined by the Ordinary; The Bishop, before proceeding with ordination, must be informed that the candidates have duly completed these exercises.

1040
1040. Those who are affected by any impediment, whether perpetual, which is called irregularity, or simple, are excluded from receiving orders; no other impediment is contracted except those enumerated in the canons that follow.

1041
1041. They are irregular to receive orders:
1 who suffers from some form of dementia or other mental illness due to which, according to the opinion of experts, he or she is incapable of correctly carrying out the ministry;
2 who has committed the crime of apostasy, heresy or schism;
3 who has attempted a marriage, even just a civil one, being prevented from contracting it, either by the marriage bond itself, or by the sacred order or by a perpetual public vow of chastity, or because he did so with a woman already united in a valid marriage or bound by that same vow;
4 who has committed voluntary manslaughter or procured abortion after it has been verified, as well as all those who have cooperated positively;
5 who intentionally and seriously mutilated himself or another, or attempted suicide;
6 who has carried out an act of power of an order reserved either to Bishops or priests, without having received that order or being prohibited from exercising it by a declared or imposed canonical penalty.

1042
1042. They are simply prevented from receiving orders:
1 the married man, unless he is legitimately assigned to the permanent diaconate;
2 who performs a position or administrative task that is prohibited to clerics under cc. 285 and 286 and must render accounts, until, having left that position or task and rendered accounts, he has been free;
3 the neophyte, unless, in the opinion of the Ordinary, it has been sufficiently proven.

1043
1043. The faithful are obliged to inform the Ordinary or the parish priest, before ordination, of any impediments to receiving orders of which they are aware.

1044
1044. § 1 . They are irregular in exercising the orders received:
1 who has received the orders illegitimately, being affected by an irregularity;
2 who has committed the crime referred to in c. 1041, 2, if the crime is public;
3 who has committed any crime mentioned in c. 1041, 3, 4, 5, 6.
§ 2 . They are prevented from exercising the orders received:
1 who has illegitimately received orders while affected by an impediment;
2 who suffers from amentia or another mental illness discussed in c. 1041, 1, until the Ordinary, having consulted an expert, allows the exercise of order.

1045
1045. Ignorance of irregularities and impediments does not exempt from them.

1046
1046. Irregularities and impediments multiply when they come from various causes; but not for repetition of the same cause, unless it is an irregularity due to voluntary homicide or for having attempted an abortion if it occurs.

1047
1047. § 1 . The dispensation of all irregularities is reserved exclusively to the Apostolic See if the fact on which they are based had been brought to judicial jurisdiction.
§ 2 . The following irregularities and impediments to receiving orders are also reserved for you:
1 of irregularity due to public crimes referred to in c. 1041, 2 and 3;
2 of irregularity due to both public and hidden crimes, to which c. 1041, 4;
3 of the impediment indicated in c. 1042, 1.
§ 3 . The dispensation of irregularities for the exercise of the received order, which is dealt with in c. 1041, 3, only in public cases, and in 4 of the same canon, also in hidden cases.
§ 4 . The Ordinary can dispense with irregularities and impediments not reserved for the Holy See.

1048
1048. In the most urgent hidden cases, if you cannot go to the Ordinary, or to the Penitentiary when dealing with the irregularities indicated in c. 1041, 3 and 4, and there is a danger of serious harm or infamy, someone who is prevented by some irregularity from exercising it can exercise an order, however, the obligation remains in place to resort as soon as possible to the Ordinary or to the Penitentiary, without indicating the name and through a confessor.

1049
1049. § 1 . In the requests to obtain dispensation from irregularities and impediments, all irregularities and all impediments must be indicated; However, the general dispensation is also valid for what has not been stated in good faith, except for the irregularities discussed in c. 1041, 4 and those others that have been brought to judicial jurisdiction, but not for what has been hidden in bad faith.
§ 2 . If it is an irregularity due to voluntary manslaughter or procured abortion, for the exemption to be valid, the number of crimes must also be stated.
§ 3 . The general exemption from irregularities and impediments to receiving orders applies to all orders.

1050
1050. and 1051.
1050. In order for someone to access sacred orders, the following documents are required:
1 the certificate of studies carried out in accordance with c. 1032;
2 in the case of the ordination of priests, the certificate that they have received the diaconate;
3 in the case of the ordination of deacons, the baptism and confirmation certificate, as well as the fact that they have received the ministries referred to in c. 1035; and also the certificate that they have made the declaration prescribed in c. 1036, and, if it is a married person who is going to be promoted to the permanent diaconate, the certificates of marriage and consent of his wife.

1051
1051. With regard to the investigation of the qualities required in the ordinand, the following prescriptions must be observed:
1 the rector of the seminary or training house must certify that the candidate possesses the necessary qualities to receive the order, that is, correct doctrine, sincere piety, good morals and aptitude to exercise the ministry; and likewise, after the appropriate investigation, he will record his state of physical and mental health;
2 so that the investigation is carried out appropriately, the diocesan Bishop or the Major Superior may use other means that seem useful, taking into account the circumstances of time and place, such as testimonial letters, proclamations or other information.

1052
1052. § 1 . In order for the Bishop who confers ordination in his own right to proceed with it, he must have proof that the documents indicated in c. 1050, and that the suitability of the candidate has been positively proven, through the investigation carried out according to law.
§ 2 . For a Bishop to ordain a foreign subject, it is enough for the resignations to attest that they have those documents, that the scrutiny has been carried out in accordance with the law, and that the suitability of the candidate is established; If the ordinand is a member of a religious institute or a society of apostolic life, the resignations must also attest that he has been received into the institute or society definitively and is a subject of the Superior who gives the resignations.
§ 3 . If, despite all this, the Bishop doubts with certain reasons the suitability of the candidate to receive orders, he should not ordain him.

1053
1053. § 1 . At the end of the ordination, the name of each ordinand and the minister who ordained him, as well as the place and day of the ordination, must be noted in a special book carefully kept in the curia of the place where the sacrament has been administered, and all documents relating to each of the ordinations must also be diligently filed.
§ 2 . The Bishop must give each ordinand an authentic certificate of the ordination received; and if these were ordained with dimissaries by a foreign Bishop, they will in turn show that document to their own Ordinary, so that the ordination can be noted in a special book that will be kept in the archive.

1054
1054. The Ordinary of the place, in the case of secular people, or the competent major Superior, in the case of his subjects, must communicate the ordination to the parish priest of the place of baptism of each ordinand, so that he can note it in the book of baptisms, in accordance with the c

1055
1055. § 1 . The marital alliance, by which the man and the woman constitute among themselves a lifelong partnership, ordered by its very natural nature to the good of the spouses and the generation and education of offspring, was elevated by Christ the Lord to the dignity of a sacrament between the baptized.
§ 2 . Therefore, between the baptized, there can be no valid marriage contract that is not therefore a sacrament.

1056
1056. The essential properties of marriage are unity and indissolubility, which in Christian marriage achieve a particular firmness due to the sacrament.

1057
1057. § 1 . Marriage is produced by the consent of the parties legitimately expressed between legally competent persons, a consent that no human power can replace.
§ 2 . Marital consent is the act of the will, by which the man and the woman give themselves and accept each other in an irrevocable alliance to constitute marriage.

1058
1058. All those to whom the law does not prohibit it may contract marriage.

1059
1059. The marriage of Catholics, even if only one of the parties is Catholic, is governed not only by divine law but also by canonical law, without prejudice to the jurisdiction of civil power over the merely civil effects of the same marriage.

1060
1060. Marriage enjoys the favor of law; Therefore, there must be doubt about the validity of the marriage until the contrary is proven.

1061
1061. § 1 A valid marriage between the baptized is called only rato, if it has not been consummated; time and consummated, if the spouses have carried out in a human way the conjugal act capable of itself to generate offspring, to which marriage is ordered by its very nature and by which the spouses become one flesh.
§ 2 . Once the marriage is celebrated, if the spouses have cohabited, consummation is presumed, until the contrary is proven.
§ 3 . An invalid marriage is called putative, if it was celebrated in good faith by at least one of the parties, until both acquire certainty of the nullity.

1062
1062. § 1 . The promise of marriage, both unilateral and bilateral, which is called betrothal, is governed by the particular law established by the Episcopal Conference, taking into account customs and civil laws, if any.
§ 2 . The promise of marriage does not give rise to an action to request its celebration; but yes for compensation for damages, if in any way it is due.

1063
1063. Pastors of souls are obliged to ensure that the ecclesiastical community itself provides the faithful with assistance so that the state of marriage is maintained in the Christian spirit and progresses towards perfection. Above all, this assistance must be provided:
1 through preaching, catechesis adapted to minors, young people and adults, and even with the media of social communication, so that the faithful acquire formation on the meaning of Christian marriage and on the task of Christian spouses and parents;
2 for personal preparation to enter into marriage, by which the bride and groom prepare themselves for the sanctity and obligations of their new state;
3 for a fruitful liturgical celebration of marriage, which shows that the spouses are a sign of the mystery of unity and fruitful love between Christ and the Church and that they participate in it;
4 for the help given to married people, so that, faithfully maintaining and defending the marital alliance, they may reach an increasingly holy and fuller life within their own family.

1064
1064. It is the responsibility of the local Ordinary to ensure that this assistance is properly organized, also hearing, if it seems appropriate, men and women of proven experience and competence.

1065
1065. § 1 . Catholics not yet confirmed must receive the sacrament of confirmation before being admitted to marriage, if this is possible without serious difficulty.
§ 2 . In order to fruitfully receive the sacrament of marriage, it is strongly recommended that the couple attend the sacraments of penance and the most holy Eucharist.

1066
1066. Before the marriage is celebrated, it must be established that nothing opposes its valid and lawful celebration.

1067
1067. The Episcopal Conference will establish norms on the examination of the couple, as well as on marriage proclamations or other appropriate means to carry out the investigations that must necessarily precede the marriage, so that, diligently observed, the parish priest can attend the marriage.

1068
1068. In danger of death, if no other evidence can be obtained, unless there is evidence to the contrary, the declaration of the couple, under oath as the case may be, that they are baptized and free of all impediments is sufficient.

1069
1069. All the faithful are obliged to inform the parish priest or the local Ordinary, before the celebration of the marriage, of any impediments of which they are aware.

1070
1070. If the investigations are carried out by someone other than the parish priest who is responsible for assisting the marriage, they will communicate their results to the same parish priest as soon as possible, by means of an authentic document.

1071
1071. § 1 . Except in case of necessity, no one should attend without permission from the local Ordinary:
1 to the marriage of the lazy;
2 to a marriage that cannot be recognized or celebrated according to civil law;
3 to the marriage of someone who is subject to natural obligations arising from a previous union, towards the other party or towards the children of that union;
4 to the marriage of someone who had clearly abandoned the Catholic faith;
5 to the marriage of someone who is subject to censure;
6 to the marriage of a minor, if his parents ignore it or reasonably oppose it;
7 to marriage by attorney, which is discussed in c. 1105.
§ 2 . The local Ordinary must not grant permission to attend the marriage of someone who has notoriously abandoned the Catholic faith, if it is not observing with due adaptations what is established in c. 1125.

1072
1072. Pastors of souls should try to discourage young people from celebrating marriage who have not yet reached the age at which, according to the customs of the region, it is usually contracted.

1073
1073. Dimitory impediment disqualifies the person from validly contracting marriage.

1074
1074. The impediment that can be proven in the external forum is considered public; Otherwise it is hidden.

1075
1075. § 1 . It is the exclusive responsibility of the supreme authority of the Church to authentically declare when divine law prohibits or resolves marriage.
§ 2 . Likewise, only the supreme authority has the right to establish other impediments regarding the baptized.

1076
1076. Any custom that introduces a new impediment or is contrary to existing impediments is condemned.

1077
1077. § 1 . In a particular case, the local Ordinary may prohibit marriage to his own subjects wherever they reside and to all those who actually reside within his territory, but only temporarily, for serious reasons and for as long as it lasts.
§ 2 . Only the supreme authority of the Church can add a deciding clause to this prohibition.

1078
1078. § 1 . With the exception of those impediments whose dispensation is reserved for the Apostolic See, the local Ordinary can dispense from all impediments of ecclesiastical law to his own subjects, regardless of the place in which they reside, and to all those who actually live in his territory.
§ 2 . The impediments whose dispensation is reserved for the Apostolic See are:
1 the impediment that comes from having received sacred orders or the perpetual public vow of chastity in a religious institute of pontifical right;
2 the impediment of crime, which is discussed in c. 1090.
§ 3 . Dispensation from the impediment of consanguinity is never granted in a straight line or in the second degree of a collateral line.
1078. § 2, 1, the local Ordinary and, provided the case is hidden, all those mentioned in c. 1079 §§ 2 and 3, observing the conditions prescribed therein.
§ 2 . This power also applies to validating a marriage, if there is the same danger in delay and there is no time to resort to the Apostolic See, or to the local Ordinary when dealing with impediments from which it can dispense.
1078. § 2 , or it is an impediment of positive natural or divine law that has already ceased.

1079
1079. § 1 . In danger of death, the Ordinary of the place can exempt his own subjects, wherever they reside, and all those who actually live in his territory, both from the manner that must be observed in the celebration of marriage and from each and every one of the impediments of ecclesiastical law, whether public or hidden, except for the impediment arising from the sacred order of the presbyterate.
§ 2 . In the same circumstances as those discussed in § 1, but only for cases in which it is not even possible to go to the local Ordinary, the parish priest, the duly delegated sacred minister and the priest or deacon who attend the marriage referred to in c.
1079. §2, they must immediately communicate to the local Ordinary the dispensation granted for the external forum; and this must be noted in the marriage book.

1080
1080. § 1 . Provided that the impediment is discovered when everything is already prepared for the nuptials, and the marriage cannot be delayed without danger of serious harm until dispensation is obtained from the competent authority, they enjoy the power to dispense with all impediments, except those listed in the c

1081
1081. Both the parish priest and the priest or the deacon, to whom the c

1082
1082. Unless the rescript of the Penitentiary determines otherwise, the dispensation of a hidden impediment granted in the non-sacramental internal forum will be noted in the book that must be kept in the secret archive of the curia; and no further dispensation is necessary for the external forum, if the hidden impediment later becomes public.

1083
1083. § 1 . A man cannot contract a valid marriage before he is sixteen years old, nor a woman before she is fourteen years old.
§ 2 . The Episcopal Conference may establish a higher age for the licit celebration of marriage.

1084
1084. § 1 . The antecedent and perpetual impotence to carry out the marital act, both on the part of the man and the woman, whether absolute or relative, renders marriage null and void by its very nature.
§ 2 . If the impediment of impotence is doubtful, with doubt of law or fact, the marriage should not be prevented or, while the doubt persists, declared void.
§ 3 . Sterility does not prohibit or resolve marriage, without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 1098.

1085
1085. § 1 . A person who is bound by the bond of a previous marriage, even if it has not been consummated, invalidly attempts a marriage.
§ 2 . Even if the previous marriage is void or has been dissolved for any reason, it is not licit to contract another one before the nullity or dissolution of the previous one is legitimately and certainly established.

1086
1086. § 1 . n A marriage between two people, one of whom was baptized in the Catholic Church or received into its bosom, and the other who was not baptized, is invalid.
§ 2 . This impediment will not be waived if the conditions indicated in ccs are not met. 1125 and 1126.
§ 3 . If at the time of contracting the marriage, one party was commonly considered baptized or his or her baptism was doubtful, it must be presumed, in accordance with c. 1060, the validity of the marriage until it is proven with certainty that one of the parties was baptized and the other was not.
1086. § 1 . A marriage between two people is invalid, one of whom was baptized in the Catholic Church or received into its bosom and has not separated from it by a formal act, and the other who is not baptized.

1087
1087. Those who have received sacred orders invalidly attempt marriage.

1088
1088. Those who are bound by a perpetual public vow of chastity in a religious institute invalidly attempt marriage.

1089
1089. There can be no marriage between a man and a woman kidnapped or at least held with a view to marrying her, unless later the woman, separated from the kidnapper and finding herself in a safe and free place, voluntarily chooses marriage.

1090
1090. § 1 . Whoever, in order to marry a specific person, causes the death of that person's spouse or his own spouse, invalidly attempts that marriage.
§ 2 . Those who, with mutual cooperation, physical or moral, caused the death of the spouse, also invalidly attempt the marriage of each other.

1091
1091. § 1 . In a straight line of consanguinity, marriage between all ascendants and descendants, both legitimate and natural, is void.
§ 2 . In the collateral line, it is void up to and including the fourth degree.
§ 3 . The consanguinity impediment does not multiply.
§ 4 . Marriage should never be permitted when there remains any doubt as to whether the parties are consanguineous in some degree of direct line or in the second degree of collateral line.

1092
1092. Affinity in a straight line settles marriage to any degree.

1093
1093. The impediment of public honesty arises from an invalid marriage after life in common has been established or from notorious or public concubinage; and settles marriage in the first degree of a straight line between the man and the woman's blood relatives and vice versa.

1094
1094. Those who are united by legal kinship arising from adoption, in a direct line or in the second degree of a collateral line, cannot validly contract marriage with each other.
(n: Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canon modified by His Holiness Pope Benedict XVI (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Omnium inmentem of October 26, 2009)]:

1095
1095. The following are incapable of marriage:
1 those who lack sufficient use of reason;
2 who have a serious defect of discretion of judgment regarding the essential rights and duties of marriage that they must mutually give and accept;
3 who cannot assume the essential obligations of marriage for reasons of a psychological nature.

1096
1096. § 1 . In order for there to be marital consent, it is necessary that the parties not ignore at least that marriage is a permanent partnership between a man and a woman, ordered to the procreation of offspring through a certain sexual cooperation.
§ 2 . This ignorance is not presumed after puberty.

1097
1097. § 1 . The mistake about the person makes the marriage invalid.
§ 2 . The error regarding a quality of the person, even if it is the cause of the contract, does not settle the marriage, unless this quality is directly and primarily intended.

1098
1098. Whoever contracts the marriage deceived by fraud, provoked to obtain his consent, regarding a quality of the other contracting party, which by its nature can seriously disturb the consortium of conjugal life, contracts invalidly.

1099
1099. The error regarding the unity, indissolubility or sacramental dignity of marriage, as long as it does not determine the will, does not vitiate marital consent.

1100
1100. Certainty or opinion about the nullity of marriage does not necessarily exclude marital consent.

1101
1101. § 1 . The internal consent of the will is presumed to be in accordance with the words or signs used when celebrating the marriage.
§ 2 . But if one or both contracting parties exclude with a positive act of will the marriage itself, or an essential element of the marriage, or an essential property, they contract invalidly.

1102
1102. § 1 . Marriage cannot be validly contracted under a future condition.
§ 2 . A marriage contracted under a condition of past or present is valid or not, depending on whether what is the object of the condition is verified or not.
§ 3 . However, the condition addressed in § 2 cannot be lawfully placed without written permission from the local Ordinary.

1103
1103. A marriage contracted by violence or serious fear arising from an external cause is invalid, even that not inferred with a view to marriage, to escape from which someone is forced to marry.

1104
1104. § 1 . To validly contract a marriage it is necessary that both parties are present in the same place, either in person or through a solicitor.
§ 2 . Let the spouses express marital consent in words; or, if they cannot speak, with equivalent signs.

1105
1105. § 1 . To validly contract a marriage by proxy, it is required:
1 that a special mandate has been given to contract with a specific person;
2 that the attorney has been appointed by the principal, and personally performs that function.
§ 2 . For the mandate to be valid, it is required that it be signed by the principal and, in addition, by the parish priest or the Ordinary of the place where the mandate is given, or by a priest delegated by one of them, or at least by two witnesses; or it must be done through an authentic document in accordance with civil law.
§ 3 . If the principal cannot write, this circumstance must be stated in the mandate, and another witness will be added, who must also sign the writing; Otherwise, the mandate is void.
§ 4 . If the principal, before the attorney has contracted in his name, revokes the mandate or falls into amencia, the marriage is invalid, even if the attorney or the other contracting party is unaware of it.

1106
1106. Marriage can be contracted through an interpreter, but the parish priest must not attend if he is not satisfied with the fidelity of the interpreter.

1107
1107. Even if the marriage had been contracted invalidly due to an impediment or defect of form, it is presumed that the consent given perseveres, as long as its revocation is not recorded.

1108
1108. § 1 . Only those marriages are valid that are contracted before the local Ordinary or the parish priest, or a priest or deacon delegated by one of them to attend, and before two witnesses, in accordance with the rules established in the canons that follow, and leaving aside the exceptions discussed in the ccs. 144,
1108. § 3 .
§ 2 . For the delegation of the power to attend marriages to be valid, it must be expressly granted to specific people; If it is a special delegation, it must be given for a specific marriage, and if it is a general delegation, it must be granted in writing.
1108. § 3 .
§ 2 . A suitable layman must be chosen, capable of instructing the couple and capable of properly celebrating the marriage liturgy.

1109
1109. n The local Ordinary and the parish priest, unless by sentence or decree they were excommunicated, or interdicted, or suspended from the office, or declared such, by virtue of the office validly assist in their territory at the marriages not only of the subjects, but also of those who are not subjects, provided that at least one of the parties is attached to the Latin Church.
1109. The local Ordinary and the parish priest, unless by sentence or decree they were excommunicated, or interdicted, or suspended from the office, or declared as such, by virtue of the office, validly assist in their territory at the marriages not only of the subjects, but also of those who are not subjects, as long as one of them is of the Latin rite.

1110
1110. The Ordinary and the personal parish priest, by reason of their office, only validly assist at the marriage of those of whom at least one is their subject, within the limits of their jurisdiction.

1111
1111. § 1 .
1111. § 1 . n The local Ordinary and the parish priest, while validly carrying out their office, may delegate to priests and deacons the faculty, even general, of assisting at marriages within the limits of their territory, remaining firm, however, as prescribed by the c
1111. § 1 . The local Ordinary and the parish priest, while validly carrying out their office, may delegate to priests and deacons the power, even general, to assist at marriages within the limits of their territory.

1112
1112. § 1, 1116 and 1127 §§ 1 and 2.
§ 2 . It is understood that only those who, being present, request the manifestation of the consent of the spouses and receive it in the name of the Church, attend the marriage.
§ 3 . n Only the priest validly assists the marriage between two oriental parties or between a Latin party and a Catholic or non-Catholic oriental party
1112. § 1 . n Where there are no priests or deacons, the diocesan Bishop, with prior favorable vote of the Episcopal Conference and obtaining license from the Holy See, may delegate lay people to attend marriages, remaining firm as established in the c
1112. § 1 . Where there are no priests or deacons, the diocesan Bishop, with prior favorable vote of the Episcopal Conference and obtaining license from the Holy See, may delegate lay people to attend marriages.

1113
1113. Before granting a special delegation, everything established by law must be complied with to verify the state of freedom.

1114
1114. Whoever attends the marriage acts illicitly if he is not aware of the state of freedom of the couple under the law and if, each time he attends by virtue of a general delegation, he does not ask the priest for permission, when possible.

1115
1115. Marriages must be celebrated in the parish where one of the spouses has his domicile or quasi-domicile or has resided for a month, or, if they are vagrants, in the parish where they are at that time; With the permission of the proper Ordinary or the proper parish priest, they can be celebrated elsewhere.

1116
1116. § 3 . In danger of death, the confessor enjoys the power to dispense internally from hidden impediments, both in sacramental confession and outside of it.
§ 4 . In the case discussed in § 2, it is considered that it is not possible to go to the local Ordinary if it can only be done by telegraph or telephone.
1116. § 1 . If there is no one who is competent according to the law to attend the marriage, or it cannot be attended without serious difficulty, those who intend to contract a true marriage can do so validly and lawfully with only the witnesses present:
1 in danger of death;
2 out of danger of death, as long as it is prudently anticipated that this situation will last for a month.
§ 2 . In both cases, if there is another priest or deacon who can be present, he must be called and must witness the marriage together with the witnesses, without prejudice to the validity of the marriage only before witnesses.
§ 3 . n Under the same circumstances listed in § 1 nn. 1 and 2, the local Ordinary may grant any Catholic priest the power to bless the marriage of faithful of the Eastern Churches who do not have full communion with the Catholic Church, if they spontaneously request it, and as long as nothing opposes the valid and lawful celebration of the marriage. The priest himself, if he can prudently do so, will communicate it to the interested competent authority of the non-Catholic Church.

1117
1117. n The above established form must be observed if at least one of the contracting parties was baptized in the Catholic Church or received into it, without prejudice to what is established in the c

1117. The above established form must be observed if at least one of the contracting parties was baptized in the Catholic Church or received into it and has not departed from it by a formal act, without prejudice to what is established in the c

1118
1118. § 1 . Marriage between Catholics or between a Catholic party and a baptized non-Catholic party must be celebrated in a parish church; With the permission of the local Ordinary or the parish priest, it can be celebrated in another church or oratory.
§ 2 . The local Ordinary may allow the celebration of the marriage in another convenient place.
§ 3 . The marriage between a Catholic party and a non-baptized party may be celebrated in a church or other convenient place.

1119
1119. Except in cases of necessity, in the celebration of marriage the rites prescribed in the liturgical books approved by the Church or introduced by legitimate customs must be observed.

1120
1120. With the recognition of the Holy See, the Episcopal Conference can develop its own rite of marriage, consistent with the customs of places and peoples, adapted to the Christian spirit; The law remains in place, however, according to which whoever attends the marriage while being personally present must ask for and receive the expression of the consent of the spouses.

1121
1121. § 1 . After the marriage has been celebrated, the parish priest of the place where it was celebrated or whoever takes his place, even if none of them attended the marriage, must record as soon as possible in the marriage registry the names of the spouses, the assistant and the witnesses, and the place and day of the celebration, according to the manner prescribed by the Episcopal Conference or by the diocesan Bishop.
§ 2 . When marriage is contracted as provided in c. 1116, the priest or deacon, if he was present at the celebration, or otherwise the witnesses, are obligated jointly with the bride and groom to communicate as soon as possible to the parish priest or the Ordinary of the place that the marriage has been celebrated.
§ 3 . As regards a marriage contracted with a dispensation from the canonical form, the Ordinary of the place that granted the dispensation must ensure that the dispensation and celebration are noted in the registry of marriages, both of the curia and of the parish of the Catholic part, whose parish priest carried out the investigations regarding the state of freedom; The Catholic spouse is obliged to notify the same Ordinary and the parish priest as soon as possible that the marriage has been celebrated, also stating the place where it was contracted, and the public form that has been observed.

1122
1122. § 1 . The marriage must also be noted in the baptism records in which the baptism of the spouses is recorded.
§ 2 . If a spouse has not contracted a marriage in the parish in which he or she was baptized, the priest of the place where the marriage was celebrated must send notification of the contracted marriage as soon as possible to the parish priest of the place where the baptism was administered.

1123
1123. When a marriage is validated for the external forum, or is declared null, or is legitimately dissolved for a cause other than death, this circumstance must be communicated to the parish priest of the place where the marriage was celebrated, so that the annotation in the marriage and baptism records can be made as ordered.
