1124
1124. n Marriage between two baptized persons, one of whom has been baptized in the Catholic Church or received into it after baptism, and another attached to a Church or ecclesial community that is not in full communion with the Catholic Church, is prohibited without express license from the competent authority.
1124. Marriage between two baptized persons is prohibited, without express license from the competent authority, one of whom has been baptized in the Catholic Church or received into it after baptism and has not separated from it by a formal act, and another attached to a Church or ecclesial community that is not in full communion with the Catholic Church.
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio De concordia inter Codices, dated May 31, 2016)]:

1125
1125. If there is a just and reasonable cause, the local Ordinary can grant this license; but it should not be granted if the following conditions are not met:
1 that the Catholic party declare that it is willing to avoid any danger of departing from the faith, and sincerely promise that it will do everything possible to ensure that all children are baptized and educated in the Catholic Church;
2 that the other contracting party be informed at the time about the promises that the Catholic party must make, so that it is clear that he is truly aware of the promise and the obligation of the Catholic party;
3 that both parties be instructed about the essential purposes and properties of marriage, which cannot be excluded by either of them.

1126
1126. It is up to the Episcopal Conference to determine both the way in which these declarations and promises, which are always necessary, are to be made, as well as the way in which they are recorded in the external forum and that the non-Catholic party is informed.

1127
1127. § 2 .
1127. § 1 . n Regarding the form that must be used in mixed marriage, the prescriptions of c. 1108; but if a Catholic party contracts marriage with a non-Catholic party of the Eastern rite, the canonical form is required only for legality; but for validity the intervention of a priest is required, observing the other prescriptions of the law.
§ 2 . If there are serious difficulties in observing the canonical form, the Ordinary of the place of the Catholic part has the right to dispense from it in each case, but consulting the Ordinary of the place where the marriage is celebrated and the requirement of some public form of celebration remains for validity; It is up to the Episcopal Conference to establish norms so that said dispensation is granted with unity of criteria.
§ 3 . It is prohibited that, before or after the canonical celebration in accordance with § 1, there is another religious celebration of the same marriage to grant or renew marital consent; Likewise, a religious ceremony should not be held in which the Catholic assistant and the non-Catholic minister together, each performing their own rite, ask for the consent of the couple.
1127. and 1128 also apply to marriages for which the impediment of disparity of cults is an obstacle, which is dealt with in c. 1086 § 1 .

[Original wording of the canon modified by His Holiness Pope Benedict XVI (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Omnium inmentem of October 26, 2009)]:
1127. § 1 . Regarding the form that should be used in mixed marriage, the prescriptions of c. 1108; but if a Catholic party contracts marriage with a non-Catholic party of the Eastern rite, the canonical form is required only for legality; but for validity the intervention of a sacred minister is required, observing the other prescriptions of the law.
1127. § 2 . If the impediment cannot be proven, it is sufficient for the consent to be renewed privately and secretly by the contracting party who knows of the existence of the impediment, as long as the other party perseveres in the consent he gave; or by both contracting parties, if both know of the existence of the impediment.

1128
1128. The local Ordinaries and the other pastors of souls must ensure that the Catholic spouse, and the children born in a mixed marriage, do not lack spiritual assistance to fulfill their obligations and must help the spouses to promote the unity of their conjugal and family life.

1129
1129. The prescriptions of the cc

1130
1130. For serious and urgent reasons, the local Ordinary may allow the marriage to be celebrated in secret.

1131
1131. Permission to celebrate marriage in secret entails:
1 that the investigations that must be made before marriage be carried out in secret;
2 that the local Ordinary, the assistant, the witnesses and the spouses keep the marriage celebrated secret.

1132
1132. The obligation to maintain secrecy, which is discussed in c. 1131, 2, if due to the observance of the secret there is an imminent danger of serious scandal or serious injury to the sanctity of the marriage, and the parties must be warned of this before the celebration of the marriage.

1133
1133. A marriage celebrated in secret will be recorded only in a special register, which must be kept in the secret archive of the curia.

1134
1134. A valid marriage creates a perpetual and exclusive bond between the spouses by its very nature; Furthermore, in Christian marriage the spouses are strengthened and remain as if consecrated by a sacrament peculiar to the duties and dignity of their state.

1135
1135. Both spouses have equal obligation and right regarding everything that belongs to the consortium of married life.

1136
1136. Parents have the very serious obligation and the primary right to take care, to the best of their ability, of the education of their children, both physically, socially and culturally, as well as morally and religiously.

1137
1137. Children conceived or born from a valid or putative marriage are legitimate.

1138
1138. § 1 . Marriage shows who the father is, unless the contrary is proven with obvious reasons.
§ 2 . Children born at least 180 days after the marriage was celebrated, or within 300 days from the dissolution of marital life, are presumed legitimate.

1139
1139. Illegitimate children are legitimized by the subsequent marriage of the parents, both valid and putative, or by rescript of the Holy See.

1140
1140. As far as canonical effects are concerned, legitimized children are equal in everything to legitimate ones, unless the law expressly provides otherwise.

1141
1141. A long and consummated marriage cannot be dissolved by any human power, nor by any cause other than death.

1142
1142. An unconsummated marriage between the baptized, or between the baptized party and the unbaptized party, may be dissolved with just cause by the Roman Pontiff, at the request of both parties or one of them, even if the other objects.

1143
1143. § 1 . The marriage contracted by two unbaptized persons is dissolved by the Pauline privilege in favor of the faith of the party who has received the baptism, by the very fact that this party contracts a new marriage, provided that the unbaptized party separates.
§ 2 . The unbaptized party is considered to separate if he does not want to cohabit with the baptized party, or to cohabit peacefully without offending the Creator, unless the latter, after receiving baptism, had given him a just reason to separate.

1144
1144. § 1 . For the baptized party to validly contract a new marriage, the unbaptized party must always be questioned:
1 if she also wants to receive baptism;
2 if you want to at least cohabit peacefully with the baptized party, without offending the Creator.
§ 2 . This interpellation must be done after baptism; However, with serious cause, the local Ordinary may allow it to be done earlier, and even dispense from it, both before and after baptism, as long as it is established, at least by a summary and extrajudicial procedure, that it could not be done or that it would have been useless.

1145
1145. § 1 . The interpellation will normally be made by the authority of the Ordinary of the place of the converted party; This Ordinary must grant the other spouse, if he or she requests it, a period of time to respond, however, warning him or her that, if this period has passed unnecessarily, his silence will be understood as a negative response.
§ 2 . If the above-mentioned form cannot be observed, the interpellation made, even privately, by the converted party is valid and also lawful.
§ 3 . In the two previous cases, it must legitimately be stated in the external forum that the interpellation has been made and what its result has been.

1146
1146. The baptized party has the right to contract a new marriage with another Catholic person:
1 if the other party responds negatively to the interpellation, or if it was not legitimately made;
2 if the unbaptized party, questioned or not, having continued peaceful cohabitation without offense to the Creator, later separates without just cause, remaining in place what is prescribed in cc. 1144 and 1145.

1147
1147. However, for serious reasons, the local Ordinary may grant that the baptized party, using the Pauline privilege, contract marriage with a non-Catholic party, baptized or not, also observing the prescriptions of the canons on mixed marriages.

1148
1148. § 1 . Upon receiving baptism in the Catholic Church, an unbaptized man who simultaneously has several unbaptized wives, if it is difficult for him to remain with the first of them, he can stay with one of the others, putting the others away from him. The same applies to an unbaptized woman who simultaneously has several unbaptized husbands.
§ 2 . In the cases dealt with by § 1, the marriage must be contracted according to the legitimate form, once baptism has been received, also observing, if applicable, the prescriptions on mixed marriages and other provisions of the law.
§ 3 . Taking into account the moral, social and economic condition of places and people, the Ordinary of the place must ensure that, according to the norms of justice, Christian charity and natural equity, the needs of the first woman and the others who have been separated are sufficiently provided for.

1149
1149. The unbaptized person who, once received baptism in the Catholic Church, is unable to reestablish cohabitation with the other unbaptized spouse due to captivity or persecution, may contract a new marriage, even if the other party has received baptism in the meantime, remaining in force what is prescribed in c. 1141.

1150
1150. In case of doubt, the privilege of faith enjoys the favor of law.

1151
1151. Spouses have the duty and right to maintain conjugal coexistence, unless they are excused by a legitimate cause.

1152
1152. § 1 . Although it is strongly recommended that the spouse, moved by Christian charity and keeping in mind the good of the family, does not deny forgiveness to the adulterous partner or interrupt married life, if in spite of everything he does not expressly or tacitly forgive that fault, he has the right to break the marital coexistence, unless he had consented to the adultery, or had been the cause of it, or he had also committed adultery.
§ 2 . There is tacit condonation if the innocent spouse, after having ascertained the adultery, spontaneously continues in the marital relationship with the other spouse; Condonation is presumed if marital cohabitation continues for six months, without having resorted to ecclesiastical or civil authority.
§ 3 . If the innocent spouse interrupts conjugal coexistence of his own will, he must propose within six months a cause for separation before the competent ecclesiastical authority, which, weighing all the circumstances, must consider whether it is possible to move the innocent spouse to forgive the guilt and not separate forever.

1153
1153. § 1 . If one of the spouses puts the other or the children in serious spiritual or physical danger, or otherwise makes life together too difficult, he or she provides the other with a legitimate reason to separate, with the authorization of the local Ordinary and, if the delay implies a danger, also by his own authority.
§ 2 . When the cause of separation ceases, conjugal coexistence must always be restored, unless the ecclesiastical authority determines otherwise.

1154
1154. Once the separation of the spouses has been carried out, the proper support and education of the children must always be provided in a timely manner.

1155
1155. The innocent spouse can admit the other back to married life, and it is to be praised that he does so; and in that case, you waive the right to separate.

1156
1156. § 1 . To validate a marriage that is void due to a decisive impediment, it is necessary that the impediment cease or a dispensation from it be obtained, and that consent be renewed by at least the spouse who knew of the existence of the impediment.
§ 2 . This renewal is required by ecclesiastical law for the validity of the convalidation, even if both parties had given their consent from the first moment and had not subsequently revoked it.

1157
1157. The renewal of consent must be a new act of will regarding the marriage by the person who knows or believes that it was null from the beginning.

1158
1158. § 1 . If the impediment is public, both contracting parties must renew the consent in the canonical form, leaving aside what is prescribed by the c

1159
1159. § 1 . A marriage void due to lack of consent is validated if the person who had not previously consented consents, as long as the consent given by the other party persists.
§ 2 . If the lack of consent cannot be proven, it is sufficient for the person who had not given it to privately and secretly give consent.
§ 3 . If the lack of consent can be proven, it is necessary that consent be given in canonical form.

1160
1160. For a marriage void due to a defect of form to be made valid, it must be contracted again in canonical form, without prejudice to what is prescribed by the c

1161
1161. § 1 . The healing at the root of a void marriage is its validation, without the consent having to be renewed, granted by the competent authority; and it carries with it the dispensation of the impediment, if there is one, and of the canonical form, if it was not observed, as well as the retrotraction of the canonical effects to the past.
§ 2 . Convalidation takes place from the moment the grace is granted; and it is understood that the retrotraction extends to the moment in which the marriage was celebrated, unless otherwise expressly stated.
§ 3 . Healing at the root should only be granted when it is probable that the parties want to persevere in married life.

1162
1162. § 1 . If consent is lacking in both parties or in one of them, the marriage cannot be healed at the root, whether the consent was lacking from the beginning, or if it was given in the first moment and was later revoked.
§ 2 . If consent was lacking at the beginning, but was given later, healing may be granted from the moment consent was given.

1164
1164. Healing can also be granted if one or both parties ignore it; but it should not be granted without serious cause.

1165
1165. § 1 . Healing at the root can be granted by the Apostolic See.
§ 2 . It can be granted by the diocesan Bishop in each case, even when several grounds for annulment occur in the same marriage, meeting the conditions established in c. 1125 for the healing of mixed marriages; but the Bishop cannot grant it if there is an impediment whose dispensation is reserved to the Apostolic See in accordance with c.

1166
1166. Sacramentals are sacred signs, by which, in a certain way imitating the sacraments, mainly spiritual effects are signified and obtained through the intercession of the Church.

1167
1167. § 1 . Only the Apostolic See can establish new sacramentals, authentically interpret those that exist and suppress or modify any of them.
§ 2 . In the preparation or administration of sacramentals, the rites and formulas approved by the authority of the Church must be diligently observed.

1169
1169. § 1 . Consecrations and dedications can validly be carried out by those who enjoy episcopal character, and also by those priests who are permitted to do so by law or by legitimate concession.
§ 2 . Any priest can impart blessings, except those reserved for the Roman Pontiff or Bishops.
§ 3 . The deacon can only impart those blessings that are expressly permitted by law.

1170
1170. Blessings must be imparted first to Catholics, but they can also be given to catechumens and even to non-Catholics, unless a Church prohibition obstructs.

1171
1171. Sacred things destined for worship must be treated with reverence through dedication or blessing, and they must not be used for profane or improper use, even if they belong to individuals.

1172
1172. § 1 . Without a peculiar and express license from the local Ordinary, no one can legitimately perform exorcisms on the possessed.
§ 2 . The local Ordinary will grant this license only to a pious, learned, prudent priest with integrity of life.

1173
1173. The Church, exercising the priestly function of Christ, celebrates the Liturgy of the Hours, by which, hearing God speaking to his people and remembering the mystery of salvation, it praises him without ceasing with song and prayer while at the same time praying for the salvation of the whole world.

1174
1174. § 1 . The obligation to celebrate the liturgy of the hours binds clerics according to the norm of the c

1175
1175. When celebrating the liturgy of the hours, efforts must be made to observe the natural course of each hour as far as possible.

1176
1176. § 1 . The faithful deceased must have ecclesiastical funerals in accordance with the law.
§ 2 . Ecclesiastical funerals, with which the Church obtains spiritual help for the deceased and honors their bodies, and at the same time provides the living with the consolation of hope, must be celebrated according to liturgical laws.
§ 3 . The Church strongly advises that the pious custom of burying the corpse of the deceased be preserved; However, it does not prohibit cremation, unless it has been chosen for reasons contrary to Christian doctrine.

1177
1177. § 1 . Funerals for a faithful deceased should generally be celebrated in their own parish church.
§ 2 . However, all the faithful, or those whose responsibility it is to arrange for the funeral of a deceased faithful, are allowed to choose another church for the funeral, with the consent of the person who governs it and having communicated it to the deceased's own parish priest.
§ 3 . If the death takes place outside the parish and the body has not been transferred there nor has a church been legitimately chosen for the funeral, the funeral will be celebrated in the church of the parish where the death occurred, unless the particular law designates another.

1178
1178. The funeral of the diocesan Bishop will be celebrated in his cathedral church, unless he has chosen another.

1179
1179. The funerals of religious or members of societies of apostolic life will generally be celebrated in the church or oratory itself by the Superior, if the institute or society is clerical; or by the chaplain in other cases.

1180
1180. § 1 . If the parish has its own cemetery, the faithful must be buried there, unless the deceased himself or those whose responsibility it is to take care of his grave have legitimately chosen another cemetery.
§ 2 . Unless the law prohibits it, everyone can choose the cemetery in which they are to be buried.

1181
1181. As regards oblations on the occasion of funerals, observe the prescriptions of c. 1264, avoiding however any respect of persons, or that the poor are deprived of the proper funerals.

1182
1182. Once the burial is completed, the due entry must be made in the book of the deceased in accordance with particular law.

1183
1183. § 1 . As far as funerals are concerned, catechumens are equated with the faithful.
§ 2 . The local Ordinary may allow ecclesiastical funerals to be celebrated for those children whose parents wanted to be baptized, but died before receiving baptism.
§ 3 . According to the prudent judgment of the local Ordinary, ecclesiastical funerals may be granted to the baptized who were attached to a non-Catholic Church or ecclesial community, as long as their contrary will is not stated, and their own minister cannot perform them.

1184
1184. § 1 . Ecclesiastical funerals are to be denied, unless before death they had given some sign of repentance:
1 to those notoriously apostates, heretics or schismatics;
2 to those who requested the cremation of his body for reasons contrary to the Christian faith;
3 to the other manifest sinners, to whom ecclesiastical funerals cannot be granted without public scandal among the faithful.
§ 2 . In the event that any doubt arises, you must consult the local Ordinary and abide by his provisions.

1185
1185. Anyone who has been excluded from ecclesiastical funerals will also be denied any funeral Mass.

1186
1186. In order to promote the sanctification of the people of God, the Church recommends to the peculiar and filial veneration of the faithful the Blessed always Virgin Mary, Mother of God, whom Christ constituted Mother of all men; It also promotes the true and authentic worship of the other Saints, with whose example the faithful are edified and with whose intercession they are protected.

1187
1187. It is only licit to venerate with public worship those servants of God who have been included by the authority of the Church in the catalog of Saints or Blesseds.

1188
1188. The use of exposing sacred images in churches for the veneration of the faithful must be firmly preserved; but it must be done in moderate numbers and in proper order, so that they do not cause surprise among the Christian people or give rise to a deviant devotion.

1189
1189. When images exposed to the veneration of the faithful in churches or oratories are to be repaired, which are precious due to their antiquity, their artistic value or the cult paid to them, their restoration will never be carried out without permission from the Ordinary given in writing; and before granting it, he must consult experts.

1190
1190. § 1 . Selling sacred relics is strictly prohibited.
§ 2 . The famous relics as well as those others that enjoy great veneration of the people cannot in any way be validly alienated or transferred in perpetuity without permission from the Apostolic See.
§ 3 . The provisions of the § 2 also apply to those images that, in a church, enjoy great veneration by the people.

1191
1191. § 1 . The vow, that is, the deliberate and free promise made to God of a possible and better good, must be fulfilled by the virtue of religion.
§ 2 . Unless prohibited by law, all those who enjoy the appropriate use of reason are capable of casting a vote.
§ 3 . A vote made due to serious and unjust fear, or due to fraud, is void ipso jure.

1192
1192. § 1 . The vote is public, if it is received by the legitimate Superior on behalf of the Church; Otherwise it is private.
§ 2 . It is solemn, if the Church recognizes it as such; Otherwise it is simple.
§ 3 . It is personal, when an action is promised by the person who issues it; real, when something is promised; mixed, the one that participates in the nature of the personal and real vote.

1193
1193. In itself, the vote is binding only on the person who cast it.

1194
1194. The vote ceases due to the expiration of the predetermined time to fulfill the obligation, due to a substantial change in the subject matter of the promise, due to the condition on which the vote depends not being verified or due to the lack of its final cause, due to dispensation and commutation.

1195
1195. Whoever has power over the matter of the vote may suspend its obligation during the time in which its fulfillment causes harm.

1196
1196. In addition to the Roman Pontiff, they can dispense, with just cause, from private vows, provided that the dispensation does not infringe a right acquired by others:
1 the Ordinary of the place and the parish priest, with respect to all his subjects and also to passers-by;
2 the Superior of a religious institute or of a society of apostolic life, provided that they are clerical and of pontifical right, with regard to members, novices and people who live day and night in a house of the institute or society;
3 those to whom the Apostolic See or the local Ordinary has delegated the power to dispense.

1197
1197. Whoever cast a private vote can commute the promised work for another better or equally good one; and the person who has the power to dispense under c. 1196.

1198
1198. Vows cast before religious profession are suspended as long as the one who cast them remains in the religious institute.

1199
1199. § 1 . The oath, that is, the invocation of the Name of God as a witness to the truth, can only be taken truthfully, sensibly and with justice.
§ 2 . The oath that the canons require or admit cannot be validly taken through a procurator.

1200
1200. §1 . Whoever freely swears that he will do something acquires a peculiar religious obligation to fulfill what he confirmed with an oath.
§ 2 . An oath taken by fraud, violence or serious fear is void ipso iure.

1201
1201. §1 . The promissory oath follows the nature and conditions of the act to which it is attached.
§ 2 . If an act that directly results in harm to others or harm to the public good or eternal salvation is corroborated by oath, the act does not therefore acquire any finality.

1202
1202. The obligation arising from a promissory oath ceases:
1 if the person for whose benefit the oath was sworn condones it;
2 if the subject of the oath substantially changes or, due to the circumstances having changed, it is bad or totally indifferent, or, finally, it prevents a greater good;
3 due to lack of final cause or failure to verify the condition under which the oath was taken;
4 by dispensation or commutation in accordance with c. 1203.

1203
1203. Those who have the power to suspend, dispense or commute a vow, enjoy the same power and for the same reason with respect to the promissory oath; But if the dispensation from the oath results in the detriment of others who refuse to condone the obligation, only the Apostolic See can dispense from that oath.

1204
1204. The oath must be interpreted strictly, according to the law and intention of the one who issues it or, if he acts fraudulently, according to the intention of the person to whom the oath is given.

1205
1205. Sacred places are those that are intended for divine worship or the burial of the faithful through the dedication or blessing prescribed by the liturgical books.

1206
1206. The dedication of a place corresponds to the diocesan Bishop and those who are equivalent to him by law; Such persons may entrust any Bishop or, in exceptional cases, a priest, with the task of carrying out this dedication in their territory.

1207
1207. Sacred places are blessed by the Ordinary; However, the blessing of the churches is reserved to the diocesan Bishop; but both can delegate to a priest.

1208
1208. A record must be drawn up of the dedication or blessing of a church, and also of the blessing of a cemetery; One copy will be kept in the diocesan curia, and another in the church archive.

1209
1209. The dedication or blessing of a place, provided it does not harm anyone, is sufficiently proven by a single witness free from all suspicion.

1210
1210. Only that which favors the exercise and promotion of worship, piety and religion may be admitted in a sacred place, and that which is not in keeping with the sanctity of the place is prohibited. However, the Ordinary may allow, in specific cases, other uses, as long as they are not contrary to the sanctity of the place.

1211
1211. Sacred places are violated when, to the scandal of the faithful, seriously injurious acts are committed there which, in the opinion of the Ordinary of the place, are of such gravity and are so contrary to the sanctity of the place, that worship cannot be exercised there until the injury is repaired by a penitential rite in accordance with the liturgical books.

1212
1212. Sacred places lose their dedication or blessing if they are largely destroyed or if they are permanently reduced to profane uses by decree of the Ordinary or in fact.

1213
1213. The ecclesiastical authority freely exercises its powers and functions in sacred places.

1214
1214. By church is meant a sacred building intended for divine worship, to which the faithful have the right to enter for the celebration, especially public, of divine worship.

1215
1215. § 3, and fulfill the sacred ministries, in accordance with what is established by law.
1215. § 1 . A church cannot be built without the express consent of the diocesan Bishop, given in writing.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop should not give consent unless, after hearing the presbyteral council and the rectors of the neighboring churches, he judges that the new church can serve for the good of souls and that the necessary means will not be lacking to build it and to sustain divine worship in it.
§ 3 . Religious institutes must also obtain permission from the diocesan Bishop before building a church in a fixed and determined place, even if they already had his consent to establish a new house in the diocese or city.

1216
1216. In the construction and repair of churches, taking into account the advice of experts, the principles and norms of the liturgy and sacred art must be observed.

1217
1217. § 1 . Once the construction is properly completed, the new church must be dedicated or at least blessed as soon as possible, according to the liturgical laws.
§ 2 . Churches, especially cathedrals and parish churches, should be dedicated with a solemn rite.

1218
1218. Each church must have its own title, which cannot be changed once the dedication has been made.

1219
1219. In the legitimately dedicated or blessed church all acts of divine worship can be carried out, without prejudice to parish rights.

1220
1220. § 1 . Let all those to whom it corresponds ensure that the churches have the cleanliness and neatness that befits the house of God, and that anything that is not in keeping with the sanctity of the place is avoided.
§ 2 . To protect sacred and precious property, ordinary conservation care and appropriate security measures must be used.

1221
1221. Admission to the church must be free of charge during the time of sacred celebrations.

1222
1222. § 1 . If a church cannot be used in any way for divine worship and there is no possibility of repairing it, it may be reduced by the diocesan Bishop to a non-sordid profane use.
§ 2 . When other serious causes advise that a church should cease to be used for divine worship, the diocesan Bishop, after hearing the presbyteral council, may reduce it to a profane, non-sordid use, with the consent of those who legitimately maintain rights over it, and provided that the good of souls does not suffer any detriment as a result.

1223
1223. The name of oratory is designated a place intended for divine worship with the permission of the Ordinary, for the benefit of a community or group of faithful who go there, to which other faithful may also have access, with the consent of the competent Superior.

1224
1224. § 1 . The Ordinary should not grant the license required to establish an oratory before personally or through someone else visiting the place intended for an oratory and considering it worthily installed.
§ 2 . Once the license is granted, the oratory cannot be used for profane uses without authorization from the Ordinary himself.

1225
1225. In legitimately constituted oratories, all sacred celebrations can be held, except those excepted by law, by prescription of the local Ordinary, or where liturgical norms prevent it.

1226
1226. The name of private chapel is designated a place intended for divine worship, with license from the Ordinary of the place for the benefit of one or more natural persons.

1227
1227. Bishops may have a private chapel, which enjoys the same rights as an oratory.

1228
1228. Without prejudice to what is prescribed in c. 1227, to celebrate Mass or other sacred functions in other private chapels, permission from the local Ordinary is required.

1229
1229. It is appropriate that oratories and private chapels be blessed according to the rite prescribed in the liturgical books; and they must be reserved exclusively for divine worship and left free of any domestic use.

1230
1230. The name of sanctuary is designated a church or other sacred place to which, for a peculiar reason of piety, numerous faithful come on pilgrimage, with the approval of the Ordinary of the place.

1231
1231. The approval of the Episcopal Conference is required for a sanctuary to be called national; and the approval of the Holy See, so that it can be called international.

1232
1232. § 1 . It is the responsibility of the local Ordinary to approve the statutes of a diocesan sanctuary; to the Episcopal Conference, those of a national sanctuary; and only to the Holy See those of an international sanctuary.
§ 2 . The statutes must above all determine the purpose, the authority of the rector, and the ownership and administration of the assets.

1233
1233. Certain privileges may be granted to sanctuaries when the circumstances of the place, the attendance of pilgrims and, above all, the good of the faithful so advise.

1234
1234. § 1 . In the sanctuaries the faithful must be abundantly provided with the means of salvation, diligently preaching the word of God and carefully promoting the liturgical life mainly through the celebration of the Eucharist and penance, and also practicing other approved forms of popular piety.
§ 2 . In the sanctuaries or in adjacent places, votive offerings of popular art and piety should be conserved visibly and safely guarded.

1235
1235. § 1 . The altar, or table on which the Eucharistic Sacrifice is celebrated, is called fixed if it is built in a single piece with the ground, so that it cannot move; and mobile, if it can be moved from place to place.
§ 2 . It is advisable that in all churches there be a fixed altar; and in other places intended for sacred celebrations, the altar can be fixed or mobile.

1236
1236. § 1 . According to the traditional practice of the Church, the fixed altar table must be made of stone, and also made of a single block of natural stone; However, in the opinion of the Episcopal Conference, other worthy and solid material can be used; The columns or the base can be made of any material.
§ 2 . The mobile altar can be made of any solid material, which is in line with liturgical use.

1237
1237. § 1 . The fixed altars must be dedicated, and the mobile ones dedicated or blessed, according to the rites prescribed in the liturgical books.
§ 2 . The ancient tradition of placing relics of Martyrs or other Saints under the fixed altar must be observed, according to liturgical norms.

1238
1238. § 1 . The altar loses its dedication or blessing according to c. 1212.
§ 2 . Due to the reduction of the church or other sacred place to profane uses, fixed or mobile altars do not lose their dedication or blessing.

1239
1239. § 1 . The altar, both fixed and mobile, must be reserved only for divine worship, absolutely excluding any profane use.
§ 2 . No corpse can be buried under the altar; Otherwise, it is not licit to celebrate Mass there.

1240
1240. § 1 . Where possible, the Church should have its own cemeteries, or at least a space in civil cemeteries duly blessed, intended for the burial of the faithful.
§ 2 . If this is not possible, each grave must be blessed individually.

1241
1241. § 1 . Parishes and religious institutes can have their own cemetery.
§ 2 . Other legal entities or families may also have their own cemetery or pantheon, which will be blessed at the discretion of the local Ordinary.

1242
1242. Corpses should not be buried in churches, unless it is the Roman Pontiff or Cardinals or diocesan Bishops, even "emeritus", are buried in their own church.

1243
1243. Appropriate regulations on the operation of cemeteries must be established by private law, especially to protect and highlight their sacred character.

1244
1244. § 1 . It corresponds exclusively to the supreme authority of the Church to establish, transfer or suppress the days of celebrations and days of penitence common to the entire Church, without prejudice to what is established in the c

1245
1245. The right of diocesan Bishops contained in c. 87, with just cause and according to the prescriptions of the diocesan Bishop, the parish priest may grant, in particular cases, dispensation from the obligation to observe a day of celebration or penance, or commute it to other pious works; and the Superior of a religious institute or of a society of apostolic life, if they are clerical of pontifical right, can do the same with respect to his own subjects and others who live day and night in the house.

1246
1246. § 2 .
§ 2 . Diocesan Bishops can designate special days of celebration or penance for their dioceses or places, but only as an act.
1246. § 1 . Sunday, on which the paschal mystery is celebrated, by apostolic tradition must be observed throughout the Church as a primary feast of obligation. Likewise, the days of Christmas, Epiphany, Ascension, Most Holy Body and Blood of Christ, Holy Mary Mother of God, Immaculate Conception and Assumption, Saint Joseph, Holy Apostles Peter and Paul, and, finally, All Saints' Days must be observed.
§ 2 . However, the Episcopal Conference, with prior approval from the Apostolic See, may suppress or transfer some of the holy days of obligation to Sunday.

1247
1247. On Sunday and other holy days of obligation, the faithful have the obligation to participate in Mass; and they will also abstain from those works and activities that prevent them from worshiping God, enjoying the joy of the Lord's Day, or enjoying proper rest of the mind and body.

1248
1248. § 1 . The precept of participating in the Mass is fulfilled by those who attend it, wherever it is celebrated in a Catholic rite, both on the day of the feast and the day before in the afternoon.
§ 2 . When the absence of the sacred minister or another serious cause makes participation in the Eucharistic celebration impossible, it is strongly recommended that the faithful participate in the Liturgy of the Word, whether it is celebrated in the parish church or in another sacred place as prescribed by the diocesan Bishop, or remain in prayer for the appropriate time personally, as a family, or, if appropriate, in family groups.

1249
1249. All the faithful, each in their own way, are obliged by divine law to do penance; However, so that everyone can unite in some common practice of penance, some penitential days have been established, in which the faithful dedicate themselves in a special way to prayer, perform works of piety and charity and deny themselves, fulfilling their own obligations with greater fidelity and, above all, observing fasting and abstinence, in accordance with the canons that follow.

1250
1250. In the universal Church, penitential days and times are every Friday of the year and the season of Lent.

1251
1251. Every Friday, unless they coincide with a solemnity, abstinence from meat or other food determined by the Episcopal Conference must be observed; Fasting and abstinence will be observed on Ash Wednesday and Good Friday.

1252
1252. The law of abstinence obliges those who have reached the age of fourteen; that of fasting, to all those of legal age, until they have reached fifty-nine years. However, pastors of souls and parents should ensure that those who, because they have not reached the age, are not obliged to fast or abstinence are also trained in an authentic spirit of penitence.

1253
1253. The Episcopal Conference can determine in more detail the way to observe fasting and abstinence, as well as replace them in whole or in part with other forms of penance, especially by works of charity and practices of piety.

1254
1254. § 1 . By native law, and independently of civil power, the Catholic Church can acquire, retain, administer and dispose of temporal property to achieve its own purposes.
§ 2 . Its own purposes are mainly the following: to sustain divine worship, to honestly support the clergy and other ministers, and to carry out works of sacred apostolate and charity, especially with those in need.

1255
1255. The universal Church and the Apostolic See, and also the particular Churches and any other legal entity, both public and private, are subjects capable of acquiring, retaining, managing and alienating temporal goods, according to the legal norm.

1256
1256. The ownership of goods corresponds, under the supreme authority of the Roman Pontiff, to the legal person who has legitimately acquired them.

1257
1257. § 1 . All temporal goods that belong to the universal Church, to the Apostolic See or to other public legal entities in the Church, are ecclesiastical goods, and are governed by the canons that follow, as well as by the statutes themselves.
§ 2 . The temporary assets of a private legal entity are governed by its own statutes, and not by these canons, unless otherwise expressly indicated.

1258
1258. In the canons that follow, the name Church is designated not only the universal Church or the Apostolic See, but also any public person in the Church, unless otherwise stated by the context or by the very nature of the matter.

1259
1259. The Church can acquire temporal goods by all just means, of natural or positive law, that are permitted to others.

1260
1260. The Church has the native right to demand from the faithful the goods it needs for its own purposes.

1261
1261. § 1 . The faithful are free to contribute temporal goods in favor of the Church.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop must warn the faithful and urge them in a timely manner about the obligation referred to in c.

1262
1262. Let the faithful help the Church through the subsidies requested of them and according to the norms established by the Episcopal Conference.

1263
1263. To meet the needs of the diocese, the diocesan Bishop has the right to impose a moderate tax on public legal entities subject to his jurisdiction, which is proportionate to their income, after hearing the council of economic affairs and the presbyteral council; With respect to other natural and legal persons, you are only allowed to impose an extraordinary and moderate contribution, in case of serious need and under the same conditions, leaving aside the particular laws and customs that recognize broader rights.

1264
1264. Unless the law provides otherwise, it corresponds to the meeting of Bishops of each province:
1 determine the fees that must be paid for acts of gracious executive power or for the execution of rescripts of the Apostolic See, and that must be approved by the Apostolic See;
2 determine the oblations that are to be made on the occasion of the administration of the sacraments and sacramentals.

1265
1265. § 1 . Without prejudice to the right of mendicant religious, it is prohibited for any private person, both physical and legal, to make inquiries for any pious or ecclesiastical institution or purpose, without written permission from the own Ordinary and the local Ordinary.
§ 2 . Regarding the collection of alms, the Episcopal Conference can dictate norms, which must be observed by everyone, even those who, by institution, call themselves and are mendicants.

1266
1266. In all churches and oratories that are in fact usually open to the faithful, even if they belong to religious institutes, the local Ordinary may order that a special collection be made in favor of certain parish, diocesan, national or universal works, and that it must be diligently sent to the diocesan curia.

1267
1267. § 1 . If the contrary is not stated, oblations delivered to the Superiors or administrators of any ecclesiastical legal entity, even if it is private, are presumed to have been made to the legal entity.
§ 2 . The oblations referred to in § 1 cannot be rejected without just cause, nor things of greater importance without permission from the Ordinary when it comes to a public legal entity; The same license is required to accept those that are encumbered by a modal load or a condition, the provisions of c. 1295.
§ 3 . Oblations made by the faithful for a specific purpose can only be used for that purpose.

1268
1268. Regarding temporal goods, the Church accepts prescription as a way of acquiring or releasing them, in accordance with cc. 197-199.

1269
1269. Sacred things, if they are in the domain of private persons, can be acquired by other private persons, by virtue of the prescription, but it is not lawful to dedicate them to profane uses, unless they have lost the dedication or blessing; If they belong, however, to a public ecclesiastical legal entity, they can only be acquired by another public ecclesiastical legal entity.

1270
1270. Real estate, precious movable property and rights and actions, both personal and real, that belong to the Apostolic See expire within a period of one hundred years; those belonging to another public ecclesiastical legal entity, within a period of thirty years.

1271
1271. By reason of the bond of unity and charity, and according to the possibilities of their diocese, the Bishops contribute to ensuring that the Apostolic See has the means that, according to the different circumstances, it needs for the due service to the universal Church.

1272
1272. In the regions where benefices themselves still exist, it is up to the Episcopal Conference to determine their regime, according to norms established in accordance with the Apostolic See and approved by it, so that the income and even, to the extent possible, the endowment of the benefices themselves, gradually pass to the institution in question in the c

1273
1273. By virtue of his primacy of regime, the Roman Pontiff is the supreme administrator and distributor of all ecclesiastical goods.

1274
1274. § 1 .
1274. § 1 . In every diocese there must be a special institute that collects the goods and oblations to provide according to the c
1274. § 1, unless the founder's will was expressly stated otherwise; Otherwise, they revert to the same legal entity.

1275
1275. The mass of goods coming from different dioceses is administered according to the norms duly agreed upon by the interested Bishops.

1276
1276. § 1 . It is the responsibility of the Ordinary to diligently supervise the administration of all assets belonging to the public legal entities that are subject to him, leaving aside other legitimate titles that confer broader rights.
§ 2 . Taking into account the rights, legitimate customs and circumstances, the Ordinaries should take care to organize everything related to the administration of ecclesiastical property, giving particular instructions within the limits of universal and particular law.

1277
1277. Regarding the performance of administrative acts that, given the economic situation of the diocese, are of greater importance, the diocesan Bishop must hear the council of economic affairs and the college of consultants; but, apart from cases specially determined in universal law or in the founding deed, it requires the consent of the council itself as well as the college of consultants to carry out acts of extraordinary administration. It is up to the Episcopal Conference to determine which acts are to be considered extraordinary administration.

1278
1278. In addition to the functions indicated in the c

1279
1279. § 1 . The administration of ecclesiastical property corresponds to the person who immediately governs the person to whom those property belongs, unless otherwise determined by particular law, statutes or legitimate custom, and the right of the Ordinary to intervene in case of negligence on the part of the administrator is preserved.
§ 2 . For the administration of the assets of a public legal entity that does not have its own administrators by law, by a founding deed, or by its statutes, the Ordinary to whom it is subject will appoint suitable persons for a period of three years; This appointment is renewable.

1280
1280. Every legal entity must have its economic affairs council, or at least two advisors, who, in accordance with the statutes, help the administrator in the fulfillment of his function.

1281
1281. § 1 . The prescriptions of the statutes remaining firm, the administrators invalidly carry out acts that exceed the limits and manner of ordinary administration, unless they have previously obtained written authorization from the Ordinary.
§ 2 . It must be determined in the statutes which acts exceed the limit and method of ordinary administration; and if the statutes do not prescribe anything on this issue, it is up to the diocesan Bishop, after hearing the council on economic affairs, to determine what these acts are for the people who are subject to him.
§ 3 . Unless it has brought a benefit, and to the extent of the same, the legal entity is not obliged to respond for the acts carried out invalidly by the administrators; But for the acts that they carry out illegitimately but validly, the same legal entity will be responsible, without prejudice to its right of action or appeal against the administrators who have caused damage.

1282
1282. All those, clerics or laymen, who participate by legitimate title in the administration of ecclesiastical property, must fulfill their functions in the name of the Church, and in accordance with the law.

1283
1283. Before administrators begin to exercise their function:
1 they must promise by oath before the Ordinary or his delegate that they will administer well and faithfully;
2 an exact and detailed inventory is made, signed by them, of the real estate, of the movable property, both precious and belonging in some way to the cultural heritage, and of any others, with the description and appraisal of the same; and check it once done;
3 keep a copy of this inventory in the administration archive, and another in the Curia archive; Note in both any changes that the assets experience.

1284
1284. § 1 . All administrators are obliged to fulfill their role with the diligence of a good father of a family.
§ 2 . They must therefore:
1 ensure that the assets entrusted to their care do not perish in any way or suffer damage, signing insurance contracts for this purpose, if necessary;
2 ensure that the ownership of ecclesiastical property is ensured by civilly valid means;
3 observe the canonical and civil norms, those imposed by the founder or donor or by legitimate authority, and take care above all that no harm comes to the Church due to non-observance of civil laws;
4 diligently and timely collect the income and proceeds from the assets, safely preserve those already collected and use them according to the intention of the founder or legitimate regulations;
5 punctually pay the interest due on a loan or mortgage, and ensure that the borrowed capital is returned in due time;
6 with the consent of the Ordinary, apply to the purposes of the legal entity the money left over from the payment of expenses and that can be invested productively;
7 keep the entry and exit books in order;
8 prepare administration accounts at the end of each year;
9 duly organize and keep in a convenient and suitable file the documents and instruments on which the rights of the Church or the institute over the property are based; and, where it can be easily done, deposit authentic copies of them in the archive of the curia.
§ 3 . It is strongly advised that administrators budget for inputs and outputs each year; and it is left to individual law to prescribe it and determine in detail the way of presenting it.

1285
1285. Only within the limits of ordinary administration is it lawful for administrators to make donations for purposes of piety or Christian charity with movable property that does not belong to the stable patrimony.

1286
1286. Property administrators:
1 in employment contracts and in accordance with the principles taught by the Church, they must also carefully observe civil laws on labor and social matters;
2 must pay a fair and honest salary to the hired personnel, so that they can conveniently satisfy the personal needs and those of their loved ones.

1287
1287. § 1 . The contrary custom being condemned, the administrators, both clerical and lay, of any ecclesiastical property that is not legitimately exempt from the power of regime of the diocesan Bishop, must render accounts each year to the local Ordinary who will in charge of its review by the council of economic affairs.
§ 2 . The administrators are accountable to the faithful about the goods that they deliver to the Church, according to the norms determined by particular law.

1288
1288. Administrators must not initiate litigation on behalf of a public legal entity, nor respond to the claim in civil jurisdiction, without having obtained written permission from their own Ordinary.

1289
1289. Although they are not obliged to administer by virtue of an ecclesiastical office, administrators cannot abandon the position received at their own discretion; and if damage is caused to the Church by this arbitrary abandonment, they are obliged to make restitution.

1290
1290. What civil law establishes in each territory on contracts, both in general and in particular, and on payments, must be observed with the same effects under canon law in matters subject to the power of regime of the Church, unless it is contrary to divine law or canon law prescribes otherwise, excepting c. 1547.

1291
1291. To validly dispose of assets that by legitimate assignment constitute the stable assets of a public legal entity and whose value exceeds the amount established by law, a license from the competent authority is required in accordance with the law.

1292
1292. § 1 . Remaining safe as prescribed in the c

1293
1293. § 1 . For the disposal of assets, the value of which exceeds the determined minimum amount, the following is also required:
1 just cause, such as an urgent need, an obvious usefulness, piety, charity or another serious pastoral reason;
2 appraisal of the thing to be sold, made by experts and in writing.
§ 2 . To avoid harm to the Church, those other precautions prescribed by legitimate authority must also be observed.

1294
1294. § 1 . Ordinarily, a thing should not be sold for a price lower than that indicated in the appraisal.
§ 2 . The money collected from the alienation must be carefully placed for the benefit of the Church, or spent prudently in accordance with the purposes of said alienation.

1295
1295. The requirements established in ccs. 1291-1294, to which the statutes of legal entities must also be accommodated, must be observed not only in disposals, but also in any operation from which the financial situation of the legal entity may be harmed.

1296
1296. If ecclesiastical property was alienated without due canonical solemnities, but the alienation was civilly valid, it is up to the competent authority, after properly weighing everything, to determine whether or not an action should be brought, and of what type, that is, whether personal or real, and by whom and against whom, to vindicate the rights of the Church.

1297
1297. Taking into account the circumstances of the places, it is up to the Episcopal Conference to establish regulations on the leasing of Church property, and mainly the license that must be obtained from the competent ecclesiastical authority.

1298
1298. Unless the thing has very little value, ecclesiastical property should not be sold or leased to the administrators themselves or to their relatives up to the fourth degree of consanguinity or affinity, without special permission from the competent ecclesiastical authority given in writing.

1299
1299. § 1 . Whoever, by natural and canon law, is capable of freely disposing of his property, may leave it to pious causes, both by act inter vivos and mortis causa.
§ 2 . For mortis causa dispositions for the benefit of the Church, the solemnities prescribed by civil order must be observed, if possible; If these have been omitted, the heirs must be warned about their obligation to comply with the will of the testator.

1300
1300. Once accepted, the wills of the faithful who donate or leave their property for pious causes by acts inter vivos or mortis causa must be carried out with utmost diligence, even as regards the way of administering and investing the property, except as prescribed by the c

1301
1301. § 3 .
1301. § 1 . The Ordinary is the executor of all pious wills, both mortis causa and inter vivos.
§ 2 . By virtue of this right, the Ordinary can and must monitor, also through visits, that pious wills are fulfilled; and the other executors must be accountable to him, once their function has been fulfilled.
§ 3 . The clauses contained in the last wills that are contrary to this right of the Ordinary will be considered not included.

1302
1302. § 1 . Whoever acquired as a trustee property intended for pious causes, whether by inter vivos act or by will, must inform the Ordinary of his trust, giving him an account of all those assets, both movable and immovable, and the attached charges, but if the donor has prohibited this, expressly and totally, he must not accept the trust.
§ 2 . The Ordinary must demand that the goods given in trust be placed safely, and monitor the execution of the pious will in accordance with c. 1301.
§ 3 . When goods have been delivered in trust to a member of a religious institute, or of a society of apostolic life, if they are intended for a place or diocese, or for its inhabitants or to help pious causes, the Ordinary referred to in §§ 1 and 2 is the one of the place; Otherwise, he is the Major Superior in the clerical institute of pontifical right and in the clerical societies of apostolic life of pontifical right, or the Ordinary proper to the same member in the other religious institutes.

1303

1 the autonomous pious foundations, that is, the groups of things destined for the purposes discussed in c.

1304
1304. § 1 . For a legal entity to validly accept a foundation, a written license from the Ordinary is required; license that will not be granted without having legitimately verified that the legal entity can comply with both the new burden and those previously accepted; and he must take care above all that the income fully covers the attached charges, according to the uses of the place or the region.
§ 2 . Private law will determine more specific conditions for the constitution and acceptance of foundations.

1305
1305. The money and movable property assigned as a dowry must be deposited immediately in a safe place approved by the Ordinary, in order to preserve that money or the price of the movable property, and place it as soon as possible, cautiously and usefully, for the benefit of the foundation, with express and detailed mention of the charges, according to the prudent judgment of the Ordinary, after hearing the interested parties and his own advice on economic matters.

1306
1306. § 1 . The foundations, even those made by word of mouth, must be recorded in writing.
§ 2 . A copy of the founding deed will be kept safely in the archive of the curia, and another in the archive of the interested legal entity.

1307
1307. § 1 . In compliance with the prescriptions of cc. 1300-1302 and 1287, a table must be made of the charges of the pious foundations, and placed in a visible place, so that the obligations that must be fulfilled do not fall into oblivion.
§ 2 . In addition to the book referred to in c.

1308
1308. n § 1: The reduction of the burden of Masses, which will only be done for a just and necessary cause, is reserved to the diocesan Bishop or the Superior General of an institute of consecrated life or of a society of clerical apostolic life.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop has the power to reduce the number of Masses to be celebrated by virtue of legacies valid in themselves, when income has decreased and as long as this cause persists, taking into account the stipend legitimately in force in the diocese, provided that there is no one who is obliged and who can be effectively required to increase the alms.
§3 . The same Bishop has the power to reduce the burdens or legacies of Masses that weigh on ecclesiastical institutions, if the income has become insufficient to conveniently achieve the proper purpose of said institution.
§4 . The Superior General of an institute of consecrated life or of a society of clerical apostolic life enjoys the same faculties expressed in §§ 2 and 3.
1308. the power to transfer, for a proportionate reason, the loads of Masses to days, churches or altars other than those that were determined in the foundation.
1308. § 1 . The reduction of Mass charges, which will only be done for just and necessary reasons, is reserved to the Apostolic See, except as indicated below.
§ 2 . If this is expressly indicated in the founding deed, the Ordinary can reduce the burden of Masses due to having reduced income.
§ 3 . The diocesan Bishop has the power to reduce the number of Masses to be celebrated by virtue of legacies or other Titles valid in themselves, when income has decreased and as long as this cause persists, taking into account the stipend legitimately in force in the diocese, provided that there is no one who is obliged and who can be effectively required to increase the alms.
§ 4 . The same Bishop has the power to reduce the burdens or legacies of Masses that weigh on ecclesiastical institutions, if the income has become insufficient to conveniently achieve the proper purpose of said institution.
§ 5 . The Superior General of a clerical religious institute of pontifical right enjoys the same powers expressed in §§ 3 and 4.

1309
1309. It is also the responsibility of the authorities mentioned in the c

1310
1310. n § 1 . The Ordinary may reduce, moderate or commute the will of the faithful on pious causes, only for just and necessary cause, after hearing the interested parties, and his own council on economic matters and respecting in the best possible way the will of the founder.
§ 2 . In other cases, you must turn to the Apostolic See.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Competentias quasdam decernere of February 11, 2022)]:
1310. § 1 . If the founder expressly granted the Ordinary the power to reduce, moderate or commute the will of the faithful for pious causes, he can do so only for just and necessary cause.
§ 2 . If the fulfillment of the charges becomes impossible, due to a decrease in income or for any other reason, through no fault of the administrators, the Ordinary may equitably reduce those charges, after hearing the interested parties and his own council of economic affairs, and respecting in the best possible way the will of the founder; The reduction of Masses is excepted, however, and is governed by the prescriptions of c. 1308.
§ 3 . In other cases, you must turn to the Apostolic See.

1311
1311. § 1 . The Church has original right and 
proper to punish with criminal sanctions the faithful who have 
committed crimes.
§ 2 . Whoever presides in the Church must guard and promote the 
good of the community itself and of each of the faithful with the 
pastoral charity, the example of life, advice and 
exhortation, and, if necessary, also with the imposition or the 
declaration of penalties, in accordance with the precepts of the law, which 
must always be applied with canonical equity, and keeping in mind 
the restoration of justice, the amendment of the prisoner and the 
reparation of the scandal.

1312
1312. § 1 . The criminal sanctions in the Church are:
1st medicinal penalties or censures, which are indicated in ccs. 
1331-1333;
2nd expiatory penalties, which are discussed in c. 1336.
§ 2 . The law may establish other expiatory penalties, which deprive 
to a faithful of some spiritual or temporal good, and are in 
conformity with the supernatural purpose of the Church.
§ 3 . Penal remedies and penances are also used, indicated 
in the cc. 1339 and 1340: those, above all, to prevent 
crimes; these, rather, to apply instead of a penalty, or 
to increase it.

1313
1313. § 1 . If the law changes after 
committed a crime, the most favorable law for the crime must be applied 
prisoner
§ 2 . If a later law repeals a previous one or, at least, 
abolishes the penalty, it ceases immediately.

1314
1314. The penalty is ordinarily ferendae 
sententiae, so that it only obliges the prisoner since he has 
been imposed; but it is latae sententiae if the law or the 
precept they establish it expressly, so that it incurs ipso 
fact in it who commits the crime.

1315
1315. § 1 . Who has the power to make laws 
criminals, can also protect with a suitable penalty a law 
divine.
§ 2 . The lower legislator, keeping in mind c. 1317, may, 
furthermore:
1st protect with a suitable penalty the law promulgated by a 
higher authority, respecting the limits of its competence 
reason for the territory or the people;
2nd add other penalties to those already established by universal law 
against some crime;
3. determine or make mandatory the penalty that in universal law 
is established as indeterminate or optional.
§ 3 . The law may determine the penalty or leave its determination to 
the judge's prudent estimate.

1316
1316. Let the diocesan Bishops take care that, in the 
As far as possible, criminal laws are given uniformly 
for the same State or region.

1317
1317. Penalties must be established only in the 
to the extent that they are truly necessary to better provide for the 
ecclesiastical discipline. Expulsion from the clerical state cannot 
be established by the lower legislator.

1318
1318. Latae penalties should not be established 
sententiae, if not against some intentional crimes 
special ones that may cause a more serious scandal, or cannot 
be effectively punished with ferendae sententiae penalties; and not 
censures must be established, especially excommunication, if it is not 
with maximum moderation, and only against crimes of special 
gravity.

1319
1319. § 1 . To the extent that someone, by virtue of 
its power of regime, can impose precepts in the external forum 
according to the provisions of the ccs. 48-58, may also require 
by precept with determined penalties, except expiatory ones 
perpetual
§ 2 . If, after diligent reflection, a precept has to be imposed 
penal, observe what is established in cc. 1317 and 1318.

1320
1320. In everything that religious people depend on 
Ordinary of the place, he can punish them with penalties.

1321
1321. § 1 . Every person is considered innocent 
until proven otherwise.
§ 2 . No one can be punished unless the external violation 
of a law or precept that he has committed is seriously attributable to him 
out of fraud or guilt.
§ 3 . Whoever 
deliberately infringed them; who did it by omission of the 
due diligence, should not be punished, unless the law or the 
precept provide otherwise.
§ 4 . Once the external infraction has been committed, imputability is presumed, 
unless stated otherwise.

1322
1322. They are considered incapable of committing a crime 
those who habitually lack the use of reason, although they have 
violated a law or precept when they appeared to be healthy.

1323
1323. He is not subject to any penalty who, when 
violated a law or precept:
1st he had not yet turned sixteen years old;
2. he was blamelessly unaware that he was violating a law or precept; and 
Inadvertence and error are equated with ignorance;
3rd he acted by violence, or by chance that he could not foresee or that 
once foreseen, could not avoid;
4th he acted under coercion out of serious fear, even if it was only 
relatively, or out of necessity or to avoid serious harm, to 
unless the act was intrinsically evil or resulted in harm to 
the souls;
5. acted in legitimate defense against an unjust aggressor of himself 
or from another, maintaining due moderation;
6. lacked the use of reason, without prejudice to what is prescribed in 
the ccs. 1324, § 1 , 2nd, and 1326, § 1 , 4th;
7th judged without fault that any of the circumstances occurred 
indicated in nos. 4th or 5th.
1323. and 1324, gross, supine or ignorant ignorance can never be taken into account. 
affected.

1324
1324. § 1 . The offender is not exempt from the penalty, 
but the penalty established in the law or in the precept must be mitigated, 
or employ a penance in its place, when the crime has been 
task:
1. by someone who had only imperfect use of reason;
2. by someone who lacked the use of reason due to drunkenness or other 
similar disturbance of the mind, of which he was guilty, 
the provisions of c. 1326, § 1 , 4th;
3rd by serious impulse of passion, but which did not precede, 
preventing them, to any deliberation of the mind and consent 
of the will, provided that the passion had not been 
voluntarily provoked or encouraged;
4.º by a minor, who has reached the age of sixteen;
5. by someone who acted under coercion due to serious fear, even if it were coerced 
only relatively, or out of necessity or to avoid harm 
serious, if the crime is intrinsically bad or results in harm to the 
souls;
6. by whom he acted in legitimate defense against an unjust aggressor of 
himself or another, but without maintaining due moderation;
7. against him who seriously and unjustly provokes;
8. by whom he mistakenly but culpably judged that there was some 
of the circumstances indicated in c. 1323, nos. 4 or 5;
9.º by those who, without fault, were unaware that the law or the precept carried 
attaches a penalty;
10. by someone who acted without full imputability, provided that this 
remains serious.
§ 2 . The judge can do the same, if any other 
circumstance that reduces the seriousness of the crime.
§ 3 . Under the circumstances listed in § 1, the prisoner does not 
is bound by latae sententiae penalties, but, with the 
In order to obtain your amendment or to repair the scandal, you can 
More lenient penalties may be imposed or penances may be applied.

1325
1325. When applying the prescriptions of the cc

1326
1326. § 1 . The judge must punish more seriously 
than that established in the law or in the precept:
1. to whom, after a conviction or sentence of sentence, continues 
committing a crime in such a way that, due to the circumstances, 
prudently infer his persistence in ill will;
2nd to someone who is constituted in some dignity, or who abused his 
authority or office to commit the crime;
3rd to whom, when a penalty has been established for a crime 
guilty, he foresaw what was to happen, and yet he omitted the 
precautions to avoid it that any person would have used 
diligent;
4th to anyone who has committed a crime while intoxicated or otherwise 
disturbance of the mind, which has been caused 
intentionally to commit or excuse the crime, or out of passion 
voluntarily excited or encouraged.
§ 2 . In the cases discussed in § 1, if the penalty 
established is latae sententiae, another penalty can be added or 
penitence.
§ 3 . In the same cases, if the penalty is established as 
optional becomes mandatory.

1327
1327. In addition to the cases discussed in the 
cc. 1323-1326, the particular law may establish other 
exonerating, mitigating or aggravating circumstances, both as a rule 
general as for a particular crime. Likewise, they can 
establish in the precept circumstances that exempt from the penalty 
established by it, reduce it or aggravate it.

1328
1328. § 1 . Whoever did or omitted something to commit a crime 
crime, but, regardless of his will, he did not come to 
consummate it, is not subject to the penalty established against the crime 
consummated, unless the law or precept provides otherwise.
§ 2 . If the acts or omissions lead by their very nature to 
execution of the crime, the perpetrator may be punished with a 
penance or penal remedy, unless, once the punishment has begun 
commission of the crime, he would have voluntarily desisted from it. 
But, if there had been a scandal or other serious damage or danger, the 
author, even if he had voluntarily desisted, can be punished 
with a fair penalty, but always less than that established for the 
consummated crime.

1329
1329. § 1 . Those with the same criminal intent 
participate in the commission of a crime, and are not mentioned 
expressly in the law or precept because the 
ferendae sententiae penalties against the main author, remain 
subject to the same penalties, or others of the same or lesser 
gravity.
§ 2 . Accomplices not mentioned in the law or in the precept incur 
in the latae sententiae penalty corresponding to a crime, 
provided that this would not have been committed without their help and the penalty is 
of such a nature that it can also affect them; in case 
Otherwise, they may be punished with ferendae sententiae penalties.

1330
1330. The crime that 
consists of a declaration or other manifestation of the will, 
doctrine or knowledge, if no one perceives such declaration or 
manifestation.

1331
1331. § 1 . The excommunicated person is prohibited from:
1st the celebration of the Eucharistic Sacrifice and the others 
sacraments;
2nd receive the sacraments;
3rd administer the sacramentals and celebrate the other ceremonies of 
liturgical worship;
4th having any active part in the celebrations previously 
enumerated;
5. Perform offices, positions, ministries and functions 
ecclesiastics;
6th carry out acts of regime.
§ 2 . When the ferendae sententiae excommunication has been 
imposed or the latae sententiae has been declared, the prisoner:
1. If you want to act against what is prescribed in the § 1, 
1st-4th, must be rejected or the liturgical ceremony must cease, 
unless a serious cause obstructs;
2. invalidly performs the acts of the regime, which according to the § 1, 
6.º, they are illegal;
3. You are prohibited from enjoying the privileges that you previously received. 
would have been granted;
4.º does not acquire the remuneration that is merely entitled 
ecclesiastical;
5th is ineligible to obtain offices, positions, ministries, functions, 
rights, privileges and honorary titles.

1332. § 1 . Whoever is in question is subject to

§ 2 . Law or precept, however, may define the 
interdicted in such a way that the prisoner is prohibited only from certain actions

1333
1333. § 1 . The suspension prohibits:
1. all or some of the acts of the power of order;
2. all or some of the acts of the power of the regime;
3. the exercise of all or some rights or functions 
inherent to a job.
§ 2 . In the law or in the precept it can be established that, after 
of the sentence or decree that imposes or declares the penalty, not 
the suspended person can validly carry out regime acts.
§ 3 . The ban never affects:
1. to the offices or power of the regime that are not under the

2nd to the right of habitation that the prisoner has by reason of his

3rd to the right to manage the assets that may belong to the

§ 4 . The suspension that prohibits receiving the fruits, salary, 
pensions or other remuneration, carries with it the obligation to 
to restore what had been received illegitimately, even in good 
faith.

1334
1334. § 1 . Within the limits established in the 
preceding canon, the scope of the suspension is determined either by the

imposes the penalty.
§ 2 . The law, but not the precept, can establish a suspension
latae sententiae without adding any determination or limit: 
Such punishment produces all the effects listed in c. 1333, § 1 .

1335
1335. § 1 . The competent authority, when imposing or 
declare censorship in judicial process or by decree

deemed necessary to restore justice or repair the 
scandal.
§ 2 . If censorship prohibits celebrating the sacraments or

prohibition how many times a faithful asks for a sacrament or sacramental or
