just cause.

1336
1336. § 1 . In addition to others that could be established by the 
law, expiatory penalties, likely to affect the offender 
perpetually or for a determined or indeterminate time, are the 
that are indicated in §§ 2 -5.
§ 2 . The mandate:
1. to reside in a certain place or territory;
2nd of paying a pecuniary fine, that is, a sum of money to 
the purposes of the Church, according to the norms determined by the 
Episcopal Conference.
§ 3 . The prohibition:
1. to reside in a certain place or territory;
2nd to perform, in any place or in a certain place or 
territory or outside them, any or some offices, positions, 
ministries or functions, or only some activities inherent to the 
offices or positions;
3. to carry out any or some acts of power of order;
4. to carry out any or some acts of regime power;
5th to exercise any right or privilege, or to use insignia or 
titles;
6th to enjoy active or passive voice in canonical elections, or 
to take part with the right to vote in councils or colleges 
ecclesiastical;
7th to wear the ecclesiastical dress or the religious habit.
§ 4 . The deprivation:
1st of all or some offices, positions, ministries or functions, 
or only of some activities inherent to the trades or the 
charges;
2nd of the power to hear confessions or the power to preach;
3rd of the delegated power of regime;
4.º of any right or privilege or insignia or title;
5th of all or part of the ecclesiastical remuneration, 
according to the norms established by the Episcopal Conference, remaining 
subject to the provisions of Can. 1350, § 1.
§ 5 . Expulsion from the clerical state.

1337
1337. § 1 . The prohibition of residing in a 
certain place or territory can be imposed on both clerics 
as to the religious; the mandate to reside, to the clerics 
seculars, and, within the limits of their constitutions, to the 
religious.
§ 2 . To impose the requirement to reside in a certain 
place or territory, the consent of the Ordinary of 
that place, unless it is a house intended for them to do 
penitence or correction of extradiocesan clergy.

1338
1338. § 1 . The expiatory penalties listed in 
the c. 1336, never affect the powers, offices, positions, 
rights, privileges, faculties, graces, titles or insignia that 
They are not under the power of the Superior who establishes the penalty.
§ 2 . The deprivation of the power of order cannot occur, but 
only the prohibition to exercise this power or some of its acts; 
nor can there be the deprivation of academic degrees.
§ 3 . Regarding the prohibitions indicated in c. 1336, § 3 , has been 
to follow the rule established for censorship in c. 1335, 
§ 2 .
§ 4 . Only expiatory penalties can be latae sententiae 
indicated as prohibitions in c. 1336, § 3, or others that 
perhaps they have been established by law or precept.
§ 5 . The prohibitions discussed in c. 1336, § 3 , 
They are never under penalty of nullity.

1339
1339. § 1 . The Ordinary may, personally or by 
through another, admonish him who is on the next occasion 
of committing a crime, or about which, after carrying out a 
investigation, there is serious suspicion that he has committed a crime.
§ 2 . The Ordinary may reprimand, in a manner proportionate to the 
circumstances of the person and the fact, to the one who provokes with his 
scandalous conduct or serious disturbance of order.
§ 3 . There must always be a record of the warning and the 
reprimand, at least by some document preserved in the 
secret archive of the curia.
§ 4 . If someone, once or several times, has had 
useless reprimands or reprimands, or if it cannot be expected 
effect of them, the Ordinary must give a penal precept, in which 
dictate exactly what is to be done or avoided.
§ 5 . If the severity of the case requires it, and especially if 
someone is in danger of repeating a crime, the 
Ordinary, in addition to the penalties imposed or declared by sentence 
or decree in accordance with law, also subject it to surveillance, of the 
manner determined by singular decree.

1340
1340. § 1 . Penance, which can be imposed in the 
external forum, consists of carrying out some work of religion, of 
piety or charity.
§ 2 . Never impose public penance for a transgression 
hidden.
§ 3 . According to his prudence, the Ordinary may add penances to the 
criminal remedy of reprimand or reprimand.

1341
1341. The Ordinary must promote the procedure 
judicial or administrative to impose or declare penalties when there is 
given that not even the means of pastoral care, especially 
fraternal correction, neither reprimand nor rebuke are enough 
to restore justice, obtain the amendment of the prisoner and 
repair the scandal.

1342
1342. § 1 . When just causes make it difficult to make a 
judicial process, the penalty may be imposed or declared by decree 
extrajudicial, observing c. 1720, especially so 
regards the right of defense and moral certainty in the spirit of 
who gives the decree according to c. 1608. In any case, the 
Penal remedies and penances can be applied through 
decree.
§ 2 . Life sentences cannot be imposed or declared by decree, 
nor those others that the law or precept that establishes them 
prohibits applying by decree.
§ 3 . What is prescribed in the law or in the precept about the 
judge, regarding the imposition or declaration of a sentence in court, 
It also applies to the Superior who imposes or declares a penalty by means of 
extrajudicial decree, unless otherwise stated and it is not 
of prescriptions that refer only to the procedure.

1343
1343. If the law or precept gives him the power to 
whether or not to apply a penalty, the judge, subject to the provisions of the 
c. 1326, § 3, define the case, according to your conscience and prudence, 
in accordance with what the restoration of justice demands, the amendment 
of the prisoner and the reparation of the scandal; The judge, however, can 
Also in these cases, if appropriate, mitigate the penalty or impose 
place a penance.

1344
1344. Although the law uses prescriptive words, 
The judge may, according to his conscience and prudence:
1. Defer the imposition of the sentence to a more opportune time, if 
foresee greater evils due to the hasty punishment of the prisoner, unless 
urge the need to repair the scandal;
2. abstain from imposing the penalty, or impose a more lenient or 
a penance, if the prisoner has made amends and has repaired the scandal 
and the damage perhaps caused, or whether it has already been sufficiently punished 
by civil authority or is anticipated to be so;
3rd suspend the obligation to observe an expiatory penalty if 
It is the first crime committed by the prisoner who until then 
would have lived without blemish, and there was no pressing need to repair the 
scandal, so that, if the prisoner commits another crime within a 
term determined by the same judge, serves the sentence due for the 
crimes unless, in the meantime, the time had elapsed 
necessary for the prescription of the criminal action by the first 
crime.
1344. and 1345.
§ 2 . The promoter of justice may appeal whenever he considers that sufficient provision has not been made to repair the scandal or to restore justice.

1345
1345. Whenever the offender had only use 
imperfect of reason, or would have committed the crime out of necessity, 
or due to grave fear or impulse of passion, or, except as prescribed in 
the c. 1326, § 1, 4th, due to drunkenness or other similar disturbance 
of the mind, the judge can also refrain from imposing punishment 
someone if they consider that in another way it is possible to achieve better 
your amendment; but the prisoner must be punished if he would not otherwise be 
possible to provide for the restoration of justice and reparation 
of the scandal perhaps caused.

1346
1346. § 1 . Ordinarily there must be so many penalties 
How many crimes are there?
§ 2 . But, when a prisoner has committed several crimes, if it seems 
excessive, the accumulation of ferendae sententiae penalties is 
the prudent discretion of the judge to temper the sentences within a few 
equitable limits and subject it to surveillance.

1347
1347. § 1 . A cannot validly be imposed 
censure if the prisoner has not previously been reprimanded at least once to 
to cease in his contumacy, giving him a reasonable time for the 
amendment.
§ 2 . It is considered that the prisoner who is 
has truly repented of the crime, and has also repaired 
conveniently scandal and damage or, at least, has promised 
Seriously do it.

1348
1348. When the accused is acquitted of the accusation, or 
no penalty is imposed on him, the Ordinary can look after his well-being and 
the public good with appropriate warnings or other means of its 
pastoral request, or also, if appropriate, with penal remedies.

1349
1349. If the penalty is indeterminate and the law does not 
provides otherwise, the judge, when determining the sentences, chooses those that 
are proportionate to the scandal caused and the seriousness of the damage; 
but it should not impose the most serious penalties unless required 
absolutely the seriousness of the case; and cannot impose penalties 
perpetual

1350
1350. § 1 . When imposing penalties on a clergyman, it must be 
always take care that it does not lack what is necessary for its honest 
support, unless it involves expulsion from the state 
clerical
§ 2 . However, let the Ordinary try to provide in the best way 
possible to the needs of those who, having been expelled from the state 
clerical, is in a state of true indigence for reasons 
of that penalty, excluding the granting of an office, ministry or 
charge.

1351
1351. The punishment binds the offender in every place, also 
when the right of the person who constituted, imposed or declared 
the penalty, unless otherwise expressly provided.

1352
1352. § 1 . If the penalty prohibits receiving sacraments or 
sacramentals, the prohibition is suspended throughout the 
time in which the prisoner is in danger of death.
§ 2 . The obligation to 
observe a latae sententiae penalty, which has not been 
declared nor is it notorious in the place where the prisoner is located, in 
to the extent that he cannot observe it without danger of serious 
scandal or infamy.

1353
1353. The appeal or the 
appeal against judicial rulings or decrees that impose or 
declare any penalty.

1354
1354. § 1 . In addition to those listed in cc. 
1355-1356, all those who can dispense from a criminal law, or 
exempt from a precept in which a penalty is imposed, they can 
also remit that penalty.
§ 2 . The law or precept, which establishes a penalty, can also 
grant others the power to refer it.
§ 3 . If the Apostolic See reserved to itself, or to others, the 
remission of a sentence, the reservation must be strictly interpreted.

1355
1355. § 1 . They can remit a penalty established by 
law, if it is a ferendae sententiae penalty already imposed 
or of a latae sententiae penalty already declared and provided that 
is not reserved for the Apostolic See:
1st the Ordinary who promoted the trial to impose or declare the 
penalty, or imposed or declared it by decree personally or by 
middle of another;
2nd the Ordinary of the place where the offender is, 
after having consulted, however, the Ordinary of whom 
deals in no. 1st, unless this is impossible due to 
extraordinary circumstances.
§ 2 . They can remit a penalty established by law, if it is 
a latae sententiae penalty not yet declared and as long as 
that is not reserved for the Apostolic See:
1st the Ordinary to his subjects;
2nd the Ordinary of the place also to those who are in his 
territory or had committed a crime there;
3rd any Bishop, but only within sacramental confession.

1356
1356. § 1 . They can remit a ferendae or latae penalty 
sententiae established by precept that has not been given by 
the Apostolic See:
1. the author of the precept;
2nd the Ordinary who promoted the trial to impose or declare the 
penalty, or imposed or declared it by decree personally or by 
middle of another;
3rd the Ordinary of the place where the offender is located.
§ 2 . Unless it is impossible due to circumstances 
extraordinary, before proceeding with the referral you must consult 
to the one who gave the precept or to the one who imposed or declared the penalty.

1357
1357. § 1 . Without prejudice to the requirements of 
the ccs. 508 and 976, the confessor can refer in the internal forum 
sacramental censure latae sententiae of excommunication or 
I question that it has not been declared, if it is difficult for the penitent 
remain in a state of serious sin for as long as 
necessary for the competent Superior to provide.
§ 2 . When granting remission, the confessor must impose on the 
penitent the obligation to appeal within a period of one month, under penalty 
of recidivism, to the competent Superior or to a priest who has 
that power, and to abide by its mandates; meanwhile, impose a 
convenient penance and, to the extent that this is urgent, the 
reparation for scandal and damage; the appeal can be made 
also through the confessor, without indicating the name of the penitent.
§ 3 . They have the same duty to appeal, when the 
danger, who, according to c. 976, were acquitted of a censure 
imposed or declared, or reserved to the Apostolic See.

1358
1358. § 1 . Referral can only be granted for a 
censures the offender who has ceased in his contumacy, in accordance with the 
c. 1347, § 2 ; but it cannot be denied to someone who has ceased in his 
contumacy, leaving aside what is prescribed in c. 1361, § 4 .
§ 2 . Whoever sends a censure can provide according to c. 1348, or 
also impose a penance.

1359
1359. If someone is subject to several penalties, the 
referral is valid only for those that are expressed in it; 
but the general remission forgives all penalties, except 
those that the offender omitted in bad faith in the request.

1360
1360. The remission of a sentence is ipso iure invalid 
obtained through violence, serious fear or fraud.

1361
1361. § 1 . Referral may also be granted to 
who is absent, or under condition.
§ 2 . The referral in the external forum must be granted in writing, 
unless a serious cause advises otherwise.
§ 3 . The request for referral or the referral is not disclosed 
itself, except to the extent that this is useful for the good reputation 
of the prisoner, or necessary to repair the scandal.
§ 4 . Referral should not be given until, according to the prudent 
discretion of the Ordinary, the prisoner may have repaired the damage 
caused; and you may be urged to make that reparation or restitution for 
means one of the penalties listed in Can. 1336, §§ 2 -4, which 
which also applies when the censure is sent in accordance with Can. 1358 , § 1 .

1362
1362. § 1 . The criminal action is extinguished by 
prescription after three years, unless it is:
1st of the crimes reserved for the Congregation for the Doctrine of 
faith, which are subject to special rules;
2nd, the provisions of n. 1st, of the action by the 
crimes dealt with in cc. 1376, 1377, 1378, 1393, § 1 , 
1394, 1395, 1397 and 1398, § 2, which prescribes after seven years, or 
well as the action for the crimes discussed in Can. 1398, § 1, which prescribes after twenty years;
3rd of crimes not punished by common law, if the law 
individual determines another period for the prescription.
§ 2 . The time for prescription, unless established 
otherwise in the law, it begins to be counted from the day on which 
the crime was committed, or, when it is a continuing crime or 
usual, from the day on which it ceased.
§ 3 . The defendant was cited in accordance with c. 1723 or informed of the way 
provided for in c. 1507, § 3, of the presentation of the document 
accusatory pursuant to c. 1721, § 1, the 
prescription of criminal action; after this period or interrupted 
the suspension due to the cessation of criminal proceedings, again 
time runs out for the prescription, which is added to the already 
elapsed. This suspension also applies if, in compliance with the 
c. 1720, 1st, proceeds for the imposition or declaration of 
the penalty by extrajudicial decree.

1363
1363. § 1 . The action to execute the sentence is 
expires by prescription if within the deadlines established in the 
c. 1362, computed from the day on which the conviction 
becomes res judicata, the prisoner has not been notified of the decree 
execution of the judge, which is discussed in c. 1651.
§ 2 . The same applies, with due differences, when the penalty 
It is imposed by extrajudicial decree.

1364
1364. § 1 . The apostate of the faith, the heretic or the 
schismatic incur latae sententiae excommunication, remaining 
sign what is prescribed in c. 194, § 1 , 2nd; can be punished 
also with the penalties listed in c. 1336, §§ 2 -4.
§ 2 . If required by prolonged contumacy or the severity of the 
scandal, other penalties may be added, without excepting expulsion 
of the clerical state.

1365
1365. Who, outside the case dealt with in c. 1364, § 
1, teaches a doctrine condemned by the Roman Pontiff or by a 
Ecumenical Council or stubbornly rejects the doctrine described in 
the c. 750, § 2, or in c. 752, and, admonished by the Apostolic See 
or by the Ordinary, does not recant, be punished with a censure and 
with the deprivation of the office; Other sanctions can be added to these sanctions 
of those listed in c. 1336, §§ 2 -4.

1366
1366. Whoever resorts to the Ecumenical Council or the 
College of Bishops against an act of the Roman Pontiff, must be 
punished with censure.

1367
1367. The parents, or those who take their place, who 
They give their children to be baptized or educated in a 
acatholic religion, must be punished with censure or other punishment 
fair

1368
1368. Whoever, in a public spectacle or meeting, 
in a disseminated writing, or in any other way by the media 
social communication, utters a blasphemy, seriously insults 
against good customs, insults religion or the Church or 
stirs up hatred or contempt against them must be punished with a 
just penalty.

1369
1369. Whoever desecrates a sacred thing, furniture or 
property, must be punished with a fair penalty.

1370
1370. § 1 . Who physically attacks the Roman 
Pontiff, incurs latae sententiae excommunication reserved for 
the Apostolic See; if it is a clergyman, another can be added 
penalty, taking into account the seriousness of the crime, without excluding expulsion 
of the clerical state.
§ 2 . Who does the same against who has the character 
episcopal, incurs a latae sententiae interdict, and, if it is 
cleric, also in suspension latae sententiae.
§ 3 . Whoever uses physical violence against a clergyman or religious, 
or against another faithful, in contempt of the faith, the Church, the 
ecclesiastical or ministry power, must be punished with a 
just penalty.

1371
1371. § 1 . Whoever disobeys the Apostolic See, 
Ordinary or Superior when they legitimately command or prohibit something, 
and persists in his disobedience after having been admonished, 
must be punished according to the seriousness of the case with a censure, with 
deprivation of office or with other penalties listed in c. 
1336, §§ 2 -4.
§ 2 . Who violates the obligations that have been imposed on him 
as a consequence of a penalty, must be punished with penalties of the 
listed in c. 1336, §§ 2 -4.
§ 3 . If someone commits perjury by stating or promising something before 
an ecclesiastical authority, must be punished with a just penalty.
§ 4 . Who violates the obligation to keep the pontifical secret 
must be punished with penalties listed in c. 1336, §§ 
2 -4.
§ 5 . Whoever has not fulfilled the duty to execute the sentence 
execution, or the enforceable criminal decree, must be punished with 
a fair penalty, without excluding censorship.
§ 6 . Whoever omits the communication of the news of the crime, to the 
who was bound by canon law, must be punished according to the 
Can. 1336, §§ 2 -4, with the addition of other penalties depending on severity 
of the crime.

1372
1372. They must be punished according to c. 1336, §§ 2 -4:
1. those who impede the freedom of the ministry, or the exercise of the 
ecclesiastical power, or the legitimate use of sacred things or 
ecclesiastical property, or intimidate anyone who exercised power or 
ecclesiastical ministry;
2nd those who prevent the freedom of choice or coerce the 
elector or the chosen one.

1373
1373. Whoever publicly arouses aversion or hatred 
hatred against the Apostolic See or the Ordinary, because of some act 
of office or ecclesiastical office, or induces disobedience to them, must 
be punished with interdict or other just penalties.

1374. Whoever registers in an association that 
machine against the Church must be punished with a just penalty;

interdicted

ecclesiastic must be punished with a just penalty.
§ 2 . Illegitimate retention after

1376
1376. § 1 . He must be punished with penalties of the 
listed in the Can. 1336, §§ 2 -4, the obligation to 
repair the damage:
1st who steals ecclesiastical property or prevents it from being received 
its fruits;
2nd who, without the prescribed consultation, consensus or license, or 
either without any other requirement imposed by law for the validity or 
for legality, alienates ecclesiastical property or carries out acts of 
administration over them.
§ 2 . Be punished with a just penalty, without excluding deprivation 
of the office, the obligation to repair the damage remains firm:
1st who through his own serious fault has committed the crime of which 
deals with § 1, 2nd;
2nd who has otherwise been seriously negligent in the 
administration of ecclesiastical assets.

1377
1377. § 1 . He who gives or promises things, so that whoever 
exercises an office or function in the Church does or omits something 
illegitimately, must be punished with a just penalty according to c. 
1336, §§ 2 -4; and likewise whoever accepts those gifts or promises must 
be punished according to the seriousness of the crime, without excluding deprivation 
of the office, the obligation to repair the damage remains firm.
§ 2 . Whoever, in the exercise of his office or position, requests a 
offer higher than what is established or added sums or something in itself 
benefit is punished with an appropriate financial fine or with 
other penalties, without excluding the deprivation of the office, remaining firm 
obligation to repair the damage.

1378
1378. § 1 . Who, apart from the cases already provided 
by law, abuses ecclesiastical power, office or 
charge must be punished according to the seriousness of the act or 
omission, without excluding the deprivation of the office or position, remaining 
sign the obligation to repair the damage.
§ 2 . Whoever, through culpable negligence, performs or omits 
illegitimately, and with harm to others or scandal, an act of power 
ecclesiastical, office or position, must be punished with a 
just penalty according to c. 1336, §§ 2 -4, the obligation to 
repair the damage.

1379
1379. § 1 . Incurs latae sententiae penalty 
of interdict or, if it is a clergyman, also of suspension:
1st who, without having been promoted to the priestly order, pays attention 
perform the liturgical action of the Eucharistic Sacrifice;
2nd who, outside the case discussed in c. 1384, not being able

penalties, according to the seriousness of the crime, without excommunication.

The clergyman can also be punished with expulsion from the state 
clerical
§ 4 . He who deliberately administers a sacrament to those 
are prohibited from receiving it should be punished with suspension, 
that other penalties than those listed in c. 1336, §§ 
2 -4.
§ 5 . Who, outside of the cases discussed in the §§ 1 -4 
and in c. 1384, simulates the administration of a sacrament, must be

1380
1380. Whoever celebrates or receives a sacrament with 
simony, must be punished with interdict or suspension or with penalties 
of those listed in c. 1336, §§ 2 -4.

1381
1381. The accused of communicatio in sacris 
prohibited must be punished with a just penalty.

1382
1382. § 1 . Who throws the species to the ground 
consecrated, or taken or retained for a purpose 
sacrilegious, incurs latae sententiae excommunication reserved for

punishment, without excluding expulsion from the clerical state.
§ 2 . The guilty of consecration, with a sacrilegious purpose, of a

1383
1383. Whoever illegitimately obtains a profit with the 
stipend of the Mass, must be punished with a censure or with

1384
1384. The priest who acts against what is prescribed in 
the c. 977, incurs latae sententiae excommunication reserved for

1385
1385. The priest who, during confession, or with 
occasion or pretext thereof, requests the penitent to commit a sin against 
the sixth commandment of the Decalogue, must be punished, according to the 
seriousness of the crime, with suspension, prohibitions or deprivations; and, 
in the most serious cases, he must be expelled from the clerical state.

1386
1386. § 1 . The confessor who directly violates the 
sacramental secrecy, incurs latae sententiae excommunication 
reserved for the Apostolic See; who violates it only indirectly, 
It must be punished in proportion to the seriousness of the crime.
§ 2 . The interpreter and those others, which are discussed in c. 
983, § 2, if they violate the secrecy, they must be punished with a penalty 
fair, without excommunication.
§ 3 . The provisions of §§ 1 and 2 remain firm, 
whoever records with any technical means, or disseminates with 
malice, on social media, things said by 
the confessor or by the penitent in a sacramental confession, 
true or feigned, must be punished according to the severity of the 
crime, without excluding, if it is a clergyman, the expulsion of the 
clerical state.

1387
1387. The Bishop who confers on someone the 
episcopal consecration without pontifical mandate, as well as that which 
receives consecration from him, they incur latae excommunication 
sententiae reserved to the Apostolic See.

1388
1388. § 1 . The Bishop who, contrary to what is prescribed in the 
c. 1105, orders a foreign subject without legitimate resignations, 
incurs a ban from ordering for one year. And who received 
the ordering, is ipso facto suspended in the order that 
received.
§ 2 . Whoever accesses sacred orders affected by censorship 
or an irregularity, voluntarily concealed, in addition to what 
established in c. 1044, § 2, 1st, is suspended ipso facto 
in the order you received.

1389
1389. Who, outside of the cases in question 
in the cc. 1379-1388, illegitimately exercises a priestly function or 
another sacred ministry, must be punished with a just penalty, without 
exclude censorship.

1390
1390. § 1 . Whoever falsely reports to a 
Ecclesiastical superior to a confessor, for the crime in question 
in the c. 1385, incurs latae sententiae interdict; and, yes 
He is a clergyman, also in suspension.
§ 2 . Who presents another complaint to the ecclesiastical Superior 
slanderous for any crime, or otherwise illegitimately injures 
the good reputation of his neighbor, must be punished with a just penalty 
according to c. 1336, §§ 2 -4, to which a censorship may be added.
§ 3 . The slanderer must also be forced to give a 
consistent satisfaction.

1391
1391. He must be punished with penalties of the 
listed in c. 1336, §§ 2 -4, depending on the severity of the crime:
1st who falsifies a public ecclesiastical document, or alters, 
destroys or hides a real one, or uses a false or altered one;
2nd who, in an ecclesiastical matter, uses another false document or 
altered;
3rd who states something false in a public ecclesiastical document.

1392
1392. The clergyman who voluntarily abandons 
illegitimately the sacred ministry for six continuous months, 
with the intention of evading the competent authority of the Church, 
must be punished according to the seriousness of the crime with suspension or 
also with penalties of those listed in c. 1336, §§ 2 -4, and in the 
More serious cases may be expelled from the clerical state.

1393
1393. § 1 . The clergyman or religious person who exercises 
commerce or business against the prescriptions of the canons 
must be punished according to the seriousness of the crime with penalties 
of those listed in c. 1336, §§ 2 -4.
§ 2 . The clergyman or the religious who, apart from the cases already 
provided for by law, commits a crime in economic matters, or 
seriously violates the prescriptions indicated in c. 285, § 4 , 
must be punished with penalties listed in c. 1336, §§ 
2 -4, the obligation to repair the damage remains firm.

1394
1394. § 1 . Remaining firm what the ccs prescribe. 
194, § 1, 3rd, and 694, § 1, 2nd, the clergyman who attempts a marriage, 
even if only civilly, incurs latae suspension 
sententiae; and if, after having been reprimanded, he does not change his 
behavior or continues to cause scandal, must be punished gradually 
with deprivation or even expulsion from the clerical state.
§ 2 . The religious person with perpetual vows, not a clergyman, who attempts 
contracting marriage, even if only civilly, incurs 
interdicted latae sententiae, in addition to what is established in the 
c. 694, § 1, 2nd.

1395
1395. § 1 . The concubinary cleric, apart from the case 
which is discussed in c. 1394, and the clergyman who with scandal 
remains in another external sin against the sixth commandment of the 
Decalogue, must be punished with suspension, to which, if it persists 
the offense after the warning, can be added gradually 
other penalties, up to expulsion from the clerical state.
§ 2 . A clergyman who otherwise commits a crime against the sixth 
commandment of the Decalogue, if the crime has been committed publicly, 
must be punished with just penalties, without excluding expulsion from the 
clerical state when the case requires it.
§ 3 . He must be punished with the same penalty as indicated in § 2. 
cleric who, with violence, threats or abuse of his authority, commits 
a crime against the sixth commandment of the Decalogue or obliges 
someone to perform or suffer sexual acts.

1396
1396. Whoever seriously fails to comply with the obligation to 
reside to which he is subject by reason of an ecclesiastical office, 
must be punished with a just penalty, without excluding, after the 
reprimand, deprivation of office.

1397
1397. § 1 . Who commits homicide, or kidnaps or retains 
to a human being with violence or fraud, or mutilates or injures him

persons indicated in c. 1370 is punishable by penalties 
established there and also in the § 3 of this canon.
§ 2 . Whoever seeks abortion, if it occurs, incurs 
excommunication latae sententiae.
§ 3 . When it comes to crimes listed in this canon, 
In the most serious cases, the clergyman who has committed the crime must be 
expelled from the clerical state.

1399
1399. Apart from the cases established in this u 
other laws, the external infringement of a divine or canonical law only

1400
1400. § 1 . The following are subject to trial:
1 the claim or vindication of rights of natural or legal persons, or the declaration of legal facts;
2 crimes, as regards inflicting or declaring a penalty.
§ 2 . However, disputes arising from an act of administrative power can only be brought to the Superior Court or to the administrative court.

1401
1401. The Church judges with its own and exclusive right:
1 causes that refer to spiritual things or annexed to them;

1402
1402. All the courts of the Church are governed by the canons that follow, leaving the norms of the courts of the Apostolic See safe.

1403
1403. § 1 . The causes of canonization of the Servants of God are governed by a peculiar pontifical law.
§ 2 . The provisions of this Code also apply to these causes, when that law makes reference to universal law or when it involves norms that, by their very nature, also govern those causes.

1404
1404. The First See by no one can be judged.

1405
1405. § 1 . It is the exclusive right of the Roman Pontiff to judge in the causes dealt with in c. 1401:
1 to those who exercise the supreme authority of a State;
2 to the Cardinals;
3 to the Legates of the Apostolic See and, in criminal cases, to the Bishops;
4 other causes that he himself has invoked.
§ 2 . No judge can rule on an act or instrument specifically confirmed by the Roman Pontiff, without his prior mandate.
§ 3 . It is reserved for the Roman Rota to judge:
1 to the Bishops in contentious cases, the provisions of c.
1405. § 3, as well as others that the Roman Pontiff, both motu proprio and at the request of a party, would have invoked to his court and entrusted to the Roman Rota; and, if the commission rescript does not indicate otherwise, the Rota also judges these cases in the second and subsequent instance.

1406
1406. § 1 . In case of violation of c. 1404, the minutes and decisions are considered non-existent.
§ 2 . Regarding the causes listed in c. 1405, the incompetence of the other judges is absolute.

1407
1407. § 1 . No one can be summoned in the first instance, if not before a competent ecclesiastical judge by one of the titles determined in ccs. 1408-1414.
§ 2 . The incompetence of the judge who does not enjoy any of these Titles is called relative.
§ 3 . The actor follows the jurisdiction of the defendant, and when the defendant has several jurisdictions, the actor can choose between them.

1408
1408. Anyone can be sued before the court of their domicile or quasi-domicile.

1409
1409. § 1 . The lazy person has his jurisdiction in the place where he is at that moment.
§ 2 . The person whose domicile or quasi-domicile and place of residence are unknown may be sued according to the plaintiff's jurisdiction, unless another legitimate jurisdiction applies.

1410
1410. Due to the place where the thing is located, a lawsuit may be filed before the court of the place where the object in dispute is located, provided that the action is real or involves plunder.

1411
1411. § 1 . By reason of contract, a party may be sued before the court of the place where the contract was made or where it must be performed, unless the parties, by mutual agreement, have chosen another court.
§ 2 . If the case concerns obligations that come from another Title, the party can be sued before the court of the place where the obligation arose or must be fulfilled.

1412
1412. In criminal cases, the accused, even if he is absent, may be brought before the court of the place where the crime was committed.

1413
1413. The party may be sued:
1 in cases dealing with administration, before the court of the place where it was carried out;
2 in cases that refer to inheritances or legacies, before the court of the last domicile or quasi-domicile, or place of residence, in accordance with cc. 1408-1409, of the one whose inheritance or pious legacy is in question, unless the issue refers to the mere execution of the legacy, which must be processed according to the ordinary rules of jurisdiction.

1414
1414. Due to the connection, the same court and in the same process must judge the cases related to each other, unless prevented by a legal provision.

1415
1415. For reasons of prevention, when two or more courts are equally competent, the one that first legitimately summoned the defendant has the right to judge the case.

1416
1416. Conflicts of jurisdiction between courts subject to the same court of appeal must be resolved by it; If they are not subject to the same court of appeal, the Apostolic Signature resolves.

1417
1417. § 1 . Due to the primacy of the Roman Pontiff, any faithful can bring or introduce a case before the Holy See, both contentious and criminal, at any stage of the trial and whatever the state of the dispute.
§ 2 . However, outside of the case of appeal, this petition filed with the Apostolic See does not suspend the exercise of the jurisdiction of the judge who has already begun to deal with the case; The latter, therefore, may continue the trial until the final sentence, unless the Apostolic See informs the judge that he has taken the case to himself.

1418
1418. Every court has the right to request the help of another court for the investigation of the case or to make judicial summonses.

1419
1419. § 2 ;
2 to the Primate Abbot, to the Superior Abbot of a monastic congregation, and to the Superior General of religious institutes of pontifical right;
3 to dioceses or other ecclesiastical persons, both physical and legal, who have no Superior below the Roman Pontiff.
1419. § 1 . In each diocese, and for all causes not expressly excepted by law, the judge of first instance is the diocesan Bishop, who may exercise judicial power by himself or through others in accordance with the canons that follow.
§ 2 . However, when it comes to rights or temporal goods of a legal person represented by the Bishop, the court of appeal judges in the first degree.

1420
1420. § 1 . Every diocesan Bishop must appoint a judicial Vicar or Official with ordinary power to judge, distinct from the Vicar General, unless the size of the diocese or the scarcity of causes advise otherwise.
§ 2 . The judicial Vicar constitutes a single court with the Bishop, but cannot judge cases that the Bishop has reserved for himself.
§ 3 . The judicial Vicar may be appointed assistants called Deputy Judicial Vicars or Vice Officials.
§ 4 . Both the Judicial Vicar and the Deputy Judicial Vicars must be priests, of good reputation, doctors or at least graduates in canon law and at least thirty years of age.
§ 5 . When the see becomes vacant, such judicial Vicars do not cease in office nor can they be removed by the diocesan Administrator; but they need to be confirmed when the new Bishop takes office.
1420. § 5, and cannot be removed unless it is for legitimate and serious cause.

1421
1421. § 1 . The Bishop must appoint diocesan judges in the diocese, who are clerics.
§ 2 . The Episcopal Conference can also allow lay people to be appointed judges, one of whom, if necessary, can join the collegiate court.
§ 3 . The judges must be of good reputation, doctors or at least graduates in canon law.

1422
1422. The judicial Vicar, the deputy judicial Vicars and the other judges are appointed for a specific period, remaining in place what is prescribed by the c

1423
1423. § 1 . Replacing the diocesan courts, mentioned in ccs. 1419-1421, several diocesan Bishops, with the approval of the Apostolic See, may constitute by common agreement a single court of first instance for their dioceses; In this case, the group of Bishops or the Bishop designated by them have all the powers that correspond to the diocesan Bishop over his court.
§ 2 . The courts referred to in § 1 may be established for all cases or only for a specific class of cases.

1424
1424. In any trial, the sole judge may use two advisors, clerics or laymen of upright life, to help him with their advice.

1425
1425. § 1 . The contrary custom being disapproved, they are reserved to a collegiate court of three judges:
1 contentious causes: a) on the bond of sacred ordination; b) on the bond of marriage, remaining in force what is prescribed in cc. 1686 and 1688;
2 criminal cases: a) on crimes that can be punished by expulsion from the clerical state; b) if it involves inflicting or declaring an excommunication.
§ 2 . The Bishop may entrust the most difficult or most important cases to a college of three or five judges.
§ 3 . To judge each case, the Judicial Vicar will call the judges in turn, unless in a specific case the Bishop establishes otherwise.
§ 4 . If it is not possible to constitute a collegiate court in the first degree of trial, the Episcopal Conference may allow, while this impossibility lasts, the Bishop to entrust the cases to a single clerical judge, who, where possible, uses the collaboration of an advisor and an auditor.
§ 5 . Once the judges are appointed, the Judicial Vicar must not change them, except for very serious reasons, which must be stated in the decree.
1425. § 4, the court of second instance must act collegially.
1425. § 1 .

3 lacks the signatures prescribed by law;
4 does not bear an indication of the year, month, day and place in which it was issued;
5 is based on a judicial act affected by a nullity that has not been corrected in accordance with c. 1619;
6 was given against a legitimately absent party, in accordance with c.

1426
1426. § 1 . The collegiate court must proceed collegially, and issue a sentence by majority vote.
§ 2 . To the extent possible, it must be presided over by the Judicial Vicar or an Assistant Judicial Vicar.

1427
1427. § 1 . Unless the constitutions provide otherwise, when a controversy arises between religious or houses of the same clerical religious institute of pontifical right, the judge of first instance is the Provincial Superior or, if it is an autonomous monastery, the local Abbot.
§ 2 . Unless the constitutions prescribe otherwise, if the conflict occurs between two provinces, it will be judged in the first instance by the Superior General, personally or through a delegate; and the Superior Abbot of the monastic Congregation, if the dispute is between two monasteries.
§ 3 . Finally, the diocesan court judges in the first instance disputes between natural or legal religious persons of various religious institutes, or also of the same clerical or lay institute of diocesan law, or between a religious person and a secular clergyman or a layman or a non-religious legal person.

1428
1428. § 1 . The judge, or the president of the collegiate court, may appoint an auditor to carry out the investigation of the case, choosing him from among the judges of the court or among the people approved by the Bishop for this function.
§ 2 . For the position of auditor, the Bishop can approve clerics or lay people, who stand out for their good habits, prudence and doctrine.
§ 3 . The auditor is only responsible for collecting the evidence and delivering it to the judge, according to his mandate; and if not prohibited by the mandate, he can provisionally decide what evidence is to be collected and in what way, in the event that these issues are discussed while carrying out his task.

1429
1429. The president of the collegiate court must appoint a rapporteur or rapporteur among the judges of the college, who will inform the court meeting about the case and will write the sentence in writing; The president may replace him with another, when there is just cause.

1430
1430. For contentious cases in which the public good is involved, and for criminal cases, a promoter of justice must be established in the diocese, who by office is obliged to ensure the public good.

1431
1431. § 1 . In contentious cases, it is up to the diocesan Bishop to judge whether or not the public good is at stake, unless the intervention of the promoter of justice is prescribed by law or is evidently necessary due to the nature of the matter.
§ 2 . If the promoter of justice had intervened in the preceding instance, it is presumed that his intervention is necessary at the next level.

1432
1432. For cases in which the annulment of sacred ordination or the annulment or dissolution of a marriage is discussed, a defender of the bond must be appointed in the diocese, who, by office, must propose and express everything that can reasonably be argued against the annulment or dissolution.

1433
1433. In those cases that require the presence of the promoter of justice or the defender of the bond, if they have not been cited, the acts are null, unless, however, they are actually present or, at least, they have been able to fulfill their mission before the sentence, through the examination of the minutes.

1434
1434. Unless otherwise expressly established:
1 when the law mandates that the judge hear the parties or one of them, the promoter of justice and the defender of the bond must also be heard, if they intervene in the trial;
2 when a party's request is required so that the judge can decide something, the request of the promoter of justice or the defender of the bond has the same value, if they intervene in the trial.

1435
1435. It is the responsibility of the Bishop to appoint the promoter of justice and the defender of the bond, who must be clerics or laymen of good repute, doctors or graduates in canon law and of proven prudence and zeal for justice.

1436
1436. § 1 . The same person can perform the role of promoter of justice and defender of the bond but not in the same cause.
§ 2 . The promoter and the defender can be established for all the causes in general or for each of them in particular; and may be removed by the Bishop for just cause.

1437
1437. § 1 . A notary must intervene in every process, so the minutes are void if they are not signed by him.
§ 2 . The minutes drawn up by a notary become public evidence.

1438
1438. Remaining in place what is prescribed in the c
1438. and 1439, the judge's incompetence is absolute.
1438. and 1439.
§ 2 . If another party appeals to a different court, the case will be resolved by the court with a higher degree, subject to what is prescribed in c. 1415.

1439
1439. § 1 . Yes, according to c. 1423, there is a single court of first instance for several dioceses, the Episcopal Conference, with the approval of the Apostolic See, must establish a court of second instance, unless all those dioceses are suffragans of the same archdiocese.
§ 2 . The Episcopal Conference may establish one or more courts of second instance, with the approval of the Apostolic See, even outside the cases provided for in the § 1.
§ 3 . Regarding the courts of second instance referred to in §§ 1 -2, the Episcopal Conference or the Bishop designated by it has all the powers that correspond to the diocesan Bishop over his court.
1439. § 3, the appeal court is subject.
§ 3 . Among religious, the execution of the sentence corresponds to the Superior who dictated it or who appointed the delegate judge.

1440
1440. If competition is not observed due to the degree, in accordance with the cc

1441
1441. The court of second instance must be constituted in the same way as the court of first instance. But if in the first degree of the trial a single judge handed down a sentence, according to the c

1442
1442. The Roman Pontiff is supreme judge for the entire Catholic world and dictates judgment either personally, or through the ordinary courts of the Apostolic See, or by judges to whom he delegates.

1443
1443. The Roman Rota is the ordinary tribunal constituted by the Roman Pontiff to receive appeals.

1444
1444. § 1, 1:
1 from the court of a suffragan Bishop, appeal is made to that of the Metropolitan, except as indicated in c. 1439;
2 when the case is heard in the first instance before the Metropolitan, the appeal is filed before the court that he himself has designated in a stable manner, with the approval of the Apostolic See;
3 for cases dealt with before the Provincial Superior, the court of second instance is that of the Superior General; for cases brought before the local Abbot, it is the court of the superior Abbot of the monastic congregation.
1444. § 1 . The Roman Rota judges:
1 in the second instance, cases decided by ordinary courts of first instance and that have been elevated to the Holy See by legitimate appeal;
2 in the third or subsequent instance, cases already judged by the same Roman Rota or by any other court, unless they have become res judicata.
§ 2 . This court also judges in the first instance the cases provided for in c.

1445
1445. § 1 . The Supreme Court of the Apostolic Signature judges:
1 complaints for annulment and requests for restitution in integrum and other appeals against rotational sentences;
2 appeals in cases regarding the status of persons whom the Roman Rota refuses to admit for new examination;
3 the exceptions of suspicion and other causes against the Auditors of the Roman Rota for acts carried out in the exercise of their function;
4 the conflicts of jurisdiction referred to in c. 1416.
§ 2 . This same Court settles disputes arising from an act of ecclesiastical administrative power that are legitimately brought to it, as well as other administrative controversies that have been referred to it by the Roman Pontiff or by the dicasteries of the Roman Curia, and conflicts of jurisdiction between said dicasteries.
§ 3 . It also corresponds to this Supreme Court:
1 monitor the proper administration of justice and determine that proceedings be taken against lawyers or attorneys, if necessary;
2 extend the jurisdiction of the courts;
3 promote and approve the erection of the courts referred to in the ccs. 1423 and 1439.

1446
1446. § 1 . All the faithful, and first of all the Bishops, must diligently ensure that, without prejudice to justice, disputes among the people of God are avoided as much as possible and settled peacefully as soon as possible.
§ 2 . At the beginning of the litigation, and at any other time, whenever there is some hope of success, the judge will not fail to exhort and help the parties to try by common agreement to seek an equitable solution to their controversy, and will indicate to them the appropriate means to achieve this end, even resorting to serious people as mediators.
§ 3 . But when the dispute concerns the private good of the parties, the judge considers whether it can usefully be concluded by transaction or by arbitration judgment in accordance with the ccs. 1713-1716.
1446. § 2 proves useless, if the judge considers that the claim has some basis, he will order within three days, by means of a decree recorded at the bottom of the document, that a copy of it be sent to the defendant, granting him the power to send his written responses to the chancery of the court within a period of fifteen days.
§ 2 . This notification produces the effects of the judicial summons indicated in c. 1512.

1447
1447. Whoever has intervened in a case as a judge, promoter of justice, defender of the bond, attorney, lawyer, witness or expert, cannot later validly define the same case as a judge in another instance or perform the office of advisor.

1448
1448. § 1 . The judge does not accept to hear a case in which he has an interest by reason of consanguinity or affinity in any degree of straight line and up to the fourth degree of collateral line, or by reason of guardianship or curatorship, intimate friendship, great aversion, obtaining a profit or preventing harm.
§ 2 . In the same circumstances, the promoter of justice, the defender of the bond, the advisor and the auditor must refrain from carrying out their duties.

1449
1449. § 1 . In the cases indicated in c. 1448, if the judge himself does not recuse himself, the party may challenge him.
§ 2 . The judicial Vicar decides on the challenge; and, if he himself is challenged, the Bishop who presides over the tribunal decides.
§ 3 . If the Bishop himself acts as judge and is challenged, he must refrain from judging.
§ 4 . If the challenge is opposed against the promoter of justice, the defender of the bond or another minister of the court, the president of the collegiate court, or the judge, if it is the only one, decides on said exception.

1450
1450. Once the challenge is admitted, the persons must be changed, but without changing the degree of the trial.

1451
1451. § 1 . The challenge must be resolved as quickly as possible by hearing the parties and the promoter of justice or the defender of the bond, if they participate in the trial and are not themselves the ones challenged.
§ 2 . The acts carried out by the judge before being challenged are valid; but those carried out after the challenge was filed must be rescinded, if the party requests it within a period of ten days from when the challenge was admitted.

1452
1452. § 1 . In matters that are of interest only to individuals, the judge can only proceed at the request of a party. But, once a criminal case or other case that refers to the public good of the Church or the salvation of souls has been legitimately introduced, the judge can, and even must, proceed ex officio.
§ 2 . The judge may also compensate for the negligence of the parties in presenting evidence or raising exceptions, whenever he considers it necessary to avoid a seriously unjust sentence, remaining firm the prescriptions of c. 1600.

1453
1453. Judges and courts must ensure that, without loss of justice, all cases are completed as soon as possible, and that they do not last more than one year in the court of first instance, nor more than six months in the court of second instance.

1454
1454. All those who are part of the court or collaborate with it must take an oath that they will carry out their task correctly and faithfully.

1455
1455. § 1 . Judges and court assistants are obliged to maintain ex officio secrecy in all criminal trials, and also in litigation when some harm may result for the parties from the disclosure of any procedural act.
§ 2 . Without prejudice to the provisions of c.

1456
1456. The judge and all court ministers are prohibited from accepting gifts of any kind on the occasion of judicial proceedings.

1457
1457. § 1 . Judges who refuse to administer justice even though they are certainly and evidently competent, or who declare themselves competent without any legal title that legitimizes that competence, and hear and decide the cases, or violate the law of secrecy, or through fraud or gross negligence cause other damage to the parties, may be punished with appropriate penalties by the competent authority, including deprivation of office.
§ 2 . The ministers and assistants of the court are subject to the same sanctions if they fail in their duty, as indicated above; The judge can also punish all of these.

1458
1458. The causes must be heard following the same order in which they were proposed and registered, unless one of them requires a faster expedition than the others, which must be determined by special reasoned decree.

1459
1459. § 1 . Those defects from which the annulment of the sentence may result can be proposed as an exception or raised ex officio by the judge at any phase or level of the trial.
§ 2 . Outside of the cases indicated in the § 1, dilatory exceptions, and especially those that refer to the persons and the manner of the trial, must be proposed before the litigation, unless they arise after it has taken place, and must be decided as soon as possible.

1460
1460. § 1 . If the exception is proposed against the jurisdiction of the judge, the decision corresponds to the same judge.
§ 2 . In case of exception of relative incompetence, if the judge declares himself competent, his decision does not allow appeal, but a complaint for annulment and restitution in integrum can be proposed.
§ 3 . If the judge declares himself incompetent, the party that considers itself harmed may appeal to the court of appeal within a period of fifteen business days.

1461
1461. At any stage of the case, the judge who recognizes his absolute incompetence must declare it.

1462
1462. § 1 . The exceptions of res judicata, compromise and other peremptory ones that are called "finished litigation" must be proposed and dealt with before the litigation is filed; Whoever proposes them later must not be rejected, but must be ordered to pay the costs, unless he proves that he has not maliciously delayed the opposition.
§ 2 . The other peremptory exceptions must be proposed in the answer to the claim, and must be dealt with at the appropriate time, according to the rules of incidental issues.

1463
1463. § 1 . Counterclaims can only be validly proposed within a period of thirty days from the response to the claim.
§ 2 . They must be treated at the same time as the conventional action, that is, at the same pace as it, unless it is necessary to hear it separately or the judge considers that this is more appropriate.

1464
1464. Questions regarding the provision of security regarding the payment of judicial costs, or the granting of free sponsorship, if it has already been requested from the first moment, and other similar matters, must ordinarily be dealt with before the response is filed.

1465
1465. § 1 . The so-called fatal deadlines, that is, the deadlines determined by law for the expiration of rights, cannot be extended, nor can they validly be shortened, except at the request of the parties.
§ 2 . However, judicial and conventional deadlines, before their expiration, may be extended by the judge when there is a just cause, having heard the parties or at their request; but they can never be validly abbreviated, except with the consent of the parties.
§ 3 . The judge should take care, however, that the litigation does not drag on too long due to the extension.

1466
1466. When the law does not establish deadlines for carrying out procedural acts, the judge must determine them, taking into account the nature of each act.

1467
1467. If the court is closed on the day designated for a judicial act, the deadline is understood to be extended to the first business day.

1468
1468. Every court must have, as far as possible, a fixed headquarters, which will be open at specific times.

1469
1469. § 1 . The judge forcibly expelled from his territory or prevented from exercising his jurisdiction therein, may exercise it outside the territory and issue a sentence, but informing the diocesan Bishop.
§ 2 . In addition to what was said in § 1, the judge, for just cause and after hearing the parties, may leave his own territory to collect evidence, but with the license of the diocesan Bishop of the place to which he is going and at the headquarters determined by him.
1469. § 2 .

1470
1470. § 1 . If a particular law does not provide otherwise, while the case is being heard in court, only those who the law or the judge determines are necessary to carry out the process should be present in the courtroom.
§ 2 . The judge may force those who attend the trial and seriously lack the respect and obedience due to the court to observe proper conduct with proportionate penalties; and, in addition, lawyers and attorneys can be suspended from the exercise of their function before ecclesiastical courts.

1471
1471. If a person who is to be interrogated uses a language unknown to the judge or the parties, a sworn interpreter, designated by the judge, must be used. However, the statements must be recorded in writing in the original language, adding the translation. An interpreter will also be used when a deaf or mute person must be questioned, unless the judge prefers that he respond in writing to the questions presented.

1472
1472. § 1 . Judicial acts must be drafted in writing, whether they refer to the substance of the dispute, or to acts of the case, or to the manner of proceeding, or acts of the process.
§ 2 . Each sheet of the minutes must be numbered and authenticated.

1473
1473. When the signature of the parties or witnesses is required in the judicial minutes, if the party or the witness cannot or does not want to sign, this must be recorded in the same minutes, and at the same time the judge and the notary will attest that this record has been read in its entirety to the party or the witness and that neither the party nor the witness could or wanted to sign.

1474
1474. § 1 . In the event of an appeal, a copy of the records must be sent to the higher court, with the notary attesting to their authenticity.
§ 2 . If the records are written in a language unknown to the superior court, they must be translated into another language known to it, taking precautions to ensure the fidelity of the translation.

1475
1475. § 1 . At the end of the trial, the documents that belong to them must be returned to the individuals, although a copy of them must be preserved.
§ 2 . Without a judge's order, notaries and the chancellor are prohibited from providing a copy of the court records and documents that are part of the process.

1476
1476. Any person, whether baptized or not, may sue in court; and the legitimately sued party has the obligation to respond.

1477
1477. Even if the plaintiff or the defendant appoints a solicitor or lawyer, they have the obligation to personally attend the trial whenever the law or the judge prescribes it.

1478
1478. § 1 . Minors and those who lack the use of reason can only appear in court through their parents, guardians or curators, except as prescribed in § 3.
§ 2 . If the judge considers that the rights of minors are in conflict with those of their parents, guardians or curators, or that they cannot sufficiently protect their rights, they will appear in court through a guardian or curator designated by the judge.
§ 3 . However, in spiritual cases and those related to them, minors who have reached the use of reason can sue and answer for themselves, without the consent of the parents and guardian, if they have reached the age of fourteen; If not, they must do so through a curator appointed by the judge.
§ 4 . Those who suffer property interdiction or a mental disorder can only appear in court to answer for their own crimes or by order of the judge; In other cases, they must sue and respond through their guardians.

1479
1479. When the civil authority has already appointed a guardian or curator, this can be admitted by the ecclesiastical judge, after hearing, if possible, the diocesan Bishop of the one to whom it was given; but if it is not appointed or if it is considered that it should not be admitted, the judge will appoint a guardian or curator for the case.

1480
1480. § 1 . Legal entities act in the trial through their legitimate representatives.
§ 2 . But if they do not have a representative or the latter is negligent, the Ordinary can act in court, by himself or by another, on behalf of the legal entities that are under his jurisdiction.

1481
1481. § 1 . The party may freely designate its lawyer and attorney; but, except in the cases indicated in §§ 2 -3, you can also sue and respond personally, unless the judge considers the help of the procurator or lawyer necessary.
§ 2 . In a criminal trial, the accused must always have a lawyer, chosen by him or herself or appointed by the judge.
§ 3 . In the contentious trial, if it involves minors or a trial in which the public good comes into play, with the exception of matrimonial cases, the judge must appoint a defender ex officio to the party that does not have one.
1481. §§ 1 and 3, the instance is meanwhile suspended.
§ 2 . The judge must appoint another guardian or curator as soon as possible; and may also constitute a solicitor for the case, if the party does not do so within the short period to be determined by the same judge.
1481. § 1, within the period determined by the same judge.
§ 2 . If the prisoner does not name him, the judge himself must appoint him a lawyer before the answer to the complaint, who will remain in office as long as the prisoner does not name another.

1482
1482. § 1 . Each litigant can appoint only one attorney, who cannot be replaced by another, if that power is not expressly granted.
§ 2 . However, when a person appoints several attorneys for just cause, he or she will do so in such a way that there is room for prevention among them.
§ 3 . Several lawyers can be appointed at the same time.

1483
1483. The procurator and the lawyer must be of legal age and of good reputation; Furthermore, the lawyer must be Catholic, unless the diocesan Bishop allows otherwise, and a doctor, or, at least, truly expert in canon law, and have the approval of the Bishop himself.

1484
1484. § 1 . The procurator and the lawyer, before beginning their function, must present their authentic mandate to the court.
§ 2 . However, to prevent the extinction of a right, the judge may admit a solicitor even if he does not present the mandate, requiring due guarantee, if he deems it appropriate; but the act is absolutely ineffective if the attorney does not present the legitimate mandate within the peremptory period set by the judge.

1485
1485. Without a special mandate, the attorney cannot validly renounce the action, the instance or the judicial acts; nor carry out a transaction, pact or arbitration commitment, nor, in general, that for which the law requires a special mandate.

1486
1486. § 1 . For the removal of the attorney or attorney to take effect, it is necessary that he be notified and, if the complaint has already been answered, that the judge and the opposing party be notified of the removal.
§ 2 . After the final sentence, the attorney continues to have the right and obligation to appeal, as long as the principal does not object.

1487
1487. Both the procurator and the lawyer can be rejected by the judge by decree, both ex officio and at the request of a party, but always for serious reasons.

1488
1488. § 1 . Both are prohibited from buying the lawsuit, or agreeing on excessive emoluments or on a claimed part of the disputed item. If they do that, the agreement is void, and they may be fined by the judge. In addition, the lawyer may be suspended from his office or, if he is a repeat offender, eliminated from the list of lawyers by the Bishop who presides over the court.
§ 2 . In the same way, lawyers and attorneys who, by fraud of law, remove cases from the competent courts so that they can be sentenced by others in a more favorable manner can be punished.

1489
1489. Lawyers and attorneys who, due to gifts or promises or for any other reason, violate their office, must be suspended from their sponsorship and punished with a fine or other proportionate penalties.

1490
1490. To the extent possible, in every court there must be stable patrons, who receive their fees from the court itself, and who perform the function of lawyer or attorney, especially in matrimonial cases, in favor of the parties who freely prefer to appoint them.

1491
1491. Every right is protected not only by an action, as long as nothing else is expressly established, but also by an exception.

1492
1492. § 1 . Every action is extinguished by prescription according to law, or in another legitimate manner, except those that refer to the state of persons, which are never extinguished.
§ 2 . Except as prescribed in c. 1462, the exception can always be opposed, and is perpetual by nature.

1493
1493. The actor may bring several actions against someone at the same time, as long as they do not conflict with each other, on the same matter or on several, as long as they do not exceed the jurisdiction of the court to which he or she goes.

1494
1494. § 1 . The defendant may propose a counterclaim against the plaintiff before the same judge and in the same trial, either due to the connection of the case with the main action, or to neutralize or reduce the plaintiff's request.
§ 2 . Counterclaim against counterclaim is not admissible.

1495
1495. The counterclaim action must be proposed to the judge before whom the preceding action was presented, even if he is delegated only for one case or is otherwise affected by relative incompetence.

1496
1496. § 1 . He who makes it appear at least with probable arguments that he has the right to something that is in the possession of another, and that harm may be caused if it is not taken into custody, has the right to obtain from the judge the seizure of the same thing.
§ 2 . In similar circumstances, you can claim that another be prohibited from exercising a right.

1497
1497. § 1 . The seizure of a thing to secure a credit is also permitted, as long as the creditor's right is sufficiently established.
§ 2 . The embargo can also extend to the debtor's assets that are under any title in the possession of other people, as well as to the debtor's credits.

1498
1498. In no way can the seizure of a thing or the inhibition of the exercise of a right be decreed if the damage feared can be repaired in another way and a suitable guarantee is offered for its repair.

1499
1499. Anyone to whom the judge grants the seizure of a thing or the inhibition of the exercise of a right may require a prior guarantee for compensation for damages, in the event that he or she does not prove that he or she has the right.

1500
1500. Regarding the nature and effects of the possessory action, the rules of civil law of the place where the thing whose possession is disputed is located must be observed.

1501
1501. The judge cannot judge any case if the interested party or the promoter of justice has not made a request in accordance with the canons.

1502
1502. Whoever wishes to sue someone must present a document to the competent judge indicating the object of the controversy and requesting the judge's authority.
1502. and 1504.
§ 2 . Before the superior court, the promoter of justice of that same court plays the role of actor.

1503
1503. § 1 . The judge may admit an oral request when the actor is unable to present it in writing, or if it is a cause that is easy to investigate and of little importance.
§ 2 . However, in both cases the judge will order the notary to draw up a record, which must be read to the actor and approved by him, and which replaces the actor's writing for all legal purposes.

1504
1504. The statement of claim must:
1 specify before which judge the case is brought, what is requested and against whom;
2 indicate what law the actor relies on and, at least in general terms, what facts and evidence he relies on to prove what he claims;
3 be signed by the plaintiff or his attorney, indicating the day, month and year, as well as the place where they live or say they have residence for the purposes of receiving documents;
4 indicate the domicile or quasi-domicile of the defendant.

1505
1505. § 1 . The single judge or the president of the collegiate court, after verifying that the matter is within his jurisdiction and that the plaintiff has the legal capacity to act in court, must admit or reject the document of claim as soon as possible, by decree.
§ 2 . The statement of claim can only be rejected:
1 if the judge or court is incompetent;
2 if it is certain that the actor lacks procedural capacity;
3 if what is mandated by c. 1504, 1-3;
4 if from the same document of claim it is deduced with certainty that the petition lacks any foundation and that no foundation can be expected to emerge from the process.
§ 3 . If the document has been rejected due to defects that can be corrected, the plaintiff can present a new, correctly written one before the same judge.
§ 4 . Within the useful period of ten days, the party may file a reasoned appeal against the rejection of the writing before the court of appeal, or before the college if it was rejected by the president; and the question of rejection must be decided as quickly as possible.

1506
1506. If within one month from when the document of claim was presented the judge does not issue a decree admitting or rejecting it in accordance with c. 1505, the interested party can urge the judge to fulfill his obligation; and if, despite everything, the judge remains silent, ten days have passed unnecessarily since the application was filed, the document of claim is considered admitted.

1507
1507. § 1 . In the decree by which the plaintiff's claim is admitted, the judge or president must call the trial or summon the other parties to answer the claim, determining whether they should respond in writing or appear before him to resolve the doubts. And if, based on the written responses, he deduces the need to convene the parties, he can order it by means of a new decree.
§ 2 . If the claim is considered admitted pursuant to c. 1506, the decree summoning a trial must be given within a period of twenty days from the date the instance mentioned in that canon was presented.
§ 3 . When the litigants actually appear before the judge to discuss the case, a summons is not necessary; but the actuary must record in the minutes that the parties were present.
1507. § 3 .
1507. § 1, the judge must declare him absent from the trial and order that the case, observing what is ordered, continue until the final sentence and its execution.
§ 2 . Before giving the decree referred to in § 1, it must be stated, reiterating if necessary the summons, that the legitimately issued one reached the defendant in a timely manner.

1508
1508. § 1 . The decree of judicial summons must be notified immediately to the defendant, and at the same time to those others who must appear.
§ 2 . The statement of claim must be attached to the summons, unless, for serious reasons, the judge considers that it should not be made known to the party before he or she testifies at trial.
§ 3 . If a person is sued who does not have the free exercise of their rights or the free administration of the things being litigated, the summons must be made, as the case may be, to the guardian, curator, special attorney or to the one who, according to the law, is obliged to assume the trial on their behalf.

1509
1509. § 1 . The notification of summonses, decrees, sentences and other judicial acts must be done through the public postal service or by another very secure procedure, observing the rules established by particular law.
§ 2 . The notification and the manner in which it was made must be recorded in the minutes.

1510
1510. The defendant who refuses to receive the summons card, or who prevents it from reaching his hands, must be considered legitimately summoned.

1511
1511. If the summons is not legitimately notified, the acts of the process are null and void, except as prescribed by the c

1512
1512. Once the summons has been legitimately notified or the parties have appeared before the judge to discuss the case:
1 the thing ceases to be whole;
2 the case becomes the responsibility of that judge or the court before which the action has been brought, as long as they are competent;
3 the jurisdiction of the delegated judge is consolidated, in such a way that it is not extinguished when the right of the delegated judge ceases;
4 the prescription is interrupted, if nothing else has been established;
5 the lis pendens begins, and, therefore, the principle "while the litigation is pending, nothing should be innovated" is immediately applied.

1513
1513. § 1 . The litiscontestation occurs when, by decree of the judge, the limits of the controversy are set, taken from the requests and responses of the parties.
§ 2 . The requests and responses of the parties can be made not only in the document of claim, but also in the response to the summons or in oral statements made before the judge; But, in the most difficult cases, the parties must be summoned by the judge to agree on the doubt or doubts that must be answered in the sentence.
§ 3 . The parties must be notified of the judge's decree; and, if they do not agree, they can appeal within ten days, to modify it, before the same judge, who must decide the issue by decree very quickly.
1513. § 1 and 1639 § 1, we must proceed to the discussion of the case and the sentence.

1514
1514. The terms of the controversy, once defined, cannot be validly modified, except by means of a new decree, for serious reasons, at the request of one party and after hearing the rest, whose reasons must be duly considered.

1515
1515. Litigation interrupts the good faith of the possessor of another's property; Therefore, if he is sentenced to restitution, he must also return the fruits and compensate for the damages caused since that moment.

1516
1516. After the response, the judge will set a convenient time for the parties to propose and carry out the tests.

1517
1517. The instance begins with the summons; concludes not only by the final sentence, but also in other ways established by law.

1518
1518. When a litigant dies, or changes state, or ceases in the office for which he acts:
1 if the case has not yet concluded, the instance is suspended until it is resumed by the heir of the deceased or his successor or the legitimately interested party;
2 if the case is concluded, the judge must continue it, summoning the attorney; and if there is not one, to the heir of the deceased or his successor.

1519
1519. § 1 . If the guardian or curator or the attorney required by the c

1520
1520. The instance expires when, without an impediment, the parties do not carry out any procedural act for six months. Other expiration periods may be established by particular law.

1521
1521. Expiry takes place ipso iure and against everyone, even against minors and those equal to them, and must also be declared ex officio, leaving aside the right to request compensation from guardians, curators, administrators or attorneys who do not prove they are free of fault.

1522
1522. Expiration extinguishes the minutes of the process, but not those of the cause; Furthermore, these can also be effective in another instance, as long as the litigation takes place between the same people and on the same object; but, in relation to strangers, they only have the value of documents.

1523
1523. If the trial expires, each of the litigants will have to bear the expenses incurred.

1524
1524. § 1 . The actor may waive the instance at any stage and level of the trial; Likewise, both the actor and the defendant can renounce the acts of the process, either all or only some of them.
§ 2 . In order to renounce the request, the guardians and administrators of legal entities need the advice or consent of those whose participation is necessary to carry out acts that exceed the limits of ordinary administration.
§ 3 . For the waiver to be valid, it must be made in writing, which will be signed by the party themselves, or by their attorney with a special mandate; It must be notified to the other party, and be accepted, or at least not challenged by it, and admitted by the judge.

1525
1525. The waiver accepted by the judge produces the same effects on the waived acts as the expiration of the instance; and also obliges the renouncer to bear the costs of the acts he has renounced.

1526
1526. § 1 . The burden of proof lies with the person who affirms.
§ 2 . No proof needed:
1 those things that the law itself presumes;
2 the facts affirmed by one of the contenders and admitted by the other, unless despite this the law or the judge requires proof.

1527
1527. § 1 . Any evidence that is considered useful to elucidate the cause and that is lawful may be provided.
§ 2 . If a party insists that evidence rejected by the judge be admitted, the same judge must decide the issue as quickly as possible.

1528
1528. If a party or witness refuses to appear before the judge to respond, they may also be heard through a layman designated by the judge, or their statement may be required before a notary public or by another legitimate means.

1529
1529. If it is not for serious reasons, the judge does not proceed to collect evidence before filing the response.

1530
1530. To better discover the truth, the judge can question the parties at any time, and must even do so at the request of a party or to prove a fact that is in the public interest to leave beyond all doubt.

1531
1531. § 1 . The legitimately questioned party must respond and tell the whole truth.
§ 2 . If you refuse to respond, it is up to the judge to evaluate that attitude in order to prove the facts.

1532
1532. When the public good comes into play in a case, the judge must ask the parties to swear that they will tell the truth, or at least that what they have said is true, unless a serious case advises otherwise; In other cases, you can do so, according to your prudence.

1533
1533. The parties, the promoter of justice and the defender of the bond may present to the judge articles or questions about which the party must be questioned.

1534
1534. For the interrogation of the parties, the rules established regarding witnesses in the CC must be observed.

1535
