1535. Judicial confession is the written or oral statement about some fact before the competent judge, expressed by one of the parties about the subject matter of the trial and against itself, both spontaneously and in response to questions from the judge.

1536
1536. § 1 . The judicial confession of one of the parties, when it is a private matter and the public good is not at stake, relieves the others of the burden of proof.
§ 2 . However, in cases that affect the public good, the judicial confession and the statements of the parties that are not confessions may have probative force, which the judge must evaluate together with the other circumstances of the case, but they cannot be attributed full evidentiary force, unless other elements fully corroborate them.
1536. § 2 .

1537
1537. Regarding the extrajudicial confession brought to the trial, it is up to the judge, weighing all the circumstances, to estimate what value should be attributed to it.

1538
1538. The confession or any other statement of a party has no value if it is established that it has been issued by mistake of fact or extracted by violence or serious fear.

1539
1539. In all types of trials, evidence is admitted through documents, both public and private.

1540
1540. § 1 . Public ecclesiastical documents are those that have been drawn up by a public person in the exercise of his function in the Church and observing the solemnities prescribed by law.
§ 2 . Public civil documents are those that, according to the laws of each place, are recognized as such.
§ 3 . The other documents are private.

1541
1541. Unless otherwise stated by contrary and obvious arguments, public documents attest to everything that is directly and mainly stated in them.

1542
1542. The private document, both the one issued by the party and the one recognized by the judge, has the same probative force as the extrajudicial confession against its author or whoever signed it or his successors in title; against strangers, has the same force as the statements of the parties that are not confessions, according to the c

1543
1543. If it is demonstrated that the documents are scratched, corrected, interpolated or affected by another defect, it is up to the judge to assess whether they can be taken into account and to what extent.

1544
1544. Documents have no probative force in the trial if their original or authentic copy is not presented, and they are deposited in the chancery of the court, so that they can be examined by the judge and the adversary.

1545
1545. The judge may order that a document common to both parties be presented in the process.

1546
1546. § 1 . No one is obliged to present documents, even common ones, that cannot be shown without danger of damage, according to the c

1547
1547. In all cases, testimonial evidence is admitted under the direction of the judge.

1548
1548. § 2, 1, 1552 and 1558-1565.
1548. § 2 , 2, or without danger of violating the obligation to maintain secrecy.
§ 2 . However, if it is possible to transcribe at least part of the document and show it without the aforementioned inconveniences, the judge may order that it be presented.
1548. § 1 . Witnesses must declare the truth to the judge who legitimately interrogates them.
§ 2 . Remaining safe from what is prescribed in c.

1549
1549. Everyone can be a witness, unless they are expressly rejected by law in whole or in part.

1550
1550. § 2, 2, are exempt from the obligation to respond:
1 the clerics, in what has been entrusted to them by reason of the sacred ministry; civil magistrates, doctors, midwives, lawyers, notaries and others who are obliged to maintain ex officio secrecy even by reason of the advice given, with regard to matters that fall under that secrecy;
2 who fear that their testimony will cause infamy, dangerous humiliation or other serious harm to themselves, to their spouse, or to blood relatives or close relatives.
1550. § 1 . Minors under fourteen years of age and the mentally weak are not admitted as witnesses, but they may be heard if the judge by decree states that it is appropriate.
§ 2 . They are considered incapable:
1 those who are parties to the case or appear at trial on behalf of the parties, the judge and his assistants, the lawyer and those others who provide or have provided assistance to the parties in the same case;
2 priests, with respect to everything they know through sacramental confession, even if the penitent asks them to manifest it; Furthermore, what someone has heard in any way for the purpose of confession cannot be accepted even as an indication of the truth.

1551
1551. The party who presented a witness may waive his examination; but the opposing party may nevertheless request that the witness be heard.

1552
1552. § 1 . When evidence is requested from witnesses, their names and addresses must be indicated to the court.
§ 2 . Within the period determined by the judge, the articles on which the interrogation of witnesses is requested must be presented; Failure to do so will result in the request being withdrawn.

1553
1553. It is up to the judge to avoid an excessive number of witnesses.

1554
1554. Before examining witnesses, their names must be notified to the parties; But if, according to the judge's prudent assessment, this cannot be done without serious difficulty, it should be done at least before the publication of the testimonies.

1555
1555. Remaining safe from what is prescribed in c. 1550, the party may request that a witness be excluded if, before his or her examination, it is proven that there is just cause for the exclusion.

1556
1556. The summons of a witness is made by decree of the judge legitimately notified to him.

1557
1557. The witness duly summoned must appear or inform the judge of the reason for his absence.

1558
1558. § 1 . Witnesses must be examined at the courthouse, unless the judge considers otherwise appropriate.
§ 2 . The Cardinals, Patriarchs, Bishops and those who according to the law of their nation enjoy that favor, must be heard in the place chosen by them.
§ 3 . The judge must decide where those who, due to distance, illness or other impediment, make it impossible or difficult to go to the court headquarters should be heard, without prejudice to what is prescribed in cc. 1418 and

1559
1559. The parties cannot attend the examination of witnesses, unless the judge, especially when private property is at issue, considers that they should be admitted. However, their lawyers or attorneys may attend, unless, due to the circumstances of the matter and the people, the judge deems that it should be proceeded in secret.

1560
1560. § 1 . Each witness must be examined separately.
§ 2 . If the witnesses disagree with each other or with the party on a serious issue, the judge can confront them, avoiding, as far as possible, dissension and scandal.

1561
1561. The judge, his delegate or an auditor examines the witness, which must be attended by a notary; Therefore, if the parties, the promoter of justice, the defender of the bond, or the lawyers who attend the interrogation, want to ask other questions to the witness, they must not ask them directly to him, but must propose them to the judge or whoever is acting in his place, so that he can ask them, unless the particular law establishes otherwise.

1562
1562. § 1 . The judge must remind the witness of his serious obligation to tell the whole truth and only the truth.
§ 2 . The judge must ask the witness to swear an oath according to c. 1532; and if the witness refuses, he must be heard without oath.

1563
1563. The judge must first verify the identity of the witness, and must ask him what his relationship is with the parties and, when he asks him specific questions about the case, he must also investigate the sources of his knowledge and at what specific moment he learned of what he states.

1564
1564. The questions must be brief, adapted to the capacity of the person being questioned, that they do not cover several issues at the same time, that they are not misleading or fallacious or that suggest an answer, that they offend no one and that they are relevant to the case.

1565
1565. § 1 . Questions should not be made known in advance to witnesses.
§ 2 . However, if the facts to be testified are so difficult to remember that they cannot be stated with certainty unless they are previously remembered, the judge may announce some points to the witness in advance, if he considers that it is possible to do so without danger.

1566
1566. Witnesses will give oral testimony and must not read writings, unless they are calculations or accounts; In this case they will be able to consult the notes they carry with them.

1567
1567. § 1 . The notary must immediately put the response in writing, recording the same words as the statement, at least as far as they directly refer to the object of the trial.
§ 2 . The use of a tape recorder may be permitted, provided that the answers are subsequently recorded in writing and signed, if possible, by those who have given evidence.

1568
1568. The notary must record in the minutes whether the oath was taken or whether it was waived or refused, and also whether the parties or other persons were present; as well as the questions added ex officio and, in general, everything that has happened during the interrogation of the witnesses and that deserves to be remembered.

1569
1569. § 1 . At the end of the examination, the notary's writing of his statement must be read to the witness, or he must hear what has been recorded on tape, giving him the opportunity to add, delete, correct or modify what he deems necessary.
§ 2 . Finally, the witness, the judge and the notary must sign the record.

1570
1570. If the judge considers it necessary or useful, as long as there is no danger of fraud or corruption, at the request of a party or ex officio, witnesses may be called again to testify before publishing the records or testimonies even if they have already been examined.

1571
1571. In accordance with the judge's fair assessment, witnesses must be reimbursed for both the expenses they have incurred and the income not received due to the testimony given.

1572
1572. When evaluating the testimonies, the judge must consider the following aspects, requesting testimonial letters, if necessary:
1 what is the condition of the person and their honesty;
2 if he declares on his own knowledge, mainly what he has seen or heard, or if he expresses his opinion, or what is common feeling or what he has heard from others;
3 whether the witness is constantly and firmly consistent with himself, or whether he is variable, uncertain or hesitant;
4 if there are competing testimonies, or if the statement is confirmed or not with other elements of evidence.

1573
1573. The statement of a single witness does not have full probative force, unless it is a qualified witness who testifies about what he has done due to his job, or unless objective or subjective circumstances persuade otherwise.

1574
1574. The help of experts must be resorted to whenever, by prescription of the law or the judge, their study and opinion is required, based on the rules of a technique or science, to verify a fact or determine the true nature of a thing.

1575
1575. It is up to the judge to appoint the experts, after hearing the parties or at their proposal; and, if appropriate, assume the opinions already prepared by other experts.

1576
1576. Experts are excluded or may be challenged for the same reasons as witnesses.

1578
1578. § 1 . Each expert must prepare his own opinion separately, unless the judge orders that only one be presented, which must be signed by all: in this case, any discrepancies, if any, must be diligently noted.
§ 2 . The experts must clearly state by what documents or other suitable means they have ascertained the identity of the people, things or places, how they have proceeded to fulfill the task entrusted to them and, above all, on what arguments they base the conclusions they have reached.
§ 3 . The expert can be called by the judge to add any explanations that seem necessary.

1579
1579. § 1 . The judge must carefully weigh not only the conclusions of the experts, even if they are consistent, but also the other circumstances of the case.
§ 2 . When you give the reasons for your decision, you must state the reasons for which you have accepted or rejected the experts' conclusions.

1580
1580. The experts will be paid the expenses and fees that the judge fairly determines, observing particular law.

1581
1581. § 1 . The parties can appoint private experts, who need the approval of the judge.
§ 2 . These, if the judge allows it, can see the minutes of the case, to the extent necessary, and attend the carrying out of the expert opinion; and they can always present their own opinion.

1582
1582. If, to decide the case, the judge considers it advisable to move to some place or examine something, he must establish this by means of a decree in which, having heard the parties, he summarily indicates the specific content of the recognition.

1583
1583. A record of the recognition carried out will be drawn up.

1584
1584. Presumption is a probable conjecture about an uncertain thing. It can be of right, when it is determined by law, or of man, if it comes from the judge's reasoning.

1585
1585. Whoever has a presumption of law in his favor is exonerated from the burden of proof, which falls on the opposing party.

1586
1586. The judge must not formulate any presumption that is not established by law, unless it is about a certain and determined fact that has a direct relationship with what is the subject of controversy.

1587
1587. An incidental cause occurs whenever, after the trial has begun by the summons, an issue is raised that, although not expressly included in the statement of claim, concerns the cause in such a way that it will normally have to be resolved before the main issue.

1588
1588. The incidental cause is proposed in writing or orally, indicating the relationship that exists between it and the main cause, before the judge who is competent to judge the latter.

1589
1589. § 1 . Once the petition has been received and the parties have been heard, the judge must decide as quickly as possible whether the proposed incidental question appears to have merit and is related to the main trial, or whether it should be rejected from the outset; and, if admitted, whether its seriousness is such that it must be resolved by interlocutory ruling or by decree.
§ 2 . If he judges that the incidental issue should not be resolved before the final ruling, he will decree that it be taken into account when the main cause is defined.

1590
1590. § 1 . If the incidental issue must be resolved by means of a ruling, the rules on the oral contentious process must be observed, unless the judge deems otherwise taking into account the seriousness of the matter.
§ 2 . But if it must be resolved by decree, the court may entrust the matter to an auditor or the president.

1591
1591. Before finishing the main case, for a just reason, the judge or the court may revoke or reform the decree or interlocutory sentence, both at the request of a party and ex officio, after hearing the parties.

1592
1592. § 1 . If the defendant does not appear when summoned or give a reasonable excuse for his absence, or respond in accordance with the c

1593
1593. § 1 . If the defendant appears later in the trial or responds before the definition of the case, he can add conclusions and evidence, remaining in place what is prescribed in c. 1600; But the judge must ensure that the trial is not intentionally prolonged with long and unnecessary delays.
§ 2 . Even if you had not appeared or responded before the definition of the case, you can challenge the sentence; and may file a complaint for annulment, if he proves that he did not appear due to a legitimate impediment, which, through no fault of his own, he was not able to prove before.
1593. § 2 .

1594
1594. If on the day and time set for the response, the plaintiff does not appear or provide an adequate excuse:
1 the judge will summon him again;
2 if the actor does not obey this new summons, it is presumed that he renounces the instance, according to ccs. 1524-1525;
3 if you later wish to intervene in the process, comply with the provisions of c. 1593.

1595
1595. § 1 . The party absent from the trial, whether the plaintiff or the defendant, who does not prove to have a just impediment, must pay the legal costs incurred by his absence and, if necessary, also compensate the other party.
§ 2 . If neither the plaintiff nor the defendant appear, they have a joint and several obligation to pay the legal costs.

1596
1596. § 1 . Whoever has an interest in the case may be admitted to intervene in any instance of the litigation, both as a party defending his or her own right and, incidentally, to help one of the litigants.
§ 2 . But, to be admitted, you must present to the judge, before the conclusion of the case, a document in which you briefly explain your right to intervene.
§ 3 . Whoever intervenes in the case must be admitted in the state in which it is located, giving him a short and peremptory period to present his evidence, if the case has already reached the evidentiary period.

1597
1597. After hearing the parties, the judge must call a third party to the trial, whose intervention he considers necessary.

1598
1598. § 1 . Once the evidence has been received, the judge, by decree, must allow, under penalty of annulment, the parties and their lawyers to examine the minutes that they do not yet know in the court's chancery; and you can even give a copy of the minutes to the lawyers who request it; However, in cases that affect the public good, the judge, to avoid very serious dangers, may decree that an act not be disclosed to anyone, taking care that the right of defense is always safeguarded.
§ 2 . To complete the evidence, the parties can propose others to the judge; and, after receiving these, if the judge considers it necessary, the decree referred to in § 1 must be issued again.

1599
1599. § 1 . Once everything related to the presentation of evidence is finished, the case is concluded.
§ 2 . This conclusion takes place when the parties declare that they have nothing more to add, or the useful period established by the judge to present the evidence has elapsed, or the judge states that the case is sufficiently investigated.
§ 3 . The judge will issue the decree concluding the case, regardless of the way in which it occurred.

1600
1600. § 1 . After the conclusion of the case, the judge may call the same or other witnesses, or order that tests not previously requested be performed, only:
1 in cases in which only the private good of the parties is involved, if all of them agree;
2 in other cases, after hearing the parties and provided there is a serious reason and any danger of fraud or bribery is avoided;
3 in all cases, when it is probable that, if new evidence is not admitted, the sentence will be unjust, for the reasons set forth in c.

1601
1601. Once the case has been concluded, the judge will establish a convenient period for the defenses or allegations to be presented.

1602
1602. § 1 . The defenses and arguments must be made in writing, unless the judge, with the consent of the parties, considers the discussion before the court in session to be sufficient.
§ 2 . The prior permission of the judge is necessary to print the defenses together with the main documents, safeguarding the obligation to maintain secrecy if such an obligation exists.
§ 3 . Regarding the extension of the defenses, number of copies and other similar circumstances, the court regulations will be observed.
1602. § 1 and 1604 § 2, a notary must attend to immediately prepare minutes of the matters discussed and the conclusions, provided that the judge orders it or consents to it at the request of the party.

1603
1603. § 1 . Once the parties have exchanged defenses and arguments, both may present replies, within a short period determined by the judge.
§ 2 . This right belongs to the parties only once, unless, for serious reasons, the judge deems that he should grant it again; and, in that case, the concession made to one party is also understood to have been granted to the other.
§ 3 . The promoter of justice and the defender of the bond have the right to reply again to the responses of the parties.

1604
1604. § 1 . It is strictly prohibited for the parties, lawyers or other persons, to transmit to the judge information that is outside the minutes of the case.
§ 2 . If the discussion of the case has been done in writing, the judge may order that a moderate oral debate be held before the court, in order to clarify some issues.

1605
1605. To the oral debate discussed in the cc

1606
1606. If the parties neglect to present the defense within the useful period, or if they refer to the science and conscience of the judge, the latter may immediately issue a sentence, if, based on what has been alleged and proven, he has full knowledge of the matter, after requesting the observations of the promoter of justice and the defender of the bond, if they intervene in the trial.

1607
1607. A case dealt with judicially, if it is main, is decided by the judge through a final sentence; if it is incidental, by means of an interlocutory sentence, without prejudice to what is established in the c

1608
1608. § 1 . To issue any sentence, moral certainty is required in the judge's mind about the matter he must resolve.
§ 2 . The judge must achieve this certainty of what is alleged and proven.
§ 3 . The judge must evaluate the evidence according to his conscience, respecting the rules on the effectiveness of certain evidence.
§ 4 . If this certainty has not been reached, the judge must rule that the actor's right is not established and must acquit the defendant, unless it is a case that enjoys the favor of the law, in which case he must rule in favor of it.

1609
1609. § 4, are also always obliged to keep secret about the discussion that takes place between the judges of the collegiate court before issuing a sentence, as well as about the different votes and opinions that have been expressed in it.
§ 3 . Furthermore, whenever, due to the nature of the case or the evidence, the reputation of others may be jeopardized by the disclosure of the records or evidence, or quarrels may arise or a scandal or other similar inconvenience may be caused, the judge may require witnesses and experts, as well as the parties and their lawyers or attorneys, to maintain secrecy under oath.
1609. § 1 . When the court is collegial, the president will establish the day and time in which the judges must meet to deliberate; and, unless a special cause advises otherwise, the meeting will be held at the same headquarters of the court.
§ 2 . On the appointed day, each judge will present his written conclusions on the subject of the litigation, with the reasons on which they are based, both in law and in fact; and these conclusions, which must be kept secret, will be added to the minutes of the case.
§ 3 . After invoking the Name of God, reading the conclusions of each one in order of precedence, but always starting with the rapporteur or rapporteur of the case, there is a discussion, under the direction of the president of the court, especially to determine what should be established in the operative part of the sentence.
§ 4 . In the discussion any judge can modify his previous conclusion. But the judge who does not want to join the decision of the others can demand that, if there is an appeal, his conclusions be transmitted to the higher court.
§ 5 . If the judges are unwilling or unable to issue a ruling in the first discussion, the decision may be deferred until a new meeting, but not for more than one week, unless the investigation of the case is to be completed in accordance with c. 1600.

1610
1610. § 1 . If the judge is the only one, he will write the sentence himself.
§ 2 . In the collegiate court, it is up to the rapporteur or rapporteur to write the sentence, taking the reasons from among those that the judges presented in the discussion unless the majority of the judges expressly determines the reasons that are to be preferred; The sentence must then be submitted to the approval of each of them.
§ 3 . The sentence must be given within one month from the day on which the case was defined, unless, for a serious reason, the judges of a collegiate court establish a longer period.

1611
1611. The sentence must:
1 settle the controversy discussed before the court, giving each doubt the appropriate answer;
2 determine what the obligations of the parties derived from the trial are, and how they are to be fulfilled;
3 present the reasons or motives, both legal and factual, on which the operative part of the sentence is based;
4 determine what refers to the costs of the litigation.

1612
1612. § 1 . After invoking the Name of God, the sentence must state, in order, who is the judge or the court; who are the plaintiff, the defendant and the attorney, indicating their names and addresses; as well as the promoter of justice and the defender of the bond, if they took part in the trial.
§ 2 . Afterwards, you must briefly explain the fact in question, the conclusions of the parties and the formula of the doubts.
§ 3 . The operative part of the sentence will follow, preceded by the reasons on which it is based.
§ 4 . It concludes with the indication of the day and place in which it was issued, with the signature of the judge or of all the judges, if the court is collegial, and of the notary.
1612. § 4, the sentence must be corrected or completed at the request of a party, or ex officio, by the same court that issued it, but always after hearing the parties and adding a decree at the bottom of the sentence.
§ 2 . If either party objects, the incidental issue will be decided by decree.

1613
1613. The rules set out above regarding the final sentence must also be adapted to the interlocutory sentence.

1614
1614. The ruling must be published as soon as possible, indicating how it can be challenged; and does not produce any effect before its publication, even when the operative part has been notified to the parties, with the permission of the judge.

1615
1615. The publication or notification of the judgment can be done either by delivering a copy of it to the parties or their attorneys, or by sending it in accordance with c. 1509.

1616
1616. § 1 . If in the text of the ruling there was a calculation error, or a material error had slipped in the transcription of the operative part or in the presentation of the facts or the requests of the parties, or the requirements of the c

1617
1617. Outside of the sentence, the other pronouncements of the judge are decrees, which, unless they are mere formalities, lack any effectiveness if they do not state, at least in a summary manner, the reasons, or do not refer to reasons already expressed in another act.

1618
1618. The interlocutory sentence or the decree has the force of a final sentence if it prevents or puts an end to the trial or an instance thereof, at least as regards one of the parties in the case.

1619
1619. Whenever it is a case that refers to the good of private persons, the nullities of the acts established by positive law that, being known by the party proposing the complaint, have not been reported to the judge before the sentence are healed by the sentence, remaining in place what is prescribed by the cc.

1620
1620. The sentence suffers from an irremediable defect of nullity if:
1 was ruled by a completely incompetent judge;
2 was issued by someone who lacks the power to judge in the court before which the case has been tried;
3 the judge issued the sentence under duress due to violence or serious fear;
4 the trial has been carried out without the judicial request referred to in c. 1501, or was not filed against any defendant;
5 occurred between parties of which at least one does not have the capacity to act in court;
6 someone acted on behalf of another without a legitimate mandate;
7 one of the two parties was denied the right of defense;
8 did not settle the controversy, not even partially.
1620. can be proposed perpetually as an exception and as an action, within a period of ten years from the date of the sentence, before the judge who issued it.

1621
1621. The annulment complaint referred to in the c

1622
1622. and 1623.
1622. The sentence suffers from a defect of curable nullity, exclusively if:
1 has been given by a non-legitimate number of judges, contrary to what is prescribed in c.

1623
1623. In the cases referred to in c. 1622, the annulment complaint can be filed within a period of three months from when the publication of the ruling became known.

1624
1624. The annulment complaint is examined by the same judge who issued the sentence; but if the party fears that said judge is prejudiced and, therefore, considers him suspicious, he may demand that he be replaced by another judge, in accordance with c. 1450.

1625
1625. The annulment complaint may be proposed together with the appeal, within the period established for it.

1626
1626. § 1 . Not only the parties who consider themselves harmed, but also the promoter of justice or the defender of the bond can file an annulment complaint, when they have the right to intervene.
§ 2 . The same judge may revoke or amend ex officio the null sentence he issued, within the period determined in c. 1623, unless, in the meantime, an appeal has been filed together with the complaint for nullity, or the nullity has been corrected due to expiration of the period indicated in c. 1623.

1627
1627. Cases involving a nullity complaint can be dealt with according to the rules of the oral contentious process.

1628
1628. The party that considers itself harmed by a sentence, as well as the promoter of justice and the defender of the bond in cases that require their presence, have the right to appeal to the superior judge against the sentence, subject to what is prescribed in c. 1629.

1629
1629. There is no appeal:
1 against the sentence of the Supreme Pontiff himself or of the Apostolic Signature;
2 against the sentence that suffers from a defect of nullity, unless the appeal is joined with the complaint for nullity, in accordance with c. 1625;
3 against the sentence that has become res judicata;
4 against the judge's decree or interlocutory sentence that does not have the force of a final sentence, unless it is combined with the appeal against the final sentence;
5 against the sentence or decree in a case that according to the law must be resolved as quickly as possible.

1630
1630. § 1 . The appeal must be filed before the judge who handed down the sentence, within the peremptory period of fifteen business days from when the publication of the sentence became known.
§ 2 . If it is filed orally, the notary will draft it in writing in the presence of the appellant.

1631
1631. If a question arises regarding the right to appeal, it must be resolved as quickly as possible by the court of appeal, according to the rules on the oral contentious process.

1632
1632. § 1 . If the appeal does not indicate which court it is addressed to, it is presumed to have been made to the court that the ccs deal with.

1633
1633. The appeal must be continued before the judge ad quem within a period of one month from when it was filed, unless the judge a quo had granted the party a longer period to pursue it.

1634
1634. § 1 . To continue the appeal, it is required and sufficient for the party to invoke the intervention of the superior judge to correct the contested sentence, attaching a copy of it and indicating the reasons why it is appealing.
§ 2 . But if the party cannot obtain from the court a quo a copy of the contested ruling in a timely manner, the deadlines do not run in the meantime, and said impediment must be notified to the judge of appeal who must order the judge a quo to fulfill his obligation as soon as possible.
§ 3 . Meanwhile, the judge a quo must send the minutes to the judge of appeal, in accordance with c. 1474.

1635
1635. Once the deadlines for appeal before the judges a quo or ad quem have elapsed in vain, the appeal is considered void.

1636
1636. § 1 . The person who has appealed may waive the appeal, with the effects specified in c. 1525.
§ 2 . If the appeal has been filed by the defender of the bond or by the promoter of justice, the waiver may proceed, if the law does not establish otherwise, by the defender of the bond or the promoter of justice of the court of appeal.

1637
1637. § 1 . The plaintiff's appeal also benefits the defendant, and vice versa.
§ 2 . If there are several defendants or actors and only one or against one of them challenges the ruling, it is considered that the challenge has been filed by all and against all, provided that the thing requested is indivisible or is a joint obligation.
§ 3 . If a party appeals on any chapter of the sentence, the opposing party, even if the deadline for appealing has elapsed, may do so incidentally on other chapters of the sentence, within the peremptory period of fifteen days from when the main appeal was notified.
§ 4 . Unless otherwise stated, the appeal is presumed to have been made against all chapters of the sentence.

1638
1638. The appeal suspends the execution of the sentence.

1639
1639. § 1 . Except as provided by c. 1683, at the level of appeal a new cause of action cannot be admitted, not even as a useful accumulation; Therefore, the response can only address whether the previous ruling is confirmed or reformed in whole or in part.
§ 2 . New evidence is only admitted in accordance with c. 1600.

1640
1640. At the level of appeal, the procedure must be followed, with due adaptations, in the same way as in the first instance, but, unless the evidence must be completed, immediately after the response, made in accordance with the cc

1641
1641. Remaining safe from what is prescribed in c. 1643, res judicata occurs:
1 if there are two conforming rulings between the same litigants, on the same request made for the same reasons;
2 if an appeal against the sentence had not been filed within the useful period;
3 if, at the level of appeal, the instance had expired or had been waived;
4 if a final sentence was handed down, against which there is no appeal, in accordance with c. 1629.

1642
1642. § 1 . Res judicata enjoys the finality of the law, and cannot be challenged directly, if it is not in accordance with the c

1643
1643. Cases regarding the status of persons, including separation of spouses, never become res judicata.

1644
1644. § 1 . If two consistent sentences are pronounced in a case regarding the status of persons, an appeal may be made at any time to the court of appeal, alleging new and serious evidence or reasons, within the peremptory period of thirty days from when the challenge was proposed. And, within one month from the presentation of the new evidence and reasons, the appeal court must decide by decree whether or not to admit the new proposition of the case.
§ 2 . The petition to the superior court to obtain a new proposal of the case does not suspend the execution of the sentence, unless the law establishes otherwise or the court of appeal orders that it be suspended in accordance with c.

1645
1645. § 2, 1 -3.
§ 2 . The judge can, however, order or allow a document to be presented, which perhaps could not be presented before through no fault of the interested party.
§ 3 . The new tests must be published, complying with c.
1645. § 1 .
§ 2 . It makes law between the parties and gives rise to action and with the exception of res judicata, which the judge can also declare ex officio to prevent the same case from being reintroduced.
1645. § 1 . Restitution in integrum is possible against a sentence that has become res judicata, as long as its injustice is clearly established.
§ 2 . Only injustice is considered manifest:
1 if the sentence is so based on evidence, which has subsequently been discovered to be false, that without such evidence the operative part of the sentence becomes unsustainable;
2 if documents are subsequently discovered that unequivocally prove new facts that require a contrary decision;
3 if the sentence has been caused by fraud on the part of one party and damage to the other;
4 if it is evident that the prescription of a law that is not merely procedural has been disregarded;
5 if the sentence contradicts a preceding decision that has become res judicata.
1645. § 2 , 1-3, the judge who handed down the sentence must be requested within a period of three months, starting from the day on which those reasons became known.
§ 2 . Restitution in integrum for the reasons indicated in c.
1645. § 2 , 4 and 5, the appeal court must be requested within three months from when the publication of the ruling became known; but in the case of c. 1645
§ 2, 5, if the preceding decision became known later, the period begins to run from then on.
§ 3 . The deadlines established above do not begin to be computed while the injured party is a minor.

1646
1646. § 1 . Restitution in integrum for the reasons indicated in the c

1647
1647. § 1 . The request for restitution in integrum suspends the execution of the sentence if it has not yet begun to be carried out.
§ 2 . However, when due to probable indications it is suspected that the request has been made to delay execution, the judge may order that the sentence be executed, giving the appropriate guarantees to the petitioner in the event that restitution in integrum is granted.

1648
1648. Once restitution in integrum has been granted, the judge must rule on the substance of the case.

1649
1649. § 1 . The Bishop, who is responsible for moderating the tribunal, must dictate norms regarding:
1 the order of the parties to pay or compensate legal costs;
2 the fees of attorneys, lawyers, experts and interpreters, as well as compensation for witnesses;
3 the granting of free sponsorship or reduced costs;
4 the compensation for damages owed by the person who not only lost the lawsuit, but also litigated recklessly;
5 the deposit of money or guarantee that must be provided for the payment of costs and compensation for damages.
§ 2 . There is no separate appeal against the ruling on costs, fees and compensation for damages, but the party may appeal within fifteen days, before the same judge, who may modify the assessment.

1650
1650. § 3 .
1650. § 1 . A sentence that has become res judicata may be executed, except as prescribed in c. 1647.
§ 2 . The judge who handed down the sentence, and also the appeal judge, where appropriate, may order ex officio or at the request of a party the provisional execution of a sentence that has not yet become res judicata, when it concerns provisions or benefits ordered for necessary support, or when another just cause is urgent, establishing the appropriate guarantees, if necessary.
§ 3 . When the ruling in question is challenged in the § 2, if the judge who must decide on the challenge sees that it has a probable basis and that irreparable damage may result from the execution, he may suspend the execution or make it subject to the provision of a guarantee.

1651
1651. Execution cannot proceed before obtaining the executive decree of the judge, by which he orders that the sentence be executed; and, depending on the nature of the case, this decree may be included in the same sentence or given separately.

1652
1652. If the execution of the sentence requires prior rendering of accounts, an incidental question arises, which must be decided by the same judge who issued the sentence whose execution is in question.

1653
1653. § 1 . Unless the particular law establishes otherwise, the Bishop of the diocese in which the first degree sentence was issued must order it to be executed, personally or through another.
§ 2 . If this refuses or is negligent, the execution, at the request of the interested party or also ex officio, corresponds to the authority to which, according to c.

1654
1654. § 1 . The executor must execute the sentence according to the obvious meaning of his words, unless something was left to his discretion in the sentence itself.
§ 2 . The executor can decide on exceptions regarding the manner and effectiveness of the execution, but not about the substance of the cause; but if you know on the other hand that the sentence is null or manifestly unjust, in accordance with cc. 1620, 1622, 1645, must refrain from executing it and refer the matter to the court that issued the sentence, notifying the parties.

1655
1655. § 1 . As regards real actions, when something has been awarded to the actor, it will be delivered to him as soon as res judicata occurs.
§ 2 . But with regard to personal actions, when the defendant has been sentenced to deliver a movable thing, or to pay an amount of money, or to give or do something else, the judge in the same sentence or the executor, according to his discretion and prudence, will determine a period of time to fulfill the obligation, not less than fifteen days nor more than six months.

1656
1656. § 1 . Through the oral litigation process regulated in this section, all causes not excluded by law can be dealt with, unless one of the parties requests that the ordinary litigation process be followed.
§ 2 . Judicial acts are void if the oral process is used outside the cases permitted by law.

1657
1657. The oral contentious process is carried out in the first degree before a single judge, in accordance with c. 1424.

1658
1658. § 1 . In addition to what is indicated in c. 1504, the statement of claim must:
1 state briefly, completely and clearly the facts on which the actor's requests are based;
2 indicate the evidence by which the actor intends to demonstrate the facts and that he cannot provide with the claim, so that the judge can collect them immediately.
§ 2 . The documents on which the request is based must be added to the application, at least in an authentic copy.

1659
1659. § 1 . When the attempt at conciliation in accordance with the c
1659. and 1660, and in view of the minutes, the judge will determine the formula of the doubt; and then summon all those who must attend the hearing, which must be held within a period of no more than thirty days, communicating to the parties the formula of the doubt.
§ 2 . In the summons, advise the parties that, at least three days before the hearing, they may submit to the court a brief brief to demonstrate their claims.

1660
1660. If the exceptions proposed by the defendant require it, the judge will set a deadline for the actor to respond, so that, in view of the allegations of both parties, he can take charge of the object of the controversy.

1661
1661. § 1 . After the deadlines indicated in the cc

1662
1662. At the hearing, the issues referred to in ccs must be addressed first. 1459-1464.

1663
1663. § 1 . The evidence is collected at the hearing, except as determined in c. 1418.
§ 2 . Each party and its lawyer may attend the examination of the others and of witnesses and experts.

1664
1664. The notary must collect in writing the responses of the parties, the witnesses and the experts, as well as the requests and exceptions of the lawyers, but in a summarized manner and only as pertinent to the substance of the litigation; and these writings must be signed by the declarants.

1665
1665. Evidence that has not been presented or requested in the petition or response can only be admitted by the judge in accordance with c. 1452; but after even a single witness has been heard, the judge can only order other evidence in accordance with c. 1600.

1666
1666. If it is not possible to collect all the evidence in a hearing, another hearing will be called.

1667
1667. Once the evidence is gathered, the oral discussion will proceed in the same hearing.

1668
1668. § 1 . If the discussion does not reveal the need for a supplementary instruction or the existence of another impediment to handing down a sentence, the judge must decide the case immediately, at the end of the hearing and alone; and the operative part of the sentence must be read immediately before the parties present.
§ 2 . Due to the difficulty of the matter or other just cause, the court may defer the sentence for five business days.
§ 3 . The full text of the ruling must be notified to the parties as soon as possible, stating the reasons, ordinarily within a period of no more than fifteen days.

1669
1669. If the court of appeal observes that at the lower level the oral contentious process has been used in a case excluded by law, it must declare the sentence null and void and return the case to the court that issued that sentence.

1670
1670. In other matters relating to the procedure, the rules on the ordinary contentious trial must be observed. However, the court may, by reasoned decree, and without detriment to justice, repeal procedural rules whose compliance is not required for validity, in order to achieve greater speed.

1671
1671. § 1 . The matrimonial causes of the baptized correspond to the ecclesiastical judge in his own right.
§ 2 . Causes regarding the merely civil effects of marriage belong to the civil judge, unless particular law establishes that such causes can be treated and decided by the ecclesiastical judge when they are raised in an incidental and accessory manner.
1671. The matrimonial causes of the baptized correspond to the ecclesiastical judge in his own right.

1672
1672. For causes of annulment of marriage not reserved to the Apostolic See, the following are competent: 1° the court of the place where the marriage was celebrated; 2° the court of the place in which one or both parties have their domicile or quasi-domicile; 3° the court of the place where the majority of the evidence is to be collected.
1672. Causes regarding the merely civil effects of marriage belong to the civil judge, unless particular law establishes that such causes can be treated and decided by the ecclesiastical judge when they are raised in an incidental and accessory manner.

1673
1673. § 1 . In each diocese the judge of first instance for causes of marriage annulment, for which the law does not expressly make an exception, is the diocesan Bishop, who can exercise judicial power himself or through others, in accordance with the law.
§ 2 . The Bishop constitutes for his diocese the diocesan court for causes of marriage annulment, leaving aside the right for the same Bishop to access another nearby court, diocesan or interdiocesan.
§ 3 . Causes of marriage annulment are reserved to a panel of three judges. This must be presided over by a clerical judge, the other judges can also be laymen.
§ 4 . The Moderating Bishop, if it is not possible to establish the collegiate court in the diocese or in the nearby court that has been chosen in accordance with § 2, entrusts the cases to a single judge, a clergyman, who, where possible, associates two advisors of exemplary life, experts in legal or human sciences, approved by the Bishop for this task; The same single judge is responsible, unless it is otherwise, of the functions attributed to the college, the president or the rapporteur.
§ 5 . The court of second instance, for validity, must always be collegial, as provided in the § 3.
§ 6 . From the court of first instance an appeal is made to the metropolitan court of second instance, except as provided in canons 1438-1439 and 1444.

[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Mitis Iudex Dominus Iesus, dated August 15, 2015)]:
Art. 1 — COMPETENT COURT
1673. For causes of marriage annulment not reserved for the Apostolic See, the following are competent:
1 the court of the place where the marriage took place;
2 the court of the place where the defendant has his domicile or quasi-domicile;
3 the court of the place where the plaintiff has his domicile, provided that both parties reside in the territory of the same Episcopal Conference and the judicial Vicar of the domicile of the defendant gives his consent, having heard the latter;
4 the court of the place where the majority of the evidence is actually to be collected, provided that it is consented to by the Judicial Vicar of the domicile of the defendant, after consulting the defendant in case he has any objection.
1673. § 4 .
§ 4 . If, however, the shorter process is ordered, the Judicial Vicar will proceed in accordance with Can. 1685.
§ 5 . The formula of doubt must determine by which chapter or chapters the validity of the nuptials is challenged.

1674
1674. § 1 . nThey are able to challenge the marriage:
1° the spouses; 2° the promoter of justice, when the annulment has already been disclosed if it is not possible or convenient to validate the marriage.
§ 2 . A marriage that was not accused during the lifetime of both spouses cannot be challenged after the death of one or both of them, unless the question of its validity is prejudicial to resolving another controversy, either in the canonical jurisdiction or in the civil jurisdiction.
§ 3 . If the spouse dies while the case is pending, the provisions of the Can. 1518 must be observed.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Mitis Iudex Dominus Iesus, dated August 15, 2015)]:
1674. The following are competent to challenge the marriage:
2 the promoter of justice, when the annulment has already been disclosed if it is not possible or convenient to validate the marriage.

1675
1675. § 1 . A marriage that was not accused during the lifetime of both spouses cannot be challenged after the death of one or both of them, unless the question of its validity is prejudicial to resolving another controversy, either in the canonical jurisdiction or in the civil jurisdiction.
§ 2 . If the spouse dies while the case is pending, the provisions of c. 1518
1675. The judge, before accepting a case, must be certain that the marriage has irreparably failed, so that it is impossible to reestablish conjugal coexistence.

1676
1676. § 1 . Once the claim is received, the Judicial Vicar, if he considers that it has some basis, admits it and, with a decree attached to the foot of the claim, orders that a copy be notified to the defender of the bond and, if the claim has not been signed by both parties, to the defendant, giving him a period of fifteen days to express his position regarding the claim.
§ 2 . Once the predicted period has elapsed, after having reprimanded the other party, if he sees it appropriate and to the extent he deems it appropriate, so that he can express his position, after hearing the defender of the bond, the judicial Vicar with his decree determines the formula of doubts and establishes whether the cause should be treated with the shortest process in accordance with canons 1683-1687. This decree must be notified immediately to the parties and the defender of the bond.
§ 3 . If the case must be treated with the ordinary process, the Judicial Vicar, with the same decree, provides for the constitution of the college of judges or the sole judge with the two advisors according to Can. 1676. Before accepting a case and whenever he sees some hope of success, the judge will use pastoral means to induce the spouses, if possible, to validate their marriage and reestablish conjugal coexistence.

1677
1677. § 1 . The defender of the bond, the lawyers and also the promoter of justice, if he intervenes in the trial, have the right: 1° to attend the examination of the parties, the witnesses and the experts, subject to what is prescribed by the Can. 1559; 2° to know the judicial records, even when they are not published, and to examine the documents presented by the parties.
§ 2 . The parties cannot attend the exam discussed in § 1, n. 1.
1677. § 1 . Once the demand is accepted, the president or the rapporteur will proceed to notify the summons decree, in accordance with c. 1508.
§ 2 . Fifteen days after notification, the president or rapporteur, unless one of the parties has requested a session to answer the claim, within ten days will determine by decree and ex officio the formula of the doubt or doubts, and will notify the parties.
§ 3 . The doubt formula must not only raise whether the nullity of the marriage is established in the case in question, but also specify by which chapter or chapters its validity is challenged.
§ 4 . After ten days from the notification of the decree, if the parties have not objected anything, the president or the rapporteur will order the investigation of the case with a new decree.

1678
1678. § 1 . In cases of annulment of marriage, the judicial confession and the statements of the parties, supported by possible witnesses regarding their credibility, may have the value of full evidence, which the judge must evaluate considering all the evidence and devices, if there are no other elements that refute them.
§ 2 . In the same cases, the deposition of a single witness may have full probative force, if it is a qualified witness who testifies about what he has done based on his job, or if the objective or subjective circumstances so suggest.
§ 3 . In cases involving impotence or lack of consent due to mental illness or an anomaly of a psychic nature, the judge will use one or more experts, unless, due to the circumstances, there is evidence that this expertise will be useless; In other cases, what is indicated in Can. 1574 must be observed.
§ 4 . When a very probable doubt arises in the investigation of the case that the consummation of the marriage has not occurred, the court may, after hearing the parties, suspend the cause of annulment, carry out the investigation of the process for the dispensation of the marriage for a while, and then transmit the minutes to the Apostolic See together with the request for dispensation made by both spouses or by one of them, and with the vote of the court and the Bishop.
(n Indicates that the text corresponds to the new version)
[Original wording of the canons modified by His Holiness Pope Francis (cf. Apostolic Letter in the form of Motu Proprio Mitis Iudex Dominus Iesus, dated August 15, 2015)]:
Art. 3 — OF THE OFFICE OF JUDGES
1678. § 1 . The defender of the bond, the lawyers and also the promoter of justice if he intervenes in the trial, have the right:
1 to attend the examination of the parties, witnesses and experts, keeping in mind what is prescribed in c. 1559.
2 to know the judicial records, even when they are not published, and to examine the documents presented by the parties.
§ 2 . The parties cannot attend the exam discussed in § 1, 1.

1679
1679. The sentence that for the first time declared the nullity of the marriage, once the terms established in canons 1630-1633 have been fulfilled, becomes executive.
1679. Unless the evidence is otherwise complete, to evaluate the statements of the parties in accordance with c. 1536, the judge must require, if possible, witnesses to testify about the credibility of the parties; and will also use other clues and gadgets.

1680
1680. § 1 . The right of the party who considers himself harmed, as well as the promoter of justice and the defender of the bond, to file a complaint for annulment or appeal against the same sentence, remains intact, according to canons 1619-1640.
§ 2 . Once the terms established by law for the appeal and its prosecution have elapsed, after the court of the higher instance has received the judicial records, the college of judges is constituted, the defender of the bond is appointed and the parties are warned to present observations within an established period; After that period, the collegiate court, if it is evident that the appeal is merely dilatory, confirms the first instance ruling with a decree.
§ 3 . If the appeal has been admitted, the same procedure must be followed as in the first instance, with the appropriate adaptations.
§ 4 . If in the level of appeal a new chapter is adduced requesting the declaration of nullity of a marriage, the court of appeal may admit it and judge about it as in the first instance.
1680. In cases involving impotence or lack of consent due to mental illness, the judge will use one or more experts, unless, due to the circumstances, there is evidence that this expertise will be useless; In other cases, what c. indicates must be observed. 1574.

1681
1681. If an executive sentence has been pronounced, an appeal may be made at any time to the third degree court for the new proposal of the case in accordance with the Can. 1644, alleging new and serious evidence and reasons, within the peremptory period of thirty days from the challenge.
1681. When during the investigation of the case a very probable doubt arises that the consummation of the marriage has not occurred, the court may, suspending the cause of annulment with the consent of the parties, carry out the investigation of the process for the dispensation of the marriage for a while, and then transmit the minutes to the Apostolic See together with the request for dispensation made by both spouses or by one of them, and with the vote of the court and the Bishop.

1682
1682. § 1 . After the sentence declaring the nullity of the marriage has become enforceable, the parties whose marriage has been declared null may contract new marriages, unless this is prohibited by a veto included in the same sentence, or established by the local Ordinary.
1682. § 1 . The ruling declaring for the first time the annulment of a marriage, together with the appeals, if any, and other minutes of the process, must be transmitted ex officio to the court of appeal within a period of twenty days from the publication of the ruling.
§ 2 . If the ruling in favor of annulment has been issued in the first instance, the court of appeal, considering the observations of the defender of the bond and, if any, also those of the parties, must, by decree, either confirm the decision without delay or admit the case so that it can be examined with ordinary procedures in the new instance.
1682. § 2 .

1683
1683. The same Bishop is responsible for judging the causes of nullity whenever:
1° the request has been proposed by both spouses or by one of them, with the consent of the other;
2° there are circumstances of the people and the facts, supported by testimonies or documents, that do not require an investigation or a more precise instruction, and make the nullity evident.
1683. If in the level of appeal a new chapter is adduced requesting the declaration of nullity of a marriage, the court of appeal may admit it and judge about it as in the first instance.

1684
1684. The document of claim with which the briefest process is introduced, in addition to the elements listed in the Can. 1504, must: 1° briefly state, in a comprehensive and clear manner, the facts on which the petition is based; 2° indicate the evidence that can be immediately collected by the judge; 3° display as attachments the documents on which the request is based.
1684. § 1 . When the sentence that first declared the nullity of a marriage has been confirmed at the level of appeal by decree or new sentence, those whose marriage has been declared null may contract new marriages from the moment in which they have been notified of the decree or new sentence, unless this is prohibited by a veto included in the sentence or decree, or established by the local Ordinary.
§ 2 . The prescriptions of c. 1644 must be observed even if the sentence declaring the nullity of the marriage had been confirmed not with another sentence, but by decree.

1685
1685. The judicial Vicar, with the same decree with which he determines the formula of doubts, appoints the instructor and the advisor, and summons to the session, which must be held in accordance with Can. 1686, no more than thirty days, all those who must participate.
1685. As soon as the sentence has been made enforceable, the judicial Vicar must notify it to the Ordinary of the place where the marriage was celebrated. And he must ensure that the nullity that has been declared and the prohibitions that may have been added are noted as soon as possible in the marriage book and in the baptism book.

1686
1686. The instructor, to the extent possible, collects the evidence in a single session, and sets a period of fifteen days for the presentation of observations in favor of the bond and the defenses of the parties, if any.
1686. Once the request made in accordance with c. 1677, the judicial Vicar or the judge designated by him may declare by sentence the annulment of a marriage, omitting the solemnities of the ordinary process but summoning the parties and with the intervention of the defender of the bond, if a document to which no objection or exception can be opposed establishes with certainty the existence of a decisive impediment or the defect of legitimate form, as long as it is stated with equal certainty that no dispensation was granted, or that the attorney lacks a valid mandate.
1686. or the lack of dispensation are not true, you must appeal against this declaration to the judge of second instance, to whom the records must be sent, warning him in writing that this is a documentary process.
§ 2 . The party that considers itself aggrieved retains the right to appeal intact.
1686. whether the sentence should be confirmed or rather whether the case should proceed according to the ordinary legal procedure; and, in this case, it will be referred to the court of first instance.

1687
1687. § 1 . Having received the minutes, the diocesan Bishop, consulting the instructor and the advisor, examining the observations of the defender of the bond and, if they exist, the defenses of the parties, if he reaches moral certainty about the nullity of the marriage, gives the sentence. Otherwise, refer the case to the ordinary process.
§ 2 . The full text of the ruling, with the reasons, must be notified to the parties as soon as possible.
§ 3 . Against the Bishop's sentence an appeal is made to the Metropolitan or to the Roman Rota; If the sentence was given by the Metropolitan, an appeal is made to the oldest suffragan; and against the sentence of another Bishop who has no other superior authority below the Roman Pontiff, an appeal is made to the Bishop stably appointed by him.
§ 4 . If it becomes evident that the appeal is merely dilatory, the Metropolitan or the Bishop mentioned in the § 3, or the Dean of the Roman Rota, will reject it by decree from the first moment; If, however, the appeal is admitted, the case is sent to the ordinary examination in the second degree.

Art. 5 — JUDGMENT AND APPEAL
1687. § 1 . If the defender of the bond prudently considers that the defects indicated in the c

1688
1688. Once the request made in accordance with the Can. 1676 has been received, the diocesan Bishop, or the judicial Vicar or the designated judge, may declare by means of a ruling the annulment of a marriage, omitting the solemnities of the ordinary process, but summoning the parties and with the intervention of the defender of the bond, if a document to which no objection or exception can be opposed establishes with certainty the existence of a disposing impediment or the defect in legitimate form, provided that It is stated with equal certainty that no dispensation was granted, or that the attorney lacks a valid mandate.
1688. or the lack of dispensation are not true, you must appeal against this declaration to the judge of second instance, to whom the records must be sent, warning him in writing that this is a documentary process.
§ 2 . The party that considers itself aggrieved retains the right to appeal intact.

Art. 6 — THE DOCUMENTARY PROCESS
1688. The judge of second instance, with the intervention of the defender of the bond and having heard the parties, will decide in the manner indicated in the c

1689
1689. § 1 . If the defender of the bond prudently considers that the defects indicated in the can
1689. In the sentence, the parties must be warned about the moral or even civil obligations that may weigh on them with respect to the other party and their children, with regard to sustenance and education.

1690
1690. The judge of second instance, with the intervention of the defender of the bond and having heard the parties, will decide in the manner indicated in Can. 1690. Causes for declaration of marriage nullity cannot be processed through the oral contentious process.

1691
1691. § 1 . In the sentence, the parties must be warned about the moral or even civil obligations that they may have with respect to the other party and their children, with regard to sustenance and education.
§ 2 . The causes of declaration of nullity of marriage cannot be processed through the oral contentious process referred to in canons 1656-1670.
§ 3 . In other things that refer to the procedure, if the nature of the matter does not prevent it, the canons on trials in general and on the ordinary contentious trial must be applied, complying with the special rules for causes about the state of people and for those that refer to the public good.
