Art. 7 — GENERAL RULES
1691. In other things that refer to the procedure, if the nature of the matter does not prevent it, the canons on trials in general and on the ordinary contentious trial must be applied, complying with the special rules for causes about the state of people and for those that refer to the public good.

1692
1692. § 1 . Unless otherwise legitimately provided for in a given place, the personal separation of baptized spouses can be decided by decree of the diocesan Bishop, or by ruling of the judge, in accordance with the canons that follow.
§ 2 . Where the ecclesiastical decision does not produce civil effects, or if it is anticipated that the civil sentence will not be contrary to divine right, the Bishop of the diocese of residence of the spouses, taking into account peculiar circumstances, may grant permission to resort to civil jurisdiction.
§ 3 . If the case also concerns the purely civil effects of the marriage, the judge ensures that, in compliance with the provisions of the § 2, the case is brought to the civil jurisdiction from the first moment.

1693
1693. § 1 . If one of the parties or the promoter of justice does not request the ordinary contentious process, the oral contentious process will be followed.
§ 2 . If the ordinary contentious process has been followed and there is an appeal, the court of second instance will proceed, with due proportions, in accordance with c.

1694
1694. Regarding the jurisdiction of the court, the provisions of c. 1673.

1695
1695. Before accepting a case and whenever there is hope of success, the judge must employ pastoral means so that the spouses are reconciled and induced to reestablish the conjugal community.

1696
1696. The causes of separation of spouses also affect the public good and, therefore, the promoter of justice must always intervene in them, in accordance with c. 1433.

1697
1697. Only the spouses, or one of them even if the other objects, have the right to request the grace of dispensation from temporary and unconsummated marriage.

1698
1698. § 1 . Only the Apostolic See judges the fact of the inconsummation of the marriage and the existence of just cause to grant the dispensation.
§ 2 . The dispensation is granted only by the Roman Pontiff.

1699
1699. § 1 . To receive the document requesting the dispensation, the diocesan Bishop of the domicile or quasi-domicile of the speaker is competent, who, if it is established that the request is founded, must order the instruction of the process.
§ 2 . But if the proposed case raises special legal or moral difficulties, the diocesan Bishop must consult the Apostolic See.
§ 3 . Against the decree by which the Bishop rejects the request, there is an appeal to the Apostolic See.

1700
1700. § 1 . Remaining in force what is mandated by c. 1681, the Bishop will entrust the investigation of these processes, stably or in each case, to the tribunal of his diocese or another diocese, or to a suitable priest.
§ 2 . But if a judicial claim was made for the declaration of nullity of that marriage, the instruction must be entrusted to the same court.

1701
1701. § 1 . The defender of the bond must always intervene in these processes.
§ 2 . A lawyer is not admitted, but, due to the difficulty of the case, the Bishop may allow the speaker or the defendant to use the collaboration of a legal expert.
1701. § 2 , may examine the minutes of the proceedings at the seat of the court, but not the vote of the Bishop, and consider whether any serious reason can be adduced that would allow the petition to be presented again.

1702
1702. During the investigation, both spouses must be heard, and to the extent possible, the canons on how to collect evidence in the ordinary contentious trial and in cases of annulment of marriage must be observed, provided that they can be reconciled with the nature of these processes.

1703
1703. § 1 . Minutes are not published; However, if the judge considers that a serious obstacle to the speaker's request or to the defendant's objection may arise from the evidence presented, he or she will prudently inform the interested party.
§ 2 . The judge may show the party that requests it a document presented or a testimony received and set a deadline for presenting conclusions.

1704
1704. § 1 . Once the instruction is concluded, the instructor will transmit all the minutes with the appropriate report to the Bishop, and he will express his vote regarding the truth both about the fact of the inconsummation and about the just cause for the dispensation and the opportunity for that grace to be granted.
§ 2 . Yes, according to c. 1700, the investigation of the process was entrusted to a foreign court, the observations in favor of the link must be made in that same court, but the vote referred to in § 1 corresponds to the Bishop who carried out the commission, to whom the instructor will deliver the appropriate report, along with the minutes.

1705
1705. § 1 . The Bishop will send all the minutes to the Apostolic See, along with his vote and the observations of the defender of the bond.
§ 2 . If, in the opinion of the Apostolic See, additional instruction is required, the Bishop will be informed, indicating the aspects on which it should address.
§ 3 . If in the rescript of the Apostolic See it is declared that, from what has been deduced, there is no evidence of inconsummation, the legal expert referred to in c.

1706
1706. The Apostolic See sends the rescript of dispensation to the Bishop; and he will notify the parties, and will also order as soon as possible the parish priests of the place where the marriage was celebrated and where they received the baptism to note in the books of marriages and baptized persons the dispensation granted.

1707
1707. § 1 . When the death of one spouse cannot be proven by an authentic document, ecclesiastical or civil, the other spouse cannot be considered free from the marriage bond before the diocesan Bishop has issued the declaration of presumed death.
§ 2 . The diocesan Bishop can only issue the declaration referred to in § 1 when, after carrying out the appropriate investigations, through the statements of witnesses, through fame or through evidence, he reaches moral certainty about the death of the spouse. The mere fact of the spouse's absence is not enough, even if it lasts for a long time.
§ 3 . In doubtful and complicated cases, the Bishop must consult the Apostolic See.

1708
1708. The clergyman himself, the Ordinary on whom he depends, or the person of the diocese where he was ordained have the right to accuse the validity of the sacred ordination.

1709
1709. § 1 . The prayers must be sent to the competent Congregation, which will decide whether the cause will be heard by the Congregation of the Roman Curia itself or by a tribunal designated by it.
§ 2 . Once the petition is sent, the clergyman is ipso jure prohibited from exercising his orders.

1710
1710. If the Congregation refers the case to a court, the canons on trials in general and on ordinary contentious trials must be observed, unless the nature of the matter prevents it, leaving the prescriptions of this title without prejudice.

1711
1711. In these cases, the defender of the bond enjoys the same rights and has the same obligations as the defender of the marriage bond.

1712
1712. After a second sentence confirming the nullity of the sacred ordination, the cleric loses all the rights of the clerical state and is free from all his obligations.

1713
1713. To avoid judicial disputes, it is useful to use compromise or reconciliation, or submit the controversy to the judgment of one or more arbitrators.

1714
1714. For the transaction, the commitment and the arbitration trial, the norms established by the parties will be observed or, failing this, the law given by the Episcopal Conference, if there is one, or the civil law in force in the place where the agreement is concluded.

1715
1715. § 1 . No transaction or commitment can be validly made on what belongs to the public good or on other things that the parties cannot freely dispose of.
§ 2 . When dealing with temporary ecclesiastical property, the solemnities required by law for the alienation of ecclesiastical property must be completed, whenever its purpose requires it.

1716
1716. § 1 . If the civil law does not recognize the effectiveness of the arbitration ruling that is not confirmed by the judge, for the arbitration ruling on an ecclesiastical controversy to be effective in the canonical jurisdiction, it also needs confirmation by the ecclesiastical judge of the place in which it was given.
§ 2 . If the civil law allows the challenge of the arbitration ruling before the civil judge, in the canonical jurisdiction the same challenge can be proposed before the ecclesiastical judge who is competent to judge the controversy in the first instance.

1717
1717. § 1 . Whenever the Ordinary has at least credible news of a crime, he must carefully investigate, personally or through a suitable person, the facts and their circumstances as well as the imputability, unless this investigation seems completely superfluous.
§ 2 . We must prevent someone's good reputation from being put at risk as a result of this investigation.
§ 3 . Whoever carries out the investigation has the same powers and identical obligations as the auditor in a process; and, if a judicial process is carried out afterwards, he cannot perform the role of the judge in it.

1718
1718. § 1 . When it is estimated that sufficient elements have already been gathered, the Ordinary determines:
1 whether the process to inflict or declare a penalty can be launched;
2 if it is appropriate to do so, keeping in mind c. 1341;
3 whether the judicial process should be used or, when the law does not prohibit it, proceeding by extrajudicial decree.
§ 2 . The Ordinary must revoke or modify the decree referred to in § 1, whenever, due to new elements arising, it seems to him that he should decide otherwise.
§ 3 . When giving the decrees referred to in §§ 1 and 2, it is appropriate that the Ordinary, according to his prudence, hear two judges or other legal experts.
§ 4 . Before making a determination in accordance with § 1, the Ordinary must consider whether, to avoid useless lawsuits, it is advisable that, with the consent of the parties, he himself or the investigator settle the matter regarding damages in accordance with equity.

1719
1719. If they are not required for the criminal process, the minutes of the investigation and the decrees of the Ordinary with which the investigation begins or concludes, as well as everything that precedes the investigation, must be kept in the secret archive of the curia.

1720
1720. If the Ordinary deems that it should be proceeded by extrajudicial decree:
1 will inform the accused of the accusation and the evidence, giving him the opportunity to defend himself, unless the accused, legitimately summoned, does not wish to appear;
2 must carefully weigh all evidence and arguments with two advisors;
3 if the crime is established with certainty and the criminal action has not been extinguished, a decree will be issued in accordance with cc. 1342-1350, setting out, at least briefly, the legal and factual reasons.

1721
1721. § 1 . If the Ordinary decrees that a criminal judicial process must be initiated, he will deliver the minutes of the investigation to the promoter of justice, so that he can present the accusatory document to the judge, in accordance with the cc

1722
1722. To avoid scandals, defend the freedom of witnesses and guarantee the course of justice, the Ordinary may, after hearing the promoter of justice and having summoned the accused, remove him, at any stage of the process, from the exercise of the sacred ministry or an ecclesiastical office or office, impose or prohibit him from residing in a place or territory, or also prohibit him from publicly receiving the Holy Eucharist, but all these provisions must be revoked when the cause that causes them ceases. motivated, and cease ipso jure to have force at the end of the criminal process.

1723
1723. § 1 . When summoning the prisoner, the judge must invite him to appoint a lawyer, in accordance with the c

1724
1724. § 1 . The promoter of justice may renounce the instance at any stage of the trial, by order or with the consent of the Ordinary who made the decision to initiate the process.
§ 2 . For the resignation to be valid, it must be accepted by the prisoner, unless he has been declared absent from the trial.

1725
1725. In the discussion of the case, whether in writing or orally, the accused always has the right to write or speak last, either personally or through his lawyer or attorney.

1726
1726. In any degree and phase of the criminal trial, if it is clearly established that the crime has not been committed by the accused, the judge must declare it so by means of a sentence and acquit the accused, even if it is at the same time stated that the criminal action has been extinguished.

1727
1727. § 1 . The prisoner may appeal, even when the sentence had not convicted him only because it was an optional sentence, or because the judge made use of the power mentioned in the ccs.

1728
1728. § 1 . Subject to the canons of this title, in the criminal trial the canons on trials in general and the ordinary contentious trial must be applied, if the nature of the matter does not prevent it, complying with the special rules regarding causes that refer to the public good.
§ 2 . The accused is not obliged to confess to the crime, nor can he be required to swear an oath.

1729
1729. § 1 . The injured party may exercise contentious action in the same criminal trial for compensation for the damages caused by the crime, in accordance with c. 1596.
§ 2 . The intervention of the injured party, referred to in § 1, is not admitted if it was not carried out in the first instance of the trial.
§ 3 . In a case for damages, the appeal is made in accordance with cc. 1628-1640, even when the appeal does not fit in the criminal process; and if both appeals are proposed, even if they are by different parties, a single appeal trial will be held, except as prescribed in c. 1730.

1730
1730. § 1 . To avoid excessive delays in the criminal trial, the judge may defer the judgment on damages until he has given a final ruling in the criminal trial.
§ 2 . The judge who has acted in this way must judge the damages after issuing a ruling in the criminal trial, even if it is still pending because a challenge has been filed, and also if the defendant has been acquitted for a reason that does not exempt him from the obligation to repair the damages caused.

1731
1731. Even if it has become res judicata, the sentence given in a criminal trial does not constitute a right with respect to the injured party, unless the latter had intervened in accordance with c. 1729.

1732
1732. What is established in the canons of this section on decrees must also apply to all singular administrative acts that occur in the external extrajudicial forum, except those that emanate directly from the Roman Pontiff himself or from the Ecumenical Council itself.

1733
1733. § 1 . It is highly desirable that, when someone considers themselves harmed by a decree, conflict between them and the author of the decree is avoided, and that efforts are made to reach an equitable solution by common agreement, even resorting to mediation and the efforts of prudent people, so that the controversy is avoided or resolved by an appropriate means.
§ 2 . The Episcopal Conference can order that in each diocese a department or council be permanently created, which, according to the norms of the same Conference, has the function of seeking and suggesting equitable solutions; and if the Conference does not adopt that decision, the Bishop may establish that council or department.
§ 3 . The department or council referred to in § 2 will act mainly when the revocation of a decree is requested under c. 1734 and before the deadlines for appeal have expired; But if the appeal against the decree has already been filed, the same Superior who judges it must exhort the appellant and the author of the decree to seek those solutions, as long as he hopes for a happy result.

1734
1734. § 1 . Before imposing an appeal, the interested party must request its author in writing to revoke or amend the decree; Once this request is made, the suspension of the execution of the decree is also automatically considered requested.
§ 2 . The request must be made within the peremptory period of ten working days from the legitimate notification of the decree.
§ 3 . The rules of §§ 1 and 2 are not valid when it comes to:
1 to appeal to the Bishop against the decrees given by the authorities that are subordinate to him;
2 to appeal against the decree that decides on a hierarchical appeal, unless this decision is issued by the Bishop;
3 to file the resources referred to in cc. 57 and 1735.
1734. produces the same effect.
§ 2 . In other cases, if, within ten days after receiving the request from c. 1734, the author of the decree does not decide to suspend its execution, this suspension can be provisionally requested from his hierarchical Superior, who has the power to grant it only for serious reasons and always taking care that the good of souls is not detrimental.
§ 3 . When the execution of a decree has been suspended in accordance with the § 2, if the appeal is subsequently filed, the person who must resolve it will decide whether the suspension should be confirmed or revoked, in accordance with c.
1734. § 3, run from the day on which the decree has been issued, and in other cases in accordance with c. 1735.
§ 3 . Even in cases in which the appeal does not ipso jure suspend the execution of the decree, nor has the suspension been decreed according to c.

1735
1735. If the author of the decree, within thirty days from receiving the petition mentioned in c. 1734, issues a new decree by which it corrects the previous one or decides that the petition should be rejected, the deadlines for appealing are counted from the date of the notification of the new decree; but if no decision has been made within thirty days, the period is counted from the thirtieth day.

1736
1736. § 1 . When in a matter the hierarchical appeal suspends the execution of a decree, the request of the c
1736. § 2, the Superior may, for serious reasons, order that the execution be suspended, ensuring that all harm to the good of souls is avoided.

1737
1737. § 3 .
§ 4 . If no appeal is filed against the decree within the prescribed period, the suspension of execution provisionally decided in accordance with §§ 1 or 2 therefore ceases.
1737. § 1 . Whoever considers himself harmed by a decree may appeal for any fair reason to the hierarchical superior of the person who issued the decree; The appeal can be filed before the same author of the decree who must immediately transmit it to the competent hierarchical superior.
§ 2 . The appeal must be filed within the peremptory period of fifteen working days, which, in the cases dealt with in c.

1738
1738. The appellant always has the right to use a lawyer or attorney, but avoiding useless delays; and even an ex-officio patron must be appointed, if the appellant lacks one and the Superior considers it necessary; but at any time the Superior may order the same appellant to appear for questioning.

1739
1739. As the case requires, the Superior who resolves the appeal may not only confirm or declare the decree null and void, but also rescind or revoke it or, if he deems it more appropriate, correct it, replace it with another or abrogate it.

1740
1740. When, for any reason, even without serious fault of the person concerned, the ministry of a parish priest is harmful or at least ineffective, he may be removed from his parish by the diocesan Bishop.

1742
1742. § 1 . If the file carried out confirms the existence of one of the causes indicated in c. 1740, the Bishop will discuss the matter with two parish priests belonging to the group stably designated for this purpose by the presbyteral council, at the proposal of the Bishop; and if he later judges that the removal should be carried out, he will paternally advise the parish priest to resign within a period of fifteen days, but for validity it is necessary that he indicate the cause and the arguments.
§ 2 . Concerning parish priests who are members of a religious institute or society of apostolic life, the provisions of c.
1742. § 1 unless, due to their impossibility, others have to be appointed;
3 will finally decide whether the parish priest has to be removed or not, and will immediately issue the pertinent decree.
1742. § 1 the reasons for and against the transfer; and if even then he considers that said transfer should be carried out, he will reiterate the paternal exhortations to the parish priest.

1743
1743. The parish priest can resign not only purely and simply, but also under a condition as long as it can be legitimately accepted by the Bishop, and he really accepts it.

1744
1744. § 1 . If the parish priest does not respond within the established period, the Bishop will reiterate the invitation, extending the useful period to respond.
§ 2 . If it is known to the Bishop that the parish priest received the second invitation and did not respond without being affected by an impediment, or if the parish priest refuses to resign without giving any reason, the Bishop will issue the decree of removal.

1745
1745. But if the parish priest challenges the alleged cause and its reasons, alleging reasons that the Bishop considers insufficient, he, in order to act validly:
1 will invite the parish priest so that, once the file has been examined, he presents his objections in writing and provides evidence to the contrary, if he has any;
2 after this and completing the file if necessary, he will study the matter with the parish priests referred to in c.

1746
1746. The Bishop must provide for the needs of the removed parish priest, either by entrusting him with another office, if he is suitable, or by means of a pension, as the case warrants and circumstances permit.

1747
1747. § 1 . The removed parish priest must refrain from exercising the parish function, leave the parish house free as soon as possible and hand over all the belongings of the parish to the person to whom the Bishop has entrusted it.
§ 2 . But if it is a case of a sick person, who cannot move without difficulty from the parish house to another place, the Bishop will allow him its use, which may be exclusive, while that need lasts.
§ 3 . While the appeal against the decree of removal is pending, the Bishop cannot appoint a new parish priest, but must provide temporarily through a parish administrator.

1748
1748. When the good of souls or the need or usefulness of the Church requires that a parish priest be transferred from the parish that he governs with fruit, to another parish or to another office, the Bishop will propose the transfer in writing, advising him to agree for the love of God and souls.

1749
1749. If the parish priest is not willing to follow the advice and exhortations of the Bishop, he must explain in writing his reasons for this.

1750
1750. If, despite the reasons given, the Bishop judges that he should not modify his decision, he will examine with two parish priests chosen according to the c

1751
1751. § 1 . Once these procedures are concluded, if the parish priest continues to refuse and the Bishop considers that the transfer should be made, he will issue the corresponding decree, providing that the parish will be vacant at the end of the period determined.
§ 2 . Once that period has elapsed unnecessarily, the parish will be declared vacant.

1752
1752. In cases of transfer, c. 1747, maintaining canonical equity and taking into account the salvation of souls, which must always be the supreme law in the Church.
